Process Scheduling

Like every time-sharing system, Linux achieves the effect of simultaneous execution by rapidly switching the CPU among runnable processes. Process switching (the mechanics of saving and restoring register state) was discussed in Chapter 3; this chapter addresses scheduling — the policy and algorithms that decide when to switch and which process to run next. Linux 7.0.12's scheduler bears almost no resemblance to the 2.6-era O(1) scheduler: it is now a modular class-based framework where the Earliest Eligible Virtual Deadline First (EEVDF) algorithm handles fair scheduling, a hard-deadline EDF class serves real-time workloads, and a BPF-extensible class (sched_ext) allows user-defined scheduling policies — all coordinated by topology-aware load balancing and energy-aware placement.

Scheduling Policy

The scheduling policy defines when Linux should preempt the current task, which runnable task should run next, and how much CPU time should be charged to each class of workload. Linux 7.0.12 no longer tries to guess “interactive” behavior with a pile of special-case heuristics; instead, the scheduler combines a strict class hierarchy with an EEVDF-based fair class so that latency-sensitive sleepers naturally become eligible sooner, while throughput-oriented jobs still receive their proportional share.

Before any of that machinery makes sense, it helps to see what it is being pulled between. A scheduler is not solving one problem; it is arbitrating between requirements that are mutually exclusive at the limit:

  • Responsiveness pulls against throughput. Reacting to a wakeup immediately means switching often, and every switch costs a pipeline flush, a TLB and cache disturbance, and time in the scheduler itself rather than in the workload.
  • Fairness pulls against determinism. A proportional-share algorithm deliberately gives everyone a turn, which is exactly the guarantee a real-time task cannot tolerate. The two cannot be reconciled inside one algorithm, only separated into different ones.
  • Policy pulls against mechanism. Each of these workloads wants a different selection rule, but they all need the same context-switch machinery, the same runqueue locking, and the same accounting.
  • Preemption pulls against kernel invariants. Deciding that a task should be preempted is cheap; being allowed to act on that decision is not, because the kernel may be holding locks or running in a context where switching is illegal.

Linux resolves the first two by refusing to compromise: it separates workloads into scheduling classes, each with its own selection rule, and orders the classes strictly. It resolves the third with the sched_class interface, and the fourth with a deliberate split between recording that a reschedule is due and performing it. The rest of this section works through five questions in that order:

  1. What is the scheduler actually being asked to optimize, and how do real workloads sort into categories a kernel can act on?
  2. What policies does Linux expose to user space, and what does each one actually promise?
  3. What do the various numbers called priority mean, and how do the user-visible ranges map onto the kernel-internal one?
  4. How does a scheduling decision travel from the event that triggers it to a context switch that acts on it?
  5. What determines how quickly that switch is permitted to happen, and how much CPU time each policy consumes once it does?

Version history. Nearly everything except the vocabulary. The 2.6 kernel had one scheduler with a built-in notion of priority, dynamic priority bonuses that tried to detect interactivity by measuring sleep time, and a fixed time slice per task. Linux 7.0.12 has none of these: interactivity heuristics were removed with CFS in 2.6.23 and the remaining latency machinery was replaced again by EEVDF in 6.6; the fixed time slice became a virtual deadline; and the single scheduler became six classes consulted in strict order, one of which (sched_ext, merged in 6.12) is not in the kernel at all but supplied at runtime as a BPF program. The preemption model has changed most recently of all: as of 7.0, x86_64 and ARM64 can no longer build the two low-preemption models this book’s predecessor would have described as the normal choice.

What the Policy Must Optimize

A modern general-purpose kernel has to satisfy incompatible goals at the same time. It is worth being precise about why they are incompatible rather than merely difficult, because that is what dictates the shape of the solution.

Latency and throughput conflict over the frequency of switching: the only way to react to an event quickly is to switch to it quickly, and every switch is overhead that the workload does not benefit from. Fairness and determinism conflict over whose turn it is: proportional sharing exists precisely to guarantee that a task which has had less CPU time gets some, which is the guarantee a control loop must never be subject to. And energy efficiency conflicts with all of them, because the cheapest CPU in power terms is one that stays idle long enough to reach a deep C-state, while every other goal is served by waking it.

No single algorithm optimizes all five. What Linux does instead is assign each goal to a different mechanism and let the class hierarchy decide which mechanism applies to which task:

Table 7-1. Scheduler Goals and the Mechanisms Used to Reach Them
GoalWhy It MattersMain Mechanism in Linux 7.0.12
LatencyTerminals, browsers, shells, and UI threads must run quickly after wakeupEEVDF eligibility, wakeup placement, preemption checks, higher timer frequency on low-latency kernels
FairnessCompeting normal tasks should receive CPU time proportional to weightVirtual runtime, weighted average virtual time, virtual deadlines
DeterminismReal-time threads need bounded response and strict priority rulesRT class, deadline class, throttling and admission control
ThroughputBatch jobs should keep CPUs busy with minimal scheduling overheadLarger effective slices, fewer wakeup preemptions, lower timer overhead
Energy efficiencyMobile and heterogeneous systems care about placement and idle timeCPU selection, idle-sibling search, EAS, nohz and capacity awareness

Linux addresses these goals with a class-based priority hierarchy. The core scheduler does not implement one monolithic algorithm. Instead, it asks each class in descending priority order whether that class has runnable work. This lets stop, deadline, real-time, fair, sched_ext, and idle policies coexist without entangling their internal logic.

Workload Classification in Practice

Kernel textbooks traditionally divide tasks into I/O-bound and CPU-bound processes. That distinction still matters, but it is a description of behavior, not an instruction to the kernel, and the difference is the whole story of how Linux scheduling changed.

Older kernels tried to bridge that gap by inferring the category. The 2.6 scheduler measured how long each task slept and granted an interactivity bonus of up to five priority levels to tasks that slept a lot, on the theory that a sleeper is waiting for a user. The theory is reasonable and the implementation was a persistent source of trouble: a task could be misclassified, the classification could be gamed by sleeping deliberately, and the bonus interacted badly with nice values because it moved a task’s effective priority without changing its weight.

Linux 7.0.12 does not infer anything. A task’s treatment follows from the policy it declares and from measured facts — how much CPU time it has consumed relative to its weight — rather than from a guess about its intent. The behavioral categories below therefore describe what the scheduler observes, and the rightmost column shows which policy expresses that behavior to the kernel explicitly.

Table 7-2. Behavioral Classification of Runnable Tasks
BehaviorObservable PatternScheduler InterpretationTypical Policy
InteractiveShort CPU bursts separated by sleeps for input, I/O, epoll, futexes, or timersTask is often behind the queue average and quickly becomes eligible againSCHED_NORMAL
BatchLong CPU bursts, rarely blocks, little sensitivity to wakeup latencyTask usually consumes full slices and competes mostly on weightSCHED_BATCH
Background scavengerShould use spare cycles onlyRuns in fair class but with extreme de-prioritizationSCHED_IDLE
Fixed-priority RTMust preempt ordinary work immediately when runnableBypasses fair accounting and uses strict priority queuesSCHED_FIFO, SCHED_RR
Deadline-driven RTPeriodic or sporadic job with explicit runtime and deadlineUses EDF/CBS and admission controlSCHED_DEADLINE

The crucial change from older kernels is that interactive response no longer depends on sleeper bonus heuristics. Under CFS, Linux accumulated a family of latency patches because “pick smallest vruntime” by itself did not always bring a newly awakened task back quickly enough. Under EEVDF, a task that slept while others ran typically returns with negative lag and becomes eligible, so its low virtual deadline pulls it forward naturally. In other words, interactivity is inferred from service deficit, not guessed from ad hoc bonuses.

EEVDF does not make normal tasks real-time. It simply gives the fair class a principled way to trade fairness and latency. Interactive tasks still share the CPU proportionally with other normal tasks; they just stop depending on historical sleeper heuristics.

User-Visible Scheduling Policies

A scheduling policy is the contract between a task and the kernel: it tells the scheduler what kind of service the task needs and, in return, what rules the task is willing to follow. A web server needs fast wakeup response (low latency), a video encoder needs sustained CPU throughput, and an industrial controller needs a guaranteed time budget every 10 ms. No single algorithm can satisfy all three, so Linux provides multiple policies — each backed by a scheduling class that implements the appropriate algorithm.

Linux 7.0.12 exposes seven policy constants to user space, defined in <linux/sched.h>. These seven values map onto only six kernel scheduling classes, because SCHED_NORMAL, SCHED_BATCH, and SCHED_IDLE all share the fair class — they are different behavioral profiles within the same EEVDF algorithm. A task selects its policy through sched_setscheduler() or the newer sched_setattr() system call; the kernel validates permissions, applies admission control (for real-time and deadline policies), and assigns the task to the corresponding class.

Table 7-3. Linux 7.0.12 Scheduling Policies
PolicyClassMain Selection RuleWho Uses It
SCHED_NORMALfairEligible task with earliest virtual deadlineDefault processes and threads
SCHED_BATCHfairSame EEVDF machinery, but tuned for throughput and reduced wakeup urgencyCompilers, encoders, analytics jobs
SCHED_IDLEfairFair class, but only when no ordinary fair work is runnableBackground maintenance, best-effort daemons
SCHED_FIFOrtHighest static RT priority wins; no quantum rotationControl loops, audio, industrial tasks
SCHED_RRrtHighest static RT priority wins; same-priority peers rotate by quantumRT tasks that need peer fairness
SCHED_DEADLINEdeadlineEarliest absolute deadline wins, subject to CBS runtimePeriodic hard/firm real-time workloads
SCHED_EXTextKernel dispatches to BPF-defined policyExperimental or site-specific policies

Two details of that table are less fixed than they look. The first is the list of constants itself: the seven policies span the values 0 to 7 rather than 0 to 6, because value 4 is a hole. It was reserved for SCHED_ISO, an “isochronous” policy proposed in the 2000s for soft real-time multimedia work that was never implemented; the uapi header still carries the line /* SCHED_ISO: reserved but not implemented yet */ where the definition would be. It is a small archaeological artefact, but one that matters if you ever iterate over policy numbers.

The second is the policy-to-class column, which is not a lookup table at all. The mapping is computed, and it is short enough to read in full:

/* kernel/sched/core.c */
const struct sched_class *__setscheduler_class(int policy, int prio)
{
    if (dl_prio(prio))
        return &dl_sched_class;

    if (rt_prio(prio))
        return &rt_sched_class;

#ifdef CONFIG_SCHED_CLASS_EXT
    if (task_should_scx(policy))
        return &ext_sched_class;
#endif

    return &fair_sched_class;
}

Notice that the deadline and RT decisions are made on prio, not on policy — which is what allows priority inheritance to move a task into the RT or deadline class without its policy changing at all, as the previous subsection described. Only the last two decisions consult the policy.

The sched_ext case is the one that breaks the table outright, because SCHED_EXT does not reliably mean the ext class. task_should_scx() returns false whenever no BPF scheduler is loaded, so on an ordinary system a task that successfully set SCHED_EXT is running in the fair class under EEVDF. The kernel is explicit about this elsewhere: normal_policy() counts SCHED_EXT alongside SCHED_NORMAL, which makes fair_policy(SCHED_EXT) true, so such a task is treated as a nice-0 fair task throughout the fair-class code.

The relationship also runs the other way. When a BPF scheduler is loaded in its all-encompassing mode, task_should_scx() consults scx_switching_all and returns true for every non-RT, non-deadline task — so ordinary SCHED_NORMAL tasks are pulled into the ext class without any policy change on their part. Loading or unloading a BPF scheduler therefore re-classes tasks system-wide, which is why __setscheduler_class() is called again from kernel/sched/ext.c when that happens. The right way to read Table 7-3 is that the class column names the maximum a policy can ask for; what a task actually gets depends on prio and on whether a BPF scheduler is loaded.

Understanding the distinctions among the three fair-class policies is important, because they are often confused:

  • SCHED_NORMAL — the default policy assigned to every task at creation. The scheduler treats wakeups as latency-sensitive: when a SCHED_NORMAL task wakes from sleep, the kernel checks whether it should preempt the currently running task immediately, giving interactive applications (shells, editors, browsers) responsive behavior without any special configuration.
  • SCHED_BATCH — signals to the scheduler that this task is throughput-oriented and does not need fast wakeup preemption. The EEVDF algorithm still applies — the task receives its fair share of CPU time — but the kernel avoids the overhead of preemption checks on wakeup. This allows batch workloads like compilers and data pipelines to run with fewer context switches and better cache utilization. A user sets this policy with sched_setscheduler(pid, SCHED_BATCH, &param) or chrt -b 0 ./program.
  • SCHED_IDLE — the lowest-priority fair policy. Tasks under SCHED_IDLE receive CPU time only when no SCHED_NORMAL or SCHED_BATCH tasks are competing. This is ideal for truly optional background work — log rotation, filesystem scrubbing, speculative pre-computation — that should never interfere with foreground tasks. Note: SCHED_IDLE is not the same as the idle scheduling class (which runs only the per-CPU idle thread when no tasks are runnable at all).

The real-time policies (SCHED_FIFO and SCHED_RR) provide strict priority-based scheduling. They always preempt fair-class tasks regardless of the fair task's nice value. Among RT tasks, the one with the highest rt_priority (1–99, where 99 is highest) always runs. The difference between FIFO and RR only matters when two or more RT tasks share the same priority level: FIFO gives the CPU to whichever task arrived first and never forces a switch until the task blocks or a higher priority task arrives, while RR rotates the CPU among same-priority peers using a configurable time slice (default 100 ms). Setting an RT policy requires CAP_SYS_NICE capability or a sufficiently high RLIMIT_RTPRIO limit.

SCHED_DEADLINE sits above both RT and fair classes in the priority hierarchy — a deadline task preempts any RT or fair task. Rather than a fixed priority, the task declares three parameters: how much CPU time it needs (runtime), how soon it needs it (deadline), and how often the work repeats (period). The kernel's EDF algorithm guarantees the declared bandwidth as long as the system's total deadline utilization does not exceed available capacity. This makes SCHED_DEADLINE the strongest timing guarantee available in mainline Linux and the policy of choice for hard real-time applications such as robotics control loops and audio processing pipelines. Setting a deadline policy requires CAP_SYS_NICE.

SCHED_EXT, the newest policy (merged in kernel 6.12), delegates all scheduling decisions to a BPF program loaded by the administrator. The kernel provides a framework of hooks (task enqueue, CPU selection, dispatch) that the BPF scheduler implements. This allows entirely new scheduling algorithms — workload-specific, site-specific, or experimental — to run in production without recompiling the kernel. If the BPF scheduler misbehaves (fails to dispatch a task within a watchdog timeout), the kernel automatically falls back to the built-in EEVDF algorithm.

Priority Names and Numeric Ranges

One of the most confusing aspects of Linux scheduling is that the word "priority" means different things in different contexts. User space talks about nice values (-20 to +19) for normal tasks and real-time priorities (1–99) for RT tasks, while the kernel internally maps everything onto a single numeric range (0–139) where lower numbers mean higher priority. The deadline class is often said to sidestep numeric priority entirely, and while it is true that scheduling within the class uses temporal parameters rather than a priority number, the class does still occupy a slot on the same scale — below zero.

The internal mapping works as follows: kernel priorities 0–98 are reserved for RT tasks (user rt_priority 99 maps to kernel priority 0, the highest), while kernel priorities 100–139 correspond to fair-class tasks (user nice -20 maps to kernel priority 100, and nice +19 maps to 139). The task_struct carries three priority fields to track this: static_prio (the base priority from nice), normal_prio (the priority without priority-inheritance boosting), and prio (the effective priority, which may be temporarily elevated by the PI mechanism when a low-priority task holds a lock needed by a high-priority one).

All three are produced by one small function, which is the most economical statement of the whole mapping:

/* kernel/sched/syscalls.c */
static inline int __normal_prio(int policy, int rt_prio, int nice)
{
    int prio;

    if (dl_policy(policy))
        prio = MAX_DL_PRIO - 1;              /* MAX_DL_PRIO is 0, so prio = -1 */
    else if (rt_policy(policy))
        prio = MAX_RT_PRIO - 1 - rt_prio;    /* 99 - rt_priority */
    else
        prio = NICE_TO_PRIO(nice);           /* nice + 120 */

    return prio;
}

Three things in that function are worth pausing on, because none of them follows from the number line alone.

First, deadline tasks are assigned priority −1. The scale is not really 0–139 but −1–139, and the negative value is load-bearing rather than decorative: dl_prio(prio) is defined as prio < MAX_DL_PRIO, that is, prio < 0, and it is what __setscheduler_class() tests to route a task into the deadline class. The class hierarchy and the priority number agree with each other by construction.

Second, the RT arithmetic inverts the user-space number, which is the source of most confusion in this area. A user asking for rt_priority = 99 — the most urgent RT request — is stored as prio = 0. The two scales run in opposite directions, so any code that compares priorities and any ps output that prints them must be read with that inversion in mind.

Third, the arithmetic leaves a hole at kernel priority 99. Valid rt_priority values run 1–99, which map to 98–0; priority 99 would correspond to rt_priority = 0, which no RT task may have. Nothing is ever assigned that value, and the fair range starts one above it at MAX_RT_PRIO = 100.

The distinction the kernel actually cares about is not static-versus-dynamic priority but whether a task's prio can change while its policy does not. A 7.0 commit made this explicit after it caused a real bug: “While FIFO/RR have static priority, DEADLINE is a dynamic priority scheme. Notably it has static priority −1. Do not assume the priority doesn’t change for deadline tasks just because the static priority doesn’t change.” The fix appears in rt_mutex_setprio() as the clause if (oldprio == prio && !dl_prio(prio)), which withholds the “nothing moved” shortcut from deadline tasks because their effective urgency may have changed even though the integer did not.

Table 7-4. Priority Domains Across Classes
DomainUser ControlOrdering RuleNotes
stop classnoneAlways highestMigration and hotplug stop threads only
deadline classruntime, deadline, periodEarlier absolute deadline winsAdmission control required
RT classrt_priority 1-99Higher RT priority preempts lower RT priorityPolicy decides FIFO vs RR rotation
fair classnice -20 to +19Higher weight gets larger share and slower vruntime advancenice 0 corresponds to weight 1024
idle classnoneRuns only when nothing else doesRepresents the per-CPU idle thread

From Wakeup to Preemption Decision

Scheduling decisions do not happen continuously — they are triggered by discrete events. The two most common triggers are: (1) a blocked task becomes runnable (the wakeup path), and (2) the running task's time allowance expires (the tick path). Of these, the wakeup path is by far the most critical for interactive latency because it determines how quickly a sleeping task regains the CPU after the event it was waiting for occurs — a keystroke, a network packet arrival, a disk read completion, or a lock release.

The entire journey from "I/O completes" to "task is running" passes through try_to_wake_up() in kernel/sched/core.c. This function is the single most performance-sensitive path in the scheduler: it runs millions of times per second on a busy system, must handle concurrent wakeups from multiple CPUs, and must make the CPU-selection and preemption decision with minimal latency. Understanding its steps reveals how scheduling policy translates into real behavior:

  1. The waker checks whether the target task's current state matches the wakeup mask.
  2. The check happens under p->pi_lock for ordinary wakeups, so state transitions and PI interactions are serialized.
  3. Memory barriers order the task-state load against p->on_rq and p->on_cpu so the waker does not race incorrectly with __schedule().
  4. If the task is still owned by a CPU in the middle of context switching, the wakeup may be queued onto a remote wake list instead of spinning.
  5. The scheduler asks the class-specific select_task_rq() hook which CPU should receive the task.
  6. The task is enqueued, and the destination runqueue decides whether the currently running task must be preempted.
/* kernel/sched/core.c — try_to_wake_up() structure, simplified */
int try_to_wake_up(struct task_struct *p, unsigned int state, int wake_flags)
{
    if (p == current) {
        if (!ttwu_state_match(p, state, &success))
            return 0;
        ttwu_do_wakeup(p);
        return 1;
    }

    raw_spin_lock_irqsave(&p->pi_lock, flags);
    smp_mb__after_spinlock();

    if (!ttwu_state_match(p, state, &success))
        goto out;

    smp_rmb();
    if (READ_ONCE(p->on_rq) && ttwu_runnable(p, wake_flags))
        goto out;

    smp_acquire__after_ctrl_dep();
    WRITE_ONCE(p->__state, TASK_WAKING);

    if (smp_load_acquire(&p->on_cpu) &&
        ttwu_queue_wakelist(p, task_cpu(p), wake_flags))
        goto out;

    smp_cond_load_acquire(&p->on_cpu, !VAL);
    cpu = select_task_rq(p, p->wake_cpu, &wake_flags);
    if (task_cpu(p) != cpu)
        set_task_cpu(p, cpu);
    ttwu_queue(p, cpu, wake_flags);
out:
    raw_spin_unlock_irqrestore(&p->pi_lock, flags);
    return success;
}

Two details matter for policy analysis. First, there is a fast path for waking current; that happens when the running task sets a sleep state and immediately satisfies the wake condition before it actually blocks. Second, CPU placement is deliberately deferred until the task is known to need enqueuing, because a runnable task already sitting on a runqueue may be handled by lighter-weight logic.

The state match in try_to_wake_up() is not a cosmetic check. It is the line that prevents stale wakeups from reviving tasks that are no longer waiting for the relevant condition, and it is paired carefully with the barriers used by set_current_state() and __schedule().

The Meaning of TIF_NEED_RESCHED

The kernel does not context-switch instantly every time something “better” appears. Instead, it records that a reschedule is needed by setting the per-thread flag TIF_NEED_RESCHED. Conceptually, this is a deferred interrupt to the scheduler: “at the next safe preemption point, call schedule().”

The flag is typically set by resched_curr() or resched_curr_lazy() — the difference between the two is the subject of the next subsection — when one of the following happens:

  • a higher-priority class becomes runnable on this CPU,
  • a same-class competitor has become more entitled to run,
  • the current task exhausted its class-defined slice or budget,
  • core-scheduling or migration logic needs a fresh selection,
  • the periodic tick or hrtick notices that the current task should yield.

Just as important are the places where the flag is observed. Linux checks it at every boundary where switching is legal and useful:

Table 7-5. Common TIF_NEED_RESCHED Checkpoints
CheckpointWhy It Is SafeTypical Cause
Return from interrupt to user modeNo in-kernel lock context is being preservedTimer tick, device interrupt waking a task
Return from system call to user modeKernel work for the syscall is completeWakeup on another CPU, fair preemption, RT arrival
preempt_enable() when preempt_count drops to zeroKernel preemption becomes legal againLock release or end of a preempt-disabled section
cond_resched() and relativesVoluntary preemption point inside long kernel loopsNeed for latency in non-preempt or voluntary kernels — patched to a no-op under the full and lazy models
Explicit blocking pathThe current task is already yielding the CPUschedule(), io_schedule(), mutex sleep
 
/* representative preemption checks */
if (test_tsk_need_resched(current))
    schedule();

preempt_enable();
if (unlikely(should_resched(PREEMPT_OFFSET)))
    __preempt_schedule();

exit_to_user_mode_loop() {
    while (ti_work & _TIF_WORK_MASK) {
        if (ti_work & _TIF_NEED_RESCHED)
            schedule();
    }
}

In the hot path of __schedule(), once the next task has been chosen, the kernel clears the current thread's need-resched bit and the CPU-local preemption need-resched state before switching. If a race reasserts the flag immediately afterward, the next return-to-kernel or return-to-user checkpoint will catch it.

That phrase — “the CPU-local preemption need-resched state” — hides a piece of machinery worth unpacking, because it explains how a check that appears in thousands of places costs almost nothing. Taken literally, every preempt_enable() would have to ask two questions: is preemption now allowed? and is a reschedule pending? The first is a test of preempt_count, the second a test of a thread flag in a different word. Two loads and two branches, on one of the hottest paths in the kernel.

Linux collapses them into one by folding the need-resched signal into the preemption counter itself, using inverted polarity so that the interesting case is a comparison against zero. On x86_64:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/preempt.h */
#define PREEMPT_NEED_RESCHED    0x80000000

/*
 * We use the PREEMPT_NEED_RESCHED bit as an inverted NEED_RESCHED such
 * that a decrement hitting 0 means we can and should reschedule.
 */
#define PREEMPT_ENABLED (0 + PREEMPT_NEED_RESCHED)

static __always_inline bool should_resched(int preempt_offset)
{
    return unlikely(raw_cpu_read_4(__preempt_count) == preempt_offset);
}

The bit is set when a reschedule is not needed and cleared when one is. Combined with a counter that is zero when preemption is enabled, the whole condition “preemption is allowed and a reschedule is pending” becomes the single test __preempt_count == 0. On the release path this is better still: __preempt_count_dec_and_test() compiles to a single decl instruction followed by a branch on the zero flag, so an uncontended preempt_enable() costs one arithmetic instruction and one not-taken branch. This is also why preempt_count() masks the bit off before returning — callers that merely want the nesting depth must not see it.

ARM64 reaches the same result with different hardware assumptions, and the contrast is instructive. Rather than a per-CPU word, the counter lives in thread_info, and the folding is done with a union rather than a spare bit:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/thread_info.h */
union {
        u64     preempt_count;      /* 0 => preemptible, <0 => bug */
        struct {
                u32     count;
                u32     need_resched;
        } preempt;
};

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/preempt.h */
#define PREEMPT_NEED_RESCHED    BIT(32)

The 64-bit preempt_count overlays two 32-bit halves: the nesting count in the low word and the inverted need-resched flag in the high one, so BIT(32) names the whole upper field. Setting and clearing are plain 32-bit stores to preempt.need_resched — no atomic read-modify-write on the counter — while the combined test still reads the full 64-bit value and compares it with zero. The field order is swapped under CONFIG_CPU_BIG_ENDIAN so that the layout, not the arithmetic, absorbs the endianness.

The practical consequence for reading scheduler code is that preempt_count is not simply a counter, and the two need-resched indications are not redundant. TIF_NEED_RESCHED in thread_info->flags is the authoritative per-task record, checked on the return-to-user path along with every other pending-work flag; the folded bit is a per-CPU (x86_64) or per-thread (ARM64) accelerator for the in-kernel preemption checks. set_preempt_need_resched() and clear_preempt_need_resched() keep the second in step with the first, which is exactly what __schedule() is doing when it clears both before switching.

Two Flags, Not One: Lazy Preemption

The description so far treats TIF_NEED_RESCHED as a single bit, which is how it worked for twenty years. Since Linux 6.13 there are two, and the reason is worth following because it explains a change that has since reshaped the whole preemption model.

The problem with a single bit is that it forces one answer to two different questions. A fully preemptible kernel acts on the flag at the first legal opportunity — typically the next preempt_enable() — which is exactly what a real-time task needs. But most flag-setting events are not real-time arrivals; they are one ordinary task becoming marginally more eligible than another. Acting on those immediately produces over-scheduling: switches that gain nothing in latency terms, and that frequently preempt a task while it holds a lock, so the next task to want that lock blocks too. PREEMPT_RT suffered measurably from this, and it is the reason a fully preemptible kernel could be slower than a voluntary one on throughput workloads.

Lazy preemption splits the question. resched_curr() keeps the old meaning — switch as soon as it is legal — and a second entry point marks the weaker request:

/* kernel/sched/core.c */
static __always_inline int get_lazy_tif_bit(void)
{
    if (dynamic_preempt_lazy())
        return TIF_NEED_RESCHED_LAZY;

    return TIF_NEED_RESCHED;
}

void resched_curr_lazy(struct rq *rq)
{
    __resched_curr(rq, get_lazy_tif_bit());
}

The difference between the two bits is entirely in where they are examined. TIF_NEED_RESCHED is checked at every preemption checkpoint in Table 7-5. TIF_NEED_RESCHED_LAZY is checked only on the return-to-user-mode path. A task that sets the lazy bit therefore keeps running through preempt_enable(), through lock releases, and through the rest of its kernel work — it is allowed to finish what it is doing and get out of the kernel, still holding no locks by the time it leaves.

That would be an unbounded delay if nothing escalated it, so the tick does:

/* kernel/sched/core.c, sched_tick() */
if (dynamic_preempt_lazy() && tif_test_bit(TIF_NEED_RESCHED_LAZY))
    resched_curr(rq);

A lazy request that has not been satisfied by the next tick is promoted to a real one, which bounds the extra delay at one tick period rather than leaving it open-ended. __resched_curr() makes one further exception: a lazy request against the idle task is promoted immediately, since there is nothing to be gained by letting the idle loop finish anything.

Which requests are lazy is a deliberate policy choice, and a narrow one. Only the fair class uses resched_curr_lazy(), at three sites in kernel/sched/fair.c: slice exhaustion, wakeup preemption, and a nice-value change. Every RT and deadline preemption still goes through resched_curr() and still takes effect at the first legal instruction. The weaker guarantee applies only where nobody was promised a strong one.

On architectures without ARCH_HAS_PREEMPT_LAZY, include/linux/thread_info.h defines TIF_NEED_RESCHED_LAZY as an alias for TIF_NEED_RESCHED, so the second flag silently collapses into the first and the code above degenerates to the classic behavior. Both of this book’s reference architectures support it: x86 and ARM64 each select ARCH_HAS_PREEMPT_LAZY, with ARM64 giving the lazy flag its own bit at TIF_NEED_RESCHED_LAZY = 2.

Kernel Preemption Models

Even if the scheduler's policy decides that task A should preempt task B, the preemption can only happen at a point where the kernel is in a safe state to perform a context switch. Code holding a spinlock, executing in an interrupt handler, or manipulating per-CPU data structures cannot be interrupted mid-stream without risking corruption. The preemption model — selected at kernel compile time via CONFIG_PREEMPT_* — controls how aggressively the kernel allows these interruptions, creating a fundamental trade-off between scheduling latency and system complexity.

At one extreme — historically the server default, and, as we will see, no longer a build-time option on x86_64 or ARM64 — PREEMPT_NONE never interrupts kernel code involuntarily: a task executing a system call runs to completion (or until it voluntarily sleeps) before the scheduler can pick a different task. This maximizes throughput for server workloads by minimizing context-switch overhead, but it means that a long-running copy_to_user() or filesystem operation can delay an interactive task for milliseconds. At the other extreme, PREEMPT_RT converts nearly all kernel locks into preemptible sleeping locks (mutexes) and runs interrupt handlers as kernel threads, making almost every instruction in the kernel a legal preemption point — achieving worst-case latencies under 100 µs on properly configured hardware, at the cost of higher overhead and more complex synchronization.

The choice of preemption model affects every scheduling class equally: it does not change which task is selected, but how quickly the selection can take effect when made inside kernel context. Linux 7.0.12 offers five, though as the next paragraphs explain, not all five are available to any given kernel.

Table 7-6. Preemption Models and Their Scheduling Consequences
ConfigurationWhere Preemption HappensLatency ProfileTrade-off
PREEMPT_NONEMainly at blocking points and return to user modeHighest worst-case kernel latencyLowest preemption overhead and simplest locking
PREEMPT_VOLUNTARYcond_resched() sites plus user boundariesBetter responsiveness in long loopsStill depends on explicit yield points
PREEMPTAlmost any point where preempt_count == 0Low general-purpose latencyMore synchronization pressure and more frequent scheduler entries
PREEMPT_LAZYSame points as PREEMPT for RT and deadline requests; fair-class requests wait for return-to-user or the next tickLow latency where it was promised, without preempting ordinary tasks mid-lockAdds a second need-resched flag; fair tasks may wait up to one tick longer
PREEMPT_RTNearly all kernel code except raw non-preemptible regionsBest bounded latencyHigher complexity, different lock semantics, threaded interrupt model

Which Models a 7.0 Kernel Can Actually Build

The table above lists five models; on the two architectures this book uses as examples, only two of them can be selected. Commit 7dadeaa6e851, merged for 7.0, restricted the Kconfig choice sharply:

/* kernel/Kconfig.preempt */
choice
	prompt "Preemption Model"
	default PREEMPT_LAZY if ARCH_HAS_PREEMPT_LAZY
	default PREEMPT_NONE

config PREEMPT_NONE
	depends on ARCH_NO_PREEMPT          /* i.e. only where preemption
	                                       is not supported at all */
config PREEMPT_VOLUNTARY
	depends on !ARCH_HAS_PREEMPT_LAZY   /* i.e. only where lazy is
	                                       not yet implemented */
config PREEMPT_LAZY
	depends on ARCH_HAS_PREEMPT_LAZY

Both x86 and ARM64 select ARCH_HAS_PREEMPT_LAZY and neither sets ARCH_NO_PREEMPT. On those architectures PREEMPT_NONE and PREEMPT_VOLUNTARY are therefore not offered at all, and the default is PREEMPT_LAZY. A 7.0 x86_64 or ARM64 kernel is fundamentally preemptible; the only remaining question is whether fair-class preemption is eager or lazy.

The reasoning in the commit message is worth repeating, because it inverts a long-standing assumption. Server kernels chose PREEMPT_NONE for throughput, and the cost of that choice was that any kernel code path which might run long had to be sprinkled with cond_resched() calls to stay responsive. Peter Zijlstra describes the result as “the endless and uncontrolled sprinkling of cond_resched() — mostly cargo cult”: a maintenance burden spread across the entire tree, in service of a latency property that lazy preemption now provides structurally. Making preemptibility a guarantee also lets new code rely on it — folio_zero_user() can issue one large memset() without interleaving preemption checks — rather than working around its absence. The stated long-term goal is to empty the PREEMPT_VOLUNTARY set entirely and remove cond_resched() along with it.

Choosing the Model at Boot: PREEMPT_DYNAMIC

There is a second reason the compile-time choice matters less than it appears. With CONFIG_PREEMPT_DYNAMIC — enabled by default on any architecture with HAVE_PREEMPT_DYNAMIC_CALL, which includes x86_64 — the model is selected at boot, not at build. This exists for distributions, which would otherwise have to ship a separate kernel flavor per latency profile.

The implementation is a set of static calls and one static key, patched at runtime by __sched_dynamic_update():

/* kernel/sched/core.c, abridged */
switch (mode) {
case preempt_dynamic_none:
	preempt_dynamic_enable(cond_resched);
	preempt_dynamic_disable(might_resched);
	preempt_dynamic_disable(preempt_schedule);
	preempt_dynamic_disable(irqentry_exit_cond_resched);
	preempt_dynamic_key_disable(preempt_lazy);
	break;
case preempt_dynamic_voluntary:
	preempt_dynamic_enable(cond_resched);
	preempt_dynamic_enable(might_resched);
	preempt_dynamic_disable(preempt_schedule);
	preempt_dynamic_disable(irqentry_exit_cond_resched);
	preempt_dynamic_key_disable(preempt_lazy);
	break;
case preempt_dynamic_full:
	preempt_dynamic_disable(cond_resched);
	preempt_dynamic_disable(might_resched);
	preempt_dynamic_enable(preempt_schedule);
	preempt_dynamic_enable(irqentry_exit_cond_resched);
	preempt_dynamic_key_disable(preempt_lazy);
	break;
case preempt_dynamic_lazy:
	preempt_dynamic_disable(cond_resched);
	preempt_dynamic_disable(might_resched);
	preempt_dynamic_enable(preempt_schedule);
	preempt_dynamic_enable(irqentry_exit_cond_resched);
	preempt_dynamic_key_enable(preempt_lazy);
	break;
}

Reading down the columns shows what the models actually are, stripped of their descriptions. cond_resched() and preempt_schedule() are exact opposites: every mode enables one or the other, never both. The low-latency modes patch cond_resched() into a no-op — which is the concrete sense in which its removal is already half-complete, since on a default 7.0 x86_64 kernel the thousands of cond_resched() calls in the tree do nothing at all. And lazy differs from full in exactly one line: the preempt_lazy static key, which is what dynamic_preempt_lazy() tests to decide whether get_lazy_tif_bit() returns the separate flag or aliases it back to TIF_NEED_RESCHED.

The mode is chosen with the preempt= boot parameter, taking none, voluntary, full or lazy. Note that this is a wider set than Kconfig now offers: a 7.0 x86_64 kernel cannot be built as PREEMPT_NONE, but can still be booted with preempt=none, because the dynamic mechanism patches the same call sites the old model would have compiled out. When no parameter is given, preempt_dynamic_init() falls back to whichever model was compiled in. The mode can also be changed on a running system by writing to /sys/kernel/debug/sched/preempt, which is the quickest way to measure a workload against several models without rebooting.

What PREEMPT_RT Actually Changes

The PREEMPT_RT patch set — maintained out-of-tree for over 15 years before being progressively merged into mainline (largely complete by kernel 5.x, fully integrated by 6.x) — represents a philosophical shift in kernel design. In a standard kernel, mutual exclusion is achieved primarily through spinning: a task that cannot acquire a spinlock busy-waits with preemption disabled until the holder releases it. This is fast when contention is brief, but creates unbounded non-preemptible sections when a lock holder is interrupted or when lock chains are deep. For real-time workloads — audio processing with 1 ms deadlines, industrial control loops, or robotic actuators — even a single multi-millisecond non-preemptible section is unacceptable.

PREEMPT_RT is not simply "turn preemption up to maximum." It fundamentally reshapes kernel synchronization so that long non-preemptible sections disappear, replacing spinning with sleeping wherever possible:

  • Most spinlocks become sleeping RT mutexes. The lock still provides mutual exclusion, but a waiter can block rather than spin with preemption disabled for an unbounded time.
  • Priority inheritance becomes pervasive. Because these locks may sleep, PI must propagate priority through lock chains to avoid unbounded inversion.
  • Hard IRQ work is minimized. Device interrupts are forced into kernel threads (threadirqs-style execution), so most interrupt handling itself becomes schedulable and preemptible.
  • Softirq work moves out of unbounded non-preemptible regions. Work that once ran in hard interrupt context is pushed toward thread context where the scheduler can intervene.
  • Some locks remain raw spinlocks. The runqueue lock, low-level interrupt controller locks, and a handful of architecture-critical locks cannot sleep, so they stay raw and delimit truly non-preemptible sections.

This is why the scheduler still guards the runqueue with raw_spinlock_t on RT kernels: the scheduler itself cannot afford to block while manipulating the per-CPU runqueue. A PREEMPT_RT system therefore reduces latency not by making everything sleepable, but by shrinking the set of places where raw locking remains necessary.

The RT preemption model affects the scheduler in subtle ways. For example, schedule_rtlock() and TASK_RTLOCK_WAIT exist specifically to preserve task state across “sleeping spinlock” waits, and the wakeup path redirects lock-related wakeups through saved_state so they are not lost.

Tick Frequency, CONFIG_HZ, and Scheduling Granularity

The tick is a periodic timer interrupt that fires on each CPU, giving the scheduler a regular opportunity to re-evaluate the running task. Chapter 6 covered the tick layer in detail; here we focus on its impact on scheduling decisions. Even though EEVDF uses virtual deadlines rather than fixed time quanta for fair-class tasks, the tick still serves essential scheduling roles: it triggers sched_tick() which updates PELT load averages, checks whether SCHED_RR tasks have exhausted their time slice, drives periodic load balancing, and provides a fallback preemption check for tasks that are not subject to hrtick-based high-resolution preemption.

The tick frequency is selected at kernel compile time through CONFIG_HZ. A higher frequency means the scheduler notices scheduling events sooner (lower worst-case latency for tick-driven decisions), but at the cost of more frequent timer interrupts — each costing several microseconds of overhead and polluting caches. On modern kernels with NO_HZ_IDLE or NO_HZ_FULL enabled, the tick is suppressed when a CPU is idle or running a single task, so the overhead applies mainly to CPUs actively multiplexing runnable tasks. The choice of CONFIG_HZ therefore matters most for SCHED_RR granularity and for systems that have not enabled the hrtick feature (which provides per-task high-resolution preemption independent of the periodic tick).

Table 7-7. Common CONFIG_HZ Choices
CONFIG_HZTick PeriodTypical UseImpact on Scheduling
10010 msLarge servers, throughput-oriented systemsLower timer overhead, coarser RR rotation, slower fallback resched observation
2504 msGeneral-purpose distro default on many systemsBalanced latency and overhead
3003.33 msHistorically favored by some desktop vendorsConvenient divisor for multimedia frame timing, slightly better UI responsiveness
10001 msLow-latency desktops, RT-adjacent systemsFast periodic checks and tighter RR behavior, but more timer traffic

Higher HZ does not directly mean fair tasks get one-millisecond slices. Fair scheduling uses virtual runtime and deadlines, often with hrtick support, so the tick is mainly an accounting and safety net mechanism. But for SCHED_RR, watchdogs, and general “how soon will the kernel notice that it should reschedule if nothing else happens?” behavior, HZ still has visible effects.

How Different Policies Consume CPU Time

One of the most common misconceptions about Linux scheduling is that every task receives a fixed "time slice" and is preempted when it expires — the way the 2.6 O(1) scheduler worked. In the modern kernel, this is only true for SCHED_RR tasks. Each scheduling class has its own distinct mechanism for deciding how long a task may run before giving way to another. The fair class uses a virtual deadline — a mathematical construct that ensures proportional sharing without a fixed quantum. The deadline class uses a runtime budget that depletes as the task runs and replenishes at the start of each period. SCHED_FIFO tasks have no time limit at all and run until they voluntarily block or are preempted by a higher-priority task.

Understanding these differences is essential for choosing the right policy for a workload. A developer who sets SCHED_FIFO expecting a "guaranteed time slice" will instead get a task that monopolizes the CPU — potentially hanging the system. Conversely, a developer who uses SCHED_NORMAL expecting strict timing guarantees will find that their task's actual runtime varies with system load, because the EEVDF algorithm distributes CPU proportionally among all competing fair-class tasks.

Table 7-8. Time Allocation Rules by Class
ClassAccounting UnitPreemption TriggerWho Decides
fairvirtual runtime and virtual deadlineMore eligible earlier-deadline entity, expired protected slice, wakeup competitionfair.c
rt / FIFOnone for peer rotationHigher RT priority onlyrt.c
rt / RRfixed RR quantumHigher RT priority or RR quantum expirationrt.c
deadlineruntime budget inside a periodEarlier absolute deadline or exhausted CBS runtimedeadline.c
idleimplicitAny runnable non-idle taskcore scheduler

From the core scheduler's point of view, all of this boils down to the same contract: each class enqueues tasks, updates current runtime, answers preemption questions, and supplies the next runnable task when asked. That abstraction is what allows Linux to combine general-purpose fairness, fixed-priority real-time scheduling, deadline scheduling, and BPF-defined policies in a single kernel.

Scheduling Classes and Their Hierarchy

The most important design decision in the modern Linux scheduler is the separation of mechanism from policy through a class-based architecture. Rather than implementing one monolithic scheduling algorithm that tries to handle every workload type (as the 2.6 O(1) scheduler did), the kernel defines a small, stable interface — struct sched_class — that each scheduling class implements independently. The core scheduler (kernel/sched/core.c) provides only the mechanism: locking the per-CPU runqueue, updating clocks, performing the context switch, and iterating through classes to find the next task. All policy decisions — how to rank tasks, when to preempt, how to account time — live behind the class interface.

This design has proven remarkably extensible. When CFS replaced the O(1) scheduler in 2.6.23, only the fair class changed — RT scheduling was unaffected. When SCHED_DEADLINE was added in 3.14, it was a new class with zero modifications to existing fair or RT code. When sched_ext arrived in 6.12, it added BPF extensibility as yet another class without touching any existing scheduling logic. Each class is a self-contained module that can be developed, tested, and reasoned about in isolation, yet they all cooperate through the common priority chain.

The class framework also establishes a strict priority hierarchy: the core scheduler always consults classes from highest to lowest priority, and returns the first runnable task found. This means a deadline task always preempts an RT task, an RT task always preempts a fair task, and so on — regardless of nice values or RT priorities within those classes. The hierarchy ensures that each class's guarantees are never violated by a lower class.

That summary is easy to state and surprisingly hard to implement, because a class boundary is a place where several requirements meet that do not naturally agree:

  • Independence pulls against ordering. Each class is supposed to know nothing about the others, yet something has to decide that deadline outranks RT — and that decision must be cheap enough to consult on every context switch.
  • Extensibility pulls against the hot path. A dispatch table of function pointers is the standard answer to “let each class implement its own policy,” but indirect calls are exactly what a per-context-switch code path cannot afford to make unconditionally.
  • Class membership is not static. A task can move between classes at runtime — sched_setscheduler(), priority inheritance, a BPF scheduler being loaded or unloaded — and during that move it belongs to neither the class it is leaving nor the one it is joining.
  • “Priority” does not mean the same thing in every class. For RT it is a small integer, for the fair class a weight, and for deadline an absolute time. A shared interface has to accommodate all three without pretending they are comparable.

Linux answers the first two with the linker: the class order is baked into the kernel image at build time, so iteration is a plain array walk over adjacent memory rather than a pointer chase, and the hot path is allowed to skip the walk entirely when it can prove the answer. It answers the last two with a structured change protocol that brackets every mutation of a task’s scheduling properties. The rest of this section works through eight questions in that order:

  1. What is the class order, and what does “strict” actually mean at a class boundary?
  2. What are the six classes, and what does each one exist to provide that the others cannot?
  3. What does the sched_class interface look like, and how does a task safely change its class or priority through it?
  4. Where is the class ordering defined, and why does it need no runtime initialization?
  5. How does pick_next_task() walk the classes, and how does it usually avoid walking them at all?
  6. Why does the run queue now distinguish the task that is executing from the task that is being scheduled?
  7. How is a class assigned to a brand-new task, and which policies refuse to be inherited?
  8. Why is the hierarchy deliberately unfair across classes, and what stops that unfairness from becoming a hang?

Version history. Linux 2.6.11 had no scheduling classes at all: schedule() contained one algorithm, and priority was a single integer that the algorithm interpreted directly. The class interface arrived with CFS in 2.6.23 and has been growing ever since — the original had roughly ten hooks, the 7.0.12 version has about thirty. Three of the six classes did not exist in 2.6 (deadline in 3.14, ext in 6.12, and stop in its current form), and two of the mechanisms described below are newer than most of the material in this chapter: the sched_change pattern that brackets every class and priority change was introduced in 6.19, and rq->next_class tracking in 7.0.

The Class Priority Chain

The six classes are consulted in a fixed order that never changes at runtime, shown in Figure 7-3. Read it left to right as a sequence of questions the scheduler asks on every context switch: is there a stop task? no — is there a deadline task? no — is there an RT task? and so on, stopping at the first class that answers yes. The order is not a heuristic and not a tunable; it is the definition of what the classes mean relative to one another.

The chain is strict: if any stop-class task is runnable, nothing else can run on that CPU; if no stop task exists but a deadline task is runnable, fair tasks are not even considered; and so on. This keeps policy reasoning local inside each class.

All Six Classes at a Glance

The following table summarizes all six scheduling classes as they exist in the Linux 7.0.12 kernel. Each class is implemented in its own source file under kernel/sched/ — stop_task.c, deadline.c, rt.c, fair.c, ext.c, and idle.c — and registers itself through the DEFINE_SCHED_CLASS() macro. The classes differ not just in their selection algorithms, but in their fundamental purpose: the stop class exists purely for kernel-internal CPU management, deadline provides formal bandwidth guarantees, RT gives strict priority ordering, fair handles the vast majority of tasks with proportional sharing, ext enables user-defined policies via BPF, and idle represents the "nothing to do" state. Notice that each class uses a different data structure optimized for its selection rule — from a simple single-task pointer (stop/idle) to bitmap arrays (RT) to RB-trees (fair/deadline) to BPF-managed dispatch queues (ext).

“Six” is the maximum rather than a constant. The ext class is compiled only when CONFIG_SCHED_CLASS_EXT is set — build_policy.c includes ext.c inside an #ifdef — so a kernel built without it has a five-element class array, and the linker simply produces an empty __ext_sched_class section that contributes nothing between fair and idle. The other five are unconditional. This is worth keeping in mind when reading the class-skipping logic later in this section: next_active_class() is concerned with a class that exists but has nothing to do, which is a different situation from a class that was never built at all.

Table 7-9. Linux 7.0.12 Scheduling Classes Compared
ClassTypical TasksSelection Data StructureCore RuleWhy It Exists
stopCPU stopper thread, migration and hotplug worksingle distinguished taskAlways winsLets the kernel move or quiesce CPUs reliably
deadlineSCHED_DEADLINE tasksRB-tree sorted by absolute deadlineEarliest deadline firstProvides explicit runtime/deadline contracts
rtSCHED_FIFO, SCHED_RRbitmap plus per-priority FIFO listsHighest static RT priority firstProvides fixed-priority preemptive semantics
fairSCHED_NORMAL, SCHED_BATCH, SCHED_IDLEaugmented RB-tree of sched_entitiesEligible earliest virtual deadlineGeneral-purpose proportional sharing
extSCHED_EXT tasksdispatch queues controlled by BPFDefined by BPF opsAllows experimentation without rewriting the kernel scheduler
idleper-CPU idle threadsingle distinguished taskRun only if nothing else canKeeps the CPU alive when the runqueue is empty

The struct sched_class Interface

The struct sched_class (defined in kernel/sched/sched.h) is the scheduler's equivalent of a virtual function table in object-oriented programming. Each scheduling class — stop, deadline, RT, fair, ext, idle — provides its own implementation of these callbacks, and the core scheduler invokes them through function pointers without knowing or caring about the internal algorithm. This interface has grown substantially since its introduction in 2.6.23: the original CFS patch defined roughly 10 hooks, while the 7.0.12 version has about 30, reflecting the scheduler's expanded responsibilities (SMP balancing, cgroup hierarchy, core scheduling, energy awareness, and BPF extensibility).

One thing worth noticing in the real header, which the abbreviated listing below omits for space, is that since 6.19 every hook carries a comment naming its callers and the lock each of them holds — rq->lock, task_rq_lock, p->pi_lock, or none. This is not decoration. A scheduling class is written by someone who cannot see the core scheduler's call sites, and getting the locking wrong in a class callback produces exactly the kind of rare, load-dependent corruption that is almost impossible to debug afterwards. Encoding the contract next to the function pointer makes the interface self-documenting for the one audience that needs it.

The callbacks fall into natural families that correspond to different phases of a task's lifecycle on a CPU. When a task wakes up, the core calls select_task_rq() to choose a CPU, then enqueue_task() to insert it into the class's ready structure. When the scheduler needs to pick the next task, it calls pick_next_task() (or the split pick_task() + set_next_task() pair on core-scheduling-enabled systems). On every tick, task_tick() gives the class a chance to update accounting and trigger preemption. When a task's priority changes or it switches policy, prio_changed() and switched_to() let the class adjust its internal state. The update_curr() hook is called frequently — on every tick, wakeup, and dequeue — to charge the currently running task for elapsed CPU time.

Three groups in that table are easy to overlook because they never appear in a description of “how the scheduler picks a task,” yet they account for a third of the interface. The migration group is the largest: beyond select_task_rq() and balance() it includes migrate_task_rq(), called when a task’s CPU assignment actually changes so the class can fix up per-CPU accounting; task_woken(), which lets a class react to a task becoming runnable on this run queue (the RT and deadline classes use it to push work elsewhere if the wakeup created an overload); set_cpus_allowed(), so a class can maintain its own affinity-derived state; and find_lock_rq(), which the push machinery uses to locate and lock a destination run queue. rq_online() and rq_offline() then tell the class when a CPU enters or leaves an active scheduling domain, which is how the RT and deadline classes keep their overload masks accurate across hotplug.

These hooks are worth calling out because until recently they were not always there. Through 6.16 the whole block sat inside #ifdef CONFIG_SMP, so a uniprocessor kernel compiled a visibly smaller sched_class and the classes carried matching #ifdefs around their implementations. Commit cac5cefbade9 (“sched/smp: Make SMP unconditional”, v6.17-rc1) removed the distinction: the scheduler is now built as if SMP were always present, and a uniprocessor build is simply the degenerate case of one CPU. For a reader comparing against older source this is one of the more visible cosmetic differences in the file, and it is the reason this chapter no longer needs to qualify migration behaviour with “on SMP systems.”

The remaining two groups are small but reveal something about the interface’s scope. yield_task() and yield_to_task() exist because sched_yield() cannot have one meaning across classes — for RT it moves the task to the tail of its priority list, for the fair class it manipulates the entity’s position on the timeline, and for others it may be a no-op. get_rr_interval() backs the sched_rr_get_interval() system call, letting each class answer “how long a slice would this task get?” in its own terms. And task_is_throttled(), compiled only under CONFIG_SCHED_CORE, is consulted when core scheduling looks for a cookie-compatible task to steal, so that it does not import a task the class would immediately refuse to run.

One field in the structure is not a function pointer at all. uclamp_enabled, present only under CONFIG_UCLAMP_TASK, is a plain int at the very top of the vtable, and it lets the core scheduler skip utilization-clamp bookkeeping for classes that do not participate. Putting a data member in what is otherwise a dispatch table is unusual enough to look like an oversight; it is deliberate, and it is the same optimisation instinct that produced the class-skipping and fast-path logic described later in this section.

A key design principle is that not every class needs to implement every hook. The stop and idle classes, for example, have trivial implementations for most callbacks because they manage only a single distinguished task per CPU. The fair class, by contrast, has the most complex implementations because it handles the vast majority of tasks and must support hierarchical task groups, PELT load tracking, NUMA balancing, and CFS bandwidth control.

/* kernel/sched/sched.h — abbreviated sched_class */
struct sched_class {
    void (*enqueue_task)(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *p, int flags);
    bool (*dequeue_task)(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *p, int flags);
    void (*wakeup_preempt)(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *p, int flags);
    int  (*balance)(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *prev,
                    struct rq_flags *rf);
    struct task_struct *(*pick_task)(struct rq *rq, struct rq_flags *rf);
    struct task_struct *(*pick_next_task)(struct rq *rq,
                    struct task_struct *prev, struct rq_flags *rf);
    void (*put_prev_task)(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *p,
                    struct task_struct *next);
    void (*set_next_task)(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *p, bool first);
    void (*task_tick)(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *p, int queued);
    int  (*select_task_rq)(struct task_struct *p, int prev_cpu, int flags);
    void (*task_fork)(struct task_struct *p);

    /* Class-transition and priority-change hooks; see the change pattern below */
    void (*switching_from)(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *p);
    void (*switched_from)(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *p);
    void (*switching_to)(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *p);
    void (*switched_to)(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *p);
    u64  (*get_prio)(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *p);
    void (*prio_changed)(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *p, u64 oldprio);

    void (*update_curr)(struct rq *rq);
};
Table 7-10. Callback Families in sched_class
FamilyRepresentative HooksWhat They Control
Runqueue membershipenqueue_task(), dequeue_task()How tasks enter and leave the class-specific ready set
Preemptionwakeup_preempt(), task_tick()When the current task should be displaced
Task selectionpick_task(), pick_next_task(), put_prev_task(), set_next_task()How the next runnable task is chosen and installed
Placement and migrationselect_task_rq(), balance(), migrate_task_rq(), task_woken(), set_cpus_allowed(), find_lock_rq()Which CPU should host the task, when to pull or push work, and how the class reacts to a task arriving on a new run queue
Run queue availabilityrq_online(), rq_offline()How the class reacts when a CPU joins or leaves a scheduling domain
Voluntary yieldingyield_task(), yield_to_task()What sched_yield() and directed yielding mean inside this class
Lifecycle eventstask_fork(), task_dead()How scheduling state is created for a new task and torn down for a dying one
Introspection and groupingget_rr_interval(), task_change_group(), task_is_throttled()What the class reports to sched_rr_get_interval(), and how it handles cgroup moves and core scheduling
Class transitionsswitching_from(), switched_from(), switching_to(), switched_to()How a class hands a task over to another class, and what each side may assume while it happens
Priority changesget_prio(), prio_changed(), reweight_task()What “priority” means to this class, and how it reacts when that value changes
Accountingupdate_curr()How the running task is charged for CPU time

Changing a Task Safely: the sched_change Pattern

The hooks listed above answer the question “what can a class do?” A harder question is when the core scheduler is allowed to change a task’s scheduling properties at all. Almost every field the scheduler cares about — priority, weight, policy, class, cgroup, uclamp bounds — is not just a value but a position: the task’s place in an RB-tree, its slot in a priority bitmap, its contribution to a run queue’s load sum. Writing a new value into p->prio while the task is sitting in an RT priority list does not move it; it silently corrupts the list’s invariant instead.

The rule, therefore, is that a task must be removed from the scheduler’s view before its properties are modified and reinserted afterwards. For most of Linux’s history this was open-coded at every site that needed it, and the sequence was long enough to get wrong: check whether the task is queued, check whether it is currently running, dequeue it if queued, call put_prev_task() if running, make the change, enqueue it again if it had been queued, call set_next_task() if it had been running, and finally notify the class. The same fifteen lines appeared in rt_mutex_setprio(), __sched_setscheduler(), set_user_nice(), the cgroup migration path, and several places in sched_ext.

Linux 6.19 replaced all of them with a scoped guard. sched_change_begin() captures the task’s queueing state into a context structure and takes it off the run queue; sched_change_end() puts it back and fires the appropriate notifications; and the DEFINE_CLASS machinery wraps the pair so that the caller writes an ordinary block:

/* kernel/sched/syscalls.c — set_user_nice(), abbreviated */
scoped_guard (sched_change, p, DEQUEUE_SAVE) {
    p->static_prio = NICE_TO_PRIO(nice);
    set_load_weight(p, true);
    p->prio = effective_prio(p);
}
/* on leaving the block the task is re-enqueued and the class is notified */

The interesting part is what sched_change_end() does on the way out, because the notification it sends depends on what kind of change just happened. The caller signals this with the DEQUEUE_CLASS / ENQUEUE_CLASS flag pair, which — unusually for the dequeue and enqueue flag spaces — deliberately share the same bit value so that one flag word can serve both halves of the operation.

  • Without *_CLASS: a priority change within one class. sched_change_begin() snapshots the old priority and sched_change_end() calls prio_changed() with it, so the class can compare old against new and decide whether a reschedule is warranted.
  • With *_CLASS: the task is moving between classes. Four hooks fire in a fixed order that brackets the dequeue and enqueue from both sides: switching_from() before the old class releases the task, switched_from() after; switching_to() before the new class accepts it, switched_to() after. The kernel comment is explicit that none of the switch*_from() methods know the new class and none of the switch*_to() methods know the old one — each side sees only its own transition, which is what keeps the classes independent.

A class promotion is also the one place where a running task can suddenly outrank itself. If the task was running and its new class is above its old one, sched_change_end() notifies the run queue’s current highest class that it has been preempted and updates rq->next_class; if the change was a demotion, it simply calls resched_curr(), because the task may no longer be the right one to run.

The snapshotted priority is where the get_prio() hook earns its place. “Priority” is not a single quantity across the class hierarchy, and p->prio — an int in the 0–139 range — cannot express what the deadline class orders tasks by. So sched_change_begin() asks the class: if it implements get_prio(), that value is snapshotted instead of p->prio. The deadline class is currently the only implementer, and it returns p->dl.deadline — an absolute time in nanoseconds, after first calling update_curr_dl() so the value is not stale. This is why prio_changed() takes a u64 rather than the int it carried for fifteen years: the parameter is no longer a priority number but whatever ordering key the class uses, and prio_changed_dl() interprets its argument as a deadline and compares it with dl_time_before().

The change pattern is a good example of how kernel interfaces mature. The correct sequence was always the same; what changed in 6.19 is that it stopped being knowledge each caller had to have and became a construct the compiler enforces. The DEFINE_CLASS-based scoped guard means the re-enqueue cannot be forgotten on an early return or an error path — the same reasoning that motivates RAII in C++ and defer in Go, applied to run queue membership.

How the Classes Are Linked Together

A natural question arises once we know there are six scheduling classes: how does the scheduler know the order in which to consult them, and where is that order defined? In many object-oriented systems the answer would be a hand-maintained array or linked list initialized at boot. Linux takes a more elegant approach: it uses the linker itself to impose the ordering, so the class hierarchy is physically baked into the kernel image at compile time and requires zero runtime initialization.

Each scheduling class is defined with the DEFINE_SCHED_CLASS macro, which places the class descriptor into a dedicated ELF section named after the class. The six class definitions live in six separate source files — stop_task.c, deadline.c, rt.c, fair.c, ext.c, and idle.c — yet they do not reference one another. No file includes a central "register my class" call. Instead, the macro arranges the compiler output so that each struct sched_class instance lands in its own named section:

/* kernel/sched/sched.h */
#define DEFINE_SCHED_CLASS(name) \
const struct sched_class name##_sched_class \
    __aligned(__alignof__(struct sched_class)) \
    __section("__" #name "_sched_class")

The linker script in include/asm-generic/vmlinux.lds.h then collects these sections in a specific order and lays them out as a contiguous array in the final kernel image:

/* include/asm-generic/vmlinux.lds.h — sched class ordering */
__sched_class_highest = .;
    *(__stop_sched_class)
    *(__dl_sched_class)
    *(__rt_sched_class)
    *(__fair_sched_class)
    *(__ext_sched_class)
    *(__idle_sched_class)
__sched_class_lowest = .;

The result is an array of six struct sched_class instances, stored contiguously in memory from highest priority (stop) to lowest (idle). The symbols __sched_class_highest and __sched_class_lowest mark the beginning and one-past-the-end of this array. A comment in the source warns: "*CAREFUL* they are laid out in *REVERSE* order!!!" — meaning the highest-priority class sits at the lowest memory address. The comparison sched_class_above(a, b) is therefore a simple pointer comparison (a) < (b).

This design has several important advantages. First, iteration is a plain array walk — the for_each_class() macro is simply a for loop incrementing a pointer from __sched_class_highest to __sched_class_lowest. There are no linked-list pointers to follow and no indirect branches until the scheduler actually invokes a callback. Second, each class is fully self-contained: a developer adding a new class only needs to write a single source file with DEFINE_SCHED_CLASS(name) and add the corresponding section to the linker script — no modification of any existing class file is needed. Third, since the ordering is compile-time constant, the compiler and CPU branch predictor can optimize the iteration path aggressively.

Linux 7.0.12 refines this design further with an active-class iteration mechanism that can dynamically skip classes at runtime. The helper function next_active_class() decides whether to skip a class during the walk:

/* kernel/sched/sched.h */
static inline const struct sched_class *
next_active_class(const struct sched_class *class)
{
    class++;
#ifdef CONFIG_SCHED_CLASS_EXT
    if (scx_switched_all() && class == &fair_sched_class)
        class++;
    if (!scx_enabled() && class == &ext_sched_class)
        class++;
#endif
    return class;
}

When sched_ext is compiled in, two conditions are checked: if the BPF scheduler has taken over all fair tasks (scx_switched_all()), the fair class is skipped entirely because ext will handle those tasks; conversely, if sched_ext is not enabled, the ext class itself is skipped. This is why the core scheduler's hot path uses for_each_active_class() rather than the unconditional for_each_class(). The skipping logic costs only a branch or two per iteration — a negligible overhead — but saves the cost of invoking an empty class's pick_task() callback that would always return NULL.

Both loop forms are built on a lower-level range primitive rather than being written out directly, and the two primitives do not terminate the same way:

/* kernel/sched/sched.h */
#define for_class_range(class, _from, _to) \
    for (class = (_from); class < (_to); class++)

#define for_active_class_range(class, _from, _to)                \
    for (class = (_from); class != (_to); class = next_active_class(class))

The plain form advances with ++ and stops on <; the active form advances through next_active_class() and stops on !=. The change of comparison is forced by the change of step. A loop that can jump more than one element at a time cannot rely on landing exactly on its terminator — unless something guarantees it will — and here the guarantee is that the two skippable classes, fair and ext, both sit in the middle of the array. The idle class is last and is never a skip candidate, so the walk always arrives precisely at __sched_class_lowest. It is a small piece of coupling between the linker script and a loop macro, and worth noticing because a future class added after idle with skip logic of its own would turn this loop into a buffer overrun rather than an off-by-one.

The range forms exist as separate primitives because not every walk starts at the top. prev_balance(), which runs just before the class walk in the slow path, uses for_active_class_range(class, start_class, &idle_sched_class) — beginning at the previous task’s own class rather than at stop, and stopping short of idle. There is no point asking a class above the outgoing task to balance, because if such a class had work the outgoing task would not have been running in the first place.

Alongside the iteration machinery sit three one-line predicates — sched_stop_runnable(), sched_dl_runnable(), and sched_rt_runnable() — that answer “does this class have anything to run on this run queue?” by reading a counter directly rather than by calling through the class interface. They are what allow paths that only need a yes-or-no answer about a specific class to avoid an indirect call entirely; the recurring theme of this section is that the class abstraction is honoured everywhere except where it would cost something measurable.

Linux 7.0 adds a second, related field: rq->next_class, which caches the highest class with runnable work on this run queue. It is set from the picked task’s class on every pass through __schedule() and ratcheted upward by each wakeup, and it exists to solve a problem in the wakeup path rather than the pick path. When a task wakes, some class has to be told about it so it can decide whether the current task should be preempted — but before 7.0 that notification went only to the class of the task already running, so a wakeup that crossed a class boundary was invisible to the class that needed to act on it. With next_class available, wakeup_preempt() can compare the waking task’s class against the run queue’s current highest and handle the cross-class case explicitly:

/* kernel/sched/core.c — wakeup_preempt(), abbreviated */
if (p->sched_class == rq->next_class) {
    rq->next_class->wakeup_preempt(rq, p, flags);

} else if (sched_class_above(p->sched_class, rq->next_class)) {
    rq->next_class->wakeup_preempt(rq, p, flags);
    resched_curr(rq);
    rq->next_class = p->sched_class;
}

A wakeup into a class below the current highest falls through both arms and does nothing at all, which is correct: a fair task waking while an RT task is runnable cannot preempt anything.

The same field supports a second use with a different flavour. Several scheduler paths must drop the run queue lock — sched_balance_newidle() while it looks at other CPUs, sched_ext while it consults the BPF scheduler — and when they take it again the run queue may no longer be the one they reasoned about. rq_modified_begin() lowers next_class to the level the caller is about to operate at, and rq_modified_above() afterwards answers the question “did anything of a higher class become runnable while I was not holding the lock?” If it did, the caller abandons its result rather than installing a task that is no longer the right choice. Both helpers are pointer comparisons against a field already in cache, which is what makes it affordable to ask on a path this hot.

This linker-section arrangement is one of those classic Linux design patterns: make the fast path look like a plain array walk, and push complexity into build-time organization rather than runtime pointer chasing. It is also a pattern used elsewhere in the kernel — for example, the __initcall mechanism uses the same trick to order initialization functions by level.

How pick_next_task() Walks the Classes

The function pick_next_task() is arguably the single most important function in the scheduler: it is called on every context switch to answer the question "which task should run next on this CPU?" Its design reflects decades of optimization, because it sits directly on the hottest path in the kernel — every nanosecond spent here is a nanosecond stolen from useful work.

The implementation is split into two layers. The outer function pick_next_task() deals with core scheduling (CONFIG_SCHED_CORE) — the mechanism that coordinates task selection across SMT siblings to enforce security boundaries. When core scheduling is not enabled, it immediately delegates to __pick_next_task(), which contains the actual class-walking logic. When core scheduling is enabled, pick_next_task() performs a much more elaborate cross-CPU coordination dance, selecting compatible tasks for all sibling threads simultaneously. We will examine core scheduling in detail later (Section 11); here we focus on the common non-core path.

The inner function __pick_next_task() has two distinct code paths: a fast path for the overwhelmingly common case, and a slow path for everything else.

/* kernel/sched/core.c — __pick_next_task() */
static inline struct task_struct *
__pick_next_task(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *prev, struct rq_flags *rf)
{
    const struct sched_class *class;
