    struct task_struct *p;

    rq->dl_server = NULL;

    if (scx_enabled())
        goto restart;

    /*
     * Optimization: we know that if all tasks are in the fair class we
     * can call that function directly, but only if the @prev task wasn't
     * of a higher scheduling class, because otherwise those lose the
     * opportunity to pull in more work from other CPUs.
     */
    if (likely(!sched_class_above(prev->sched_class, &fair_sched_class) &&
               rq->nr_running == rq->cfs.h_nr_queued)) {

        p = pick_next_task_fair(rq, prev, rf);
        if (unlikely(p == RETRY_TASK))
            goto restart;

        if (!p) {
            p = pick_task_idle(rq, rf);
            put_prev_set_next_task(rq, prev, p);
        }

        return p;
    }

restart:
    prev_balance(rq, prev, rf);

    for_each_active_class(class) {
        if (class->pick_next_task) {
            p = class->pick_next_task(rq, prev, rf);
            if (unlikely(p == RETRY_TASK))
                goto restart;
            if (p)
                return p;
        } else {
            p = class->pick_task(rq, rf);
            if (unlikely(p == RETRY_TASK))
                goto restart;
            if (p) {
                put_prev_set_next_task(rq, prev, p);
                return p;
            }
        }
    }

    BUG(); /* The idle class should always have a runnable task. */
}

The fast path is the first if branch, and it is taken on the vast majority of scheduling decisions in a typical system. It checks two conditions: (1) the previously-running task was not from a class above fair (i.e., no RT or deadline task was just running), and (2) every runnable task on this CPU belongs to the fair class (rq->nr_running == rq->cfs.h_nr_queued). When both are true, the scheduler can skip the stop, deadline, and RT classes entirely and jump straight to pick_next_task_fair(). This saves several function pointer dereferences and the overhead of the class iteration loop. If even the fair class has nothing (all tasks just went to sleep), it falls through to pick_task_idle(). One subtle detail: if sched_ext is enabled, the fast path is bypassed entirely (the goto restart at the top), because sched_ext may need to intercept fair-class tasks.

The very first statement in the function, rq->dl_server = NULL, is easy to read past. It clears a field that a deadline server — a CBS reservation fronting for another class, described later in this chapter — sets when it wants the class beneath it to supply the actual task to run. Clearing it at the top of every pick means the field only ever carries a value that the current pick just established, so a server selection from a previous pass cannot leak into this one. The value is consumed a few lines later, in a helper we will come to shortly.

The slow path (after restart:) handles the general case. Before walking the classes, it calls prev_balance(), which gives each class from the previous task's class downward a chance to perform load balancing — for example, pulling RT tasks from overloaded CPUs. Then it iterates through all active classes from highest to lowest using for_each_active_class(). For each class, it tries two callback variants: pick_next_task() if the class provides one (this combined callback handles both putting the previous task away and selecting the next one), or the simpler pick_task() which only selects without side effects, followed by an explicit put_prev_set_next_task(). The first class to return a non-NULL task wins.

Two details of the balance pass are worth drawing out, because both are ordering constraints rather than optimisations. First, it terminates early: the loop breaks as soon as some class’s balance() returns true, which the kernel comment explains as knowing there is now “a runnable task of @class priority or higher.” Once that is established, asking lower classes to go looking for work would be wasted effort, since whatever they found could not be selected anyway. Second, the pass must happen before put_prev_task() rather than after. Balancing drops the run queue lock to inspect other CPUs, and the invariant the scheduler wants is that when the lock is released the outgoing task is in exactly the state it was in when the lock was taken. Putting it away first would break that: a concurrent observer on another CPU would see a task that is neither running nor queued.

The other helper that carries real weight here is put_prev_set_next_task(), which the pick_task() branch uses to swap one task for another. It does three things beyond the obvious pair of class calls. It hands off the deadline-server pointer — clearing it from the outgoing task, transferring rq->dl_server to the incoming one, and resetting the run queue field — which is where the NULL assignment at the top of the function finally pays off. It short-circuits entirely when the incoming task is the outgoing one, a common case worth not paying for. And it opens with WARN_ON_ONCE(rq->donor != prev), asserting that the task being put away is the one whose scheduling context the run queue believes it has been using — a check that only becomes meaningful once curr and donor can differ, which is the subject of the next subsection.

Two special return values merit attention. A class can return RETRY_TASK, which means "I changed something that invalidates the current iteration — start over." This happens, for example, when the RT class pushes or pulls tasks between CPUs during the balance phase, or when sched_ext decides to restart the pick after re-dispatching tasks. The BUG() at the end is a safety net: since the idle class always has a runnable task (the per-CPU idle thread), falling through all classes means something is seriously wrong.

The fast path optimization is a textbook example of the Linux kernel's "optimize for the common case" philosophy. On a server running hundreds of ordinary processes with no RT tasks, the scheduler never even looks at the stop, deadline, or RT classes — it goes straight to the EEVDF algorithm. The slow path exists only for the relatively rare moments when RT, deadline, or sched_ext tasks are present.

The donor Concept: Scheduling Context vs Execution Context

In older kernels, rq->curr served a single purpose: it pointed to the task currently running on this CPU. That task owned the CPU's time, its scheduling class determined preemption decisions, and its runtime accounting was charged accordingly. The concepts of "who is executing" and "who is being charged" were inseparable — they were always the same task.

Linux 7.0.12 introduces a fundamental separation of these two concepts through CONFIG_SCHED_PROXY_EXEC (proxy execution). The motivation comes from a long-standing problem: priority inversion in the scheduler itself. Consider a high-priority RT task that blocks on a mutex held by a low-priority fair task. Traditional priority inheritance (PI) boosts the lock holder's priority so it runs sooner and releases the lock. But what if the lock holder is itself blocked on another lock, or sleeping in the kernel? PI cannot help — the boosted task isn't runnable. Proxy execution solves this by allowing the high-priority task to execute on behalf of the lock holder, physically running the lock holder's code using the high-priority task's scheduling entitlement.

To make this work, the run queue now carries two distinct pointers:

  • rq->curr — the task whose register state (program counter, stack, etc.) is physically loaded on the CPU. This is the task whose code is actually executing.
  • rq->donor — the task whose scheduling context (class, priority, vruntime, bandwidth) is being used for all scheduling decisions and runtime accounting. This task "donates" its scheduling entitlement to whoever is actually executing.

The implementation in kernel/sched/sched.h reveals the elegance of this design — when proxy execution is disabled, both fields occupy the same memory via a union, so there is zero overhead:

/* kernel/sched/sched.h — struct rq (abbreviated) */
#ifdef CONFIG_SCHED_PROXY_EXEC
    struct task_struct __rcu *donor;  /* Scheduling context */
    struct task_struct __rcu *curr;   /* Execution context */
#else
    union {
        struct task_struct __rcu *donor; /* Scheduler context */
        struct task_struct __rcu *curr;  /* Execution context */
    };
#endif

It is worth being precise about how rarely that second field exists. CONFIG_SCHED_PROXY_EXEC is not merely default-off: in 7.0.12 it depends on EXPERT, on !PREEMPT_RT, and — most consequentially — on !SCHED_CLASS_EXT, because the sched_ext hooks have not yet been taught about split scheduling and execution contexts. Since general-purpose distribution kernels enable sched_ext, proxy execution cannot be built into them at all; and the real-time kernels that would benefit most from it are excluded by the PREEMPT_RT dependency. The feature is, for now, something you compile deliberately for yourself. When it is compiled in it is enabled by default and can be turned off with sched_proxy_exec=0 on the kernel command line, the test being a static key so that the disabled case costs nothing.

When CONFIG_SCHED_PROXY_EXEC=n, the union ensures rq->curr and rq->donor are the same pointer — writing to one writes to both, and the compiler optimizes away any distinction. Code that references either field works correctly regardless of the configuration. When proxy execution is enabled, the two become independent fields, and the helper rq_set_donor() updates the donor pointer using RCU semantics.

This separation permeates the scheduler. The tick handler (sched_tick()) charges time to rq->donor, not rq->curr. The fair scheduler's cfs_rq->curr corresponds to the donor's scheduling entity. Preemption checks use the donor's class and priority. The helper task_current_donor(rq, p) tests whether a task is the current donor (as opposed to task_current(rq, p) which tests physical execution).

Table 7-11. curr Versus donor
FieldMeaningUsually Equal?When It Can Differ
rq->currExecution context — task whose registers are loaded on the CPUYesProxy execution: a high-priority task runs the lock holder's code
rq->donorScheduling context — task whose priority/class/runtime is chargedYesThe blocked high-priority task donates its scheduling entitlement while another task physically executes

For a reader trained on older kernels, the donor concept explains why some code paths say "current task" while others say "current donor." Anywhere the scheduler makes a policy decision (which class to pick, whether to preempt, how much runtime to charge), it uses the donor. Anywhere the kernel needs to know whose code is running (signal delivery, fault handling, register saving), it uses curr. In the common case without proxy execution these are the same task, but the distinction exists throughout the code to support the general case.

Class Assignment at Fork Time

When a process calls fork() or clone(), the new child task must be assigned a scheduling class before it can ever be enqueued on a run queue. This assignment cannot happen at arbitrary points — it must occur in a carefully controlled window where the child exists but is not yet visible to the rest of the scheduler. Getting this wrong would allow a half-initialized task to be woken up by a signal or timer, leading to crashes in class-specific callbacks that expect fully initialized state.

The fork path is split into three distinct phases, each with a specific scheduler responsibility:

  1. sched_fork() — called early in copy_process(), before the child has a PID or is on any hash table. This is where the scheduling class is selected and basic scheduling entity fields are zeroed.
  2. sched_cgroup_fork() — called after the child's cgroup membership is determined. This assigns the task group, sets the initial CPU, and invokes the class-specific task_fork() callback (e.g., the fair class uses this to set the child's initial vruntime relative to the parent).
  3. wake_up_new_task() — called after copy_process() returns successfully. This is where the child is finally enqueued on a run queue and transitions from TASK_NEW to TASK_RUNNING.

The key function is sched_fork(). It begins by calling __sched_fork(), which zeroes all per-entity accounting fields (vruntime, vlag, exec_start, sum_exec_runtime) and initializes the RT, deadline, and sched_ext sub-entities. The child is then marked TASK_NEW — a state that prevents any external event from enqueueing it prematurely. Next, the child's priority is set to the parent's normal_prio (not prio), which deliberately strips any PI boosting the parent may have — the child should not inherit a temporarily elevated priority that was granted to solve someone else's lock contention.

/* kernel/sched/core.c — sched_fork() */
int sched_fork(u64 clone_flags, struct task_struct *p)
{
    __sched_fork(clone_flags, p);
    p->__state = TASK_NEW;

    /* Do not leak PI boosting priority to the child. */
    p->prio = current->normal_prio;

    uclamp_fork(p);

    /* Revert to default priority/policy on fork if requested. */
    if (unlikely(p->sched_reset_on_fork)) {
        if (task_has_dl_policy(p) || task_has_rt_policy(p)) {
            p->policy = SCHED_NORMAL;
            p->static_prio = NICE_TO_PRIO(0);
            p->rt_priority = 0;
        } else if (PRIO_TO_NICE(p->static_prio) < 0)
            p->static_prio = NICE_TO_PRIO(0);

        p->prio = p->normal_prio = p->static_prio;
        set_load_weight(p, false);
        p->se.custom_slice = 0;
        p->se.slice = sysctl_sched_base_slice;
        p->sched_reset_on_fork = 0;
    }

    if (dl_prio(p->prio))
        return -EAGAIN;

    scx_pre_fork(p);

    if (rt_prio(p->prio))
        p->sched_class = &rt_sched_class;
    else if (task_should_scx(p->policy))
        p->sched_class = &ext_sched_class;
    else
        p->sched_class = &fair_sched_class;

    init_entity_runnable_average(&p->se);
    return 0;
}

Several policy decisions encoded in this function deserve attention:

  • sched_reset_on_fork — A parent can set this flag (via sched_setattr()) to ensure that forked children always start at SCHED_NORMAL with nice 0, regardless of the parent's policy. This is a security and resource-control feature: it prevents an RT process from spawning unlimited RT children that could monopolize the system. Once the reset is applied, the flag is cleared so it does not propagate further.
  • Deadline rejection — If the child would inherit a deadline policy, sched_fork() returns -EAGAIN. Deadline tasks have admission-control budgets that cannot be automatically split between parent and child, so forking a deadline task requires explicit re-admission.
  • Class selection logic — The mapping is priority-first: the test is rt_prio(), which is a two-sided range check (prio < MAX_RT_PRIO && prio >= MAX_DL_PRIO), so it accepts 0 through 99 and rejects the negative values the deadline class uses — though as the earlier discussion of __normal_prio() showed, 99 is never actually produced. If the priority is in that range, the RT class is assigned. Otherwise, the policy field determines whether the task goes to sched_ext (if enabled and the policy qualifies) or to the fair class. Note that the stop and idle classes are never assigned here — those are reserved for kernel-internal tasks created through separate paths.
  • scx_pre_fork() — When sched_ext is active, this hook allows the BPF scheduler to prepare for the new task before class assignment, potentially allocating per-task BPF storage.

Two things about this function are not visible from the body alone. The first is its clone_flags parameter, which sched_fork() uses for nothing itself but passes down to __sched_fork() and from there to init_numa_balancing(). The flag that matters is CLONE_VM, and it distinguishes the two cases that fork() and pthread_create() represent. A new process gets a fresh address space, so its NUMA placement history is meaningless and numa_preferred_nid is reset to NUMA_NO_NODE. A new thread shares its parent’s address space and therefore its page placement, so the preferred node is kept and only the scan start is staggered. The same call also resets the address space’s scan sequence when it sees that mm_users is 1, which is the signal that this really is a brand-new address space rather than a shared one.

The second is that sched_fork() can leave state behind that must be undone. scx_pre_fork() may allocate per-task storage on behalf of a loaded BPF scheduler, and copy_process() can still fail after this point for reasons that have nothing to do with scheduling — a PID allocation failure, a signal pending, a security hook refusal. The fork path therefore has an explicit unwind entry point, sched_cancel_fork(), whose entire body is a call to scx_cancel_fork(). It is a one-line function today, but its existence is what lets the BPF scheduler assume that every prep it is asked to perform is matched by either a completion or a cancellation.

The -EAGAIN returned for a deadline parent propagates all the way out: fork() fails with EAGAIN, the same errno the kernel uses for “resource temporarily unavailable” when a process limit is hit. This is deliberate rather than a borrowed code — from the caller’s point of view a bandwidth reservation that cannot be subdivided is an exhausted resource, and the correct response is the same one: reduce demand, or ask again with different parameters. A deadline application that needs child processes must therefore fork first and set the deadline policy afterwards, in each child independently, so that each passes admission control on its own.

After sched_fork() returns, sched_cgroup_fork() assigns the task’s cgroup-based task group and invokes the class’s task_fork() callback. For the fair class, this callback places the child’s vruntime near the parent’s position on the timeline, ensuring the child does not get an unfair head start or an unfair penalty. Two details are worth noting. The group is passed through autogroup_task_group() before being stored, so a child inherits its parent’s autogroup rather than the raw cgroup when autogrouping is active. And this is where the child’s CPU is set for the first time, using __set_task_cpu() rather than the migrating variant — there is no previous CPU to migrate away from, so none of the per-CPU accounting fixups a real migration would require apply.

Finally sched_post_fork() runs, and it does three things rather than the two usually mentioned: sched_mm_cid_fork(), then uclamp_post_fork(), then scx_post_fork(). The first assigns the task a memory-map concurrency ID, a small dense integer identifying which of an address space’s concurrently running threads this one is. User space reads it through rseq to index per-CPU-like data structures without needing a real CPU number, and because the identifier is scoped to the address space rather than the machine, the range stays proportional to a process’s actual concurrency rather than to the CPU count. It is scheduler-adjacent rather than scheduling proper, but it is maintained on the scheduler’s fork, migrate, and exit paths because those are the only places that know when the set of running threads changes.

Why the Hierarchy Is Deliberately Unfair Across Classes

A common question from developers first encountering the scheduler architecture is: "Why doesn't Linux provide fairness between classes the way it provides fairness within the fair class?" After all, if an RT task runs continuously, it will starve every fair-class task on the same CPU indefinitely. Isn't that a bug?

The answer is no — it is a fundamental design decision rooted in the realization that different classes make different promises, and those promises are mutually incompatible. The RT class promises bounded-latency dispatch based on static priority alone. The deadline class promises that declared timing constraints will be met. The fair class promises proportional CPU sharing over time. There is no single definition of "fairness" that can satisfy all three simultaneously. Any attempt to blend them would weaken every guarantee: RT tasks would no longer have predictable response times, deadline tasks could miss their deadlines, and fair tasks would experience unpredictable latency spikes.

The kernel therefore draws sharp, non-negotiable boundaries between classes:

  • Stop outranks everything — migration and CPU hotplug must complete regardless of what else is running; there is no task important enough to delay a stop-class operation.
  • Deadline outranks RT — a task with a declared deadline carries a mathematically provable temporal guarantee (via EDF admission control). Fixed-priority RT makes no such global guarantee, so deadlines take precedence.
  • RT outranks fair — fixed-priority real-time response must not be diluted by proportional sharing. An audio thread at priority 90 must preempt a compilation job, every time, without exception.
  • Fair outranks idle — the idle class runs only when no other useful work exists. It is literally the absence of demand, not a competitor.

This strict layering means starvation across class boundaries is possible by design. Linux mitigates the practical risk through several mechanisms without breaking the semantic boundaries. The RT bandwidth throttle (sched_rt_runtime_us / sched_rt_period_us, defaulting to 950ms/1000ms) ensures that RT tasks cannot consume 100% of a CPU indefinitely — after using 95% of each period, they are throttled and fair tasks get the remaining 5%. Similarly, deadline admission control rejects tasks whose combined utilization would exceed CPU capacity. But these are safety valves, not fairness mechanisms — they protect liveness without compromising the priority model.

The philosophical consequence is that class selection is a strong commitment. Placing a task in the RT class means accepting that it may starve fair tasks. Placing a task in the deadline class means accepting admission-control constraints. The scheduler does not second-guess these decisions at runtime. This strict separation is also what makes the architecture extensible: when sched_ext was added, it slotted between fair and idle without requiring any changes to the RT or deadline class — each class operates independently within its assigned priority band.

Data Structures Used by the Scheduler

The algorithms described in the previous sections — EEVDF selection, RT bitmap lookup, deadline ordering — are only as good as the data structures that support them. A scheduling decision happens on every context switch (potentially millions of times per second across all CPUs), so the structures that hold runnable tasks must be optimized for the operations that matter most: enqueue, dequeue, and "pick the best task." Each class uses a different internal representation tuned to its specific algorithm:

  • The fair class uses an augmented red-black tree keyed by virtual deadline, giving O(log n) enqueue/dequeue and O(1) pick (leftmost cached).
  • The RT class uses a bitmap + linked list per priority level, giving O(1) pick via find_first_bit() and O(1) enqueue/dequeue.
  • The deadline class uses a red-black tree keyed by absolute deadline, giving O(log n) operations with earliest-deadline-first selection.
  • The sched_ext class uses dispatch queues (DSQs) — FIFO lists that BPF programs fill, giving O(1) consumption from the local DSQ.

All of these class-specific structures are embedded inside a single per-CPU run queue (struct rq), which is the scheduler's central data structure. The run queue also holds shared state: clock values, CPU capacity metrics, the current and donor task pointers, load tracking accumulators, and topology links. Understanding the run queue's layout — and especially its cache-line organization — is essential for understanding why the scheduler performs well under pressure.

Beyond the per-CPU state, each task carries its own scheduling entity (struct sched_entity, struct sched_rt_entity, struct sched_dl_entity) embedded in its task_struct. These per-task structures hold the accounting state that the class algorithms operate on: virtual runtime, deadline parameters, time slices, and group membership. The separation between per-CPU structures (where runnable tasks are organized) and per-task structures (where individual accounting lives) is a key architectural pattern — it minimizes lock contention by ensuring that most operations touch only the local CPU's run queue.

Two tensions shape almost every choice in these structures, and they are worth naming before the field lists start:

  • Locality pulls against compactness. The obvious way to make a structure fast is to make it small. struct rq does the opposite: it is deliberately large, with fields grouped and padded so that the handful read together in a hot loop share one cache line. What is being minimized is not bytes but cache misses per operation.
  • Precision pulls against the width of a register. Virtual time is a 64-bit quantity that wraps, and the fair scheduler needs weighted averages of it. Multiplying a full-width wrapping value by a weight overflows immediately, so several fields exist for no reason other than to keep the arithmetic inside 64 bits — which is also where one of the subtler bugs fixed in this release lived.

The rest of this section works through seven questions in that order:

  1. What does the per-CPU run queue hold, and why is its field order part of its design?
  2. What exactly does __schedule() do to the run queue, and what does its lock actually protect?
  3. How does the fair class represent virtual time so that averaging it does not overflow?
  4. How is a nice value turned into a share of the CPU?
  5. What state does an individual schedulable entity carry, and what did EEVDF add to it?
  6. How do the RT and deadline classes represent their ready sets, and why do they need different structures?
  7. Which scheduling fields live in task_struct itself, and how does the whole object graph fit together?

Version history. The 2.6 run queue held two arrays of 140 priority lists and swapped them when the active array drained; none of that survives. Priority arrays remain only inside the RT class, the fair class replaced them with a red-black tree, and the deadline and sched_ext classes did not exist. Two of the structures below also changed inside the 6.12–7.0 window: cfs_rq lost exec_clock and, more consequentially, lost min_vruntime — the monotonic floor that every previous description of CFS was built around — while sched_entity gained a protected-deadline field and a signed lag.

The Per-CPU Runqueue: struct rq

Every CPU in the system has exactly one struct rq, allocated statically as a per-CPU variable. This is the scheduler's most important data structure — it is accessed on every context switch, every timer tick, every wakeup, and every load-balancing pass. On a busy system, a single CPU's run queue may be touched thousands of times per second.

The run queue serves three roles simultaneously. First, it is a container: it embeds the class-specific sub-runqueues (cfs_rq, rt_rq, dl_rq, scx_rq) directly inside itself, avoiding an extra pointer dereference on the fast path. Second, it is a cache: it stores frequently-read values (clock timestamps, CPU capacity, task counts) that would otherwise require expensive cross-structure lookups. Third, it is a synchronization point: its raw_spinlock_t __lock protects all enqueue, dequeue, and task-state transitions on this CPU.

The kernel developers have painstakingly organized the fields within struct rq by cache-line affinity, and the source comments state the reasoning explicitly. The first cache line holds nr_running, ttwu_pending, cpu_capacity, curr/donor, and idle, and the comment above them is worth quoting for the one qualifier that carries the whole argument: these members are loaded together “without holding the rq->lock, in an extremely hot loop in update_sg_lb_stats().”

That the reads are lockless and remote is the point. A CPU running a load-balancing pass inspects every other CPU’s run queue without taking their locks, so these fields are pulled across the interconnect constantly by machines other than their owner. The comment adds that “even though some of them are frequently modified, they are loaded much more frequently than they are stored,” which is the trade being made: accept that writes to nr_running will invalidate the line, because reads outnumber them heavily.

Once that is understood, the placement of the lock follows. nr_switches is marked ____cacheline_aligned and the run queue lock sits immediately after it, so the lock lands on the next line rather than the first one. Had it shared the first line, every remote CPU acquiring the lock — a write — would invalidate the copy that every other CPU was holding for its lockless statistics read, turning an uncontended lock acquisition into a system-wide cache storm. The first line even ends with a bare /* padding left here deliberately */, confirming that the gap is intentional rather than an accident of field ordering.

Two more lines are aligned the same way. The utilization-clamp array gets one under CONFIG_UCLAMP_TASK, and the clock fields (clock_task, clock_pelt, clock, and the idle-tracking timestamps) get another, because they are “frequently referenced and updated together” on every tick.

This deliberate layout means that struct rq is large (several hundred bytes depending on configuration), but the scheduler rarely touches more than two or three cache lines in any single operation. The design prioritizes access locality over structure compactness.

/* kernel/sched/sched.h — rq fields, condensed */
struct rq {
    unsigned int        nr_running;
    unsigned int        ttwu_pending;
    unsigned long       cpu_capacity;

#ifdef CONFIG_SCHED_PROXY_EXEC
    struct task_struct __rcu *donor;
    struct task_struct __rcu *curr;
#else
    union {
        struct task_struct __rcu *donor;
        struct task_struct __rcu *curr;
    };
#endif
    struct task_struct  *idle;
    /* padding left here deliberately */

    /* --- next cache line: the hot lock --- */
    u64                 nr_switches ____cacheline_aligned;
    raw_spinlock_t      __lock;

    struct cfs_rq       cfs;
    struct rt_rq        rt;
    struct dl_rq        dl;
    struct scx_rq       scx;      /* CONFIG_SCHED_CLASS_EXT */

    unsigned long       nr_uninterruptible;
    struct sched_dl_entity *dl_server;
    struct task_struct  *stop;
    const struct sched_class *next_class;
    unsigned long       next_balance;
    struct mm_struct    *prev_mm;

    /* --- next cache line: clocks, updated together --- */
    u64                 clock_task ____cacheline_aligned;
    u64                 clock_pelt;
    u64                 clock;
    unsigned int        clock_update_flags;

    struct root_domain  *rd;
    struct sched_domain *sd;
    int                 cpu;
    int                 online;
};
Table 7-12. Important struct rq Fields
FieldMeaningWhy the Scheduler Needs It
nr_runningTotal runnable tasks visible on this CPUFast emptiness and overload tests
currExecution context actually runningContext-switch bookkeeping and return-path checks
donorScheduling context being chargedProxy execution and class accounting correctness
idlePer-CPU idle threadGuaranteed fallback runnable task
stopPer-CPU stopper threadHighest-priority migration and hotplug work
clockRunqueue clock including broader scheduler timingGeneral runqueue event ordering
clock_taskTask accounting clock excluding some IRQ timeFair and RT runtime charging
next_classHighest scheduling class with runnable work on this run queueLets a wakeup notify the right class, and detects class changes across a dropped rq lock
cfs, rt, dl, scxEmbedded class runqueuesPer-class ready sets without extra indirection
nr_uninterruptibleCount of tasks on this CPU in uninterruptible sleep — but see below, only the sum across CPUs is meaningfulFeeds the 1/5/15-minute load average
rd, sdRoot-domain and sched-domain topology pointersLoad balancing, overload detection, EAS placement

The runqueue clock deserves special attention. update_rq_clock() is called near the top of __schedule(), on wakeup paths, and in balancing code so all subsequent accounting uses a coherent notion of time. clock_task is the number the fair and RT schedulers normally care about because it excludes time the CPU was busy handling unrelated interrupts.

One of the easiest ways to misread scheduler code is to assume every timestamp refers to “wall clock.” In reality, the scheduler keeps several clocks because not every nanosecond of elapsed time should be charged to the running task.

One field in that table carries a warning label in the source, and it explains a number that every Linux user has read at some point. nr_uninterruptible counts tasks in uninterruptible sleep — the D state in ps output, typically a thread blocked in the middle of I/O. It exists because Linux’s load average, uniquely among Unix systems, counts those tasks as load rather than counting only runnable ones. The reasoning is that a machine with ten threads stuck waiting on a saturated disk is under load in every sense a user cares about, even though its CPUs may be idle. This is also why a Linux load average can exceed the CPU count on a machine that is not CPU-bound at all, and why the number is better read as “demand for resources” than as “CPU utilization.”

The warning concerns the per-CPU value. A task can be counted into one CPU’s nr_uninterruptible when it blocks, migrate while it sleeps, and be counted out of a different CPU’s when it wakes. The individual counters therefore drift and an isolated one can even be negative when read as a signed quantity; the kernel comment states plainly that this “is part of a global counter where only the total sum over all CPUs matters.” It is a legitimate use of a per-CPU variable as nothing more than a shard of a global sum, chosen because the alternative — one shared counter touched on every block and wakeup — would be a far worse bottleneck than an occasionally meaningless intermediate value.

How rq Participates in __schedule()

The run queue is not just a passive data structure that holds tasks — it is the active participant at the center of every context switch. The function __schedule() (the heart of the scheduler, called on every voluntary sleep, preemption, and idle entry) interacts with the run queue through a precise sequence of lock acquisition, clock update, task selection, and pointer rotation. Understanding this sequence is key to understanding how all the pieces — classes, entities, locks, and clocks — fit together in practice.

The critical insight is that __schedule() holds the run queue lock for the entire duration of the scheduling decision. No other code path on this CPU can enqueue or dequeue tasks while the decision is in progress. Remote CPUs performing wakeups must acquire this same lock (via rq_lock() in ttwu_do_activate()) before modifying this CPU's run queue. This single-lock-per-CPU design is what makes per-CPU scheduling decisions serializable without global locks — and it is why the lock is placed on its own cache line.

The core scheduling path uses rq as the shared ledger for selection and switching:

  1. __schedule() disables local interrupts and acquires rq->__lock. The smp_mb__after_spinlock() ensures full memory ordering so subsequent reads see all prior stores by remote CPUs (critical for wakeup correctness).
  2. It promotes pending clock-update flags and calls update_rq_clock(rq) to advance the run queue's timestamps. All subsequent accounting in this scheduling pass will use this coherent snapshot of time.
  3. If the current task is voluntarily sleeping (non-preempt path with non-zero state), try_to_block_task() either deactivates it from the run queue or, under proxy execution, leaves it on-queue but marked as blocked.
  4. pick_next_task(rq, rq->donor, &rf) walks the class hierarchy to choose the next scheduling context, using the donor (not curr) as the "previous" reference for put-prev operations.
  5. rq_set_donor(rq, next) updates the policy owner, and rq->next_class is set to the new task's class, re-establishing the highest-runnable-class cache that wakeups consult.
  6. If the selected task differs from the previous one, RCU_INIT_POINTER(rq->curr, next) publishes the new execution owner under RCU, the switch counter is incremented, and context_switch() performs the actual MM switch + register state swap.
/* kernel/sched/core.c — __schedule(), highly condensed */
local_irq_disable();
rq_lock(rq, &rf);
smp_mb__after_spinlock();
update_rq_clock(rq);

if (!preempt && prev_state)
    try_to_block_task(rq, prev, &prev_state, !task_is_blocked(prev));

next = pick_next_task(rq, rq->donor, &rf);
rq_set_donor(rq, next);
rq->next_class = next->sched_class;
clear_tsk_need_resched(prev);

if (prev != next) {
    RCU_INIT_POINTER(rq->curr, next);
    rq = context_switch(rq, prev, next, &rf);
} else {
    raw_spin_rq_unlock_irq(rq);
}

Step 2 of that sequence — “promotes pending clock-update flags” — and the &rf argument threaded through every listing in this chapter both refer to the same small mechanism, which is worth unpacking because it enforces a rule the rest of the scheduler depends on.

The rule is that you may not read the run queue clock without having updated it first. Reading a stale rq->clock does not crash anything; it silently charges a task the wrong amount of runtime, which surfaces later as a fairness anomaly with no obvious cause. To make the mistake noisy instead of silent, rq_clock() and rq_clock_task() are not plain field accesses but inline functions that assert on every call:

/* kernel/sched/sched.h */
#define RQCF_REQ_SKIP        0x01
#define RQCF_ACT_SKIP        0x02
#define RQCF_UPDATED         0x04

static inline void assert_clock_updated(struct rq *rq)
{
    /*
     * The only reason for not seeing a clock update since the
     * last rq_pin_lock() is if we're currently skipping updates.
     */
    WARN_ON_ONCE(rq->clock_update_flags < RQCF_ACT_SKIP);
}

static inline u64 rq_clock(struct rq *rq)
{
    lockdep_assert_rq_held(rq);
    assert_clock_updated(rq);

    return rq->clock;
}

The three flags encode a small state machine. RQCF_REQ_SKIP is a request, set by rq_clock_skip_update() when a caller knows the clock was just updated and the imminent call to __schedule() need not redo the work. RQCF_ACT_SKIP means that skipping is actually in effect and update_rq_clock() calls are being ignored. RQCF_UPDATED is purely a debug bit recording that an update has happened since the lock was last pinned.

The promotion from requested to active is where the trick lies: it is a single left shift, because the constants were chosen so that RQCF_REQ_SKIP << 1 == RQCF_ACT_SKIP. One shift instruction on the context-switch path converts a pending request into an active state, and the same shift conveniently moves RQCF_UPDATED out of its usual position — which is why the kernel comment warns that inside __schedule() the test must be written if (rq->clock_update_flags >= RQCF_UPDATED) rather than as a bit test. The value is never shifted more than one position, because the next unpin shifts it back.

That brings us to rq_flags, the rf parameter. Beyond carrying these flags across a lock release, it holds a lockdep pin cookie. rq_pin_lock() pins the lock and clears the update bits; rq_unpin_lock() unpins it and saves whether an update occurred. The pin exists so that lockdep can catch a specific class of bug: a function that receives the run queue lock already held, drops it internally, and returns without having reacquired it. Because the caller cannot see this happen, it goes on operating under an assumption that is no longer true. Pinning makes the release visible — unpinning must be explicit, so an accidental drop is caught at the point it occurs rather than at whatever corruption it eventually causes. The pattern recurs throughout the scheduler precisely because the paths that legitimately drop the lock, such as load balancing and the sched_ext hooks, are exactly the ones where an illegitimate drop would be hardest to find.

The Fair-Class Runqueue: struct cfs_rq

While struct rq is the scheduler's global per-CPU container, struct cfs_rq is where the fair class keeps its own private world. It is the data structure that implements the EEVDF algorithm's state: the red-black tree of runnable entities, the weighted-average virtual time that determines eligibility, and the bandwidth accounting that enforces cgroup CPU quotas.

A crucial design point is that cfs_rq does not operate on task_struct objects directly. Instead, it manages sched_entity objects. This level of indirection exists to support hierarchical group scheduling: a sched_entity can represent either a single task or an entire task group (cgroup). When task groups are in use, the scheduler builds a tree of cfs_rq instances — one per group per CPU — with each group's sched_entity competing in its parent's cfs_rq. The top-level cfs_rq is embedded directly in struct rq; lower-level ones are allocated dynamically when cgroups are created. This hierarchical design allows the scheduler to first distribute CPU time fairly among groups, and then within each group, fairly among individual tasks.

On a system without cgroups or task groups, there is exactly one cfs_rq per CPU (the one embedded in struct rq), and every fair-class task's sched_entity is enqueued directly into it. In this flat case the hierarchy collapses to a single level, and the overhead of indirection is negligible because the compiler can inline most of the group-scheduling paths away when CONFIG_FAIR_GROUP_SCHED is disabled.

Before the field list, the four counters at the top deserve a note, because their naming encodes the hierarchy and the chapter has already relied on one of them without saying so. nr_queued counts the entities enqueued on this cfs_rq — and since a group entity counts as one, that is a count of immediate children, not of tasks. The h_ prefix on the other three means hierarchical: they count actual tasks in the entire subtree below this queue. h_nr_queued is every fair task beneath it, h_nr_runnable excludes the ones sitting in delayed-dequeue state, and h_nr_idle counts only the SCHED_IDLE ones.

The distinction is what makes the pick fast path shown earlier in this chapter correct. That test was rq->nr_running == rq->cfs.h_nr_queued, and it is asking “is every runnable task on this CPU a fair task?” Written with nr_queued instead it would compare a machine-wide task count against a count of top-level cgroups and would be wrong on any system using group scheduling — which, given that systemd places services in cgroups by default, is essentially all of them. Maintaining a hierarchical count costs a walk up the parent chain on every enqueue and dequeue; it buys an O(1) answer to a question the scheduler asks on every context switch.

The structure's fields fall into four functional groups: task counts (how many entities are queued, how many are in idle policy), EEVDF state (the weighted virtual-time sums and the rb-tree), load tracking (PELT averages for balancing and cpufreq), and bandwidth control (remaining runtime quota and throttle state). Let us examine the key fields:

/* kernel/sched/sched.h — key cfs_rq fields */
struct cfs_rq {
    struct load_weight  load;
    unsigned int        nr_queued;
    unsigned int        h_nr_queued;
    unsigned int        h_nr_runnable;
    unsigned int        h_nr_idle;

    s64                 sum_w_vruntime;
    u64                 sum_weight;
    u64                 zero_vruntime;
    struct rb_root_cached tasks_timeline;

    struct sched_entity *curr;
    struct sched_entity *next;

    struct sched_avg    avg;

    int                 runtime_enabled;
    s64                 runtime_remaining;
    struct list_head    throttled_list;
};
Table 7-13. EEVDF-Oriented Fields in struct cfs_rq
FieldMeaningRole in EEVDF
sum_w_vruntimeWeighted sum of entity vruntime offsets from zero_vruntimeLets the scheduler compute average virtual time without overflow
sum_weightTotal scaled weight represented in the treeDenominator for average virtual runtime
zero_vruntimeMoving base value near the current vruntime regionKeeps deltas small and arithmetic stable
tasks_timelineAugmented RB-tree of runnable entitiesStores deadline order plus subtree eligibility hints
currCurrent fair entityCharged by update_curr() and considered during selection
nextLatency hint (“buddy”)Improves handoff locality without violating fairness
avgPELT utilization and load stateFeeds balancing, cpufreq, and energy-aware placement
runtime_remainingPer-runqueue CFS bandwidth budgetThrottles cgroups that exceed quota

One member omitted from the condensed listing is worth showing, because it is the only place in cfs_rq with a lock of its own:

/* kernel/sched/sched.h — inside struct cfs_rq */
struct {
    raw_spinlock_t  lock ____cacheline_aligned;
    int             nr;
    unsigned long   load_avg;
    unsigned long   util_avg;
    unsigned long   runnable_avg;
} removed;

This exists to solve an ordering problem in PELT accounting. When a task migrates away or dies, its accumulated load must be subtracted from the queue it is leaving — but the code performing the removal is generally running on the other CPU and does not hold the departed queue’s run queue lock. Taking that lock would mean acquiring two run queue locks at once on a very hot path, with all the deadlock-ordering care that implies.

The removed struct is the workaround: a small mailbox with its own dedicated lock, into which the departing side merely adds up the amounts to be subtracted and bumps nr. Nothing is decayed and no averages are recomputed there. Later, whenever the owning CPU next runs update_cfs_rq_load_avg(), it checks removed.nr, and if anything is pending it takes the small lock, swap()s the three accumulators out to local variables, zeroes the counter, and releases it immediately — doing the real arithmetic afterwards, outside the critical section. The whole structure is a deferred-subtraction queue that lets one CPU hand accounting work to another without either of them waiting on the other’s run queue lock. Giving that lock its own cache line, inside a structure whose other fields are written constantly under the run queue lock, is the same false-sharing reasoning that shaped struct rq.

Older CFS descriptions emphasize min_vruntime, a monotonically advancing floor that served as the queue’s reference point. It is worth being clear that this field no longer exists. Linux 6.19 removed cfs_rq::min_vruntime and replaced it with zero_vruntime, and the reason is precisely the property the old name advertised.

The reference point exists so that virtual runtimes can be stored as small offsets rather than full-width values: EEVDF needs the weighted average of the entities’ vruntimes, and multiplying a wrapping 64-bit vruntime by a weight overflows at once. Subtracting a nearby base first keeps the products small. For that trick to work the base must stay close to the average it is helping to compute — and the average is not monotonic. When a task that was running behind its entitlement leaves the queue, the weighted average of what remains moves backward. A floor that can only advance cannot follow it, so over time the two drift apart, the offsets stop being small, and the arithmetic they were protecting breaks down.

zero_vruntime is the same idea without the monotonicity: a moving base kept near the current average, free to move in either direction. The cost is that the average has to be recomputed more often; the benefit is that the offsets stay bounded. The fix carries a Fixes: tag pointing back at the original EEVDF merge and was sent to stable, which is a fair indication of how long the flaw went unnoticed.

What has not changed is the shape of the question. The queue is no longer “pick leftmost vruntime.” It is “compute which entities are owed service, then among those, pick the earliest virtual deadline.” The extra fields in cfs_rq — sum_w_vruntime, sum_weight, and the base they are measured against — exist to make that first step cheap enough to run on every pick.

Weights: struct load_weight and Nice Values

The entire fair-scheduling algorithm rests on a single idea: tasks with higher weight deserve proportionally more CPU time. The struct load_weight is the tiny structure that encodes this share. Every sched_entity carries one, and the fair scheduler uses it in every virtual-runtime calculation — making it one of the most frequently accessed fields in the kernel. Its design reflects that frequency: it pre-computes a reciprocal to turn divisions into multiplications on the hot path.

/* include/linux/sched.h — simplified */
struct load_weight {
    unsigned long weight;
    u32           inv_weight;
};

weight is the task's share parameter, derived from nice. inv_weight is a cached reciprocal used to avoid expensive division in the hot path. For nice 0 the nominal weight is 1024; each nice step changes weight by roughly a factor of 1.25.

Table 7-14. Selected Nice-to-Weight Mappings
NiceWeightInterpretation
-2088761Massively favored relative to background work
-109548About 9.3x the share of nice 0
01024Baseline share
10110About one ninth of nice 0
1915Tiny best-effort share

Two refinements matter when reading real values rather than the table. The first is that inv_weight is not an arbitrary reciprocal but specifically 232/weight, pre-computed for all forty nice levels in a companion table, sched_prio_to_wmult[]. Storing the inverse at a fixed binary scale is what turns a division into a multiply followed by a shift, and division is expensive enough on the accounting path — which runs on every tick and every dequeue — to justify a second forty-entry table.

The second refinement is that the weight stored in load_weight is not the number in the table. On 64-bit builds the scheduler carries load at higher resolution than the user-visible weight:

/* kernel/sched/sched.h */
#ifdef CONFIG_64BIT
# define NICE_0_LOAD_SHIFT  (SCHED_FIXEDPOINT_SHIFT + SCHED_FIXEDPOINT_SHIFT)
# define scale_load(w)      ((w) << SCHED_FIXEDPOINT_SHIFT)
# define scale_load_down(w)                    \
({                                             \
    unsigned long __w = (w);                   \
                                               \
    if (__w)                                   \
        __w = max(2UL, __w >> SCHED_FIXEDPOINT_SHIFT); \
    __w;                                       \
})
#else
# define NICE_0_LOAD_SHIFT  (SCHED_FIXEDPOINT_SHIFT)
# define scale_load(w)      (w)
# define scale_load_down(w) (w)
#endif

#define NICE_0_LOAD     (1L << NICE_0_LOAD_SHIFT)

SCHED_FIXEDPOINT_SHIFT is 10, so on any 64-bit machine a nice-0 task’s stored weight is 1024 << 10, and NICE_0_LOAD is 220 = 1,048,576 rather than 1024. The extra ten bits of headroom exist for group scheduling: a cgroup’s share is divided among its children, and without the additional resolution a deep hierarchy would round small children’s shares to zero. The header notes that the higher resolution is “really only required when CONFIG_FAIR_GROUP_SCHED=y… but to increase coverage and consistency always enable it on 64-bit platforms” — a deliberate choice to have one code path exercised by everyone rather than a rarely-tested variant.

The asymmetry between the two macros is worth a second look. scale_load() is a plain shift, but scale_load_down() clamps its result to a minimum of 2 for any non-zero input. That floor is a division guard: downscaled weights end up as denominators, and a group whose share rounded down to zero or one would produce either a division by zero or a task with effectively infinite entitlement. Clamping to 2 costs an unmeasurable amount of fairness at the very bottom of the nice range and removes an entire class of failure.

If two runnable tasks have weights 1024 and 2048, the second should receive about twice as much CPU time over the long run. Fair scheduling enforces this by making virtual runtime advance more slowly for the heavier task: consuming one millisecond of wall time costs it fewer virtual nanoseconds.

The Universal Fair-Scheduler Atom: struct sched_entity

If cfs_rq is the container, then struct sched_entity is what it contains. This structure is the fundamental unit of accounting in the fair scheduler — it carries all the state that EEVDF needs to decide how much CPU time an entity has consumed, how much it is owed, and where it should sit in the timeline. Every fair-scheduled task embeds one in its task_struct, and every fair-scheduled task group owns one too. This dual use is what makes hierarchical scheduling possible: the same algorithm and the same code paths can schedule a thread among peer threads or schedule a cgroup among peer cgroups, without special-casing either.

The structure has evolved significantly from the original CFS days. Early CFS needed only vruntime and a tree node. EEVDF adds deadline (the virtual deadline that determines tree ordering and selection), vlag (how far behind or ahead the entity is relative to its ideal service — the eligibility criterion), and slice (the requested time quantum that controls how far into the future the next deadline is placed). Together, these fields implement the "eligible entity with earliest deadline" selection without requiring any global sorting or linear scans — the rb-tree augmentation encodes all necessary information for an O(log n) pick.

Two details in that list are easy to skim past and worth stating plainly. First, vlag is signed. It has to be: lag is defined as the difference between the service an entity should have received and what it actually received, so a negative lag means the entity is running ahead of its entitlement and a positive lag means it is owed time. An unsigned lag would be a contradiction in terms, and the eligibility test — an entity is eligible when its lag is non-negative — is a sign test.

Second, slice is not the only quantum-related field. vprot, added in 6.17, is a protected deadline: it marks a point in virtual time before which the entity should not be preempted by an ordinary wakeup, so that a task that has just been scheduled in is guaranteed some minimum amount of run time rather than being displaced immediately by the next arrival. Without it, a stream of wakeups can preempt a task repeatedly before it accomplishes anything, and the resulting context-switch storm costs far more than the fairness it buys. min_slice and max_slice are subtree aggregates in the same spirit as min_vruntime: they let the group-scheduling code answer questions about an entire cgroup subtree without descending into it.

The four unsigned char flags are similarly load-bearing. sched_delayed marks an entity that has blocked but is deliberately being left on the runqueue for a while (discussed under delayed dequeue later in this chapter); custom_slice records that user space set an explicit slice length through sched_setattr(), so the scheduler must not overwrite it with the default; and rel_deadline notes that the entity’s stored deadline is relative rather than absolute, which is how a deadline survives being dequeued and requeued.

The hierarchy pointers — parent, cfs_rq, and my_q — deserve special attention. cfs_rq points to the runqueue this entity is enqueued on (its parent's queue). my_q points to the runqueue this entity owns (only non-NULL for group entities). parent links upward in the hierarchy. For a leaf task, my_q is NULL and parent points to the group entity representing its cgroup. For the root-level entity, parent is NULL. This three-pointer linkage allows the scheduler to walk up and down the hierarchy during enqueue, dequeue, and update_curr() propagation.

There is a fourth hierarchy field, and it is the one that makes cross-group preemption decisions possible. depth records how many levels down the task-group tree this entity sits. Its purpose becomes clear the moment you try to answer “should this waking task preempt the current one?” in a hierarchy: two entities at different depths are not comparable, because their vruntimes are measured against different queues with different weights. Comparing them directly would be a category error.

find_matching_se() resolves this by walking both entities up the tree until they are at the same depth, then continuing to walk both in lockstep until they share a parent. Only then are they siblings on one cfs_rq, measured in the same units, and only then does comparing their deadlines mean anything. Storing the depth rather than counting parents on each call turns the first half of that walk into subtraction. Under CONFIG_FAIR_GROUP_SCHED=n the whole function compiles down to an empty body, because there is only ever one level.

The listing below also omits three timing fields that the accounting paths rely on constantly. exec_start is the timestamp at which the entity most recently began running, and it is what update_curr() differences against the current clock to work out how much to charge. sum_exec_runtime accumulates that charge over the entity’s lifetime in real nanoseconds — not virtual ones — which is what makes it the value reported to user space rather than vruntime. prev_sum_exec_runtime snapshots sum_exec_runtime at schedule-in, so the difference between them gives the runtime consumed during the current slice alone. And nr_migrations simply counts how many times the entity has changed CPU, which the balancer and the NUMA code consult as a rough measure of how settled a task is.

Two comments in the real declaration are worth noticing as well. A bare /* hole */ follows the four unsigned char flags, documenting the padding the compiler inserts there so that a future flag can be added without silently growing the structure. And avg carries its own note explaining that it is placed on a separate cache line “so it does not collide with read-mostly values above” — the same locality argument that governs struct rq, applied one level down.

/* include/linux/sched.h — key fields of sched_entity */
struct sched_entity {
    struct load_weight  load;
    struct rb_node      run_node;
    u64                 deadline;
    u64                 min_vruntime;   /* subtree minimum, for the augmented tree */
    u64                 min_slice;      /* subtree minimum slice */
    u64                 max_slice;      /* subtree maximum slice */

    unsigned char       on_rq;
    unsigned char       sched_delayed;
    unsigned char       rel_deadline;
    unsigned char       custom_slice;

    u64                 vruntime;
    s64                 vlag;           /* signed: ahead of or behind ideal */
    u64                 vprot;          /* protected deadline, minimum quantum */
    u64                 slice;
    u64                 sum_exec_runtime;

    struct sched_entity *parent;
    struct cfs_rq       *cfs_rq;
    struct cfs_rq       *my_q;

    struct sched_avg    avg;
};
Table 7-15. Key sched_entity Fields
FieldMeaningUsed By
vruntimeWeighted service consumed so farFairness accounting, placement, lag
deadlineVirtual deadline for EEVDF orderingRB-tree ordering and selection
vlagVirtual lag relative to ideal service; signed — negative means running ahead of entitlementEligibility (a sign test) and wakeup placement
sliceCurrent requested slice lengthDeadline computation
vprotProtected deadline: virtual time before which an ordinary wakeup should not preempt this entityGuarantees a minimum quantum and suppresses preemption storms
min_slice, max_sliceSubtree aggregates of sliceGroup scheduling, without descending the subtree
sched_delayedBlocked but deliberately left queuedDelayed dequeue
run_nodeRB-tree linkRunqueue membership
parentParent entity in task-group hierarchyRecursive enqueue/dequeue
my_qChild runqueue owned by this entityGroup scheduling

The Real-Time Runqueue: struct rt_rq

The RT class has a fundamentally different problem to solve compared to the fair class. Fair scheduling asks "which entity has received proportionally the least service?" — a question that requires continuous accounting and comparison. RT scheduling asks a much simpler question: "which runnable task has the highest static priority?" The answer must come in O(1) time, because RT is all about bounded worst-case latency — spending O(log n) to walk a tree would add unpredictable delay that varies with the number of runnable tasks.

The solution is the rt_prio_array: a bitmap of 100 bits (one per RT priority level, 0–99) plus an array of 100 linked lists. When a task at priority p becomes runnable, its bit is set and the task is appended to queue[p]. When the scheduler needs the highest-priority task, it calls sched_find_first_bit() on the bitmap — a single machine instruction on most architectures (__ffs / clz) — then takes the first entry from the corresponding list. Dequeue clears the bit only when the list becomes empty. The result is O(1) pick regardless of how many RT tasks are running.

The linked lists within each priority level preserve FIFO ordering for SCHED_FIFO tasks and round-robin rotation for SCHED_RR tasks. When an RR task's time slice expires, task_tick_rt() moves it to the tail of its priority list and sets TIF_NEED_RESCHED, giving the next same-priority task a turn. The rr_nr_running counter tracks how many RR tasks are present so the scheduler can skip time-slice logic when none are active.

/* kernel/sched/sched.h — simplified rt_rq */
struct rt_prio_array {
    DECLARE_BITMAP(bitmap, MAX_RT_PRIO+1); /* include 1 bit for delimiter */
    struct list_head queue[MAX_RT_PRIO];
};

struct rt_rq {
    struct rt_prio_array active;
    unsigned int         rt_nr_running;
    unsigned int         rr_nr_running;
    struct { int curr; int next; } highest_prio;
    bool                 overloaded;
    struct plist_head    pushable_tasks;
#ifdef CONFIG_RT_GROUP_SCHED
    int                  rt_throttled;
    u64                  rt_time;
    u64                  rt_runtime;
#endif
};

The +1 in the bitmap declaration is not a rounding allowance; it is a sentinel, and the kernel spells out why with the comment “include 1 bit for delimiter.” There are 100 priority levels and 100 list heads, but 101 bits, and init_rt_rq() permanently sets the extra one:

/* kernel/sched/rt.c — init_rt_rq(), abridged */
for (i = 0; i < MAX_RT_PRIO; i++)
    __clear_bit(i, array->bitmap);

/* delimiter for bitsearch: */
__set_bit(MAX_RT_PRIO, array->bitmap);

Because bit 100 is always set, sched_find_first_bit() can never fail. It is written as an unrolled pair of __ffs() calls with no bounds check and no “nothing found” return path, because an empty run queue is not a case it has to handle — the delimiter guarantees a result, and callers distinguish “no RT tasks” by checking rt_nr_running rather than by inspecting the search result. Removing a branch from a function called on every RT pick is worth one bit of memory, and this is the sort of trade that recurs whenever a data structure must offer a hard latency bound: pay a fixed cost up front so that the runtime path has no conditionals left in it.

The bitmap gives O(1)-ish lookup of the highest priority with queued tasks. The lists preserve FIFO order among peers of the same static priority. The pushable_tasks plist supports SMP push/pull migration: an overloaded CPU can identify which RT tasks are eligible to move.

The Deadline Runqueue: struct dl_rq

The deadline class implements Earliest Deadline First (EDF) scheduling, which requires a very different data structure than the RT class's bitmap. In EDF, the task with the nearest absolute deadline runs first — and deadlines are arbitrary 64-bit timestamps, not a fixed set of 100 priority levels. A bitmap is useless here; what the scheduler needs is a structure that efficiently answers "which runnable task has the smallest deadline?" while supporting dynamic insertions and removals as tasks wake up, block, and have their deadlines replenished.

The answer is a cached red-black tree (rb_root_cached), which maintains a pointer to the leftmost (earliest-deadline) node. Insertion and removal are O(log n), but picking the next task is O(1) — just read the cached leftmost pointer. Since deadline tasks are rare (typically only a handful per system), the O(log n) operations are practically instantaneous.

/* kernel/sched/sched.h — dl_rq */
struct dl_rq {
    struct rb_root_cached root;
    unsigned int          dl_nr_running;
    struct {
        u64 curr;
        u64 next;
    } earliest_dl;
    bool                  overloaded;
    struct rb_root_cached pushable_dl_tasks_root;
    u64                   running_bw;
    u64                   this_bw;
    u64                   extra_bw;
    u64                   max_bw;
    u64                   bw_ratio;
};

The earliest_dl cache stores two deadlines: curr (the deadline of the currently executing task) and next (the earliest deadline among tasks waiting in the tree). This allows the class to answer "would a newly awakened task preempt the current one?" with a simple integer comparison rather than a tree walk. The overloaded flag signals that this CPU has more than one runnable deadline task — meaning push migration should consider moving excess tasks to less-loaded CPUs.

The pushable_dl_tasks_root is a separate rb-tree dedicated to SMP balancing. It contains only those deadline tasks that are eligible for migration (not affinity-pinned to this CPU). Maintaining a separate tree avoids corrupting the primary scheduling tree during migration scans and keeps the migration path efficient.

Two bandwidth tracking fields — running_bw and this_bw — are central to the CBS (Constant Bandwidth Server) admission control that underpins deadline scheduling. running_bw is the "active utilization": the sum of (runtime/period) ratios for all currently runnable tasks. this_bw is the "total utilization": it includes both runnable and blocked tasks that are assigned to this CPU. The difference (this_bw - running_bw) is the "inactive utilization" — bandwidth reserved for tasks that are currently sleeping but will return. Together these fields let the admission controller determine whether accepting a new deadline task would overcommit the CPU.

The final three fields belong to a mechanism the rest of the structure does not hint at: bandwidth reclaiming, implemented by the GRUB algorithm (Greedy Reclamation of Unused Bandwidth) and requested per-task with SCHED_FLAG_RECLAIM. Ordinary CBS behaviour is strict — a task that is admitted for 2 ms every 10 ms gets exactly that, and if it finishes early the remaining capacity simply goes unused even when another deadline task could have consumed it. That is the correct default, because it is what makes the schedulability analysis hold. But it wastes real CPU time on workloads whose execution times vary, so GRUB offers an opt-in relaxation: a reclaiming task may run past its allotted runtime using bandwidth that admitted-but-idle tasks are not currently spending.

The three fields hold what that calculation needs. max_bw is the ceiling on bandwidth available for reclaiming on this runqueue, derived from the global RT bandwidth limits at initialisation. extra_bw tracks bandwidth that has been handed out beyond the ordinary accounting — it starts equal to max_bw and is adjusted as tasks join and leave. bw_ratio is the multiplicative inverse of the maximum utilisation, stored as 1/Umax scaled by 2RATIO_SHIFT; the same reciprocal-multiplication trick used for load_weight, and for the same reason, since the GRUB scaling factor is recomputed every time a reclaiming task’s runtime is charged. With these in place the runtime-decrement path multiplies by bw_ratio and shifts right rather than dividing.

Note that when the deadline bandwidth limits are disabled entirely, init_dl_rq_bw_ratio() sets bw_ratio to 1 << RATIO_SHIFT and both max_bw and extra_bw to 1 << BW_SHIFT — unity in each field’s own fixed-point scale, so the arithmetic still runs but has no effect. Neutralising a mechanism by setting its constants to identity, rather than by branching around it, keeps the hot path free of a conditional.

Scheduling Fields in task_struct

So far we have examined the per-CPU structures (struct rq) and the class-specific runqueues (cfs_rq, rt_rq, dl_rq) that organize runnable tasks. Now we turn to the other side of the equation: the scheduling state carried by each task itself. The task_struct is Linux's process descriptor — a massive structure containing everything the kernel knows about a task. Scattered throughout it are the fields the scheduler reads and writes on every wakeup, tick, and context switch.

These fields serve three distinct purposes. First, policy and priority fields record what the user (or system) has requested: which scheduling policy applies, what priority the task should run at, and whether priority inheritance has temporarily boosted it. Second, embedded scheduling entities (se, rt, dl, and scx when sched_ext is configured) contain the per-task accounting state that the class algorithms operate on — only one is "active" at any time, but all are always present in memory because a task's policy can change at runtime. Third, state flags (on_rq, on_cpu, cpus_ptr) track where the task is right now in the scheduling lifecycle: queued on a runqueue, physically executing on a CPU, or constrained to a subset of CPUs.

Table 7-16. Scheduling Fields in task_struct
FieldPurposeWhy It Can Change
policyUser-visible scheduling policysched_setscheduler(), cgroup or ext transitions
sched_classClass descriptor pointerDerived from policy or temporary boosting
prioEffective current priorityPriority inheritance, RT boosts, policy changes
normal_prioBase priority absent PI boostingnice or RT parameter changes
static_prioNice-derived base fair prioritysetpriority(), nice()
rt_priorityUser-visible RT level 1-99sched_setscheduler()
se, rt, dl, scxEmbedded class entitiesOnly one is active, but all are present
on_rqWhether the task is queued or migratingWakeup, block, migration, dequeue
on_cpuWhether the task is currently executingSet during schedule-in, cleared at schedule-out
cpus_ptrAllowed CPU maskAffinity changes, cpusets, hotplug

Once this object graph is clear, the scheduler code becomes much easier to read. Wakeup code manipulates task_struct state; fair scheduling logic manipulates sched_entity; class-neutral core code manipulates rq; and cgroup hierarchy is expressed by chaining entities and child runqueues recursively.

The EEVDF Fair Scheduler

The fair class is where the vast majority of Linux processes live — every desktop application, every web server worker, every compiler invocation, every shell script. On a typical system, well over 99% of runnable tasks belong to the fair class. Its design therefore dominates the everyday behavior and "feel" of the system. A sluggish fair scheduler makes a fast machine feel slow; a well-tuned one makes a modest machine feel responsive.

Linux 7.0.12 uses Earliest Eligible Virtual Deadline First (EEVDF), a scheduling algorithm from the real-time systems literature that replaces the original Completely Fair Scheduler (CFS) approach of "always run the task with the smallest vruntime." EEVDF keeps CFS's core insight — that virtual runtime weighted by task priority produces long-term proportional fairness — but adds an explicit latency model that CFS lacked. The key innovation is a two-step selection process: first, determine which tasks are eligible (i.e., have received less than their fair share of CPU time), and then among those eligible tasks, pick the one whose virtual deadline comes first.

This two-step approach solves a fundamental tension in fair scheduling. Pure proportional fairness (CFS's "smallest vruntime wins") provides excellent long-term fairness but offers no control over when a task gets its CPU time — a task that wakes up after sleeping might wait an unpredictable amount of time before running. EEVDF addresses this by assigning each task a virtual deadline based on its requested time slice: a task asking for a short burst gets a near deadline and runs quickly after becoming eligible; a task asking for a long slice gets a farther deadline and may wait behind short-burst tasks. This naturally separates latency-sensitive interactive tasks from throughput-oriented batch work, without any of the fragile heuristics that CFS accumulated over the years.

The figure below illustrates the complete EEVDF decision process in a single picture:

The figure above illustrates a key subtlety that distinguishes EEVDF from simpler deadline-based algorithms: eligibility acts as a fairness gate. Task E has the earliest deadline of all (990), and in a pure EDF scheduler it would win. But EEVDF recognizes that E has already consumed more than its proportional share — its vruntime is well ahead of the average — so it is filtered out in step 1. This prevents a task from monopolizing the CPU by repeatedly requesting short slices: even though short slices produce near deadlines, the eligibility check ensures the task cannot run again until it has "fallen behind" relative to its peers.

Conversely, a task that sleeps for a long time accumulates a large negative lag (its vruntime falls far behind the average). When it wakes up, it is immediately eligible and its deadline — computed from its current vruntime plus its slice — will be relatively near. This gives sleepers a natural latency advantage without any special heuristic: the math itself favors tasks that are owed service.

Two pressures shape everything in this section, and they pull against each other:

  • Fairness is a long-run property; latency is a short-run one. Proportional fairness only says that over a long enough window every task gets its weighted share. It is silent about the window itself, and a scheduler that is perfectly fair over a second can still make an interactive task wait a tenth of one. EEVDF’s two keys exist because a single ordering cannot express both.
  • Virtual time must stay inside 64 bits. Almost every piece of machinery below — the moving origin, the weighted sum kept instead of an average, the clamp on how much lag an entity may carry, the wrapping comparison helpers — exists because the obvious arithmetic overflows. These are not micro-optimizations; getting them wrong produces tasks that never run again.

The rest of this section works through nine questions:

  1. What could CFS not express, and why did adding heuristics not fix it? (Why CFS Was Not Enough)
  2. What are the four quantities the whole algorithm is built from? (The Four Core Quantities)
  3. How is the eligibility threshold computed without overflowing? (How avg_vruntime Is Computed Without Overflow)
  4. How can a tree ordered by deadline be searched by vruntime in O(log n)? (Eligibility and the Augmented RB-Tree)
  5. How is execution charged, and what stops the scheduler from switching tasks constantly? (Runtime Charging and Deadline Refresh)
  6. Where does a task that has been asleep re-enter the virtual timeline? (Placing Newly Forked and Waking Tasks)
  7. What happened to the quantity every previous account of CFS was built around? (Why min_vruntime Still Matters)
  8. Why does a sleeping task sometimes stay on the runqueue? (Delayed Dequeue)
  9. How are absolute CPU limits and group hierarchies layered on top? (SCHED_BATCH and SCHED_IDLE, CFS Bandwidth Control, Task Groups)

Version history. Essentially all of it. The Linux 2.6.11 scheduler had no virtual time at all: it used two arrays of priority lists, a dynamic priority recomputed from measured sleep time, and an interactivity estimator that tried to guess which tasks were interactive. CFS deleted that in 2007 and replaced it with vruntime; EEVDF replaced CFS’s selection rule in 6.6. Even against a description of 6.6-era EEVDF, four things in this section are new: entities may now stay queued after they block (delayed dequeue, 6.12), the queue’s reference point is no longer monotonic (6.19), bandwidth throttling no longer removes tasks from the runqueue at all (6.18), and slice protection is now anchored to a dedicated per-entity field (6.17, reworked in 7.0).

Why CFS Was Not Enough

The Completely Fair Scheduler served Linux well from 2007 to 2023. Its central idea — track each task's weighted CPU consumption as "virtual runtime" and always run the task with the smallest vruntime — was simple, elegant, and produced excellent long-term proportional fairness. But CFS had a blind spot: it offered no principled answer to the question "after a task wakes up, how quickly should it get to run?"

Consider a text editor that sleeps for 500ms waiting for a keystroke, then needs just 1ms of CPU time to redraw the screen. Under pure CFS, this task accumulates a vruntime deficit while sleeping, so it will be the leftmost entity in the tree and run immediately upon wakeup — which sounds fine. But now consider a second scenario: two CPU-bound tasks are running, and a third task wakes up with a moderate vruntime. CFS has no mechanism to express that the waking task should preempt within a bounded time. The vruntime ordering tells you who should run, but not when or for how long before yielding. There is no concept of a deadline.

To work around this, the CFS maintainers added a series of heuristics over the years: wakeup buddies (recently woken tasks get scheduling preference), sleeper fairness credits (sleeping tasks get a vruntime bonus), and various tunables like sched_wakeup_granularity_ns that controlled how large a vruntime advantage a waker needed before it could preempt the current task. Each heuristic solved a specific workload complaint but added complexity, tuning knobs, and sometimes unexpected interactions. The scheduler became harder to reason about, and different workloads required different tunable settings — a telltale sign that the core algorithm was missing a dimension.

EEVDF resolves this by adding that missing dimension: the virtual deadline. Instead of asking only "who has consumed the least?", it asks "among those owed service, who has the most urgent need?" The deadline is computed from the task's requested time slice, so a task requesting a short burst gets a near deadline (and thus low latency), while a task requesting a long slice gets a farther deadline (accepting higher latency in exchange for less preemption overhead). This single addition replaces all the CFS heuristics with a mathematically grounded framework.

Table 7-17. Conceptual Shift from Classic CFS to EEVDF
QuestionClassic CFS AnswerEEVDF Answer
Who is most underserved?Entity with smallest vruntimeEntity with non-positive lag relative to queue average
Which eligible task should run first?Still smallest vruntimeEligible task with earliest virtual deadline
How are sleepers favored?Historically by heuristics and buddy logicNaturally through preserved lag and deadline placement
What is the latency model?Implicit and heuristicExplicitly tied to slice and weight

The Four Core Quantities

The elegance of EEVDF lies in the fact that the entire algorithm can be expressed in terms of just four quantities. Two are per-entity (carried in struct sched_entity), and two are per-queue (derived from the entities on a cfs_rq). Every scheduling decision — eligibility, selection, preemption, wakeup placement — is a function of these four numbers and nothing else. Understanding them is understanding the algorithm.

EEVDF relies on four numbers per entity or queue:
