  • vruntime (per entity): how much weighted service the entity has consumed. A heavier task's vruntime advances more slowly per unit of wall-clock time, so it can run longer before "catching up" to lighter tasks. This is the same fundamental quantity CFS used, and it remains the basis of proportional fairness.
  • avg_vruntime (per queue): the load-weighted average virtual time across all runnable entities. This represents the "ideal" service level — a perfectly fair scheduler would keep every entity's vruntime exactly at this value. It serves as the dividing line between eligible and ineligible tasks.
  • vlag (per entity): the entity's lag relative to ideal service, conceptually vruntime - avg_vruntime. A negative or zero vlag means the entity has received less than or equal to its fair share and is therefore eligible to run. A positive vlag means it has received more than its share and must wait.
  • deadline (per entity): the virtual time by which the entity's current slice should complete, computed as vruntime + slice / weight. A task requesting a small slice gets a near deadline; a task requesting a large slice gets a far one. Among eligible entities, the one with the earliest deadline runs first.

The relationship is intuitive. If a task has consumed less service than its weight says it should have, its vruntime sits below the queue average and it becomes eligible. Among all such eligible tasks, the one with the shortest virtual finish time gets the CPU. This two-number interplay — lag for fairness, deadline for latency — is what gives EEVDF its power: fairness prevents starvation, and deadlines provide bounded response time.

One conversion underlies all four of them, and it is worth pinning down before going further because it appears in every listing in this section. Wall-clock nanoseconds are not virtual nanoseconds; something has to divide by weight, and that something is calc_delta_fair():

/* fair.c — delta /= w */
static inline u64 calc_delta_fair(u64 delta, struct sched_entity *se)
{
    if (unlikely(se->load.weight != NICE_0_LOAD))
        delta = __calc_delta(delta, NICE_0_LOAD, &se->load);

    return delta;
}

Read the fast path first, because it states the calibration of the entire virtual clock: for a task of nice 0, virtual time and real time are the same thing. No arithmetic is performed at all. A nice-0 task that runs for one millisecond advances its vruntime by exactly one million. Every other weight is expressed as a ratio against that reference, which is why NICE_0_LOAD appears as the numerator rather than some abstract unit: the scheduler does not have an independent notion of virtual time that real time is converted into: it has one clock, and nice 0 is the point at which the two readings coincide. A nice -20 task, being about 87 times heavier, sees its vruntime advance about 87 times more slowly, so it can run 87 times as long before it looks as though it has consumed as much.

The slow path is where the inv_weight field discussed earlier earns its place. __calc_delta() wants delta × NICE_0_LOAD / weight, and since inv_weight already holds 232/weight it can multiply by that and shift right by WMULT_SHIFT (which is 32) instead of dividing. The complication is that the intermediate product can overflow, so before multiplying, the code counts the significant bits in the high half with fls() and shifts the factor down by exactly that many bits while subtracting the same amount from the final shift. Precision is traded away only in the cases that would otherwise overflow, and only as much as those cases require. It is a small function, but it runs on every tick for every running task on every CPU, which is what justifies the care.

Note also what this implies for deadlines. The formula quoted above as vruntime + slice / weight is written in the source as se->vruntime + calc_delta_fair(se->slice, se) — the division by weight is the conversion to virtual time. A heavy task and a light task that request the same slice therefore get different virtual deadline distances, even though they asked for the same number of real nanoseconds, and that is precisely what makes a single deadline ordering fair across mixed priorities.

How avg_vruntime Is Computed Without Overflow

The average virtual runtime is the single most important derived quantity in EEVDF — it is the dividing line between eligible and ineligible tasks, checked on every selection and every wakeup. Computing it naively would mean summing vruntime × weight across all runnable entities and dividing by total weight. But vruntimes are 64-bit nanosecond counters that grow monotonically over the system's lifetime, and weights can be large (up to 88,761 for nice -20). Multiplying two large 64-bit numbers risks overflow, and doing it for every entity on every scheduling decision would be expensive.

A naming caution before the code, because the identifiers changed in this kernel and the old ones are still all over the mailing lists. Until 7.0 the queue’s running total was called cfs_rq::avg_vruntime and its weight total cfs_rq::avg_load, which was actively misleading: neither was an average, and the first was a division away from being one. Commit dcbc9d3f0e59 renamed them to sum_w_vruntime and sum_weight to say what they hold. The function avg_vruntime() kept its name, because it really does perform the division and really does return an average — so in 7.0.12 the function and the field it reads no longer share a name, which is the point.

Linux solves both problems with a clever incremental scheme. Rather than recomputing the sum from scratch, the queue maintains a running weighted sum (sum_w_vruntime) and total weight (sum_weight) that are updated incrementally as entities are enqueued, dequeued, or charged. To prevent overflow, all vruntimes are stored as offsets from a moving origin (zero_vruntime) rather than as absolute values. Since runnable tasks' vruntimes are typically clustered near the average, these offsets remain small — usually fitting comfortably in a signed 64-bit integer even after multiplication by weight.

/* fair.c — idea behind avg_vruntime(), simplified */
static inline s64 entity_key(struct cfs_rq *cfs_rq, struct sched_entity *se)
{
    return se->vruntime - cfs_rq->zero_vruntime;
}

u64 avg_vruntime(struct cfs_rq *cfs_rq)
{
    struct sched_entity *curr = cfs_rq->curr;
    long weight = cfs_rq->sum_weight;
    s64 delta = 0;

    if (curr && !curr->on_rq)
        curr = NULL;            /* running but not queued: not counted */

    if (weight) {
        s64 runtime = cfs_rq->sum_w_vruntime;

        if (curr) {             /* curr is off the tree; fold it back in */
            unsigned long w = scale_load_down(curr->load.weight);
            runtime += entity_key(cfs_rq, curr) * w;
            weight  += w;
        }

        if (runtime < 0)        /* sign flips effective floor/ceiling */
            runtime -= (weight - 1);

        delta = div_s64(runtime, weight);
    } else if (curr) {
        /* one element: it *is* the average */
        delta = curr->vruntime - cfs_rq->zero_vruntime;
    }

    update_zero_vruntime(cfs_rq, delta);
    return cfs_rq->zero_vruntime;
}

The key trick is that the queue tracks offsets from a nearby moving zero, not absolute vruntimes. Because runnable tasks are usually clustered, those offsets stay small enough for stable arithmetic. The left bias before division is also deliberate: the average is rounded so that “exactly at the boundary” counts as eligible, which keeps the logic conservative in favor of running a task that is owed service.

Two details in that routine repay attention. The first is that the currently running entity is not in the tree — set_next_entity() takes it off — so its contribution has to be folded back in explicitly by the if (curr) block, and only if it is still queued. Forgetting this is a classic source of eligibility bugs, because the running task is precisely the one whose vruntime is moving. The second is the weight == 0 branch: a queue whose only participant is the running entity has no sum to average, and that entity trivially is the average.

avg_vruntime() is not called on every scheduling decision. The source lists exactly three callers — place_entity() before an enqueue, update_entity_lag() before a dequeue, and update_deadline() at slice expiry — and notes that the value is therefore always “one entry behind.” That staleness is deliberate and bounded: it keeps entity_key() within about two lag bounds of zero, which is exactly the property the small-offset arithmetic depends on. The scheduler is designed around bounded approximation error, not symbolic exactness.

That leaves the question of how the origin moves, which is a smaller function than one might expect:

/* fair.c — moving the origin without changing what it represents */
void update_zero_vruntime(struct cfs_rq *cfs_rq, s64 delta)
{
    /* v' = v + d  ==>  sum_w_vruntime' = sum_w_vruntime - d*sum_weight */
    cfs_rq->sum_w_vruntime -= cfs_rq->sum_weight * delta;
    cfs_rq->zero_vruntime  += delta;
}

The comment is the whole specification. Every stored key is an offset from zero_vruntime, so sliding the origin forward by d makes every one of those offsets smaller by d without any of them being touched. The running total has to be corrected to match, and since it is weighted, the correction is d multiplied by the total weight. Both lines together are a change of representation and nothing more: not one entity’s position relative to any other has moved. This is why the origin can be advanced freely and often — it is free of policy, and purely a matter of keeping the numbers small.

Keeping them small is not optional, and the 7.0 development cycle produced an unusually clear demonstration of what happens when the origin stops being advanced often enough. The sequence is worth following, because it is the sort of failure that only appears when several correct-looking changes meet.

Originally the origin was pulled along from __enqueue_entity() and __dequeue_entity(). Commit b3d99f43c72b (7.0-rc2) removed that, for two good reasons. A single running task passes through neither function — pick_next_task() keeps returning it and put_prev_set_next_task() short-circuits — so its key would grow without bound until it overflowed. And the two call sites were inconsistent in any case: set_next_entity() dequeues before setting cfs_rq->curr, so the average saw neither copy of the entity, while put_prev_entity() enqueues before clearing it, so the average counted it twice. The update was moved to the tick and to genuine enqueues and dequeues.

That looked complete, and it was not. John Stultz bisected a machine hang to it, running stress-ng-yield. Take two runnable tasks that do nothing but yield. The average must lie between them, so exactly one is eligible; that one yields, forfeits its request, and is promoted a full slice — jumping over its partner, which now becomes the eligible one and does the same. The two leapfrog, each hop advancing a key by a full slice, and nothing in that loop is an enqueue, a dequeue, or a tick. If they can hop fast enough to overflow within a single tick, the keys run away. The same hazard applies less dramatically to deep cgroup hierarchies, where statistics make a timely tick overwhelmingly likely for any given group but do not guarantee one.

Commit 1319ea57529e (7.0-rc7) settled it by attaching the update to the event that actually bounds the growth: the end of a slice. This is the avg_vruntime(cfs_rq) call in the update_deadline() listing below, which exists purely for its side effect — the return value is discarded. It also explains why yield_task_fair(), discussed shortly, calls update_deadline() rather than advancing the deadline itself: routing the yield through the same function is what guarantees the origin is pulled along on that path too. With the update anchored to slice expiry, the origin can never be more than one slice behind, which is exactly the bound entity_key() needs.

Comparing Virtual Times That Wrap

One consequence of the offset representation deserves to be stated on its own, because it is easy to read past. Virtual runtimes are unsigned 64-bit counters that are allowed to wrap around. Comparing two of them with a plain < is therefore wrong: an entity whose vruntime has just wrapped past zero would compare as enormously behind everyone else and would monopolize the CPU. The correct test subtracts first and interprets the difference as signed, which stays right across the wrap.

Through 6.19 this was written out by hand at every site as (s64)(a - b) < 0, an idiom that is easy to typo and impossible to grep for. Linux 7.0 introduced two macros (commit 5758e48eefaf) that make the intent explicit and are now used at sixteen sites in fair.c:

/* fair.c — wrapped-signed virtual time helpers */
vruntime_cmp(A, "<", B)   /* == ((s64)((A) - (B)) < 0), also "<=", ">", ">=" */
vruntime_op(A, "-", B)    /* == ((s64)((A) - (B))) */

The operator is passed as a string and resolved at compile time with __builtin_strcmp(); an unrecognized operator falls through to a call to an undefined function, so a typo becomes a link-time error rather than silently wrong scheduling. When you meet vruntime_cmp(se->vruntime, "<", se->vprot) later in this section, read it simply as “vruntime is before vprot,” with the wrap handled for you.

Eligibility and the Augmented RB-Tree

The EEVDF algorithm's two-step selection creates an interesting data-structure challenge. The rb-tree is ordered by virtual deadline (so the leftmost node has the earliest deadline), but the eligibility check depends on vruntime (whether the entity's vruntime is at or below the queue average). These are two different keys. A naive implementation would walk the tree in deadline order, checking each node's eligibility until it finds a match — potentially visiting many nodes in the worst case.

Linux solves this with an augmented red-black tree. Each node in the tree carries an extra field, min_vruntime, which records the minimum vruntime in the entire subtree rooted at that node. This field is maintained automatically by the RB-tree augmentation callbacks (min_vruntime_cb) during insertions, removals, and rotations. With this metadata, the pick_eevdf() function can prune entire subtrees: if a subtree's minimum vruntime is above the eligibility threshold (i.e., all entities in that subtree have already consumed more than their fair share), the scheduler skips the entire subtree without examining a single node inside it.

The search proceeds as follows: starting at the tree root, the algorithm checks whether the left subtree contains any eligible entity (by comparing its min_vruntime against the queue average). If yes, it descends left — because the left subtree contains entities with earlier deadlines, and at least one is eligible. If no eligible entity exists on the left, it checks the current node, and finally moves right. This yields the eligible entity with the earliest deadline in O(log n) time, matching the tree's height.

The figure shows a tree with seven entities. The scheduler needs the eligible entity with the earliest deadline. Starting at root D, it checks whether D's left subtree can contain an eligible entity: the left child B has min_vrt=380, which is ≤ 500 (the avg_vruntime), so at least one entity there is eligible. It descends left. At B, the same check passes for B's left child A. At leaf A, it confirms eligibility directly (vrt=380 ≤ 500) and finds the winner — entity A with deadline 800. The entire right subtree (F, E, G) is never visited because its min_vrt=510 > 500: no entity there can be eligible. Three nodes examined instead of seven.

/* fair.c — the decisive predicate */
static int vruntime_eligible(struct cfs_rq *cfs_rq, u64 vruntime)
{
    struct sched_entity *curr = cfs_rq->curr;
    s64  avg  = cfs_rq->sum_w_vruntime;
    long load = cfs_rq->sum_weight;

    if (curr && curr->on_rq) {          /* fold the running entity back in */
        unsigned long weight = scale_load_down(curr->load.weight);

        avg  += entity_key(cfs_rq, curr) * weight;
        load += weight;
    }

    return avg >= vruntime_op(vruntime, "-", cfs_rq->zero_vruntime) * load;
}

int entity_eligible(struct cfs_rq *cfs_rq, struct sched_entity *se)
{
    return vruntime_eligible(cfs_rq, se->vruntime);
}

/* fair.c — selection shape, simplified */
static struct sched_entity *pick_eevdf(struct cfs_rq *cfs_rq, bool protect)
{
    struct rb_node *node = cfs_rq->tasks_timeline.rb_root.rb_node;
    struct sched_entity *se = __pick_first_entity(cfs_rq);
    struct sched_entity *curr = cfs_rq->curr;
    struct sched_entity *best = NULL;

    /* Only one entity: no need to test eligibility at all. */
    if (cfs_rq->nr_queued == 1)
        return curr && curr->on_rq ? curr : se;

    /* Honour the wakeup buddy: costs latency, not fairness. */
    if (sched_feat(PICK_BUDDY) &&
        cfs_rq->next && entity_eligible(cfs_rq, cfs_rq->next))
        return cfs_rq->next;

    if (curr && (!curr->on_rq || !entity_eligible(cfs_rq, curr)))
        curr = NULL;                    /* curr cannot win */

    /* Let the running entity finish its protected period. */
    if (curr && protect && protect_slice(curr))
        return curr;

    if (se && entity_eligible(cfs_rq, se)) {
        best = se;                      /* leftmost is eligible: done */
        goto found;
    }

    while (node) {                      /* heap search for the EEVD entity */
        struct rb_node *left = node->rb_left;

        if (left &&
            vruntime_eligible(cfs_rq, __node_2_se(left)->min_vruntime)) {
            node = left;                /* earlier deadlines, one eligible */
            continue;
        }
        se = __node_2_se(node);
        if (entity_eligible(cfs_rq, se)) {
            best = se;
            break;
        }
        node = node->rb_right;
    }
found:
    /* The running entity is off the tree — make it compete too. */
    if (!best || (curr && entity_before(curr, best)))
        best = curr;

    return best;
}

Two parts of that routine are easy to overlook and both matter. The first is the found: epilogue. The running entity is not in the tree, so a search that only walks the tree can never return it — a scheduler written that way would context-switch on every single tick. The tree search therefore produces a candidate, and the running entity is then compared against it with entity_before() — which is nothing more than a wrap-safe < on the two deadlines, and is also the tree’s ordering predicate — so the incumbent keeps the CPU unless something in the tree genuinely beats it. The second is the protect parameter, added in 7.0 so that the wakeup path can ask for a pick that ignores slice protection when the waking task has a shorter slice than the incumbent. That is the subject of the next subsection.

Before leaving the predicate, look at what its final line does not do. The definition of eligibility is “this entity’s key is at or below the weighted average,” and the obvious way to test that is to compute the average — sum divided by total weight — and compare. The kernel never performs that division. It multiplies the other side instead, turning key ≤ sum / load into sum ≥ key × load, which is the same statement with the division cleared. On the hottest predicate in the fair scheduler, evaluated once per node visited during every pick and again on every wakeup, a 64-bit division has been replaced by a 64-bit multiply.

This also resolves something the previous subsection left open. avg_vruntime() divides, and it has only three callers; the reason is now plain. The selection path does not need the average as a number at all. It only ever needs to know which side of it a particular key falls on, and that question can be answered exactly, without rounding, in integer arithmetic. The division survives only where the value itself is genuinely wanted: to place an entity, to compute a lag, and to nudge the origin at slice expiry.

Two familiar patterns recur in the same handful of lines. The running entity is folded back in, because curr is off the tree and so contributes to neither running total — the third time this same correction has appeared. And the key is formed with vruntime_op(…, "-", …) rather than a plain subtraction, so an entity whose vruntime has wrapped is still compared correctly. The multiplication is also what makes the offset representation load-bearing rather than merely tidy: multiplying a full absolute vruntime by a total queue weight would overflow almost immediately, whereas multiplying a small offset from a nearby origin does not.

The augmentation is maintained using Linux's rbtree_augmented.h framework. In kernel 7.0.12, each sched_entity node actually carries three augmented fields — not just min_vruntime:

  • min_vruntime — minimum vruntime in the subtree (powers eligibility pruning as shown above)
  • min_slice — minimum slice in the subtree. Read through cfs_rq_min_slice(), it answers “what is the shortest request anyone on this queue has made?” That bound sets how long the running entity is protected from preemption, and it is also the slice given to a group entity, so that a cgroup representing a latency-sensitive thread inherits that thread’s urgency instead of a generic default.
  • max_slice — maximum slice in the subtree. This one is newer than the other two (added in 7.0-rc2) and has exactly one job: bounding the lag clamp, described below.

The max_slice aggregate is worth a paragraph because it fixes a real fairness bug. When entities are added to, removed from, or reweighted on a queue, the weighted average V moves, and an entity’s lag can grow larger than the lag it started with. update_entity_lag() therefore clamps the value it stores. The clamp has to be at least as large as the biggest legitimate request on the queue, or entities with long slices get their lag truncated and permanently lose the service they were owed:

/* fair.c — the lag clamp, EEVDF limit: -r_max < lag < max(r_max, q) */
static void update_entity_lag(struct cfs_rq *cfs_rq, struct sched_entity *se)
{
    u64 max_slice = cfs_rq_max_slice(cfs_rq) + TICK_NSEC;
    s64 vlag, limit;

    vlag  = avg_vruntime(cfs_rq) - se->vruntime;
    limit = calc_delta_fair(max_slice, se);

    se->vlag = clamp(vlag, -limit, limit);
}

Until 7.0 the kernel had no cheap way to learn the largest slice on a queue, so the bound was a fixed approximation and a TODO comment sat in the source. Vincent Guittot reported the resulting over-clamping on a mixed-slice workload, and commit 6e3c0a4e1ad1 implemented the max_slice augmentation so the bound could be computed for real. This is a good illustration of why the augmented tree is worth its cost: because the aggregates are maintained during rotations, the scheduler can ask for a queue-wide minimum or maximum in O(1) at any time, and questions that would otherwise require a scan become answerable in the fast path.

All three are recomputed on every tree mutation by the min_vruntime_update() callback, registered via RB_DECLARE_CALLBACKS. This macro generates rotation fixup functions that propagate the cached values after structural changes — the scheduler never needs to manually walk ancestors.

/* fair.c — augmentation callback and tree operations */
RB_DECLARE_CALLBACKS(static, min_vruntime_cb, struct sched_entity,
                     run_node, min_vruntime, min_vruntime_update);

static void __enqueue_entity(struct cfs_rq *cfs_rq, struct sched_entity *se)
{
    sum_w_vruntime_add(cfs_rq, se);
    se->min_vruntime = se->vruntime;
    se->min_slice = se->slice;
    rb_add_augmented_cached(&se->run_node, &cfs_rq->tasks_timeline,
                            __entity_less, &min_vruntime_cb);
}

static void __dequeue_entity(struct cfs_rq *cfs_rq, struct sched_entity *se)
{
    rb_erase_augmented_cached(&se->run_node, &cfs_rq->tasks_timeline,
                              &min_vruntime_cb);
    sum_w_vruntime_sub(cfs_rq, se);
}

The cost is modest — three u64 comparisons per rotation (and rotations are O(1) per insert/delete) — but the benefit is enormous: eligibility search drops from potential O(n) to guaranteed O(log n), and the scheduler can compute queue-wide min/max slice bounds without scanning. This is a textbook example of trading a small constant-factor overhead per mutation for an asymptotic improvement in the critical selection path.

Runtime Charging and Deadline Refresh

All the elegant data structures and selection algorithms discussed so far would be meaningless without a reliable way to track how much CPU time each entity has actually consumed. The EEVDF model is driven entirely by accounting: virtual runtimes advance, deadlines expire, eligibility shifts — all as a consequence of charging execution time correctly and at the right moments. This accounting is the engine that turns static scheduling theory into dynamic fair behavior.

The function update_curr() is that engine. It sits at the intersection of nearly every scheduler path: the periodic tick interrupt calls it to keep the running entity's virtual time current; the wakeup path calls it before comparing the waker against the wakee; the dequeue path calls it to finalize accounting before removing an entity from the tree; and the pick path calls it to ensure up-to-date state before choosing the next task. In a typical system running hundreds of tasks, update_curr() executes thousands of times per second per CPU — it is one of the hottest functions in the scheduler.

What makes this function particularly interesting in the EEVDF era (compared to classic CFS) is the tight coupling between runtime charging and deadline management. Under CFS, charging vruntime was the end of the story — the entity with the smallest vruntime won. Under EEVDF, charging vruntime may trigger a deadline refresh: once an entity has consumed its entire virtual slice (vruntime catches up to its deadline), the scheduler assigns a new deadline for the next slice and signals that preemption should be considered. This creates a natural rhythm — entities cycle through their slices and periodically return to the selection pool as freshly-eligible candidates, rather than accumulating CPU time indefinitely as the "minimum vruntime" entity.

A second innovation is slice protection (protect_slice()). It is easy to state backwards, so state it forwards: se->vprot is the virtual time up to which the entity may run before the scheduler will even look for somebody else. It is a floor on how long the incumbent keeps the CPU, not a licence to overrun a deadline that has already passed — vprot is never set beyond the deadline. Without it, several entities whose deadlines expire at nearly the same moment would trade the CPU back and forth, each doing less useful work than the context switch cost to give it to them.

/* fair.c — where the protected period ends */
static inline void set_protect_slice(struct cfs_rq *cfs_rq,
                                     struct sched_entity *se)
{
    u64 slice = normalized_sysctl_sched_base_slice;
    u64 vprot = se->deadline;

    if (sched_feat(RUN_TO_PARITY))
        slice = cfs_rq_min_slice(cfs_rq);

    slice = min(slice, se->slice);
    if (slice != se->slice)
        vprot = min_vruntime(vprot, se->vruntime + calc_delta_fair(slice, se));

    se->vprot = vprot;
}

static inline bool protect_slice(struct sched_entity *se)
{
    return vruntime_cmp(se->vruntime, "<", se->vprot);
}

The two branches correspond to two different policies, selected by the RUN_TO_PARITY scheduler feature (on by default):

  • With RUN_TO_PARITY: the entity is protected all the way to its deadline — it runs to parity, consuming the whole request it asked for — except that the window is pulled in to the shortest slice anyone on the queue has requested. A queue full of long-slice batch jobs lets each run its full slice; add one short-slice latency-sensitive task and every incumbent’s protection shrinks to that task’s slice, so the queue as a whole becomes more responsive.
  • Without it: protection is just a minimum quantum of sysctl_sched_base_slice, enough to guarantee forward progress and no more.

Protection can also be revoked. cancel_protect_slice() pulls vprot back to the current vruntime, ending the protected period immediately; the wakeup path calls it when a task with a shorter slice than the incumbent wakes up (the PREEMPT_SHORT feature), which is the mechanism by which a latency-sensitive task cuts in. Conversely update_protect_slice() shrinks an existing window when the queue’s minimum slice drops beneath it. Two 7.0 fixes tightened the boundaries: commit bcd74b2ffdd0 established that protection is armed only when an entity is actually picked to run — not incidentally re-armed by the put_prev_task()/set_next_task() pair that the change pattern from earlier in this chapter performs — and commit ff38424030f9 rescales vprot in reweight_entity(), since a change of weight changes how fast virtual time passes for the entity and would otherwise silently lengthen or shorten its protection.

/* fair.c — update_curr(), simplified */
static void update_curr(struct cfs_rq *cfs_rq)
{
    struct sched_entity *curr = cfs_rq->curr;
    s64 delta_exec;
    bool resched;

    if (!curr)
        return;

    delta_exec = update_se(rq_of(cfs_rq), curr);
    if (delta_exec <= 0)
        return;

    curr->vruntime += calc_delta_fair(delta_exec, curr);
    resched = update_deadline(cfs_rq, curr);
    account_cfs_rq_runtime(cfs_rq, delta_exec);

    if (resched || !protect_slice(curr))
        resched_curr_lazy(rq_of(cfs_rq));
}

Two things happen here. First, wall-clock execution is converted into weighted virtual execution. Second, if the entity has run through its currently assigned slice in virtual time, update_deadline() assigns a new virtual deadline and may request a reschedule.

/* fair.c — update_deadline() */
static bool update_deadline(struct cfs_rq *cfs_rq, struct sched_entity *se)
{
    if (vruntime_cmp(se->vruntime, "<", se->deadline))
        return false;               /* request not consumed yet */

    /* Unless the task asked for a specific slice, reset to the default. */
    if (!se->custom_slice)
        se->slice = sysctl_sched_base_slice;

    se->deadline = se->vruntime + calc_delta_fair(se->slice, se);  /* vd = ve + r/w */
    avg_vruntime(cfs_rq);           /* nudge the queue's moving origin */

    return true;                    /* request consumed: reschedule */
}

Three things in that short function are worth naming. The comparison goes through vruntime_cmp() because deadlines wrap exactly as vruntimes do. The custom_slice test is where se->slice actually comes from: at every slice expiry the entity’s request is reset to the system-wide sysctl_sched_base_slice, so a task keeps a non-default slice only if it asked for one explicitly through sched_setattr()’s sched_runtime field — which is what custom_slice records. And the return is unconditional: consuming a request always requests a reschedule. The “is anyone else even queued?” test lives in the caller, which returns early when nr_queued == 1; combined with protect_slice(), that is what keeps a single busy task on its CPU without a pointless switch every tick.

The “slice expired” event no longer means “unconditionally rotate like round robin.” It means “this entity has reached the end of its current virtual request; see whether another eligible entity should run.” That nuance is one reason EEVDF behaves smoothly under mixed interactive and CPU-bound loads.

One deliberate way of ending a request early deserves a mention here, because it is expressed entirely in the vocabulary just developed. When a fair task calls sched_yield(), yield_task_fair() runs:

/* fair.c — yield_task_fair(), the decisive part */
    if (unlikely(rq->nr_running == 1))
        return;                     /* nobody to yield to */

    clear_buddies(cfs_rq, se);
    update_rq_clock(rq);
    update_curr(cfs_rq);
    rq_clock_skip_update(rq);       /* we just updated it */

    /* Forfeit the remaining vruntime, only if the entity is eligible. */
    if (entity_eligible(cfs_rq, se)) {
        se->vruntime = se->deadline;
        update_deadline(cfs_rq, se);
    }

Assigning the deadline to the vruntime is a compact way of saying “treat the rest of this request as though it had been consumed.” The entity immediately becomes ineligible, since its vruntime is now at or past the point where its request ended, and the following update_deadline() hands it a fresh request one slice further out. The yielding task therefore does not merely offer to step aside; it genuinely pays for the privilege, and pays exactly the amount it had left.

Before 6.19 the forfeit was absent, and the omission produced a failure that is instructive. A task that yields in a tight loop was often the only eligible entity on its queue, so the scheduler would pick it again straight away. Each round through update_deadline() pushed its deadline another slice into the future without its vruntime having moved to match, and after enough iterations the deadline had run so far ahead that the task suffered long unexplained delays once it finally did want to run. Commit 79104becf42b fixed it by making the yield real.

The eligibility guard exists for core scheduling, and it is a nice illustration of how features constrain one another. When SMT siblings must run cookie-compatible tasks, the core scheduler would rather run an ineligible task than force a sibling idle. If yielding were unconditional, such a task could be picked, yield, be picked again, and drive its vruntime away at high speed. Restricting the forfeit to entities that are currently eligible closes the loop.

The rq_clock_skip_update() call is the other detail worth connecting back. It sets RQCF_REQ_SKIP, the request flag described earlier in this chapter, so that when __schedule() runs a moment later it does not repeat a clock update this function has already performed. Two clock reads in quick succession would cost little individually, but sched_yield() is exactly the call that badly written programs make in a loop, and the fast path is where that shows up.

Placing Newly Forked and Waking Tasks

The hardest part of fairness is not charging runtime while a task runs — that is a straightforward incremental accumulation. The truly difficult problem is deciding where to insert a task that has been absent from the runqueue. A task that slept for a long time has "missed" many virtual time advances; placing it naively at the current average would erase the service debt the system owes it, while placing it at its pre-sleep vruntime could give it an unfair burst at the expense of tasks that stayed runnable.

Under CFS, this placement problem spawned years of heuristic patches: sleeper fairness bonuses, new-task penalties, trade-offs between responsiveness and starvation resistance — all with fragile edge cases. EEVDF replaces the heuristics with a single coherent principle: preserve the task's lag across sleep. When a task dequeues, the scheduler saves its current virtual lag (the difference between the queue average and the entity's vruntime). When it re-enqueues, place_entity() restores that lag relative to the new queue average, ensuring the task resumes with exactly the service credit (or debt) it had earned. No bonuses, no penalties — just continuity of fairness.

This principle interacts subtly with the weighted average itself. Adding an entity to the queue changes the denominator of the average (total weight increases), which would silently shrink the effective lag if not compensated. The kernel addresses this with lag inflation: before placement, the saved lag is scaled by (W + w_i) / W so that after the entity joins and the average shifts, its actual observed lag equals the intended value. This derivation (spelled out in the source as a full algebraic proof) is one of the clearest examples of EEVDF's mathematical foundation translating directly into code.

For brand-new tasks (fork), the situation is different — they have no history and no lag to preserve. Here the scheduler uses a gentler approach: it starts the new entity at the current average vruntime (zero lag, immediately eligible) but assigns only half a slice worth of deadline distance. This lets the child ease into competition: it gets scheduled quickly (responsive fork behavior) but cannot run for long before yielding (doesn't bulldoze established tasks). The half-slice initial placement replaces CFS's old START_DEBIT and child_runs_first knobs with a single, principled policy.

/* fair.c — placement logic, simplified */
static void place_entity(struct cfs_rq *cfs_rq,
                         struct sched_entity *se, int flags)
{
    u64 vslice, vruntime = avg_vruntime(cfs_rq);
    s64 lag = 0;

    if (!se->custom_slice)
        se->slice = sysctl_sched_base_slice;
    vslice = calc_delta_fair(se->slice, se);

    /* EEVDF: placement strategy #1 / #2 */
    if (sched_feat(PLACE_LAG) && cfs_rq->nr_queued && se->vlag) {
        struct sched_entity *curr = cfs_rq->curr;
        unsigned long load;

        lag = se->vlag;

        /* vl_i = (W + w_i) * vl'_i / W  — inflate before insertion */
        load = cfs_rq->sum_weight;
        if (curr && curr->on_rq)
            load += scale_load_down(curr->load.weight);

        lag *= load + scale_load_down(se->load.weight);
        if (WARN_ON_ONCE(!load))
            load = 1;
        lag = div_s64(lag, load);
    }

    se->vruntime = vruntime - lag;

    if (se->rel_deadline) {         /* deadline was stored relative */
        se->deadline += se->vruntime;
        se->rel_deadline = 0;
        return;                     /* keep the outstanding request */
    }

    if (sched_feat(PLACE_DEADLINE_INITIAL) && (flags & ENQUEUE_INITIAL))
        vslice /= 2;

    se->deadline = se->vruntime + vslice;   /* vd_i = ve_i + r_i/w_i */
}

The inflation arithmetic is worth reading rather than skipping, because the comment block above it in the source is a full algebraic derivation and the four lines of code are its conclusion. The quantity actually stored is the virtual lag vl_i = V − v_i, with the weight factored out so it does not have to be carried everywhere. Inserting an entity with lag changes V itself, and the derivation shows the post-insertion lag comes out as vl_i × W / (W + w_i) — strictly smaller than intended, so lag would quietly evaporate a little on every sleep-wake cycle. Inverting that relation gives the multiplier the code applies. Note too that the running entity is folded into load when it is still queued, for exactly the reason given earlier: curr is off the tree, so it is missing from sum_weight, and W must be the true total or the correction is computed against the wrong denominator.

The rel_deadline branch is the part most easily missed, and it changes what the function does rather than merely adjusting a number: when the flag is set, the routine restores the deadline and returns immediately, computing no fresh slice at all. The flag is set on the way out, in dequeue_entity(), but only when the dequeue is not a sleep:

/* fair.c — dequeue_entity(), the relative-deadline conversion */
if (sched_feat(PLACE_REL_DEADLINE) && !sleep) {
    se->deadline -= se->vruntime;
    se->rel_deadline = 1;
}

Subtracting the vruntime turns an absolute deadline into a remaining request — how much virtual time the entity still had coming — and adding the new vruntime back at placement re-anchors it wherever the entity lands. The !sleep condition is what makes this correct rather than exploitable. A task that blocks has finished with its request and should get a fresh one when it returns, which is what the lag machinery is for. A task that is dequeued while still runnable has not: it is being migrated to another CPU, or reweighted, or moved between cgroups, and in each of those cases the request it was part-way through is still legitimately owed. Without this branch, a task could shed an unfavourable deadline simply by bouncing between CPUs, and migration would become a way to jump the queue.

The same idea is applied a second time in reweight_entity(), which additionally rescales the relative deadline and the slice-protection window by the ratio of old to new weight. Changing an entity’s weight changes how fast virtual time passes for it, so a request expressed in virtual units has to be restated in the new currency — otherwise a task that renices itself would find its outstanding deadline silently stretched or compressed.

Four placement ideas are hidden in that small routine:

  • Preserve lag across sleep. If a task went to sleep while it was owed service, Linux tries to preserve that deficit rather than zeroing it away.
  • Compensate for the average shifting when a task is inserted. Adding an entity changes the weighted average, so the lag must be inflated before insertion if the post-insertion lag should remain meaningful.
  • Forked tasks ease into competition. Newly created tasks can begin with roughly half a slice worth of virtual request so they do not immediately bulldoze the tree just because they are new.
  • A request survives a move, but not a sleep. An entity dequeued while still runnable carries its outstanding request across as a relative deadline, so migration and reweighting cannot be used to escape one.

This is the place where EEVDF replaces old “interactive sleeper bonus” intuition with a mathematically cleaner idea: preserve the service history that the task earned before it slept, then resume it from a fair virtual location.

Why min_vruntime Still Matters — and Where It Went

Readers familiar with CFS might expect that EEVDF's introduction of avg_vruntime, deadlines, and eligibility would render the old min_vruntime concept obsolete. After all, the selection algorithm no longer picks "the entity with the smallest vruntime" — it picks the eligible entity with the earliest deadline. The answer is that the name survives but has moved: as a per-entity field it is more important than it ever was under CFS, while as a per-queue field it has been deleted outright.

The first form is the per-entity augmented field se->min_vruntime. Each RB-tree node caches the minimum vruntime of its entire subtree (itself and all descendants). This is the augmentation that powers O(log n) eligibility search in pick_eevdf(): by comparing a subtree's min_vruntime against the queue average, the scheduler decides instantly whether that subtree contains any eligible entity worth descending into. Without this field, the tree — ordered by deadline — would give no clue about vruntime distribution.

The second form was the queue-level floor, cfs_rq::min_vruntime, and it no longer exists. Linux 6.19 replaced it with zero_vruntime for the reasons given earlier in this chapter: the queue’s reference point has to track the weighted average, and the weighted average can move backward, which a monotonic floor by definition cannot.

The role that field played is unchanged, however, and it is the reason a per-queue reference is needed at all. When a task moves between CPUs, the two queues’ virtual clocks may differ by millions of nanoseconds, so the kernel normalizes an entity on dequeue by subtracting the source queue’s reference and denormalizes it on enqueue by adding the destination’s. This is what stops a migrating task from arriving with a hugely negative relative vruntime that would let it monopolize its new CPU, or a hugely positive one that would starve it. Only the name and the monotonicity guarantee changed; the normalization still happens, now against zero_vruntime.

In summary, min_vruntime is no longer the scheduling criterion — that role belongs to deadlines and eligibility — and it is no longer a property of the queue. What remains is se->min_vruntime, the augmentation that makes the eligibility search fast, plus a per-queue reference under a new name that makes migration between independently-clocked per-CPU trees safe.

Delayed Dequeue: Staying on the Runqueue a Little Longer

Traditional scheduler design assumes a clean binary: a task is either on the runqueue and competing for CPU time, or it is off the runqueue and invisible to the scheduler. Every transition between these states triggers a full structural update — tree insertion or removal, load-tracking recalculation, and propagation up the cgroup hierarchy. For a single entity in a flat queue, this cost is modest. But in a deep cgroup hierarchy with dozens of intermediate scheduling entities, a single dequeue can ripple through many cfs_rq objects, updating PELT averages, toggling parent-entity "empty" states, and rebalancing augmented tree metadata at every level.

Linux 6.12 introduced delayed dequeue (the DELAY_DEQUEUE scheduler feature, on by default) — a mechanism that allows a task that has gone to sleep to remain physically on the RB-tree, marked with the sched_delayed flag, rather than being removed at once. The entity is no longer runnable, and h_nr_runnable is decremented immediately so that load balancing sees the correct count, but its tree node stays exactly where it was.

The trigger condition is precise: the feature must be enabled, the dequeue must be a genuine sleep (DEQUEUE_SLEEP, and not one of the DEQUEUE_SPECIAL or DEQUEUE_THROTTLE cases, which must not be exposed to spurious wakeups), and the entity must already be ineligible — entity_eligible() returns false.

That last condition is the whole point, and it is a fairness mechanism rather than an optimization. An ineligible entity is one that has consumed more than its share; it carries positive lag, a debt. If the kernel removed it from the tree the instant it blocked, the debt would be frozen and the task could escape it simply by sleeping — repeatedly, on a workload that alternates short bursts of CPU with short sleeps. By leaving the entity queued, the scheduler keeps advancing the queue’s virtual time past it, so the sleeping task burns its debt off in absentia exactly as it would have done had it stayed runnable. A companion feature, DELAY_ZERO, closes the other side of the loophole: when the delayed dequeue is finally finished, any lag still positive is clipped to zero, so the debt is neither carried forward nor converted into credit.

The removal itself happens at the moment the entity is picked, which is the part most easily gotten wrong. A delayed entity is still in the tree and pick_eevdf() will happily return it; it is pick_next_entity(), one level up, that notices the flag:

/* fair.c — delayed entities are finished off on the pick path */
static struct sched_entity *
pick_next_entity(struct rq *rq, struct cfs_rq *cfs_rq, bool protect)
{
    struct sched_entity *se = pick_eevdf(cfs_rq, protect);

    if (se->sched_delayed) {
        dequeue_entities(rq, se, DEQUEUE_SLEEP | DEQUEUE_DELAYED);
        return NULL;            /* caller retries the pick */
    }
    return se;
}

Being selected is therefore what tells the scheduler that the entity has finished paying: it only becomes pickable once it is eligible again, and at that instant the real dequeue is performed and the caller loops back for another pick. The other exit is a wakeup before that point, which needs no tree surgery at all — the node never left, so re-enqueuing collapses into clearing a flag.

The deepest consequence is that delayed dequeue breaks an identity the scheduler relied on for thirty years: being on the runqueue no longer means being runnable. The break is visible in one line of block_task(), which calls __block_task() — the function that actually clears p->on_rq — only if dequeue_task() reports that a real dequeue took place. When the dequeue was delayed instead, the blocking task keeps p->on_rq == 1.

Once that identity is relaxed, other features can use the same room. Proxy execution is the notable one: under CONFIG_SCHED_PROXY_EXEC a task that blocks on a mutex stays enqueued, so the scheduler can still select it and then, discovering it is blocked, run the mutex’s owner on its behalf. The blocked task is the donor — it donates its scheduling context, which is where rq->donor gets its name and why it is distinct from rq->curr, the task physically executing. Priority inheritance then falls out of the pick rather than being bolted on: the owner runs with the waiter’s urgency because it is literally running in the waiter’s slot. Delayed dequeue does not implement proxy execution, but both rest on the same relaxed invariant, and code that assumes “queued implies runnable” is wrong in 7.0.12 for two independent reasons.

Table 7-18. What Delayed Dequeue Buys the Fair Scheduler
ProblemWithout DelayWith Delayed Dequeue
Sleeping to escape a service debtPositive lag is frozen at dequeue and may be forgivenLag keeps decaying while the task sleeps; residue is clipped to zero by DELAY_ZERO
Short-lived leave/reenter cyclesRepeated tree erases and insertsA wakeup before the pick collapses into clearing a flag
Hierarchical cgroup updatesMany parent entities churn immediatelyWork can be deferred until truly necessary

How SCHED_BATCH and SCHED_IDLE Differ Inside the Fair Class

A common misconception is that SCHED_BATCH and SCHED_IDLE are separate scheduling classes, or that they are simply "bad nice values." In reality, all three ordinary policies — SCHED_NORMAL, SCHED_BATCH, and SCHED_IDLE — share the same fair (EEVDF) class, use the same RB-tree, the same virtual-time accounting, and the same eligibility/deadline selection algorithm. Their differences are behavioral hints that tweak a few specific decision points within that shared machinery.

The critical distinction lies in wakeup preemption. When a SCHED_NORMAL task wakes up and is eligible, it may preempt the currently running task to get quick access to the CPU — this is what gives interactive applications their snappy feel. A SCHED_BATCH or SCHED_IDLE task, however, never triggers wakeup preemption: it waits for the natural scheduling cycle to pick it. This single difference fundamentally changes the latency profile: batch tasks trade wake-to-run latency for higher throughput (fewer context switches), while idle tasks accept arbitrarily long delays in exchange for consuming only truly spare CPU cycles.

SCHED_IDLE goes further with an additional asymmetry: an idle entity is always preempted by a non-idle entity that wakes up, regardless of eligibility or deadline. The kernel treats this as a hard boundary — a SCHED_IDLE task should never delay a normal workload. Conversely, a SCHED_IDLE task can never preempt a non-idle entity, even if it has an earlier deadline. This creates an effective priority partition within the fair class: normal and batch tasks compete among themselves via EEVDF, while idle tasks only get CPU time in the gaps between them.

Table 7-19. Internal Differences Among Fair-Class Policies
PolicyDesign IntentScheduling Consequence
SCHED_NORMALGeneral-purpose interactivity plus fairnessStandard EEVDF latency behavior
SCHED_BATCHThroughput-oriented background CPU workStill fair, but avoids pretending wakeup latency is precious
SCHED_IDLERun only when ordinary fair work is absentParticipates in fair machinery, but only at the very bottom of fair competition

It is worth seeing how these rules sit inside wakeup_preempt_fair(), because the ordering of its tests is itself the specification, and because the mechanism for the ordinary case is not a heuristic at all. The idle asymmetry is checked first: if the incumbent is an idle entity and the waker is not, preemption is immediate and the incumbent’s slice protection is cancelled, so it does not even keep its minimum quantum. If exactly one of the two is idle in the other direction, the function returns without preempting. Next comes the blanket rule that SCHED_BATCH and SCHED_IDLE wakers never preempt. Only then does the normal path run, and what it does is simply re-run the EEVDF pick and ask whether the waking task is the answer:

/* fair.c — wakeup_preempt_fair(), the decisive step */
    nse = pick_next_entity(rq, cfs_rq, preempt_action != PREEMPT_WAKEUP_SHORT);

    /* If @p has become the most eligible task, force preemption */
    if (nse == pse)
        goto preempt;

This is a genuine simplification over CFS, which compared vruntimes against a tunable wakeup granularity and got the answer approximately. In 7.0.12 the wakeup-preemption question and the task-selection question have the same answer by construction, because they are the same question. Around that core sit two features. PREEMPT_SHORT (on by default) detects that the waker’s slice is shorter than the incumbent’s and asks for the pick to be made without slice protection, which is how a latency-sensitive task cuts into a queue of batch work. NEXT_BUDDY (off by default) nominates the wakee as cfs_rq->next so that a later pick prefers it; the related PICK_BUDDY (on by default) is what honours that nomination inside pick_eevdf(), and note that it still requires the buddy to be eligible — a buddy hint can cost latency elsewhere but can never breach fairness.

SCHED_BATCH is not “worse nice.” It tells the scheduler that the task is CPU-hungry and not latency-sensitive, which affects wakeup-preemption behavior and effective slice handling. SCHED_IDLE goes further: it is for work that should consume spare cycles only, such as scavengers or maintenance tasks.

CFS Bandwidth Control: Quota, Period, Burst, Throttle

EEVDF guarantees proportional fairness among competing entities on a single CPU, but it says nothing about absolute limits. Without an additional mechanism, a task group with 1000 threads could consume 100% of every CPU it touches, as long as it competes fairly within each CPU's tree. In multi-tenant environments — containers, cloud VMs, shared build farms — administrators need a harder guarantee: "this group may use at most X microseconds of CPU time per Y-microsecond window, regardless of how many threads it has or how many CPUs are available."

CFS bandwidth control provides that guarantee. It operates as a separate accounting layer above the per-CPU EEVDF trees: a global pool tracks how much runtime the group has consumed across all CPUs during the current period, and when that pool runs dry, the per-CPU runqueues belonging to that group are throttled until the next period boundary. From the user’s perspective, the container’s CPU usage is hard-capped.

How that stop is implemented changed fundamentally in Linux 6.18, and it is worth describing the old model first because every previous account of CFS bandwidth control — and a good deal of still-current documentation — describes it. Historically, throttling a cfs_rq meant dequeuing its group entity from its parent. The group vanished from the tree, and every task inside it stopped dead wherever it happened to be.

Wherever it happened to be is the problem. A task does not stop only at safe points; it can be throttled in the middle of a kernel critical section, holding resources that have nothing to do with the cgroup. The case that forced the redesign involved percpu_rwsem: a throttled task holding a read lock would be frozen for up to a full period — typically 100 ms, an eternity — while a writer blocked behind it, further readers piled up behind the writer, and the machine eventually produced task-hung splats. A cgroup’s CPU limit had become a system-wide liveness hazard.

Linux 6.18 switched to a task-based throttle model (commit e1fad12dcb66) built on a simple principle: stop tasks at the userspace boundary, where they hold no kernel resources. Nothing is dequeued when the quota runs out. Instead:

  1. The cfs_rq is marked throttled and put on its bandwidth object’s throttled_list. It stays on the runqueue, and its tasks stay runnable.
  2. When a task from a throttled hierarchy is picked, pick_task_fair() arms a task_work on it with TWA_RESUME. The task then runs normally.
  3. On its way back to user mode the task runs that work, throttle_cfs_rq_work(), which dequeues it there — at a point where, by construction, it holds no kernel locks — and parks it on the runqueue’s limbo list via p->throttle_node.
  4. At unthrottle, the limbo list is walked and the tasks are enqueued again.

The consequences are worth spelling out because they contradict the intuition the old model built. A throttled group is not invisible to EEVDF; its entities remain in the tree. A throttled task that is executing in the kernel keeps running until it tries to return to userspace, so it will finish its critical section and release its locks. Kernel threads and exiting tasks never return to userspace at all and so are never given the work — they are simply not throttled. And because tasks may still run after the group is marked, the PELT clock can no longer be stopped when throttling begins; it is stopped only once the throttled cfs_rq has no tasks left on it.

The design is hierarchical and distributed. Each task group has a single struct cfs_bandwidth object holding the global quota, period timer, and remaining runtime pool (protected by a spinlock). Each per-CPU cfs_rq belonging to that group holds a local runtime_remaining slice drawn from the global pool. Tasks consume from the local slice first (lock-free on the fast path); only when the local slice runs out does the per-CPU runqueue request more from the global pool (taking the lock). If the global pool is empty, the entire per-CPU cfs_rq is throttled. A slack timer reclaims unused local slices from CPUs that didn't consume their full allocation, redistributing them to CPUs that need more — reducing the chance of premature throttling on bursty workloads.

The burst mechanism (exposed via cpu.max.burst in cgroup v2) adds flexibility: a group can accumulate unused quota across periods up to a burst cap, then spend it in a single busy period. This accommodates workloads with periodic spikes — a web server that is idle for 90% of each second but needs 3× its average quota during the remaining 10%. Without burst, such a workload would be throttled during spikes despite being well under budget on average.

Table 7-20. CFS Bandwidth Control Terms
TermKernel FieldMeaning
periodcfs_bandwidth.periodReplenishment interval
quotacfs_bandwidth.quotaRuntime granted each period
burstcfs_bandwidth.burstExtra runtime that may be borrowed briefly
runtime_remainingcfs_rq.runtime_remainingLocal per-runqueue budget currently usable
throttlethrottled_list, timersState entered when runtime is exhausted

The implementation is timer-driven. start_cfs_bandwidth() arms a period timer; each period, the bandwidth code refills the global pool, transfers runtime into per-CPU cfs_rq state, and unthrottles the runqueues on throttled_list — re-enqueuing, for each one, the tasks that had parked themselves on its limbo list. Slack timers can return unused runtime upward, and burst lets a group transiently exceed its nominal steady-state quota without waiting a whole period.

For the reader of cgroup v2 interfaces, this is what cpu.max and cpu.max.burst become inside the scheduler.

Task Groups: A Tree of Entities and Runqueues

Up to this point, we have discussed the fair scheduler as if every entity on a CPU's cfs_rq represents a thread. That is the simple, flat-scheduling case. But modern Linux systems — especially those running containers — need fairness guarantees at multiple levels simultaneously: between containers, between users within a container, and between threads within a process. If a container with 1000 threads competed directly against a container with 10 threads in a single flat tree, the first container would naturally receive 100× more CPU time simply by outnumbering the second. That defeats the purpose of resource isolation.

The kernel solves this with hierarchical group scheduling, controlled by cgroup v2's cpu.weight knob. The idea is elegant: a struct task_group does not participate in scheduling directly. Instead, on each CPU where it has runnable threads, the task group instantiates a per-CPU scheduling entity (task_group->se[cpu]) and a per-CPU runqueue (task_group->cfs_rq[cpu]). The per-CPU entity competes in the parent's tree as a single "representative" for the entire group, while the per-CPU runqueue holds the group's actual threads (or child-group entities). The scheduler doesn't need to know whether an entity represents one thread or a thousand — it just picks the eligible entity with the earliest deadline, descends into it if it's a group, and repeats until it finds an actual task.

This creates a tree of trees: each level of the cgroup hierarchy maps to a level of nested cfs_rq instances, connected by sched_entity objects that serve as both children in the parent's tree and owners of a child tree. The se->my_q pointer links downward (entity → the runqueue it "owns"), while se->parent links upward (entity → the group entity at the next level). The for_each_sched_entity() macro walks this chain, propagating accounting updates from a leaf task all the way to the root — ensuring that when a thread consumes CPU time, its group's vruntime advances proportionally at every hierarchy level.

When group scheduling is enabled, the fair scheduler is recursive:

  • a thread owns a leaf sched_entity,
  • that entity is queued on a leaf cfs_rq (owned by the task's immediate cgroup),
  • the cgroup's group entity is queued on its parent's cfs_rq,
  • the recursion continues upward through the cgroup hierarchy,
  • it stops at the root task group attached to the CPU's top-level rq->cfs.

This structure means fairness is enforced at every level. If two containers each have equal shares (cpu.weight = 100), they first divide CPU time evenly between containers; inside each container, the local entities divide the container's share again according to their own weights. The scheduler does not need one special algorithm for cgroups and another for tasks because both are just entities in a tree. The same EEVDF logic — eligibility, deadline, pick — applies identically at every level of the hierarchy.

Once all of these pieces are understood together, EEVDF reads as a complete story: weight defines entitlement, vruntime measures consumed service, avg_vruntime defines who is owed time, deadline defines who among the owed tasks should run next, and placement plus bandwidth control keep the whole mechanism stable across sleeps, forks, cgroups, and CPU migration.

Real-Time Scheduling (SCHED_FIFO and SCHED_RR)

The previous sections described the fair scheduler — an elaborate mechanism for dividing CPU time proportionally among tasks that are all "equally important" in the broad sense. But some workloads cannot tolerate proportional sharing at all. An audio pipeline that misses its 5ms buffer deadline produces an audible glitch regardless of how "fairly" the CPU was shared. A robotic control loop that responds 2ms late may cause physical damage. These workloads need a fundamentally different contract: when I am runnable, I run immediately, and nothing of lower importance can delay me.

Linux's real-time scheduling class provides exactly this contract through fixed-priority preemptive scheduling. The design is deliberately simple compared to EEVDF: no virtual time, no weighted sharing, no eligibility computation. Instead, RT scheduling reduces to three rules: (1) a runnable RT task always preempts all fair-class tasks, (2) among RT tasks, higher static priority always wins, and (3) among RT tasks at the same priority, the policy determines whether the current task runs to completion (SCHED_FIFO) or rotates after a time quantum (SCHED_RR). These rules make the system's behavior completely predictable to the application developer — a property that fair scheduling intentionally sacrifices for better average throughput.

This predictability comes at a cost: RT scheduling is inherently unfair and starvation-prone. A runnable priority-99 task will starve every priority-98-and-below task indefinitely. A buggy RT thread can lock out the entire system, including the kernel's own housekeeping work. Linux offers two defences against this, and it matters a great deal which one is actually present on the machine in front of you. RT bandwidth throttling caps the CPU time the class may consume per period — nominally 950ms out of every 1000ms (sched_rt_runtime_us / sched_rt_period_us), reserving 5% for everyone else — but every line of that accounting is compiled under CONFIG_RT_GROUP_SCHED, which is default n and which most distributions leave off. The second defence, the RLIMIT_RTTIME watchdog, is per-task rather than per-class and is always compiled in. Both are examined later in this section, because “Linux throttles runaway RT tasks” is one of the most widely repeated claims about this scheduler and it is not true of the kernel most readers are running.

Two pressures shape everything that follows, and they pull against each other:

  • Determinism is achieved by leaving things out. Every mechanism that makes the fair class clever — virtual time, eligibility, slice protection, periodic load balancing — is also a source of jitter, so the RT class has none of them. The price is that the class has no opinion about whether the priorities it is handed make any sense; correctness is delegated entirely to the system designer.
  • A general-purpose kernel cannot let one task own a CPU forever. Something has to be able to reclaim the machine from a runaway SCHED_FIFO thread, and every candidate mechanism dilutes the guarantee in the first bullet. Linux has shipped two answers, and the better-known of the two is absent from most kernels in the field.

The rest of this section works through seven questions:

  1. How do the RT priorities an application sets relate to the numbers the kernel actually compares? (Priority Space and Semantics)
  2. How is the highest-priority runnable task found without searching? (The rt_prio_array Bitmap)
  3. What exactly separates SCHED_FIFO from SCHED_RR, and when does the difference show up? (FIFO and RR Behavior in Detail)
  4. Which code really decides that an RT wakeup preempts the running task — because it is not the function whose name suggests it? (Immediate Preemption Rules)
  5. What stops a runaway RT thread from hanging the machine, and is that protection compiled into your kernel? (RT Throttling and the FIFO Watchdog)
  6. How is the invariant “the N highest-priority RT tasks occupy N CPUs” maintained without waiting for a periodic balancer? (Push/Pull Migration on SMP)
  7. What happens when the highest-priority task is blocked on a lock held by a low-priority one? (Priority Inheritance and rt_mutex)

Version history. The fixed-priority core is the one part of the Linux scheduler that anyone who knows the 2.6.11 code would still recognise almost line for line: the same 100-bit bitmap, the same array of per-priority FIFO lists, the same unconditional preemption rule. Everything built around it is new. RT bandwidth throttling and RT group scheduling did not exist in 2.6.11 — both arrived in 2.6.25 — and neither did the deadline class that now outranks RT entirely. Two changes fall inside the 6.12–7.0 window itself. Group scheduling gained a runtime off switch in 6.16, so CONFIG_RT_GROUP_SCHED no longer forces its cost and its awkward cgroup restrictions on every boot; and CONFIG_SMP ceased to exist as a build option in 6.17, so the push/pull machinery described below is now unconditional code rather than an SMP-only addition.

The RT class also supports SMP push/pull migration. Unlike fair tasks that are load-balanced periodically, RT tasks trigger immediate migration when a higher-priority task wakes up on a CPU already running an RT task: the lower-priority task is "pushed" to another CPU, or conversely, a newly idle CPU "pulls" the highest-priority waiting RT task from overloaded CPUs. This ensures that the N highest-priority RT tasks in the system always occupy N CPUs, with minimal latency.

Priority Space and Semantics

One of the most confusing aspects of Linux scheduling for newcomers is that the kernel uses two different priority numbering systems that run in opposite directions. User space sees RT priorities 1 through 99 (set via sched_setscheduler() or chrt), with 99 being the most urgent. Internally, the kernel maps these into a global priority space from 0 to 139, where lower numbers mean higher priority. The mapping is simple: kernel priority = MAX_RT_PRIO - 1 - rt_priority, so user RT priority 99 becomes kernel priority 0 (highest), and user RT priority 1 becomes kernel priority 98 (lowest RT). Kernel priorities 100–139 are reserved for fair-class tasks (mapped from nice values −20 to +19), guaranteeing that every RT task outranks every fair task by virtue of occupying the lower end of the priority number line.

Within the RT class, the scheduler makes no attempt at fairness or proportional sharing. Priority is static: it is set by the application (or system administrator) and never adjusted by the scheduler itself. There are no nice-value adjustments, no virtual-time calculations, no eligibility checks. The only question is "which runnable RT task has the highest static priority?" — and that task runs. If two or more tasks share the same priority level, the policy field determines behavior: SCHED_FIFO tasks run in strict arrival order (first in, first served until block or yield), while SCHED_RR tasks rotate through a time quantum (RR_TIMESLICE, 100ms, readable per task with sched_rr_get_interval() and tunable system-wide through /proc/sys/kernel/sched_rr_timeslice_ms). Note that RR rotation only occurs among peers at the same priority — a priority-50 RR task never gets CPU time while a priority-51 task is runnable.

Table 7-21. RT Priority Interpretation
ConceptMeaningConsequence
Higher rt_priorityMore urgent RT threadPreempts lower RT priorities and all fair tasks
Equal RT priority, FIFOArrival order mattersThread runs until block, yield, or higher-priority arrival
Equal RT priority, RRPeers must rotateQuantum expiration moves current thread to list tail

The rt_prio_array Bitmap

The EEVDF fair scheduler uses an augmented RB-tree with O(log n) selection — sophisticated but still logarithmic. The RT class cannot afford even that. When an interrupt handler completes and its threaded IRQ needs to run, or when a priority-99 task unblocks after waiting on a mutex, the scheduler must find and dispatch the highest-priority RT task in constant time. Any data structure that scales with the number of runnable tasks would introduce priority-dependent latency, violating the determinism guarantee that RT users rely on.

Linux achieves this with a classic real-time operating system technique: a bitmap-indexed array of per-priority FIFO lists. The rt_prio_array structure contains a 100-bit bitmap (one bit per RT priority level, 0 through 99) and an array of 100 doubly-linked list heads. When a task is enqueued, it is appended to the list for its priority level and the corresponding bit is set. When a task is dequeued and its list becomes empty, the bit is cleared. To find the next task to run, the scheduler simply finds the first (lowest-numbered, i.e. highest-priority) set bit in the bitmap — a single machine instruction on both x86_64 (bsf/tzcnt) and ARM64 (clz/rbit+clz) — then takes the head of that priority's list. The entire selection is three constant-time operations regardless of whether 1 or 1000 RT tasks are runnable.

/* kernel/sched/sched.h — RT priority array */
struct rt_prio_array {
    DECLARE_BITMAP(bitmap, MAX_RT_PRIO+1); /* include 1 bit for delimiter */
    struct list_head queue[MAX_RT_PRIO];
};

When an RT task is enqueued, the scheduler appends it to the list corresponding to its effective RT priority and sets that bit in the bitmap. To pick the next task, the scheduler finds the first set bit and takes the head of that list. This is why RT selection is effectively constant-time with respect to the number of runnable RT tasks.

Table 7-22. RT Bitmap Selection Steps
StepActionComplexity Intuition
1Find highest set bit in bitmapConstant-time machine bitmap operation
2Index queue[prio]Array lookup
3Take list headO(1) doubly linked list access
4If queue empties, clear bitO(1)
 
/* rt.c — pick shape, simplified */
static struct task_struct *_pick_next_task_rt(struct rq *rq)
{
    struct sched_rt_entity *rt_se;
    struct rt_rq *rt_rq = &rq->rt;

    do {
        rt_se = pick_next_rt_entity(rt_rq);
        if (!rt_se)
            return NULL;
        rt_rq = group_rt_rq(rt_se);
    } while (rt_rq);

    return rt_task_of(rt_se);
}

The extra loop is there because RT group scheduling can nest RT entities, just as fair group scheduling nests fair entities. The top-level pick may select a group entity, which then points to a child rt_rq until the scheduler eventually lands on a leaf task.

FIFO and RR Behavior in Detail

The two RT policies — SCHED_FIFO and SCHED_RR — share the same priority infrastructure (bitmap, per-priority lists, immediate preemption) but differ in one crucial aspect: what happens when a task has been running "long enough" at a priority level where peers exist. Understanding this difference is essential for designing RT systems where multiple threads must cooperate at the same priority.

SCHED_FIFO provides the simplest possible contract: once selected, a FIFO task runs until it voluntarily relinquishes the CPU (by blocking on I/O, sleeping, calling sched_yield(), or exiting) or is involuntarily preempted by a higher-priority task. There is no time limit and no rotation. If a FIFO task at priority 50 enters an infinite loop, every other priority-50 task starves forever. This is by design — FIFO is for workloads that know exactly how long they need and will yield promptly, such as periodic control loops that run a fixed computation and then sleep until the next period.

SCHED_RR adds exactly one mechanism on top of FIFO: a time quantum (default 100ms, configurable via /proc/sys/kernel/sched_rr_timeslice_ms). Each tick decrements the running RR task's quantum counter. When it reaches zero, the scheduler resets the quantum, moves the task to the tail of the same-priority list, and requests rescheduling — giving the next peer at that priority level a turn. This is not fairness in the EEVDF sense (there is no proportional sharing), but it prevents same-priority starvation. Note that higher-priority tasks still preempt immediately regardless of quantum state, and lower-priority tasks still get nothing until all higher-priority work completes.

Table 7-23. Detailed Comparison of SCHED_FIFO and SCHED_RR
QuestionSCHED_FIFOSCHED_RR
How long can it run?Until block, yield, or higher-priority preemptionUntil block, yield, higher-priority preemption, or quantum expiry
What happens on wakeup?Inserted according to RT queue rules at its prioritySame
How are same-priority peers treated?FIFO orderRound-robin order
Where is fairness enforced?Nowhere among peers except voluntary relinquishOnly among equal-priority RR peers

For RR tasks, the periodic tick decrements a per-task quantum. When that counter reaches zero, the scheduler resets the quantum and — if there is anyone to rotate with — moves the task to the tail of the same-priority queue and requests rescheduling.

/* rt.c — task_tick_rt(), simplified */
static void task_tick_rt(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *p, int queued)
{
    struct sched_rt_entity *rt_se = &p->rt;

    update_curr_rt(rq);
    update_rt_rq_load_avg(rq_clock_pelt(rq), rq, 1);
    watchdog(rq, p);

    if (p->policy != SCHED_RR)
        return;

    if (--p->rt.time_slice)
        return;

    p->rt.time_slice = sched_rr_timeslice;

    /* Requeue if this entity, or any ancestor, has a sibling
       to rotate with. */
    for_each_sched_rt_entity(rt_se) {
        if (rt_se->run_list.prev != rt_se->run_list.next) {
            requeue_task_rt(rq, p, 0);
            resched_curr(rq);
            return;
        }
    }
}

That last qualifier is easy to overlook but matters: the loop walks the entity and its ancestors and returns without doing anything if each is alone on its list. A lone SCHED_RR task therefore behaves exactly like a SCHED_FIFO one, running without interruption instead of pointlessly rescheduling itself every 100ms. Rotation is a property of having peers, not of the policy.

Immediate Preemption Rules

The fair scheduler uses sophisticated heuristics to decide whether a waking task should preempt the current one — checking eligibility, comparing deadlines, considering slice protection, and respecting wakeup-preemption feature flags. The RT class discards all of this complexity in favor of a single, unconditional rule: if a higher-priority RT task becomes runnable, it preempts whatever is currently running, immediately, with no negotiation. Tracing where that rule actually lives is worth the trouble, because it is not in the function whose name suggests it.

The generic entry point is wakeup_preempt() — called check_preempt_curr() until 6.7 renamed it, a name still widely quoted in older documentation. It does not simply forward the wakeup to the running task’s class. As described earlier in this chapter, Linux 7.0 routes it through rq->next_class, and the cross-class case is handled generically rather than by the RT class at all:

/* core.c — wakeup_preempt(), the cross-class part */
if (p->sched_class == rq->next_class) {
    rq->next_class->wakeup_preempt(rq, p, flags);

} else if (sched_class_above(p->sched_class, rq->next_class)) {
    rq->next_class->wakeup_preempt(rq, p, flags);
    resched_curr(rq);
    rq->next_class = p->sched_class;
}

So the headline rule — an RT wakeup preempts a running fair task — is enforced by sched_class_above() and the unconditional resched_curr() on the second branch. It requires no RT-specific code whatsoever, and indeed wakeup_preempt_rt() would refuse to do it: its first statement is if (p->sched_class != &rt_sched_class) return;. The RT hook is concerned only with RT-versus-RT decisions.

/* rt.c — wakeup_preempt_rt(), abridged */
static void wakeup_preempt_rt(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *p, int flags)
{
    struct task_struct *donor = rq->donor;

    if (p->sched_class != &rt_sched_class)
        return;

    if (p->prio < donor->prio) {
        resched_curr(rq);
        return;
    }

    if (p->prio == donor->prio && !test_tsk_need_resched(rq->curr))
        check_preempt_equal_prio(rq, p);
}

Two details in those few lines repay attention. First, the comparison is against rq->donor, not rq->curr: under proxy execution the task physically on the CPU may be a lock owner running on someone else’s behalf, and it is the scheduling context whose priority is meaningful. Second, the equal-priority case is not simply “no preemption”. check_preempt_equal_prio() handles a situation peculiar to multiprocessors: if the waking task is pinned to this CPU while the current task is free to move and cpupri_find() can locate somewhere for it to go, the kernel requeues the waker at the head of its priority list and reschedules — not so the waker preempts, but so the current task is pushed away and stops blocking a task that has nowhere else to run.

This "blunt force" approach is what makes RT scheduling analyzable. A system designer can draw a priority assignment table and know with certainty which task will run in any given scenario — there are no adaptive behaviors, no scheduler features that might change the outcome under load, and no race conditions in the decision logic.
