The tradeoff is that the designer bears full responsibility for getting the priority assignment right: if two independent subsystems both claim priority 99, one will starve the other with no automatic mitigation.

RT preemption is intentionally blunt:

  • an RT task preempts any fair task immediately if it becomes runnable,
  • a higher-priority RT task preempts a lower-priority RT task immediately,
  • a same-priority FIFO task does not time-slice the current FIFO task,
  • a same-priority RR task eventually gets a turn when the current RR task's quantum expires,
  • with one exception to the two rules above: a same-priority waker that is pinned to this CPU can force a reschedule in order to have the current task pushed elsewhere.

RT Throttling and the FIFO Watchdog

The immediate-preemption guarantee that makes RT scheduling useful is also what makes it dangerous. A SCHED_FIFO task at priority 99 that enters an infinite loop — whether due to a bug, a deadlock, or malicious intent — will permanently starve every other task on that CPU, including kernel threads responsible for memory reclaim, RCU callbacks, workqueue processing, and even the OOM killer. On a system with CPU affinity constraints, a single runaway RT thread can make the machine effectively unresponsive: SSH sessions hang, monitoring agents stop reporting, and the only recovery is a hardware reset or the NMI watchdog (if enabled).

Linux's best-known answer is RT bandwidth throttling — a safety valve that caps the total CPU time the RT class may consume per period. The mechanism is timer-driven: a period timer fires every sched_rt_period_us microseconds (default: 1,000,000 = 1 second) and resets the RT runtime budget to sched_rt_runtime_us microseconds (default: 950,000 = 950ms). As RT tasks run, update_curr_rt() charges their execution time against the per-CPU rt_rq->rt_time counter. When that counter exceeds the budget, the RT runqueue is throttled: it is dequeued from the scheduling hierarchy and its tasks become invisible to the pick path. For the remaining 50ms of the period, only fair-class and idle tasks can run — giving the system a guaranteed recovery window.

That is the description found in nearly every account of Linux real-time scheduling, and on most systems it describes code that is not in the kernel. In 7.0.12 the entire accounting body of update_curr_rt() sits inside #ifdef CONFIG_RT_GROUP_SCHED, and so does sched_rt_runtime_exceeded() itself; when the option is off, rt_rq_throttled() is a function that returns false and nothing ever increments rt_time. CONFIG_RT_GROUP_SCHED is default n in init/Kconfig and is disabled by the major distributions, for the reason given in the group-scheduling discussion below. The practical consequence is worth stating plainly: on a stock distribution kernel there is no RT throttle at all, and a SCHED_FIFO task that spins will hold its CPU indefinitely.

The /proc knobs remain, however, and remain writable, which is what makes this so easy to misread — writing to sched_rt_runtime_us succeeds and plainly does something. It does, but not what the name implies. sched_rt_handler() calls sched_rt_do_global(), which in 7.0.12 is an empty function; the substantive work is in the sched_dl_global_validate() and sched_dl_do_global() calls beside it, followed by rebuild_sched_domains(). The RT bandwidth knobs are now, in effect, the configuration interface for SCHED_DEADLINE admission control: they define the total utilisation the deadline class is willing to admit. That is why lowering sched_rt_runtime_us can make a later sched_setattr() fail with -EBUSY on a kernel where it throttles nothing at all.

One call inside the listing below deserves the same scepticism the rest of this subsection applies to the throttle as a whole. balance_runtime() is the entry point to runtime borrowing: the idea that a CPU which has exhausted its share of the RT budget may take unused runtime from sibling CPUs in the same root domain, so that an unevenly distributed RT workload is not throttled while the machine as a whole is under budget. It is a reasonable idea, and it is what a good deal of documentation still describes. Its first two lines are:

/* rt.c — balance_runtime() */
static void balance_runtime(struct rt_rq *rt_rq)
{
    if (!sched_feat(RT_RUNTIME_SHARE))
        return;
    ...
}

RT_RUNTIME_SHARE has defaulted to false since 5.10 (commit 2586af1ac187), so on every stock kernel the function returns immediately and no borrowing occurs. Borrowing was disabled because it undermined the guarantee it was meant to soften: moving runtime between CPUs makes the throttle’s behaviour depend on what unrelated CPUs happened to be doing, which is exactly the kind of coupling a real-time practitioner is trying to eliminate. A per-CPU budget that is small but predictable turned out to be more useful than a larger one that fluctuates.

The pattern is now familiar within this one subsection: a mechanism that is present in the source, described in the literature, and inert on the machine in front of you. It is worth keeping in mind that the RT class has accumulated several such layers, and that reading rt.c without checking the corresponding Kconfig defaults and sched_feat values will give a systematically overstated picture of what the kernel is doing.

Where the mechanism is compiled in, its purpose is narrower than the name suggests. It does not try to make RT scheduling fair — that would defeat the point of the class. It only ensures that no matter what RT tasks do, the system retains a minimum slice of CPU time for essential housekeeping. Production real-time deployments that have analysed their workloads and know the budget cannot be exceeded often disable it explicitly (sched_rt_runtime_us = -1), trading the safety net for the absence of any throttle-induced latency. The throttle is also coarse in a way that surprises people: it operates on the runqueue, so when it fires it stops every RT task on that CPU, including well-behaved ones that had nothing to do with exhausting the budget.

/* rt.c — throttling decision, simplified */
static int sched_rt_runtime_exceeded(struct rt_rq *rt_rq)
{
    u64 runtime = sched_rt_runtime(rt_rq);

    if (rt_rq->rt_throttled)
        return rt_rq_throttled(rt_rq);

    if (runtime >= sched_rt_period(rt_rq))
        return 0;

    balance_runtime(rt_rq);
    runtime = sched_rt_runtime(rt_rq);
    if (runtime == RUNTIME_INF)
        return 0;

    if (rt_rq->rt_time > runtime) {
        struct rt_bandwidth *rt_b = sched_rt_bandwidth(rt_rq);

        /* Do not throttle groups with no runtime that accrued
           time only through PI boosting. */
        if (likely(rt_b->rt_runtime)) {
            rt_rq->rt_throttled = 1;
            printk_deferred_once("sched: RT throttling activated\n");
        } else {
            rt_rq->rt_time = 0;
        }

        if (rt_rq_throttled(rt_rq)) {
            sched_rt_rq_dequeue(rt_rq);
            return 1;
        }
    }
    return 0;
}

The second defence is the one this section is named after, and it is the one that is actually present on a default kernel. Every tick, task_tick_rt() calls watchdog(), which compares the task's accumulated RT running time against its RLIMIT_RTTIME resource limit. Unlike the throttle, this is a per-task limit, it is enforced regardless of CONFIG_RT_GROUP_SCHED (it needs only CONFIG_POSIX_TIMERS), and it targets the offending thread instead of penalising every RT task sharing the CPU.

/* rt.c — watchdog(), called from task_tick_rt() */
static void watchdog(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *p)
{
    unsigned long soft, hard;

    soft = task_rlimit(p, RLIMIT_RTTIME);
    hard = task_rlimit_max(p, RLIMIT_RTTIME);

    if (soft != RLIM_INFINITY) {
        unsigned long next;

        if (p->rt.watchdog_stamp != jiffies) {
            p->rt.timeout++;
            p->rt.watchdog_stamp = jiffies;
        }

        next = DIV_ROUND_UP(min(soft, hard), USEC_PER_SEC/HZ);
        if (p->rt.timeout > next)
            posix_cputimers_rt_watchdog(&p->posix_cputimers,
                                        p->se.sum_exec_runtime);
    }
}

The counter p->rt.timeout is incremented once per jiffy in which the task was found running, so it measures time spent on the CPU without blocking — which is precisely the quantity that distinguishes a wedged RT thread from a busy one. Note the watchdog_stamp guard: it makes the increment idempotent within a jiffy, so a tick delivered twice cannot inflate the count. When the count passes the limit the function does not signal anything itself; it arms the POSIX CPU-timer machinery, which delivers the consequence from check_thread_timers(): SIGXCPU once per second at the soft limit, and SIGKILL at the hard limit. The soft limit is bumped by one second each time it fires, so a program that has installed a handler gets a repeating warning rather than a single one it might miss.

This makes RLIMIT_RTTIME the practical answer to the runaway-SCHED_FIFO problem on general-purpose kernels, and it is worth setting deliberately — via setrlimit(), ulimit -R, or a systemd unit's LimitRTTIME= — for any service that is granted real-time priority. Its one blind spot is that it only counts time when the task is observed by the tick, so it cannot help on a fully tickless CPU where the offending task is the only runnable one and the tick has been stopped.

Table 7-24. RT Bandwidth Knobs
KnobDefaultMeaning
/proc/sys/kernel/sched_rt_period_us1000000Length of one RT accounting period
/proc/sys/kernel/sched_rt_runtime_us950000Maximum RT runtime inside that period
/proc/sys/kernel/sched_rr_timeslice_ms100RR quantum length
CONFIG_RT_GROUP_SCHEDnBuild gate for the throttle; with it off the two knobs above affect only deadline admission control
rt_group_sched= (boot)1Runtime switch, since 6.16; 0 keeps RT group structures unbuilt on a kernel compiled with them
RLIMIT_RTTIMEunlimitedPer-task cap on uninterrupted RT running time: SIGXCPU at the soft limit, SIGKILL at the hard limit

Push/Pull Migration on SMP

On a single-CPU system, RT scheduling is trivially correct: the highest-priority runnable task runs, period. On SMP systems, the problem becomes much harder. Consider a 4-CPU machine where CPU 0 runs a priority-80 RT task and CPU 1 runs a priority-50 RT task. If a priority-70 task wakes up on CPU 1, it cannot preempt the priority-50 task locally (70 < 80 doesn't matter here — 70 > 50 means it should preempt). But if we only look locally, everything seems fine on CPU 0. The global invariant that RT scheduling must maintain on SMP is: the N highest-priority runnable RT tasks in the system must always be running on N CPUs. Any violation means a higher-priority task is waiting while a lower-priority task runs elsewhere — a priority inversion across CPUs.

The fair scheduler handles SMP load balancing through periodic rebalancing passes (every few milliseconds) and idle-CPU stealing. This lazy approach is acceptable because fair scheduling has no strict latency contracts. RT scheduling cannot afford such delay — a priority-99 task that waits even one tick because the scheduler hasn't rebalanced yet is a bug. Linux therefore uses event-driven push/pull migration that triggers immediately on state changes (enqueue, dequeue, priority change) rather than waiting for a periodic timer.

Push operates when a CPU becomes overloaded (more than one RT task runnable). It finds the second-highest-priority task on the overloaded CPU and migrates it to the CPU running the lowest-priority work in the system. Pull operates in the reverse direction: when a CPU's running priority drops (because its RT task blocked or exited), it scans for higher-priority RT tasks stuck on other overloaded CPUs and migrates the best one in. Both operations use the root domain's cpupri structure (rq->rd->cpupri) — an array indexed by priority, each entry holding the mask of CPUs currently running at that level — to find a migration target without scanning every CPU. The cost is a walk over the priority levels below the task's own, bounded by the 100 RT priorities and independent of the machine's CPU count, which is the property that matters as core counts grow. Because the structure hangs off the root domain rather than being global, an isolated cpuset partition gets its own, and RT balancing never considers CPUs outside it. Linux handles this with push and pull logic:

  • Push: if an RT runqueue is overloaded, move a movable RT task to a lower-priority destination CPU.
  • Pull: when a CPU becomes available or notices a priority inversion across CPUs, pull a better RT task in.
  • Pushable task tracking: only tasks allowed on more than one CPU and not currently running are candidates.
/* rt.c — push_rt_task(), simplified */
static int push_rt_task(struct rq *rq, bool pull)
{
    struct task_struct *next_task;
    struct rq *lowest_rq;

    if (!rq->rt.overloaded)
        return 0;

    next_task = pick_next_pushable_task(rq);
    if (!next_task)
        return 0;

    if (next_task->prio < rq->donor->prio) {
        resched_curr(rq);
        return 0;
    }

    lowest_rq = find_lock_lowest_rq(next_task, rq);
    if (!lowest_rq)
        return 0;

    move_queued_task_locked(rq, lowest_rq, next_task);
    return 1;
}

How Pull Actually Happens: RT_PUSH_IPI

Pull was described above as a CPU scanning other runqueues for a better task, and that code exists, but on a stock kernel it is not the path taken. The scan has a scaling problem that is worth stating precisely, because it is the sort that only appears at size. Suppose one CPU is overloaded with a migratable RT task, and a number of other CPUs simultaneously drop from RT work to fair work — the end of a periodic activation across a machine makes this entirely ordinary. Every one of those CPUs now wants that same task, so every one of them tries to take that one runqueue’s lock at the same instant. The comment in features.h calls this “a thundering herd attack,” and the contention grows with core count precisely on the machines that can least afford it.

The inversion is simple: rather than many CPUs reaching for one runqueue’s lock, send that CPU an interrupt and let it do the pushing itself, under its own lock, serially. That is RT_PUSH_IPI, a scheduler feature that is on by default wherever the architecture supports it — which includes both x86_64 and ARM64. pull_rt_task() therefore usually does no scanning at all:

/* rt.c — pull_rt_task(), the early exits */
    /* If we are the only overloaded CPU do nothing */
    if (rt_overload_count == 1 &&
        cpumask_test_cpu(this_rq->cpu, this_rq->rd->rto_mask))
        return;

#ifdef HAVE_RT_PUSH_IPI
    if (sched_feat(RT_PUSH_IPI)) {
        tell_cpu_to_push(this_rq);
        return;
    }
#endif
    /* ... otherwise fall back to scanning rd->rto_mask ... */

Which CPUs are worth interrupting is tracked in the root domain rather than discovered. rt_set_overload() and rt_clear_overload() maintain rd->rto_mask, the set of CPUs currently holding more RT tasks than they can run, alongside a count. Being per-root-domain rather than global means an isolated cpuset partition again gets its own, so RT balancing never sends an IPI across a partition boundary.

tell_cpu_to_push() then walks that mask through rto_next_cpu(), queuing an irq_work on each candidate in turn, so the interrupts form a chain rather than a broadcast: each target pushes what it can and hands the walk on. Two counters, rto_loop and rto_loop_next, let a CPU that joins the effort part-way through restart the sweep without holding a lock, so a newly overloaded CPU discovered late is not missed.

That restart is where 7.0 found a bug, and it is a good example of a liveness failure hiding inside a correctness mechanism. Consider one CPU that is overloaded and stays overloaded — say it holds a CPU-bound RT task, a second RT task, and a fair task stuck in the kernel. Other CPUs switching away from RT keep calling tell_cpu_to_push(), each bumping rto_loop_next. When the overloaded CPU finishes its own push and calls rto_next_cpu(), the mismatch between the two counters forces the sweep to restart from the beginning — and since it is itself still the only overloaded CPU, it selects itself, queues irq_work to itself, and sends itself an IPI. It is still overloaded when that arrives, so it does it again. The machine hard-locks under a storm of self-interrupts. Commit 94894c9c477e (7.0-rc1) fixed it with three lines — skip the current CPU when walking the mask — on the reasoning that a CPU never needs an interrupt to tell it to examine its own runqueue.

The fallback scan is still worth reading for one detail, since it is what runs if the feature is turned off. Before taking any remote lock it peeks at src_rq->rt.highest_prio.next and skips the CPU if that value is no better than the local highest_prio.curr. The comment concedes the read is racy and explains why it does not matter: if the remote value is about to improve, that CPU will push the task to us anyway, and if it is about to worsen, we did not want it. A deliberately unsynchronised read used to avoid a lock, correct because both ways of being wrong are self-correcting, is a pattern worth recognising — it recurs throughout the balancer.

Where a Real-Time Task Fits: Asymmetric CPUs

Everything described so far treats CPUs as interchangeable, and on a symmetric machine they are. On the other architecture this book follows they frequently are not. An ARM64 SoC with a DynamIQ or big.LITTLE topology has cores whose peak throughput differs by a factor of two or three, and “the CPU running the lowest-priority work” may be a little core that cannot execute the task fast enough to meet its timing requirement. Priority alone cannot express that, so the RT class carries a second, orthogonal notion: whether a task fits.

/* rt.c — does this CPU satisfy the task's capacity request? */
static inline bool rt_task_fits_capacity(struct task_struct *p, int cpu)
{
    unsigned int min_cap, max_cap, cpu_cap;

    /* Only heterogeneous systems can benefit from this check */
    if (!sched_asym_cpucap_active())
        return true;

    min_cap = uclamp_eff_value(p, UCLAMP_MIN);
    max_cap = uclamp_eff_value(p, UCLAMP_MAX);
    cpu_cap = arch_scale_cpu_capacity(cpu);

    return cpu_cap >= min(min_cap, max_cap);
}

The interesting move here is the reuse of uclamp_min. For a fair-class task, a utilisation clamp is a hint to the frequency governor: run this task at no less than this much of a CPU’s capacity. For an RT task the same number is reinterpreted as a placement constraint — a CPU whose absolute capacity is below the request is not merely slow, it is not a candidate. One tunable therefore means “raise the clock” in one class and “choose a bigger core” in another, and no new interface had to be invented for the second meaning. The min(min_cap, max_cap) guards the case where an administrator has set uclamp_max below uclamp_min; the ceiling wins, since a task capped at low utilisation has no business demanding a big core.

The first line is what makes this free on the machines that do not need it. sched_asym_cpucap_active() is a static key, set only when the topology code has detected differing capacities. On a typical x86_64 server every call returns true at essentially zero cost and the rest of the function is never reached, so the fitness machinery imposes nothing on symmetric systems. It also compiles away entirely without CONFIG_UCLAMP_TASK, where the whole function is a stub returning true.

Fitness enters the balancer at two points. find_lowest_rq() chooses between two search routines depending on the topology — cpupri_find_fitness() with the predicate above on asymmetric systems, plain cpupri_find() otherwise — so on heterogeneous hardware the mask of candidate CPUs is filtered by capacity before affinity and topology are considered at all. And select_task_rq_rt() will start a migration search for a waking task that does not fit where it woke, even when there is no priority reason to move it:

/* rt.c — select_task_rq_rt(), the capacity clause */
if (test || !rt_task_fits_capacity(p, cpu)) {
    int target = find_lowest_rq(p);

    /* Bail out if we were forcing a migration to find a better
       fitting CPU but our search failed. */
    if (!test && target != -1 && !rt_task_fits_capacity(p, target))
        goto out_unlock;
    ...
}

The bail-out is the careful part. If the only reason for considering a move was capacity, and the best CPU the search could offer does not fit either, the task stays where it is. Migrating from one unsuitable core to another unsuitable core would cost a cache-cold restart and buy nothing — so the code distinguishes “I found somewhere better” from “I found somewhere else,” and only acts on the first. This is characteristic of the RT balancer generally: it is willing to migrate aggressively, but only when it can name the improvement.

The important subtlety is that migration decisions compare against rq->donor, not just the physically executing task, because scheduling priority is about the effective scheduling context. RT balancing is therefore tightly coupled to the same donor abstraction discussed earlier.

RT Group Scheduling and Bandwidth Partitioning

The global RT bandwidth throttle (950ms/1000ms) protects the system as a whole, but it does not protect RT tasks from each other across organizational boundaries. Consider a container hosting a real-time audio pipeline alongside another container running a priority-99 industrial control thread. Without further isolation, the control thread can consume the entire 950ms RT budget, leaving zero RT time for the audio container — even though both containers were promised "up to 50% of RT bandwidth" by the platform administrator.

RT group scheduling (CONFIG_RT_GROUP_SCHED) solves this by partitioning the RT budget hierarchically among cgroups. Each task group receives its own per-CPU rt_rq and an independent rt_bandwidth allocation, configured through the cgroup v1 files cpu.rt_runtime_us and cpu.rt_period_us. The bandwidth is enforced per-group: when a group's RT tasks have consumed their allocated runtime within the period, the entire group is throttled, even if the system-wide RT budget has not yet been exhausted. Tasks in other groups can continue running unaffected.

There is no cgroup v2 equivalent. The v2 cpu controller implements weights and a bandwidth limit for the fair class only; cpu.max has no RT component, and the documentation states plainly that v2 does not yet support bandwidth control of realtime processes. The interaction is worse than a missing feature. On a kernel built with CONFIG_RT_GROUP_SCHED=y, the cgroup v2 cpu controller can only be enabled at all if every RT task is sitting in the root cgroup — and since system management software routinely places real-time threads into service cgroups during boot, enabling the option can leave an administrator unable to use the v2 CPU controller until those threads are moved back.

That conflict is why the option is default n and why, in 6.16, Michal Koutný's series converted the feature's #ifdef blocks into ordinary code guarded by a static key. A kernel may now be built with RT group scheduling and still run without it: rt_group_sched=0 on the kernel command line, or a build with CONFIG_RT_GROUP_SCHED_DEFAULT_DISABLED=y, causes the kernel to skip creating the per-group rt_rq structures entirely and to redirect every RT operation to the root task group. Distributions can therefore ship one kernel that satisfies both audiences instead of choosing between them. The transition was not entirely smooth — as late as 7.0.10 a stable fix was needed because the group-schedulability check still ran with rt_group_sched=0 and wrongly rejected writes to the global sched_rt_runtime_us.

Crucially, the fixed-priority semantics remain unchanged within a group. Inside its bandwidth envelope, a group's tasks still obey strict priority ordering, immediate preemption, and FIFO/RR policies. The group boundary only adds a bandwidth ceiling — it does not alter the fundamental RT scheduling model. In effect, Linux composes two orthogonal guarantees:

  • inside a group: static-priority RT semantics,
  • across groups: bandwidth caps that preserve system-wide liveness.

Priority Inheritance and rt_mutex

Fixed-priority scheduling guarantees that the highest-priority runnable task always gets the CPU. But what happens when that task cannot run — not because a higher-priority task preempts it, but because a lower-priority task holds a lock it needs? This is the classic priority inversion problem, first identified in the 1970s and famously responsible for the Mars Pathfinder mission anomaly in 1997. Without mitigation, priority inversion can make RT guarantees completely meaningless: a priority-99 task blocked on a mutex held by a priority-10 task can be delayed indefinitely if priority-50 tasks keep preempting the owner.

Linux solves this with priority inheritance (PI), implemented through the rt_mutex infrastructure. The idea is simple: when a high-priority task blocks on an rt_mutex, the current owner of that mutex temporarily inherits the waiter's priority. This boost propagates transitively through lock chains — if task A waits on a mutex held by B, and B waits on a mutex held by C, then C inherits A's priority through the chain. The boosted owner runs at the elevated priority until it releases the mutex, at which point its effective priority reverts to its base level (or to the next-highest waiter's priority if other PI waiters exist).

The scheduler integration is tight. The task's p->prio field reflects the effective priority (which may be boosted), not the base priority. When PI boosts a task, the scheduler moves it to the appropriate position in the RT bitmap/list structure — the boosted task literally appears as a high-priority RT task to the selection algorithm. The p->pi_lock spinlock serializes all PI state transitions, ensuring that no concurrent waker or scheduler path can observe a task's priority in a half-updated state. The pi_waiters tree (an RB-tree ordered by priority) tracks all tasks waiting on mutexes held by a given owner, allowing O(log n) determination of the highest inherited priority.

Table 7-25. Priority-Inheritance Effects on Scheduling State
StateWithout PIWith PI
Low-priority mutex owner blocks RT waiterOwner may not run soon enoughOwner is boosted toward waiter's priority
Scheduler-visible priorityStays lowp->prio becomes boosted
Runqueue orderingCan suffer inversionBoosted task moves as if it were urgent

The wakeup path's use of p->pi_lock is part of this story. That lock serializes state changes and PI updates so a task's effective scheduling priority cannot be observed half-updated by a concurrent waker.

In PREEMPT_RT kernels, PI matters even more because many ordinary-looking spinlocks become sleeping RT mutexes. The boundary between “locking semantics” and “scheduler semantics” becomes much thinner.

Real-time scheduling in Linux is therefore best understood as a bundle of mechanisms, not just a priority queue: bitmap-based selection, immediate preemption, optional round-robin peer fairness, two independent runaway defences (class-wide bandwidth throttling where it is compiled in, and the per-task RLIMIT_RTTIME watchdog everywhere), push/pull balancing, cgroup bandwidth control, and priority inheritance all cooperate to make fixed-priority semantics usable on a large general-purpose kernel. Knowing which of those are active on a given system is as much a part of understanding RT scheduling as knowing how the bitmap works.

Deadline Scheduling (SCHED_DEADLINE)

Neither SCHED_FIFO nor SCHED_RR can guarantee that a task meets a specific timing obligation. Fixed-priority scheduling gives the designer a priority ordering, but proving that deadlines are met requires complex off-line analysis (Rate-Monotonic or Deadline-Monotonic tests) and brittle assumptions about worst-case execution times. The SCHED_DEADLINE class takes a fundamentally different approach: each task explicitly declares its timing requirements — how much CPU it needs, how often it activates, and by when it must finish — and the kernel either admits the reservation or rejects it at setup time. Once admitted, the scheduler enforces the contract at runtime without any priority assignment at all.

The deadline class is Linux's implementation of the classic real-time result that Earliest Deadline First (EDF) is optimal on a uniprocessor for independent preemptible jobs: if any schedule can meet all deadlines, the EDF schedule can too. Linux does not expose raw EDF directly, however. It wraps EDF in a Constant Bandwidth Server (CBS), adds per-root-domain admission control, and drives replenishment with high-resolution timers so that a task cannot consume more bandwidth than it reserved. The CBS layer is what makes EDF safe for general-purpose use: it isolates tasks from one another so that an overrunning task is throttled to its reserved fraction rather than stealing time from peers.

Beyond user-facing deadline tasks, kernel 7.0.12 also uses the deadline infrastructure internally as DL servers — lightweight CBS instances attached to other scheduling classes. This is no longer a possibility the code merely allows for: sched_init_dl_servers() gives every online CPU a fair_server reservation of 50 ms in every 1000 ms at boot, and a kernel built with CONFIG_SCHED_CLASS_EXT gets a second ext_server beside it. A DL server wraps another class's runqueue to provide bandwidth guarantees to its tasks without requiring any of them to switch to SCHED_DEADLINE policy. The server mechanism (dl_server_start(), dl_server_stop(), dl_server_timer()) reuses the same replenishment and throttle logic, demonstrating the generality of the CBS abstraction.

The kernel also implements GRUB (Greedy Reclamation of Unused Bandwidth), an extension that lets deadline tasks reclaim bandwidth left unused by throttled or sleeping peers. Under GRUB accounting, runtime decreases not by wall-clock time but by the fraction of active bandwidth on the CPU, allowing tasks to stretch their execution when the processor would otherwise be idle. This improves throughput without violating admission guarantees. GRUB is opt-in per task: only a reservation created with the SCHED_FLAG_RECLAIM flag is charged through grub_reclaim(), and a task that does not set it is billed for wall-clock time as usual.

In Linux 7.0.12, most of the policy lives in kernel/sched/deadline.c. The file header lays out the model, the enqueue and dequeue paths keep the runqueue ordered by absolute deadline, dl_task_timer() handles replenishment and reactivation, and SMP helpers such as push_dl_task() and pull_dl_task() preserve global EDF semantics as far as affinity masks and topology allow. Admission control (sched_dl_overflow()) enforces a per-root-domain bandwidth ceiling, rejecting reservations that would make the system unschedulable. That ceiling is lower than most readers expect: it is not one unit of utilisation per CPU but sched_rt_runtime_us / sched_rt_period_us of it — 95 % by default — which is the connection back to the RT bandwidth knobs discussed in the previous section.

Two pressures shape everything the deadline class does:

  • A promise must be checkable before it is made. Fixed priorities can always be assigned; a bandwidth reservation cannot always be honoured. The class therefore needs an admission test, and that test needs an accounting domain that reflects which CPUs can actually run the task.
  • A promise to one task is a constraint on every other. EDF has no priorities to fall back on, so a task that overruns its declared budget does not merely hurt itself — it invalidates the schedulability argument for the whole set. Every deadline task must therefore be policed, continuously, by something that cannot be talked out of it.

The rest of this section works through seven questions:

  1. What exactly does a task declare, and which combinations does the kernel refuse?
  2. How does a relative deadline become the absolute deadline that EDF actually orders by?
  3. What does the deadline runqueue hold besides the task tree?
  4. What does CBS do when a task overruns, sleeps early, or wakes up late?
  5. Who un-throttles a task, and how precisely?
  6. What is the admission ceiling, and what is it measured against on an asymmetric machine?
  7. How is the same machinery reused to give non-deadline work a latency guarantee?

Version history. Everything. SCHED_DEADLINE did not exist in 2.6.11 — it was merged in 3.14, GRUB reclamation followed in 4.13, and the DL-server work that now underpins fair-class latency landed across 6.12 and 6.13. Between 6.12 and 7.0.12 alone, kernel/sched/deadline.c absorbed roughly seventy commits, the large majority of them refining server behaviour rather than task behaviour. This is the most actively changing scheduling class in the kernel, and the parts of it a reader is most likely to meet in practice — the per-CPU fair server and its deferred activation — are also the newest.

Task Parameters

Unlike SCHED_FIFO or SCHED_RR, where the application sets a single integer priority and hopes the system designer got the assignments right, a SCHED_DEADLINE task describes what it needs rather than how important it is. The interface is sched_setattr() with three timing values that together define a sporadic server reservation: how much CPU the task requires per activation, the tightest timing bound it must meet, and the minimum gap between successive activations. This trio gives the kernel enough information to make a provable schedulability decision before the task ever runs.

A SCHED_DEADLINE task declares three timing parameters through sched_setattr(). They describe a sporadic task model: every activation needs at most runtime units of CPU service, arrives no more often than once every period, and must finish by its relative deadline.

Table 7-26. SCHED_DEADLINE Parameters
ParameterMeaningConstraint
runtimeCPU time reserved for each activation (budget)runtime ≤ deadline
deadlineRelative deadline measured from release timedeadline ≤ period
periodMinimum inter-arrival time between activationsperiod ≥ deadline

Those constraints are not just documentation. Linux validates them in __checkparam_dl() because the CBS accounting rules assume a feasible reservation shape. A task that asked for runtime > deadline would demand more than one full CPU before its own deadline. A task with deadline > period would allow overlapping jobs, which would break the one-job-at-a-time server model used by the class. The kernel also enforces minimum and maximum period limits — configurable via sched_deadline_period_min_us (default 100 µs) and sched_deadline_period_max_us (default ~4 seconds) — to prevent both timer DoS from excessively tight periods and impractically long reservations. If the application omits period (sets it to zero), the kernel treats deadline as the implicit period, which is the common case for tasks whose deadline and inter-arrival interval are the same.

Joining the class also imposes obligations that have no equivalent in the other policies, and that catch out most people writing their first deadline application:

  • A deadline task cannot fork. sched_fork() returns -EAGAIN outright if dl_prio(p->prio) holds, so fork() and pthread_create() both fail from inside a SCHED_DEADLINE thread. The reason is admission control: the child would silently duplicate the parent's reservation, and there is no obvious right answer for how to split it. Deadline applications must therefore create all their threads first and switch each one into the class afterwards.
  • sched_yield() forfeits the rest of the budget, not just the rest of the slice. yield_task_dl() sets dl_yielded = 1 and drives the runtime to zero, which throttles the task until its next replenishment. This is the documented way for a job that finished early to give back the CPU without distorting its own deadline, but it is a far heavier operation than a fair-class yield.
  • Overruns can be reported. A reservation created with SCHED_FLAG_DL_OVERRUN sets dl_overrun = 1 when it exhausts its runtime; check_dl_overrun(), called from the POSIX CPU-timer path, then delivers SIGXCPU to the thread group. Without that flag an overrun is silent — the task is simply throttled — which is why a deadline task that mysteriously stalls for most of every period is usually one whose declared runtime is too small.
  • Bandwidth reclaiming is opt-in. SCHED_FLAG_RECLAIM enrols the task in GRUB, described later in this section.

From Relative Deadlines to Absolute Deadlines

The three parameters a task declares — runtime, deadline, period — are all relative values: durations measured from the start of each activation. But EDF does not schedule by duration; it schedules by urgency, and urgency is determined by when a job must finish in wall-clock time. The scheduler must therefore convert relative deadlines into absolute deadlines each time a new job is released. This conversion is what makes EDF dynamic: two tasks with identical relative deadlines can have very different absolute deadlines depending on when they wake up, so the winner of the CPU changes from moment to moment rather than being fixed at configuration time like a priority.

EDF itself orders runnable jobs by absolute deadline, not by the relative deadline configured from user space. When a new job is released at time t, the scheduler computes:

/* conceptual release-time bookkeeping */
release_time      = now;
abs_deadline      = release_time + dl_deadline;
remaining_runtime = dl_runtime;
next_period       = release_time + dl_period;

If a multimedia task reserves runtime = 3 ms, deadline = 10 ms, and period = 10 ms, then a job released at 100 ms receives an absolute deadline of 110 ms. The next release at 110 ms gets 120 ms, and so on. The deadline runqueue is an rb-tree keyed by this absolute value, so the leftmost node is always the job that must run next.

This is why EDF reacts so quickly to new urgent work. A late-arriving job with a nearer absolute deadline is inserted to the left of currently queued jobs and can preempt immediately, even if those older jobs have already consumed part of their budgets.

Comparing absolute deadlines looks trivial but is not written as a < b anywhere in the class. Every comparison goes through a one-line helper:

/* include/linux/sched/deadline.h */
static inline bool dl_time_before(u64 a, u64 b)
{
    return (s64)(a - b) < 0;
}

The subtraction is done first and the difference is interpreted as signed. This is the same idiom the kernel uses for jiffies comparisons, and it makes the test depend only on the distance between the two instants rather than on their absolute magnitudes, so the ordering stays correct across a counter wrap. It also documents intent: dl_time_before() asks “is a earlier than b?”, a question that is meaningful for two points on the same clock and meaningless for two arbitrary integers. The same helper is reused throughout the class for tests that have nothing to do with ordering the tree — whether a deadline has already passed, whether the zero-laxity instant has been reached, whether a replenishment is overdue.

The comparison a scheduler actually asks is one level up:

/* sched/sched.h — tells if entity @a should preempt entity @b */
static inline bool dl_entity_preempt(const struct sched_dl_entity *a,
                                     const struct sched_dl_entity *b)
{
    return dl_entity_is_special(a) ||
           dl_time_before(a->deadline, b->deadline);
}

That leading disjunct is a genuine exception to EDF, and it is worth knowing about because it is running on most desktops. dl_entity_is_special() tests for SCHED_FLAG_SUGOV, an internal flag (0x10000000) that does not appear in the uapi headers and that __sched_setscheduler() rejects with -EINVAL when it arrives from user space. It marks the kernel threads the schedutil cpufreq governor uses to write frequency changes on hardware where that write can sleep. Those threads are made SCHED_DEADLINE so they outrank ordinary real-time work, but they are given deliberately fake parameters — the source comments them as “Fake (unused) bandwidth; workaround to ‘fix’ priority inheritance” — and are then exempted from nearly everything else in this section: __checkparam_dl() returns success without validating them, sched_dl_overflow() returns before charging them to admission control, and dl_task_check_affinity() lets them be pinned freely. A special entity always preempts, never runs out of budget, and costs nothing against the root domain sum.

The asymmetry of the test is deliberate: only a is checked for specialness, so a special entity preempts everything while nothing gains the right to preempt it by that route. If a frequency change could be delayed by the very deadline tasks whose utilisation triggered it, the governor would be unable to raise the clock exactly when the machine most needs it.

Runqueue Ordering and Class Hooks

The fair and RT classes each have their own per-CPU runqueue structure, and the deadline class is no different. But while the RT class uses a priority-indexed array for O(1) lookup and the fair class uses an rb-tree keyed by virtual runtime, the deadline class keys its rb-tree by absolute deadline. This single ordering principle makes the class conceptually simpler than CFS: there is no weight algebra, no virtual time, and no lag calculation. The task with the earliest wall-clock deadline is always the correct choice, and the rb-tree's cached leftmost pointer delivers it in O(1).

Each CPU owns a dl_rq (defined in kernel/sched/sched.h). It contains more than just the task tree — the structure also tracks bandwidth accounting fields that feed both CBS throttling and GRUB reclamation:

  • an rb_root_cached rb-tree sorted by absolute deadline (the cached leftmost is the next task to run),
  • an earliest_dl cache holding the deadlines of the current and next-earliest tasks — used by SMP migration to decide whether this CPU has excess urgency to push away,
  • running_bw and this_bw for active and assigned utilization tracking (feeding CBS and GRUB),
  • a second rb-tree (pushable_dl_tasks_root) of migratable tasks ordered by deadline for push/pull, and
  • an overloaded flag that signals when more than one runnable deadline task exists, triggering the push path.

enqueue_task_dl() inserts a runnable entity into the rb-tree, updates the earliest-deadline cache, and makes the task visible to SMP push/pull logic if the task may migrate. dequeue_task_dl() does the inverse when the task blocks, exits, or is throttled. The operations are short because the class relies on timer callbacks and admission logic elsewhere; the enqueue path mostly maintains order, while the timer path decides when an exhausted task is allowed to reenter.

Inside the class, preemption is almost mechanical: if the task being enqueued has an earlier absolute deadline than rq->donor, wakeup_preempt_dl() requests a reschedule. This is EDF in its purest form. Unlike fair scheduling, there is no weight comparison and no notion of slice aging. The only questions are: which job has the earliest deadline, and is that job still allowed to spend runtime?

Constant Bandwidth Server (CBS) Rules

EDF's optimality proof assumes that every task runs for exactly its declared worst-case execution time and arrives exactly at its declared period. Real applications violate both assumptions routinely: a video decoder may finish a frame early and sleep, a sensor reader may be delayed by I/O, and a buggy task may try to consume more CPU than it reserved. Without a bandwidth isolation layer, any of these deviations would corrupt the EDF ordering for all other tasks on the runqueue. The Constant Bandwidth Server (CBS) is the theoretical framework that solves this: it wraps each task in a virtual server that consumes exactly its reserved fraction of the CPU regardless of actual behavior, throttling overrunners and adjusting deadlines for early sleepers.

Plain EDF alone is not enough on a general-purpose kernel because an application could wake up irregularly, sleep with unused budget, or overrun and then interfere with other tasks. Linux therefore wraps each deadline task in a CBS. The server guarantees that, over the long run, the task receives at most runtime / period fraction of one CPU. This guarantee is what makes admission control meaningful — without CBS enforcement, an admitted set of tasks could still miss deadlines due to runtime behavior that violates the static assumptions.

The CBS policy has two key rules:

  1. When runtime reaches zero, the task is throttled immediately. It is dequeued from the active deadline runqueue, and a replenishment timer is armed for the next eligible instant.
  2. When a task wakes with stale parameters, Linux may push its deadline forward and restore its budget before letting it compete again. The kernel compares the remaining runtime with the time left until the current deadline. If the old pair would imply a density higher than the reservation, the server starts a fresh period.

The second rule is the essence of CBS. Imagine a task that sleeps for most of its period, wakes just before its current deadline, and still has nearly its full budget left. If Linux let it run under that old deadline, the task would suddenly look far more urgent than its reserved bandwidth justifies. CBS prevents this by postponing the deadline and replenishing runtime, which restores the original utilization ratio and protects peer tasks.

/* conceptual CBS wakeup rule */
time_left = abs_deadline - now;

if (remaining_runtime <= 0)
    replenish_budget_and_push_deadline();
else if (time_left <= 0)
    replenish_budget_and_push_deadline();
else if (remaining_runtime / time_left > dl_runtime / dl_period)
    replenish_budget_and_push_deadline();
else
    keep_current_budget_and_deadline();

That inequality is the practical test for “would this task, if run now, exceed its reserved bandwidth?” The kernel writes it as dl_entity_overflow(), and—exactly as in the eligibility test of the fair class—it does not perform the division. Dividing two nanosecond quantities would either lose precision or require a 64-bit divide on every wakeup, so the comparison is cross-multiplied instead:

/* deadline.c: dl_entity_overflow(), the test itself */
left  = (pi_of(dl_se)->dl_deadline >> DL_SCALE) * (dl_se->runtime >> DL_SCALE);
right = ((dl_se->deadline - t) >> DL_SCALE) * (pi_of(dl_se)->dl_runtime >> DL_SCALE);

return dl_time_before(right, left);

Two operands are relative (the configured dl_deadline and dl_runtime) and two are derived from the present instant (the runtime left in this job, and the time left until the absolute deadline), but the products being compared are pure durations-squared, so the cross-multiplication is dimensionally honest. Each operand is first shifted right by DL_SCALE, which is 10: the source comment explains that this reduces the granularity of the test to one microsecond and, in exchange, removes any risk that the multiplication overflows a u64. Since the result is only ever used as a yes/no answer, the lost precision costs nothing — and, as the comment cheerfully admits, “thinking of anything below microseconds resolution is actually fiction (but still we want to give the user that illusion >;)”. Note also pi_of(dl_se) on the two configured parameters: a PI-boosted task is measured against the donor's reservation, for the reasons set out later in this section.

The function that consults this test is update_dl_entity(), called from the enqueue path. It is the true home of CBS rule 2, and it has one more branch than the sketch above suggests.

Implicit and Constrained Deadlines

Table 7-26 recorded the constraint deadline ≤ period without dwelling on the difference between the two cases it allows, but the kernel treats them as different animals and gives them different names. A task whose relative deadline equals its period has an implicit deadline; one whose deadline is strictly shorter than its period has a constrained deadline. The predicate is a one-liner:

/* deadline.c */
static inline bool dl_is_implicit(struct sched_dl_entity *dl_se)
{
        return dl_se->dl_deadline == dl_se->dl_period;
}

Constrained deadlines are the natural way to express a job that arrives every 10 ms but must be finished within 4 ms of arriving — a control loop whose output is sampled early in the cycle, or a decoder that must hand a frame to the compositor well before the next vsync. They are supported, but they are the reason the CBS rules described so far are not the whole story, because the original CBS wakeup rule is unsafe for them.

The problem is a mismatch between two ratios. Admission control charged the task runtime / period. The original CBS rule, when it detects an overflow, replenishes the budget and sets a fresh absolute deadline one relative deadline away — which permits the task to consume runtime in the next deadline units of time, a rate of runtime / deadline. That second ratio is what the CBS literature calls the task's density, and for a constrained-deadline task it is strictly larger than the bandwidth the admission test reserved. A task with runtime = 4 ms, deadline = 4 ms, period = 10 ms was admitted at 40 % but could, by waking at just the right moments, extract 100 %. The kernel comment is blunt about the consequence: “In a very loaded system, this can cause a domino effect, making other tasks miss their deadlines.”

Linux closes the hole in two places, and it is worth separating them because they handle different moments in the period.

The first is the Revisited CBS wakeup rule, taken from a 2015 paper by Abeni, Lipari and Lelli that revisited the original 1998 CBS for exactly this case. When a constrained-deadline task wakes with an overflowing budget but a deadline that is still in the future, the kernel does not replenish and push the deadline out. It leaves the deadline alone and instead reduces the remaining runtime to precisely the amount the task's declared density permits over the laxity still available:

/* deadline.c: update_dl_revised_wakeup() */
u64 laxity = dl_se->deadline - rq_clock(rq);

dl_se->runtime = (dl_se->dl_density * laxity) >> BW_SHIFT;

This is why struct sched_dl_entity carries a field the earlier discussion had no use for: dl_density, computed once at sched_setattr() time as to_ratio(dl_deadline, dl_runtime) and stored in BW_SHIFT fixed point. Having the ratio precomputed turns the rule into a multiply and a shift on the wakeup path. For an implicit-deadline task dl_density equals the ordinary bandwidth and the rule would be a no-op, which is precisely why it is only reached when dl_is_implicit() is false. The effect is that the task keeps its urgency but loses the surplus budget, so it can never run at more than the rate it was admitted at.

The second place is the gap between the deadline and the next period, which is a region that only exists for constrained-deadline tasks. If such a task wakes at 6 ms into a 10 ms period, its 4 ms deadline has already passed but its next release is still 4 ms away. The revised rule cannot help here — the laxity is negative — so the task is simply parked until the period boundary:

/* deadline.c: dl_check_constrained_dl() */
if (dl_time_before(dl_se->deadline, rq_clock(rq)) &&
    dl_time_before(rq_clock(rq), dl_next_period(dl_se))) {
        if (unlikely(is_dl_boosted(dl_se) || !start_dl_timer(dl_se)))
                return;
        dl_se->dl_throttled = 1;
        if (dl_se->runtime > 0)
                dl_se->runtime = 0;
}

The helper it uses to find that boundary is a reminder that the entity stores an absolute deadline but only relative parameters: dl_next_period() is deadline - dl_deadline + dl_period, that is, it recovers the release instant from the deadline and then adds a period. The call site, in enqueue_task_dl(), is guarded by !dl_se->dl_throttled && !dl_is_implicit(dl_se), so implicit-deadline tasks — the overwhelming majority — never execute any of this. And the is_dl_boosted() escape is the same principle seen in the replenishment timer: a task holding a mutex that someone else is waiting on must be allowed to finish its critical section, whatever its own reservation says.

The practical advice that falls out of all this is worth stating plainly. Setting deadline < period buys real expressiveness — it is the only way to tell the kernel “I need this early in the cycle, not merely once per cycle” — but it opts the task into a second, stricter rule set in which a wakeup can silently shrink the budget, and in which waking during the deadline-to-period gap costs the task the remainder of its period. Applications that genuinely need only one activation per period should leave deadline equal to period (or omit it), and get the simpler behaviour.

There is a third CBS rule that the two above do not cover, and it is the one that most often surprises readers of the code: when a deadline task blocks, its bandwidth is not released immediately. The runqueue field running_bw is the sum of the utilisations of the tasks currently considered active on that CPU, and it is what GRUB reclamation divides up. If a task's contribution were subtracted the instant it went to sleep, a task that alternates between short bursts and short sleeps could let its peers reclaim bandwidth that it is about to demand back, and the reclaiming tasks would then be holding CPU time the sleeper is still entitled to.

Linux solves this with the 0-lag time: the instant at which a sleeping task has, in the fluid-scheduling sense, finally used up everything its reservation entitled it to for the current period. Until that moment the task is non-contending — blocked, but still counted. task_non_contending() computes the instant as

/* deadline.c: task_non_contending(), 0-lag computation */
zerolag_time = dl_se->deadline -
        div64_long((dl_se->runtime * dl_se->dl_period), dl_se->dl_runtime);

and then either subtracts the bandwidth at once (if that instant has already passed) or sets dl_non_contending = 1 and arms a second per-entity timer, inactive_task_timer, to do it later. This is why struct sched_dl_entity carries two hrtimers rather than one: dl_timer puts a throttled task back, and inactive_timer takes a sleeping task's bandwidth away. If the task wakes before the 0-lag instant, the timer is simply cancelled and nothing was ever given up.

The two directions are the small helpers add_running_bw() and sub_running_bw(), and the pairing is deliberately not symmetrical with waking and sleeping. Bandwidth is added when a task becomes contending — at wakeup, or when it first joins the class — but subtracted only at the 0-lag instant, which may be long after the task blocked and may never arrive at all if it wakes up first. A task can therefore be blocked and still counted in running_bw, which is the single fact that makes the field's name misleading and the reason the kernel spells the state dl_non_contending rather than sleeping.

Coming back is the job of task_contending(), and it is not simply the inverse. It has to distinguish which of two states the task is waking from:

/* deadline.c: task_contending() */
if (dl_se->dl_runtime == 0)          /* boosted non-DL task: nothing to do */
        return;

if (flags & ENQUEUE_MIGRATED)
        add_rq_bw(dl_se, dl_rq);

if (dl_se->dl_non_contending) {
        dl_se->dl_non_contending = 0;
        cancel_inactive_timer(dl_se);    /* woke before 0-lag: nothing was given up */
} else {
        add_running_bw(dl_se, dl_rq);    /* 0-lag already passed: put it back */
}

If the task wakes before its 0-lag instant it was never removed from running_bw, so the only work is to clear the flag and cancel the timer; adding the bandwidth again would double-count it. If the 0-lag instant has already passed, the timer has run, the bandwidth is gone, and it must be added back. Getting this pair wrong in either direction is the classic running_bw bug, and the kernel guards the enqueue paths with WARN_ONs on the sum for precisely that reason.

The comment attached to cancel_inactive_timer() is worth reading carefully, because the same three sentences appear twice in the file. Cancelling an hrtimer can fail if the callback is already executing on another CPU, and the code does not retry. It does not need to: inactive_task_timer() re-checks dl_non_contending after taking the runqueue lock, sees that the waker has already cleared it, and returns without touching the sum. The cancellation is best-effort by design, with the flag rather than the timer acting as the authority.

This is also the point at which the distinction between the two dl_rq bandwidth fields becomes visible. running_bw follows contention, as described above; this_bw is the sum of the utilisations assigned to the CPU, changed only by add_rq_bw() and sub_rq_bw() when a task arrives on or leaves the runqueue altogether. That is why ENQUEUE_MIGRATED gets its own line at the top of the function: a task that woke up on a different CPU must have its assignment credited here as well as its contention. The relationship between the two is asserted directly in the accessors — WARN_ON_ONCE(dl_rq->running_bw > dl_rq->this_bw) appears on both the add and the subtract path — which states the invariant more clearly than any prose could: the bandwidth actively contending on a CPU can never exceed the bandwidth assigned to it.

Migration while non-contending is the awkward case, and migrate_task_rq_dl() handles it by giving up early:

/* deadline.c: migrate_task_rq_dl(), called under p->pi_lock with state == TASK_WAKING */
rq_lock(rq, &rf);
if (p->dl.dl_non_contending) {
        update_rq_clock(rq);
        sub_running_bw(&p->dl, &rq->dl);
        p->dl.dl_non_contending = 0;
        cancel_inactive_timer(&p->dl);
}
sub_rq_bw(&p->dl, &rq->dl);
rq_unlock(rq, &rf);

The task is leaving this CPU, so its residual claim on the old runqueue is written off at once rather than waiting for the 0-lag instant that will now never be reached there. Both sums are decremented on the source and both will be incremented on the destination, the second of them by the ENQUEUE_MIGRATED branch of task_contending(). The locking comment explains the one oddity in the fragment: the caller is set_task_cpu() from inside try_to_wake_up(), which holds p->pi_lock but has deliberately dropped the runqueue lock, so this function must take it back for the duration of the accounting.

With running_bw and this_bw maintained correctly, GRUB reclamation becomes a short calculation. Every charge to a deadline entity passes through dl_scaled_delta_exec(), which decides how much of the elapsed nanoseconds to bill:

/* deadline.c: dl_scaled_delta_exec() */
if (unlikely(dl_se->flags & SCHED_FLAG_RECLAIM)) {
    scaled_delta_exec = grub_reclaim(delta_exec, rq, dl_se);
} else {
    scaled_delta_exec = cap_scale(delta_exec, arch_scale_freq_capacity(cpu));
    scaled_delta_exec = cap_scale(scaled_delta_exec, arch_scale_cpu_capacity(cpu));
}

For a GRUB task, grub_reclaim() bills the elapsed time scaled by the active utilisation rather than in full, so a task running on a CPU whose peers are idle burns its budget more slowly and gets to keep the CPU longer. Linux implements the GRUB-PA variant, in which the reclaimed headroom is also offered to the frequency governor rather than being spent purely on extra execution.

The else branch matters just as much, and applies to every deadline task whether or not it opted into GRUB: elapsed time is scaled by the CPU's current frequency and by its capacity relative to the largest core in the system. On a symmetric x86_64 machine at full frequency both factors are 1 and the branch is a no-op. On an ARM64 big.LITTLE SoC it is not. A reservation running on a little core of capacity 400 is charged roughly 400/1024 of the wall-clock time it actually consumed — which is the correct behaviour, because the reservation was admitted against a capacity-scaled budget in the first place, but it means that a deadline task's runtime is denominated in units of the biggest core, not in wall-clock nanoseconds. A 3 ms reservation buys about 3 ms of work on a big core and rather more wall-clock time, doing the same amount of work, on a little one.

Replenishment and dl_task_timer()

CBS throttling would be useless without a mechanism to undo it. When a task exhausts its runtime budget the kernel removes it from the runqueue, but the task is not dead — it has simply consumed its current period's allocation and must wait for the next period before it may compete again. The question is: who wakes it up, and when? In a tick-driven scheduler the answer would be "at the next periodic tick that falls after the period boundary," but that introduces jitter proportional to the tick interval and fails entirely on tickless (NO_HZ_FULL) CPUs. Linux instead arms a per-task high-resolution timer that fires at exactly the nanosecond when the next period begins, making replenishment precise and independent of the tick.

The replenishment engine is the per-task high-resolution timer serviced by dl_task_timer(). This function is where Linux converts the abstract CBS rules into concrete state transitions:

/* deadline.c: dl_task_timer() simplified */
static enum hrtimer_restart dl_task_timer(struct hrtimer *timer)
{
    struct sched_dl_entity *dl_se = container_of(timer, ...);
    struct task_struct *p = dl_task_of(dl_se);
    struct rq *rq = task_rq_lock(p, &rf);

    update_rq_clock(rq);

    /* Servers do not go through the task path at all. */
    if (dl_server(dl_se))
        return dl_server_timer(timer, dl_se);

    /* Gone from the class, PI-boosted, or a spurious/duplicate timer. */
    if (!dl_task(p) || is_dl_boosted(dl_se) || !dl_se->dl_throttled)
        goto unlock;

    /* Throttled while sched-out: replenish, but let the wakeup enqueue. */
    if (!task_on_rq_queued(p)) {
        replenish_dl_entity(dl_se);
        goto unlock;
    }

    enqueue_task_dl(rq, p, ENQUEUE_REPLENISH);
    if (dl_task(rq->donor))
        wakeup_preempt_dl(rq, p, 0);
    else
        resched_curr(rq);

unlock:
    task_rq_unlock(rq, p, &rf);
    return HRTIMER_NORESTART;
}

Even this abridged form shows how much of the function is defence rather than policy. The three goto unlock guards exist because a throttled entity is a promise made to a timer, and almost anything can happen before that timer fires: the task can leave the class through sched_setscheduler(), it can be PI-boosted to a higher priority (in which case it is no longer throttled and must not be replenished here), or a previous start_dl_timer() can have raced and left a duplicate armed. The full function also handles the case where the CPU went offline while the task was throttled, calling dl_task_offline_migration() to move the task — and its reserved bandwidth — to a runqueue that still exists, then continuing on the new rq.

Two details in the tail are easy to miss. The final reschedule is conditional: only if the current scheduling context is itself a deadline task does the code call wakeup_preempt_dl(); otherwise the replenished task outranks whatever is running by class alone and the code goes straight to resched_curr(). And the real function ends with put_task_struct(p), balancing a reference taken when the timer was armed — the comment there warns that this may free the task_struct containing the very hrtimer being serviced, so nothing may touch it afterwards.

What the timer eventually calls, directly or through ENQUEUE_REPLENISH, is replenish_dl_entity(), and its core is a loop rather than an assignment:

/* deadline.c: replenish_dl_entity(), core */
if (dl_se->dl_yielded && dl_se->runtime > 0)
        dl_se->runtime = 0;

/*
 * We keep moving the deadline away until we get some
 * available runtime for the entity.
 */
while (dl_se->runtime <= 0) {
        dl_se->deadline += pi_of(dl_se)->dl_period;
        dl_se->runtime  += pi_of(dl_se)->dl_runtime;
}

The loop exists because a runtime overrun can be arbitrarily large. If a task blew through its budget by three periods' worth — a plausible outcome if it took a long non-preemptible fault, or if runtime was simply underestimated at sched_setattr() time — then advancing the deadline by a single period would still leave the runtime negative. Each iteration buys one period of budget at the cost of one period of urgency, so a task that repeatedly overruns is not merely throttled: its deadlines drift steadily further out, and it loses ground against its peers in exactly the proportion by which it over-consumed. Note also the first two lines, which are how the earlier claim about sched_yield() is enforced — a yielded entity has its remaining budget zeroed before the loop runs, so it is guaranteed at least one trip round and therefore a full period's delay.

Immediately after the loop the function checks whether the deadline it computed is still in the past, and if so gives up on incremental repair:

if (dl_time_before(dl_se->deadline, rq_clock(rq))) {
        printk_deferred_once("sched: DL replenish lagged too much\n");
        replenish_dl_new_period(dl_se, rq);
}

This is a useful string to know. sched: DL replenish lagged too much in dmesg means the runqueue clock had advanced so far past the entity's deadline that the loop above could not catch up, and the kernel abandoned the task's timeline and started a fresh period from the current instant. It is printed once, deferred to avoid printing from under the runqueue lock, and it is a symptom rather than a fault in the class itself — the usual causes are a badly under-declared runtime, a very long period combined with a stalled CPU, or a virtual machine that lost wall-clock time to the hypervisor.

The important architectural point is that replenishment is timer driven, not tick driven. A deadline task can become runnable again at an arbitrary nanosecond boundary even on a fully tickless system. That is the property the RT class's RLIMIT_RTTIME watchdog lacks, and it is why deadline scheduling — not SCHED_FIFO — is the right tool when isolated NO_HZ_FULL CPUs are in play.

Because the callback takes the task's runqueue lock, replenishment and enqueueing are serialized against wakeups and migration. That lock discipline is what lets Linux maintain a consistent view of runtime, deadline, dl_non_contending state, and the rb-tree membership at all times.

Deadline Inheritance and Priority Boosting

One of the three guards in dl_task_timer() was is_dl_boosted(dl_se), and it deserves unpacking, because priority inheritance means something different in the deadline class than it does anywhere else in the scheduler. For an RT task, inheritance is a number: a low-priority mutex owner is temporarily given the waiter's priority so it can finish the critical section. A deadline task has no priority to lend. What it has is a reservation — a runtime, a period, and an absolute deadline — and lending a deadline without lending the budget to meet it would be meaningless.

Linux therefore lends the whole entity. struct sched_dl_entity carries a self-referential pointer, pi_se, initialised in __sched_fork() to point at the entity itself:

/* sched/deadline.c */
static inline struct sched_dl_entity *pi_of(struct sched_dl_entity *dl_se)
{
    return dl_se->pi_se;
}

static inline bool is_dl_boosted(struct sched_dl_entity *dl_se)
{
    return pi_of(dl_se) != dl_se;
}

“Boosted” is thus defined structurally: an entity is boosted precisely when its pi_se points somewhere other than itself. When rt_mutex_setprio() propagates a boost to a deadline task, it does not copy parameters — it aims the pointer at the donor's entity:

/* sched/core.c: rt_mutex_setprio(), deadline branch */
if (dl_prio(prio)) {
    if (!dl_prio(p->normal_prio) ||
        (pi_task && dl_prio(pi_task->prio) &&
         dl_entity_preempt(&pi_task->dl, &p->dl))) {
            p->dl.pi_se = pi_task->dl.pi_se;
            scope->flags |= ENQUEUE_REPLENISH;
    } else {
            p->dl.pi_se = &p->dl;
    }
}

The comment above this code names the two cases it covers. In the first, an RT task holds the mutex and a deadline task blocks on it; the owner is promoted into the deadline class outright, running on borrowed parameters until it drops the lock. In the second, a deadline task holds the mutex and another deadline task with an earlier deadline blocks on it — the dl_entity_preempt() test — so the owner runs with the waiter's more urgent deadline rather than its own. If the blocked task is not more urgent, nothing is lent, since the owner would already be scheduled first.

The consequence shows up in replenishment, and it is the reason pi_of() exists at all. Every place that refills a budget or pushes a deadline reads its parameters through the indirection rather than from the entity directly:

/* sched/deadline.c: replenish_dl_new_period() */
/* for non-boosted task, pi_of(dl_se) == dl_se */
dl_se->deadline = rq_clock(rq) + pi_of(dl_se)->dl_deadline;
dl_se->runtime  = pi_of(dl_se)->dl_runtime;

So a boosted task is replenished with the donor's runtime and deadline, not its own, for as long as the boost lasts. The same indirection appears in replenish_dl_entity() and in dl_entity_overflow(), which means even the CBS wakeup test is evaluated against the lent reservation. The ENQUEUE_REPLENISH flag set alongside the pointer assignment makes the new parameters take effect immediately rather than at the next period boundary — a boost is useless if it arrives one period late.

This also explains the guard in the timer. A boosted entity is by definition not throttled: it is running on someone else's budget, outside its own CBS accounting, so a replenishment timer that fires against it would be reasoning about parameters that are not currently in force. dl_task_timer() bails out rather than trying. setup_new_dl_entity() makes the same assumption explicit with WARN_ON(is_dl_boosted(dl_se)).

Two limitations are worth stating plainly, because the mechanism is easy to over-trust. Inheritance is transitive only in the sense that pi_se is copied from the donor's pi_se rather than from the donor itself, so a chain of boosted owners all converge on the original waiter's entity. And this is not the bandwidth-inheritance protocol described in the real-time literature: the donor's bandwidth is not deducted while the owner spends it, so a long critical section under boost can consume more than the accounting strictly allows. Bandwidth Inheritance (BWI) and, more recently, proxy execution have both been proposed to close that hole; neither is what pi_se does today.

When a kernel is built without CONFIG_RT_MUTEXES, pi_of() returns the entity unchanged and is_dl_boosted() is a compile-time false, so every one of these paths folds away and the class behaves as though boosting did not exist.

Admission Control and Root-Domain Bandwidth

The entire promise of deadline scheduling rests on a single guarantee: if the kernel accepts a reservation, the task will meet its deadlines. But EDF can only deliver that guarantee when the total demand on a CPU (or set of CPUs) does not exceed supply. Without an up-front check, users could innocently create more deadline tasks than the hardware can serve, and every task — including ones that were previously meeting their deadlines — would begin missing them unpredictably. Admission control is the gate that prevents this: it rejects a new reservation at sched_setattr() time with -EBUSY rather than allowing the system to become over-committed. This makes deadline scheduling a contract: the user declares what is needed, the kernel either guarantees it or refuses cleanly.

Admission control answers the question that EDF alone does not: should the kernel accept this reservation at all? Linux computes each task's bandwidth as:

u_i = runtime_i / period_i

accept if  Σ u_i  ≤  U_root_domain_limit

The kernel writes that test as __dl_overflow(), and both of its operands repay a closer look:

/* sched/deadline.c */
static inline bool
__dl_overflow(struct dl_bw *dl_b, unsigned long cap, u64 old_bw, u64 new_bw)
{
    return dl_b->bw != -1 &&
           cap_scale(dl_b->bw, cap) < dl_b->total_bw - old_bw + new_bw;
}

dl_b->bw is not 1.0. It is initialised by init_dl_bw() to to_ratio(global_rt_period(), global_rt_runtime()) — the RT bandwidth ratio from the previous section, 950,000/1,000,000 by default. A single CPU will therefore admit at most 95 % utilisation, not 100 %, and setting sched_rt_runtime_us to -1 sets dl_b->bw to -1, which disables the test altogether. This is the other half of the observation made in the RT section: those two /proc files no longer throttle anything on a default build, but they still decide what the deadline class will accept.

The cap operand is the more interesting one for the architectures this book follows. It comes from dl_bw_capacity(), which on a symmetric x86_64 machine is simply the CPU count shifted into fixed point — four CPUs give a budget of 4 × 0.95. On a machine where sched_asym_cpucap_active() is true, which is to say a typical ARM64 big.LITTLE or DynamIQ SoC, it instead sums arch_scale_cpu_capacity() across the root domain's span. Capacity is normalised so the biggest core in the system is 1024, so four little cores at capacity 400 contribute 1600/1024 ≈ 1.56 units of budget, not 4. The practical consequence is that the same reservation that is admitted on a big core may be rejected on a little one, and a cpuset partition containing only little cores has substantially less deadline bandwidth to give away than its CPU count suggests.

The sum is maintained per root_domain, not globally for the whole machine. That matters because cpusets, affinity masks, and CPU hotplug can partition the machine into several effective scheduling islands. A task pinned to CPUs 4-7 cannot rely on capacity from CPUs 0-3, so its reservation is charged only against the root domain reachable from CPUs 4-7.

The bookkeeping in the bandwidth-check and offline-migration paths is therefore topology aware. When a task changes affinity, when a CPU goes offline, or when cpuset partitioning rebuilds root domains, Linux migrates the task's reserved bandwidth to the new accounting domain and revalidates feasibility. If the target domain would exceed its limit, admission fails rather than silently degrading all existing reservations.

All three of those events funnel into one helper, dl_bw_manage(), through three thin wrappers that name the request type:

  • dl_bw_alloc() issues dl_bw_req_alloc from cpuset_can_attach(), and may fail with -EBUSY.
  • dl_bw_free() issues dl_bw_req_free from cpuset_cancel_attach(), and cannot fail.
  • dl_bw_deactivate() issues dl_bw_req_deactivate from sched_cpu_deactivate(), and may fail with -EBUSY.

The last of these produces behaviour that surprises most people the first time they meet it: taking a CPU offline can fail because of deadline reservations. sched_cpu_deactivate() calls dl_bw_deactivate() before committing to the hotplug, and the request re-runs the overflow test as though the CPU were already gone:

/* deadline.c: dl_bw_manage(), dl_bw_req_deactivate case */
cap -= arch_scale_cpu_capacity(cpu);        /* pretend it is already off */

dl_server_bw = dl_server_read_bw(cpu);      /* servers do not follow the CPU */

if (dl_b->total_bw - dl_server_bw > 0) {
        if (dl_bw_cpus(cpu) - 1)
                overflow = __dl_overflow(dl_b, cap, dl_server_bw, 0);
        else
                overflow = 1;               /* never orphan DL tasks */
}

If the surviving CPUs cannot hold the reservations that are already admitted, dl_bw_deactivate() returns -EBUSY and the write to /sys/devices/system/cpu/cpuN/online fails. The else overflow = 1 branch is the blunter version of the same rule: the last CPU carrying deadline bandwidth can never be offlined, because there would be nowhere for those tasks to go.

Two subtleties in that fragment are worth drawing out. The capacity subtraction uses arch_scale_cpu_capacity(), not a count, so on ARM64 the answer depends on which CPU is being removed — offlining a big core can fail where offlining a little one succeeds. And the servers' bandwidth is deliberately discounted: a fair_server exists to protect the fair tasks on its own CPU, and once that CPU is gone its tasks migrate elsewhere and are covered by the servers already running there, so counting the departing server's reservation against the survivors would reject hotplug operations that are perfectly safe.

The cpuset pair is a two-phase commit. cpuset_can_attach() walks the tasks being moved, accumulates cs->sum_migrate_dl_bw for those that dl_task_needs_bw_move() says are actually changing accounting domain, and calls dl_bw_alloc() once for the total — reserving space in the destination before anything moves, because the migration itself cannot be allowed to fail partway through. If the cgroup layer later aborts the attach, cpuset_cancel_attach() hands the reservation back with dl_bw_free(). The source domain is credited separately and later, in set_cpus_allowed_dl(), which is why the two sides are not symmetrical in the code.

Table 7-27. Deadline-Related Sysctl Parameters
SysctlPurposeEffect on Deadline Scheduling
/proc/sys/kernel/sched_deadline_period_min_usLower bound for accepted deadline periodsRejects pathological tiny periods that would force impractically frequent replenishment and timer traffic
/proc/sys/kernel/sched_deadline_period_max_usUpper bound for accepted deadline periodsKeeps reservations within a range the kernel is willing to manage as deadline servers
/proc/sys/kernel/sched_rt_period_usLength of the global RT/DL reservation windowDefines the period used by the shared real-time bandwidth framework
/proc/sys/kernel/sched_rt_runtime_usCPU time reserved for RT/DL work inside that windowSets dl_bw->bw to runtime/period (0.95 by default), the per-unit-capacity admission ceiling. Setting it to -1 disables admission control entirely
debugfs sched/fair_server/cpuN/{runtime,period}Per-CPU CBS parameters of the fair-class deadline serverDefaults to 50 ms/1000 ms deferred. Writing 0 to runtime disables the fair server on that CPU
debugfs sched/ext_server/cpuN/{runtime,period}The same for the sched_ext serverPresent only with CONFIG_SCHED_CLASS_EXT; protects BPF-scheduled tasks from starvation

These knobs illustrate an important design choice: deadline scheduling is not isolated from the rest of Linux's real-time infrastructure. The admission formula is conceptually Σ(runtime/period), but the practical limit is tied to the same reservation framework that prevents RT and DL classes from consuming an entire machine forever.

Linux's admission test is intentionally conservative. A reservation is rejected as soon as the destination root domain cannot prove enough capacity under the configured RT/DL bandwidth cap. The kernel prefers a deterministic -EBUSY at setup time to a system that accepts every request and then misses deadlines at runtime.

SMP Push/Pull Migration and Affinity Constraints

On a uniprocessor, EDF is trivially correct: just run the task with the earliest deadline. On an SMP machine with M CPUs, the optimal policy is Global EDF — maintain one system-wide deadline ordering and always run the M most urgent jobs. But a true global runqueue would require a single lock contended by every CPU on every schedule event, which is unacceptable at scale.
