Linux therefore uses partitioned runqueues (one dl_rq per CPU) and approximates global EDF through reactive migration: when a local scheduling decision reveals that a task is on the wrong CPU, the kernel pushes or pulls it to restore the global invariant. This is the same strategy used by the RT class, but deadline migration uses absolute deadlines rather than priorities as the ordering criterion.

Global EDF is simple on paper but subtle on SMP hardware. If CPU 0 is running a task with deadline 140 and CPU 1 receives a newly woken task with deadline 120, the system should ideally move work so that the two earliest deadlines run first. Linux approximates this with push/pull helpers layered on top of per-CPU runqueues. The cpudl max-heap tracks, for each CPU in the root domain, the deadline of the currently running deadline task — allowing push_dl_task() to find the best migration target in O(log M) time rather than scanning all CPUs. cpudl_find() checks the heap only as a second resort: it first intersects the task's affinity mask with the root domain's free_cpus, because a CPU running no deadline task at all is always a better target than one whose deadline merely happens to be later. On an asymmetric-capacity machine that fast path does extra work, filtering the candidates through dl_task_fits_capacity() and falling back to the highest-capacity CPU if the task fits nowhere — the migration counterpart to the capacity-scaled admission test described earlier.

push_dl_task() looks for a migratory task on an overloaded CPU and tries to move it to an allowed target CPU where it is a better fit. The common case is: a CPU has more than one runnable deadline task, another allowed CPU is available, and the task being moved has a later deadline than the one the local CPU should keep. pull_dl_task() performs the mirror image from the idle or underloaded side, scanning for an earlier-deadline task that ought to run locally.

Affinity masks and cpuset policy are hard constraints on these heuristics. The scheduler never pushes a deadline task to a CPU outside p->cpus_ptr, and a cpuset partition boundary is effectively a wall: tasks may migrate inside the partition's root domain, but not across it. This is why admission and migration are tied together. A reservation is only meaningful relative to the CPUs that can actually execute the task.

“Hard constraint” understates it, because the constraint runs in the opposite direction from what most readers expect. A deadline task cannot be pinned with sched_setaffinity() at all. Two separate checks enforce this. Entering the class with a mask narrower than the root domain fails in __sched_setscheduler():

/* sched/syscalls.c: __sched_setscheduler(), user path */
if (dl_bandwidth_enabled() && dl_policy(policy) &&
    !(attr->sched_flags & SCHED_FLAG_SUGOV)) {
        cpumask_t *span = rq->rd->span;

        /*
         * Don't allow tasks with an affinity mask smaller than
         * the entire root_domain to become SCHED_DEADLINE.
         */
        if (!cpumask_subset(span, p->cpus_ptr) || rq->rd->dl_bw.bw == 0) {
                retval = -EPERM;
                goto unlock;
        }
}

and narrowing the mask after joining fails in dl_task_check_affinity(), called from __sched_setaffinity(), with -EBUSY for exactly the same reason. So the two obvious ways to say “run this reservation on CPU 3 only” return -EPERM and -EBUSY respectively, and a good deal of confused mailing-list traffic comes from people meeting one of them.

The rule follows directly from where the bandwidth sum lives. Admission is accounted per root domain, so the guarantee the kernel made was “this reservation fits in this domain”. A task allowed to run on only half the domain's CPUs has had its supply halved without the sum being recomputed, and every other reservation in the domain would silently inherit the shortfall. Rather than track per-task capacity subsets, Linux insists that a deadline task's affinity be exactly its root domain.

The supported way to confine a reservation is therefore to make the CPUs a root domain of their own, using an exclusive cpuset partition (or isolcpus). Partitioning rebuilds the domains, the task's mask then equals its new domain's span, and both checks pass — and, importantly, the admission sum for that partition is recomputed to match the capacity actually inside it. The consequence noted earlier still holds, just for a different reason: four one-CPU partitions can admit far less than one four-CPU partition, because bandwidth cannot be shared across a partition wall.

Both checks are skipped when dl_bandwidth_enabled() is false — that is, when sched_rt_runtime_us is -1. Turning admission control off to gain the freedom to pin also turns off the guarantee that made the reservation worth requesting, so it trades a schedulability proof for an affinity mask.

Deadline Servers for Non-DL Work

A recurring problem in mixed-workload systems is that best-effort tasks — CFS threads running desktop applications, background services, or kernel work — can suffer unbounded latency when RT or deadline tasks monopolise the CPUs. The traditional answer was the 95 % RT throttle, but that is a blunt tool: it reserves a fixed 5 % idle slot regardless of whether any fair task needs it. DL servers invert the model. Instead of throttling real-time work to protect fair work, a DL server gives CFS tasks their own CBS reservation that competes inside the deadline class. The server holds a sched_dl_entity with dl_server = 1, receives a budget and period just like any deadline task, and spends the CPU time it wins executing whatever CFS task is next in the fair runqueue. This way, fair-class latency gets an EDF-backed guarantee without any individual CFS thread switching to SCHED_DEADLINE policy.

Structurally a server is an ordinary struct sched_dl_entity with dl_server = 1 and no task_struct behind it. Where a task entity is picked and then simply run, a server entity is picked and then asked which task to run: it carries a server_pick_task callback, installed by dl_server_init(), that reaches into the class it fronts for. So the deadline class does the scheduling, and the fair or sched_ext class does the picking, inside a budget the deadline class enforces.

sched_init_dl_servers() runs once the CPUs are up and installs one such entity per online CPU:

/* deadline.c: sched_init_dl_servers(), abridged */
for_each_online_cpu(cpu) {
    u64 runtime =   50 * NSEC_PER_MSEC;
    u64 period  = 1000 * NSEC_PER_MSEC;

    dl_se = &cpu_rq(cpu)->fair_server;
    dl_server_apply_params(dl_se, runtime, period, 1);
    dl_se->dl_server = 1;
    dl_se->dl_defer  = 1;
    setup_new_dl_entity(dl_se);

#ifdef CONFIG_SCHED_CLASS_EXT
    dl_se = &cpu_rq(cpu)->ext_server;      /* same again for sched_ext */
    ...
#endif
}

Two things follow. First, the reservation is 50 ms in every 1000 ms — the same 5 % the old RT throttle used to hold back, but arrived at from the opposite direction. The RT throttle took time away from real-time work and hoped something useful would use it; the fair server hands the fair class a reservation it can defend inside the deadline class, above RT in the pick order. Second, this bandwidth is charged to admission control like any other: dl_bw_cpus() and the overflow test count the servers' dl_bw, which is why a machine will not admit quite as much user-facing deadline work as the raw 95 % ceiling implies.

The servers are also configurable at runtime, per CPU, through debugfs rather than /proc:

  • /sys/kernel/debug/sched/fair_server/cpuN/runtime and .../period — the fair server's CBS parameters on that CPU. Writing 0 to runtime disables the server on that CPU entirely (dl_server_update() tests dl_se->dl_runtime before accounting anything).
  • /sys/kernel/debug/sched/ext_server/cpuN/{runtime,period} — the same knobs for the sched_ext server, present only when CONFIG_SCHED_CLASS_EXT is enabled. This one is newer still: it was added during the 7.0 cycle so that a BPF scheduler, which can be arbitrarily wrong, cannot starve itself off the CPU.

Deferred Servers and the Zero-Laxity Point

Taken literally, a 5 % CBS reservation sitting above the RT class would be a disaster for real-time users: every second, the fair server would become the earliest-deadline entity on the runqueue and preempt their SCHED_FIFO threads for 50 ms, whether or not any fair task was actually waiting. This is exactly why both servers are created with dl_defer = 1. A deferred server does not try to run when its period opens. Instead it throttles itself immediately and arms its timer for the latest moment at which it could still start and finish its budget by its deadline — the zero-laxity point, deadline - runtime:

/* deadline.c: replenish_dl_entity(), deferred-server branch */
if (dl_se->dl_defer && !dl_se->dl_defer_running &&
    dl_time_before(rq_clock(dl_se->rq), dl_se->deadline - dl_se->runtime)) {
        dl_se->dl_defer_armed = 1;
        dl_se->dl_throttled   = 1;
        start_dl_timer(dl_se);
}

With the default 50/1000 parameters, that instant is 950 ms into the period. If the fair class gets scheduled normally at any point before then — which on almost every system it does, constantly — that execution is charged to the server by dl_server_update(), and when the timer does fire it finds budget already consumed and simply pushes itself forward rather than activating the server:

/* deadline.c: dl_server_timer(), deferred path */
if (dl_se->dl_defer_armed) {
    if (dl_time_before(rq_clock(dl_se->rq),
                       dl_se->deadline - dl_se->runtime - dl_server_min_res)) {
        fw = dl_se->deadline - rq_clock(dl_se->rq) - dl_se->runtime;
        hrtimer_forward_now(timer, ns_to_ktime(fw));
        return HRTIMER_RESTART;              /* still no need to run */
    }
    dl_se->dl_defer_running = 1;
}

So on a healthy system the server never becomes runnable at all and the whole mechanism costs nothing but a timer that keeps deferring itself. It activates only when fair tasks have genuinely been starved to the zero-laxity point, at which point it takes its 50 ms with EDF-backed urgency that RT tasks cannot override. The fair server is a latency backstop, not a share.

The three state bits are worth separating because they are easy to confuse. dl_defer is static configuration: this is a deferred server. dl_defer_armed means the server is currently in the deferred phase, waiting for the zero-laxity timer. dl_defer_running means it skipped or finished that phase and is genuinely executing, which is the condition that stops it from re-deferring on the next replenishment. The kernel documents the resulting five-state machine — init, zero_laxity-wait, idle-wait, running, and replenish-wait — in an ASCII diagram above dl_server_timer() in deadline.c, added in 6.19 precisely because the interaction had proved hard to reason about; a long run of fixes between 6.13 and 7.0 carries titles like “Fix dl_server getting stuck” and “Fix dl_server stopping with runnable tasks.”

One further subtlety distinguishes a deferred server from a plain one. dl_server_update_idle() charges idle time against a deferred server's budget, while a non-deferred server ignores it. The reasoning is that a deferred server exists to detect starvation; if the CPU was idle, the fair class was not being starved, so that time should count against the server's need to run rather than being banked for later.

Finally, the servers are started and stopped rather than left permanently enqueued, so a CPU running only RT or deadline work carries no server overhead at all. enqueue_task_fair() calls dl_server_start(&rq->fair_server) at the moment the fair runqueue goes from empty to non-empty, and enqueue_task_scx() does the same for ext_server. Stopping is lazier and happens where the mismatch is actually detected — in the pick path:

/* deadline.c: _pick_next_task_dl(), server branch */
if (dl_server(dl_se)) {
    p = dl_se->server_pick_task(dl_se, rf);
    if (!p) {
        dl_server_stop(dl_se);
        goto again;                  /* re-pick without this server */
    }
    rq->dl_server = dl_se;
}

If the deadline class picks a server and the class behind it turns out to have nothing runnable, the server is stopped on the spot and the pick restarts. The rq->dl_server assignment on the success path records which server supplied the current task, so that later accounting can charge the task's execution time back to the right budget.

Seen as a whole, the deadline class is less a simple priority policy than a contract engine. EDF chooses the most urgent eligible job, CBS makes urgency proportional to reserved bandwidth, the 0-lag rule decides when a sleeping task’s bandwidth may be handed to someone else, two hrtimers per entity make both transitions precise, and capacity-scaled root-domain accounting ensures that the contract is only accepted where the machine can honour it.

What has changed most in recent kernels is who the contracts are written for. For its first decade SCHED_DEADLINE was a specialist tool that most systems never used; in 7.0.12 the machinery runs on every CPU of every machine, because the fair class and sched_ext now hold reservations of their own. A reader who never writes a sched_setattr() call is still relying on this code — which is a good reason to understand the deferred server, the one part of it that is doing work on an ordinary desktop right now.

Extensible Scheduling (sched_ext)

Every scheduling class discussed so far — fair, RT, deadline — is a fixed algorithm compiled into the kernel. Changing the scheduling policy means writing C code, getting it reviewed, waiting for a kernel release cycle, and deploying a new kernel. For researchers, cloud providers, and game studios who want to experiment with custom scheduling strategies — work-conserving gang scheduling, latency-tier dispatching, or machine-learning-guided placement — that turnaround time is prohibitive. sched_ext, introduced in kernel 6.12, solves this by making scheduling policy programmable at runtime through BPF.

sched_ext gives Linux a programmable scheduling class backed by BPF. Instead of hard-coding every policy in C, the kernel exports a carefully constrained set of callbacks and kfuncs so that a BPF program can decide where tasks should wait, when they should be dispatched, and which CPU should run them next. The kernel still owns context switching, locking, safety checks, and fallback; BPF owns the policy. The key callbacks — select_cpu(), enqueue(), dispatch() — correspond to the natural scheduling decision points, and BPF kfuncs like scx_bpf_dsq_insert() and scx_bpf_dsq_move_to_local() let the policy program place tasks into dispatch queues without ever touching scheduler internals directly.

The critical design constraint is safety. A buggy BPF scheduler must not hang the machine or starve essential tasks. sched_ext achieves this through a multi-layered defense: a watchdog detects scheduling stalls and aborts the BPF scheduler, a bypass mode instantly falls back to built-in CFS scheduling during transitions, and the verifier ensures that BPF programs terminate and access only approved state. If the BPF scheduler is unloaded — whether by user request, by error, or by watchdog timeout — every task seamlessly moves back to the default scheduling class. This makes sched_ext safe for production use, not just experimentation.

The implementation has grown enough to have been split up: kernel/sched/ext.c is about 7,500 lines, with another 1,400 in ext_idle.c for built-in idle CPU selection and 1,200 of shared declarations in ext_internal.h, which puts sched_ext second only to the fair class in size and well ahead of rt.c and deadline.c combined. It is built around dispatch queues (DSQs), watchdog state, and an abort path that can instantly return the system to built-in scheduling if the BPF side misbehaves. This is why sched_ext is practical rather than experimental theater: it accepts user-defined policy without trusting that policy to keep the kernel alive.

Architecture

The sched_ext architecture is a strict division of responsibility between two trust domains. On one side, user space provides a BPF program that implements struct sched_ext_ops — a table of function pointers for the key scheduling decisions: which CPU should a waking task target (select_cpu), where should a runnable task wait (enqueue), and which task should a CPU pull from the queues when it needs work (dispatch). On the other side, the kernel's ext_sched_class validates every BPF decision, enforces time-slice accounting, maintains the DSQ data structures, and stands ready to abort the entire BPF scheduler if it stalls or crashes. The boundary between the two domains is defined by kfuncs — a curated set of BPF-callable kernel functions such as scx_bpf_dsq_insert(), scx_bpf_dsq_move_to_local(), and scx_bpf_kick_cpu() — that give BPF just enough control to implement sophisticated policies without exposing raw scheduler internals.

Table 7-28. sched_ext Architecture
ComponentRole
BPF schedulerImplements struct sched_ext_ops callbacks such as select_cpu, enqueue, and dispatch
ext_sched_classKernel scheduling class that bridges the generic scheduler core to BPF-defined policy
Dispatch queues (DSQs)Ordered holding areas from which CPUs actually consume runnable tasks
Watchdog and bypass stateDetect stalls, suppress broken behavior, and keep the machine runnable during transitions
scx_bpf_* kfuncsControlled BPF entry points for dispatch, queue consumption, CPU kicking, and state inspection

DSQ Architecture: Local, Global, and Custom Queues

In every other scheduling class, the kernel owns the runqueue data structure and the BPF or user-space side has no say in how tasks are queued. sched_ext breaks that pattern by introducing Dispatch Queues (DSQs) — an intermediate layer of ordered holding areas that sit between BPF policy decisions and the kernel's actual CPU pick logic. DSQs are the key abstraction that makes sched_ext both flexible and safe: BPF never touches the core runqueue directly, but it has full control over which DSQ a task enters, effectively controlling placement, ordering, and CPU targeting through a clean indirection. This design means the kernel can always enforce safety invariants at the DSQ boundary regardless of what the BPF policy does.

DSQs are sched_ext's central idea. A BPF scheduler does not manipulate the core runqueue directly. Instead, it places tasks into DSQs, and the kernel later consumes from those DSQs when the CPU needs a runnable task. The three built-in DSQ types cover the range from simple single-CPU targeting to arbitrarily complex multi-dimensional grouping:

  • Local DSQ — one queue per CPU. A task dispatched here is effectively targeted to that CPU and can usually run immediately when the class is picked.
  • Global DSQ — a shared queue used for simple work sharing and fallback dispatch. It behaves like the common pool that any CPU can drain.
  • Custom DSQ — BPF-created queues identified by a 64-bit ID. They let a scheduler group tasks by tenant, latency class, NUMA node, or any other policy dimension.

Internally, a DSQ may operate in FIFO mode or in virtual-time priority mode. The kernel maintains both a list and, for priority queues, an rb-tree. The iterator helpers such as nldsq_next_task() abstract this away so that dispatch code can walk a DSQ without knowing whether the next runnable task came from simple FIFO order or from a virtual-time sort key.

The helper find_dsq_for_dispatch() interprets a BPF request and maps it to the concrete DSQ that should receive the task. This is where the “direct dispatch” marker matters: BPF can say “place this task straight into CPU X's local DSQ” rather than first queueing it in some intermediate structure and waiting for a later dispatch pass.

Table 7-29. Common sched_ext kfuncs and Queue Operations
kfunc / mechanismPurposeTypical use
scx_bpf_dsq_insert()Insert a task into a DSQ with a slice and flagsMain output operation of a BPF scheduler's dispatch() or enqueue() callback
scx_bpf_dsq_insert_vtime()The same, but supplying the virtual-time key used to order a priority DSQWeighted fair sharing or latency tiers on a custom DSQ
scx_bpf_dsq_move_to_local()Move tasks from a DSQ into the consuming CPU's local DSQPull work from a shared or custom queue when a CPU needs runnable tasks
scx_bpf_dsq_move()Move an already-queued task from one DSQ to anotherRebalance or re-prioritise without going back through enqueue()
scx_bpf_kick_cpu()Force a target CPU to reevaluate schedulingWake an idle CPU or trigger a reschedule after placing urgent work
scx_bpf_create_dsq()Create a custom DSQBuild per-cgroup, per-socket, or latency-tier queues
Direct-dispatch markerRequest immediate insertion into a specific local DSQFast path for wakeups where BPF already knows the destination CPU

Two of those names changed early in sched_ext's life, and the older ones are still the ones most third-party documentation uses. scx_bpf_dispatch() and scx_bpf_dispatch_vtime() became scx_bpf_dsq_insert() and scx_bpf_dsq_insert_vtime(), and scx_bpf_consume() became scx_bpf_dsq_move_to_local(), both in 6.13. The old names do not exist in 7.0.12 at all, so a scheduler written against the 6.12 headers will fail to load. The renaming was deliberate: the original names described the event the kfunc participated in, which made dispatch() mean two different things depending on whether it was the callback or the kfunc, whereas the new names describe the DSQ operation being performed and read consistently with scx_bpf_dsq_move() and scx_bpf_dsq_peek().

The kfunc boundary is a stable ABI in a way the rest of the kernel's internals are not, and 7.0.12 contains a visible artefact of that. scx_bpf_dsq_insert() and scx_bpf_reenqueue_local() each have a second registered symbol suffixed ___v2, which is BPF's convention for versioning a kfunc whose signature has changed: the verifier strips everything from the triple underscore onward when matching, so an object file compiled against either prototype resolves to the right implementation. The older entry point remains as a thin wrapper that supplies the new argument. This is the mechanism that lets already-compiled BPF schedulers keep loading across kernel upgrades, and it is worth knowing about before concluding that a duplicated-looking symbol in ext.c is dead code.

DSQ State, Ownership, and Validation

Allowing user-defined BPF code to control task placement creates a fundamental trust problem: what happens when the BPF scheduler dispatches a task to a DSQ that has been destroyed, or dispatches the same task twice, or holds a task indefinitely without ever placing it into a queue? In a traditional compiled-in class these bugs are caught at review time, but BPF schedulers can be loaded at runtime by any privileged user. sched_ext therefore treats every BPF dispatch request as untrusted input and validates it at the kernel boundary before allowing the task to become visible to the CPU picker. This validation layer is what makes the whole system robust — it turns programmer errors into clean scx_error() aborts rather than silent corruption or deadlocks.

A task only becomes runnable to the CPU picker after sched_ext has attached it to a legal DSQ in a legal state. That sounds obvious, but it is the core safety property of the whole subsystem. A BPF scheduler may hold tasks in private data structures, but once it calls a dispatch kfunc the kernel verifies that the task is dispatchable, that the DSQ exists, that the flags are sensible, and that the task is not already owned by some incompatible queue.

This is why dispatch_enqueue() is such a central function. It is not merely a convenience wrapper around list insertion. It is the point where the kernel enforces slice accounting, direct-dispatch markers, DSQ ordering rules, and ownership transfer from BPF policy space into the scheduler's runnable state. If the request is invalid, scx_error() records the fault and deferred irq_work handles teardown from a context where sleeping and broader cleanup are safe.

Seen this way, a DSQ is both a queue and a capability boundary. BPF is allowed to describe where a task should go, but the kernel decides whether that description is currently admissible.

Dispatch Flow: enqueue → BPF policy → DSQ → pick

Understanding sched_ext requires tracing a task through its entire lifecycle from wakeup to execution. Unlike the built-in classes where a single function like enqueue_task_fair() handles the whole path, sched_ext splits the work across multiple stages with BPF callbacks interleaved between kernel checkpoints. At each stage, ownership of the task transfers: from the scheduler core to BPF, from BPF to a DSQ, and from the DSQ to the CPU's pick path. This staged design is what gives BPF schedulers their power — the policy can intervene at every decision point — but it also means the flow is more complex than any single built-in class.

The full sched_ext pipeline is easiest to understand as a chain of ownership transfers:

  1. Wakeup or enqueue event enters the class through the normal scheduler core.
  2. BPF select_cpu() may choose a preferred CPU, often using locality, idle state, or policy-private metadata.
  3. BPF enqueue() decides whether to dispatch directly to a DSQ or to place the task into a custom waiting structure.
  4. Kernel dispatch_enqueue() validates the request, resolves the DSQ, and records the runnable task.
  5. BPF dispatch() runs when a CPU needs more work and may move tasks from custom queues into consumable DSQs.
  6. Pick path drains the local DSQ first and then other eligible DSQs according to class rules.
/* conceptual sched_ext wakeup path */
cpu = ops->select_cpu ? ops->select_cpu(p, prev_cpu, wake_flags)
                      : prev_cpu;

ops->enqueue(p, enq_flags);

if (bpf_requested_direct_dispatch)
    dispatch_enqueue(local_dsq(cpu), p, slice, flags);
else
    queue_in_custom_policy_state(p);

if (cpu_needs_task)
    ops->dispatch(cpu, prev);

next = consume_from_local_or_global_dsq(cpu);

The Built-In Idle Selection Policy

Step 2 of that list — “may choose a preferred CPU, often using locality, idle state, or policy-private metadata” — understates how much help the kernel offers. Deciding which idle CPU to wake a task on is the single hardest part of writing a scheduler, and it is almost entirely about hardware topology rather than about policy. Requiring every BPF scheduler to rediscover SMT siblings, last-level cache boundaries and NUMA distances would guarantee that most of them got it wrong. Linux therefore ships the answer as a library. It lives in its own file, kernel/sched/ext_idle.c, which at roughly 1,400 lines is a substantial subsystem in its own right, and it is reachable both as the default when ops.select_cpu is not implemented and as an explicit kfunc, scx_bpf_select_cpu_dfl(), that a scheduler can call from inside its own callback.

The policy in scx_select_cpu_dfl() is a strict preference ladder, and its kerneldoc states the order plainly:

  1. A fully idle core. A CPU whose SMT siblings are also idle is preferred above everything else, including cache affinity, because a hyperthread sharing a core with a busy sibling delivers a fraction of a core's throughput while looking exactly like an idle CPU in the runqueue.
  2. The previous CPU. Failing a fully idle core, reuse prev_cpu for its warm L1 and L2 and its trained branch predictors.
  3. A CPU sharing the last-level cache. If the task must move, keep it inside the LLC domain so that its working set survives.
  4. A CPU in the same NUMA node, to bound the memory-access penalty.
  5. Any idle CPU the task is allowed to run on.

Two of those steps are conditional in a way worth noting, because it is the same static-key pattern the fair class uses for asymmetric capacity. Steps 3 and 4 are attempted only if the machine actually has multiple LLCs or multiple NUMA nodes and those domains are not simply the same set of CPUs — tracked by the scx_selcpu_topo_llc and scx_selcpu_topo_numa static branches, computed once when the scheduler is enabled. On a single-socket desktop the ladder collapses to steps 1, 2 and 5 at no cost.

The two search directives are exposed to BPF as flags, SCX_PICK_IDLE_CORE (“pick a CPU whose SMT siblings are also idle”) and SCX_PICK_IDLE_IN_NODE, and the idle masks themselves are readable through scx_bpf_get_idle_cpumask() and scx_bpf_get_idle_smtmask() for a scheduler that wants to implement its own ladder over the kernel's bookkeeping. Since 6.15 — the release that also split this code out of ext.c into its own file — there are per-node variants of both, and an ops flag, SCX_OPS_BUILTIN_IDLE_PER_NODE, that switches the kernel from one global idle mask to one per NUMA node; with it set, the search always begins in prev_cpu's node and then proceeds outward in order of increasing distance. On a large multi-socket machine a single global mask is itself a scalability problem, because every idle transition writes to a cache line that every waking CPU reads.

One footnote in the kerneldoc explains an omission that otherwise looks like a bug: tasks pinned to a single CPU never reach any of this, because the core scheduler does not call ops.select_cpu() for them at all. There is nothing to select.

Notice the split in responsibility. BPF may express policy in almost arbitrary ways, but only the kernel's DSQ machinery makes a task runnable on a CPU. This single choke point is what lets sched_ext detect illegal operations, enforce state invariants, and recover safely.

FIFO DSQs versus Virtual-Time-Ordered DSQs

A DSQ is not a single data structure — it is a choice point. When a BPF scheduler creates or uses a DSQ, the kernel needs to know whether tasks should be served in arrival order or in priority order. This is the same fundamental question every queue in the scheduler answers (the RT class chose bitmap+FIFO, the fair class chose an rb-tree keyed by vruntime), but sched_ext makes it an explicit per-queue decision rather than a class-wide constant. A simple BPF scheduler that only needs custom CPU placement can use FIFO everywhere and pay almost nothing for ordering. A sophisticated scheduler that wants weighted fair sharing across tenants can use virtual-time ordering on its custom DSQs while still using FIFO for local per-CPU queues.

A FIFO DSQ is exactly what it sounds like: insertion order determines service order. It is ideal for simple work-conserving schedulers, per-CPU queues, and prototypes that only need custom CPU placement.

A virtual-time DSQ adds a priority dimension. Each task carries a virtual time or priority key chosen by policy, and the DSQ keeps tasks ordered in an rb-tree while still preserving a list representation for generic iteration. This lets a BPF scheduler emulate fair sharing, latency classes, or custom weighted EDF-like behavior without reimplementing the scheduler core.

The trade-off mirrors classic kernel design. FIFO DSQs are cheap and predictable. Ordered DSQs cost more per insertion but encode richer policy. sched_ext deliberately supports both so that a BPF program can spend complexity only where it buys something.

Watchdog, Bypass Mode, and Abort Handling

No amount of BPF verifier checking can guarantee that a scheduler makes correct scheduling decisions. The verifier ensures the BPF program terminates, doesn't access out-of-bounds memory, and calls only approved kfuncs — but it cannot verify that the program actually dispatches every task in a timely manner, or that its logic doesn't create a circular dependency that starves a CPU. These are semantic bugs, not safety bugs, and they can only be detected at runtime by observing the system's behavior. This is why sched_ext needs a runtime safety layer in addition to the compile-time verifier: a watchdog to detect liveness failures, a bypass mode to keep the machine running while the problem is resolved, and an abort path to cleanly tear down the offending scheduler.

Because sched_ext puts user-defined code in the scheduling loop, it needs a recovery story. The file-level globals near the top of ext.c track exactly that: watchdog timing, bypass state, and abort sequencing.

  • Watchdog — detects stalled schedulers that stop dispatching runnable work. If CPUs appear runnable but no legal DSQ progress is made, the kernel treats the BPF scheduler as wedged.
  • Bypass mode — temporarily routes tasks around the custom policy and through a built-in simple path. This is used during fragile transitions such as enable/disable, teardown, or error recovery.
  • Abort path — if BPF performs an invalid operation, scx_error() records the fault and deferred work via irq_work tears down the custom scheduler from a safe context.

The watchdog is worth looking at concretely, because it is not one timer but two checks that guard each other. The outer one is a delayed work item, scx_watchdog_workfn(), which walks every online CPU and, for each, scans that runqueue's runnable_list:

/* ext.c: check_rq_for_timeouts(), core of the scan */
list_for_each_entry(p, &rq->scx.runnable_list, scx.runnable_node) {
        unsigned long last_runnable = p->scx.runnable_at;

        if (unlikely(time_after(jiffies,
                        last_runnable + READ_ONCE(scx_watchdog_timeout)))) {
                u32 dur_ms = jiffies_to_msecs(jiffies - last_runnable);

                scx_exit(sch, SCX_EXIT_ERROR_STALL, 0,
                         "%s[%d] failed to run for %u.%03us",
                         p->comm, p->pid, dur_ms / 1000, dur_ms % 1000);
                timed_out = true;
                break;
        }
}

The quantity being measured is p->scx.runnable_at, the jiffy at which the task became runnable — not the last time it ran, and not the length of any queue. That is the right definition for this class of failure: a BPF scheduler that has simply decided never to dispatch a particular task will keep every other metric looking healthy while that one task waits forever. The timeout defaults to and is capped at SCX_WATCHDOG_MAX_TIMEOUT, 30 seconds; a scheduler may request a shorter one through ops.timeout_ms when it loads, and the work re-arms itself at half the timeout so that two consecutive scans always straddle any real stall.

The second check is the one that makes the first trustworthy. scx_watchdog_workfn() runs on a workqueue, and a workqueue worker is a task — a task which, on a machine whose BPF scheduler has wedged, may itself never be dispatched. A watchdog that cannot be scheduled cannot report that nothing is being scheduled. Linux closes the loop by having the watchdog stamp scx_watchdog_timestamp each time it runs, and having scx_tick() — which is driven by the timer interrupt and therefore cannot be starved by scheduling policy — verify that the stamp is fresh:

/* ext.c: scx_tick() */
last_check = READ_ONCE(scx_watchdog_timestamp);
if (unlikely(time_after(jiffies, last_check + READ_ONCE(scx_watchdog_timeout)))) {
        u32 dur_ms = jiffies_to_msecs(jiffies - last_check);

        scx_exit(sch, SCX_EXIT_ERROR_STALL, 0,
                 "watchdog failed to check in for %u.%03us",
                 dur_ms / 1000, dur_ms % 1000);
}

So there are two distinct SCX_EXIT_ERROR_STALL messages, and the wording tells you which layer fired. “comm[pid] failed to run for N.NNNs” means the scheduler is running but is neglecting a specific task. “watchdog failed to check in for N.NNNs” means the scheduler is neglecting the watchdog itself, which is a more comprehensive failure and usually means nothing on that CPU is making progress.

What Bypass Mode Actually Does

scx_bypass() is more than a flag test scattered through the dispatch path, and it is entered from more places than error handling. It is called around scheduler enable, around disable, from the PM suspend/resume handler, and from the abort path — anywhere the BPF scheduler either does not yet own the tasks or can no longer be trusted with them. Because those windows can nest, the entry point is reference counted:

/* ext.c: scx_bypass(), entry and exit accounting */
if (bypass) {
        WRITE_ONCE(scx_bypass_depth, scx_bypass_depth + 1);
        if (scx_bypass_depth != 1)
                goto unlock;                    /* already bypassing */
        WRITE_ONCE(scx_slice_dfl, READ_ONCE(scx_slice_bypass_us) * NSEC_PER_USEC);
        ...
} else {
        WRITE_ONCE(scx_bypass_depth, scx_bypass_depth - 1);
        if (scx_bypass_depth != 0)
                goto unlock;                    /* someone else still needs it */
        WRITE_ONCE(scx_slice_dfl, SCX_SLICE_DFL);
        ...
}

Only the outermost transition does any work. That transition has two visible effects. First, the default slice is cut from SCX_SLICE_DFL (20 ms) to SCX_SLICE_BYPASS (5 ms), because while bypassing, the machine's goal is turnover rather than throughput — a shorter quantum bounds how long any one task can hold a CPU during a recovery that must not stall. Second, the function walks every possible CPU, sets SCX_RQ_BYPASSING on each runqueue, and requeues that runqueue's runnable tasks so they are ordered by the simple FIFO rule rather than by whatever the BPF policy had arranged. The source comment explains the unusual choice of for_each_possible_cpu() over the online mask in one sentence that captures the whole design stance: “This function can't trust the scheduler and thus can't use cpus_read_lock().”

While SCX_RQ_BYPASSING is set, the class stops calling into BPF for ordering decisions and serves tasks from a plain per-runqueue queue. From 6.19 there is also a global timer, scx_bypass_lb_timerfn(), that runs every 500 ms by default and shifts tasks off runqueues whose backlog has grown well past the others'. Without it, bypass mode preserves liveness but not balance: a CPU that happened to be holding a thousand tasks when the scheduler was aborted would work through them alone while its neighbours idled.

When the Machine Is Already Wedged

The watchdog assumes the kernel is still healthy enough to run a work item every few seconds. A sufficiently bad BPF scheduler defeats that assumption, and 7.0.12 carries three additional entry points, called from the kernel's existing lockup detectors, that treat sched_ext as a prime suspect:

  • scx_softlockup(), from the soft-lockup watchdog. Its kerneldoc names the reproducer: on large multi-socket machines — the comment cites a dual Intel 8480c — a BPF scheduler can live-lock the system by pointing many CPUs at a single DSQ.
  • scx_rcu_cpu_stall(), from the RCU stall detector (6.17). Its comment is careful to note that an RCU stall may well have nothing to do with the BPF scheduler, but aborting one is cheap and is tried before the kernel escalates to a panic.
  • scx_hardlockup() (6.19), from the NMI hard-lockup detector, for the case where affinitised tasks are piled into one queue and every CPU is aimed at it.

All three funnel into a small helper that aborts only if a scheduler is actually loaded and no one else has already started tearing it down, and each returns whether it initiated an abort so the caller can decide whether to proceed with its own, more drastic response. The design principle is consistent and worth stating: every kernel mechanism that would previously have concluded “this machine is broken, panic” first asks whether unloading the BPF scheduler would fix it. This is the strongest single argument that sched_ext is production infrastructure rather than a research vehicle — the failure modes were not merely anticipated, they were wired into the pre-existing panic paths.

The watchdog is especially important because a “bug” in a scheduler is not like a bug in a normal subsystem. A deadlocked storage path might hang I/O. A deadlocked scheduler can stop the whole machine from making forward progress. sched_ext therefore assumes failure will happen and bakes in a fast escape hatch.

Bypass mode is not a new policy class; it is a safety valve. While bypass is active, the kernel favors “keep runnable tasks runnable” over honoring the BPF scheduler's intended ordering. That sounds blunt, but it is exactly the right priority during abort and recovery.

Example BPF Scheduler Structure

All the machinery described so far — DSQs, dispatch flows, watchdogs, bypass mode — can make sched_ext seem complex. But the framework is designed so that a minimal scheduler requires remarkably little code: just three callbacks (ops.select_cpu(), ops.enqueue(), and ops.dispatch()) plus a struct sched_ext_ops declaration. The kernel fills in safe defaults for every callback the BPF program omits, so a scheduler that only cares about placement policy can ignore timer management, CPU hotplug, task lifecycle, and all the other optional hooks. This "progressive complexity" design is deliberate: it lets developers start with a trivially correct scheduler and layer on sophistication incrementally, testing each addition against a known-good baseline.

The minimal sched_ext example, often called scx_simple, demonstrates how little policy is required to produce a working scheduler. It typically chooses a CPU, enqueues into a global or per-CPU DSQ, and dispatches FIFO.

/* scx_simple-style structure */
SEC("struct_ops/simple_select_cpu")
s32 simple_select_cpu(struct task_struct *p, s32 prev_cpu, u64 wake_flags)
{
    return prev_cpu;   /* keep locality unless BPF wants otherwise */
}

SEC("struct_ops/simple_enqueue")
void simple_enqueue(struct task_struct *p, u64 enq_flags)
{
    scx_bpf_dispatch(p, SCX_DSQ_GLOBAL, SCX_SLICE_DFL, enq_flags);
}

SEC("struct_ops/simple_dispatch")
void simple_dispatch(s32 cpu, struct task_struct *prev)
{
    scx_bpf_consume(SCX_DSQ_GLOBAL);
}

From there, more advanced schedulers layer in custom DSQs, virtual time, socket-local queues, background throttling, or tenant-aware partitioning. The key architectural point is that the BPF code remains small because it delegates all low-level mechanics — task state transitions, locking, context switch entry, and CPU ownership — back to the kernel.

Real-World sched_ext Schedulers

The value of a programmable scheduling framework is best demonstrated by the diversity of policies it enables. Within months of sched_ext's upstream merge, the community produced schedulers spanning the entire spectrum from simple research prototypes to production-grade systems handling thousands of containers. These schedulers share the same kernel infrastructure — DSQs, dispatch hooks, watchdog protection — yet implement radically different placement and ordering strategies. Crucially, each one can be loaded and unloaded at runtime without rebooting, enabling A/B comparisons between scheduling policies on live production workloads — something previously impossible without maintaining multiple kernel builds.

Several sched_ext schedulers already illustrate different design goals:

  • scx_rusty explores richer policy code written partly in Rust, emphasizing maintainability and custom balancing logic.
  • scx_layered partitions workloads into layers so that latency-sensitive threads, batch jobs, and background maintenance can each use distinct DSQ strategies.
  • scx_lavd focuses on low-latency adaptive virtual deadlines, showing how sched_ext can prototype policies that would otherwise require invasive kernel patches.

That diversity explains why sched_ext is more than a teaching feature. It is a rapid-prototyping environment for policies that are too workload-specific to live permanently in the generic scheduler, yet too performance-sensitive to implement in user-space daemons.

Operational Model in Practice

Understanding sched_ext's operational model requires recognizing that the framework creates a contract boundary between two very different execution environments. On one side, BPF programs run with restricted capabilities — bounded loops, verified memory access, no sleeping — but enjoy full visibility into task metadata (cgroup hierarchy, CPU topology, performance counters via BPF maps). On the other side, the kernel scheduler core runs with full privilege but treats BPF output as advisory: it honors DSQ placement and CPU selection only when they pass validation checks. This asymmetry is the key to sched_ext's safety: BPF can express arbitrarily complex policy, but the kernel always has final say over what actually executes.

In practice, most sched_ext schedulers converge on the same division of labor. BPF decides grouping, priority, and CPU preference; the kernel decides legality, dispatch materialization, and final execution. That split keeps hot-path policy expressive without turning the scheduler core into a fully scriptable object system.

It also means debugging sched_ext usually begins by asking two separate questions: “did the policy put the task into the intended DSQ?” and “did the kernel later consume that DSQ the way the policy expected?” When those answers differ, the bug is often not in the high-level policy but in slice sizing, missing CPU kicks, or an unexpected bypass / abort transition.

A sched_ext policy that forgets to kick a CPU after filling a DSQ may look correct in code review yet behave as if tasks vanished. In practice, dispatch order, slice length, and CPU-kick strategy are just as important as the abstract ordering policy.

Slices, Refill Cadence, and Forward Progress

In the built-in scheduling classes, the kernel controls time-slice length: CFS derives it from the target latency and the number of runnable tasks; RT uses a sysctl-configurable bandwidth period; SCHED_DEADLINE ties it directly to the declared runtime parameter. sched_ext breaks this pattern by giving BPF programs explicit control over how long each dispatched task runs before the kernel reclaims the CPU. This makes the slice a first-class policy knob — and a first-class source of bugs. A slice that is too short floods the dispatch path with overhead and can trip the watchdog if BPF cannot refill DSQs fast enough; a slice that is too long defeats the purpose of dynamic reordering because the CPU is committed to stale decisions. Getting slices right is therefore inseparable from getting the DSQ topology and dispatch cadence right.

Every dispatch also carries a slice decision. A very short slice increases responsiveness and lets BPF reorder aggressively, but it raises dispatch overhead and watchdog sensitivity. A very long slice reduces churn but can make the policy look unfair or unresponsive because CPUs spend too long running work that BPF would now prefer to reshuffle.

For that reason real sched_ext schedulers usually tune queueing policy and slice policy together. The best DSQ topology in the world cannot help if tasks are dispatched in chunks so large that CPUs rarely come back for more work.

Viewed from the core scheduler, sched_ext is simply another class below fair and above idle. Viewed from policy space, it is a programmable runtime with DSQs as its primitive and the watchdog as its constitutional limit.

The Core Scheduler: schedule() and Context Switch

Every scheduling class eventually funnels into the same core machinery. The fair class may choose by virtual deadline, the RT class by static priority, the deadline class by absolute deadline, and sched_ext by DSQ state — but the final act of descheduling one task and running another happens in kernel/sched/core.c. This is where Linux turns a policy decision into a concrete CPU context switch.

The core scheduler is deliberately policy-agnostic. It does not know what "fair" means, has no concept of deadlines, and never inspects a BPF program. Instead, it provides a small set of class-independent services — runqueue locking, clock updates, task state transitions, memory-management switching, and the final register-state swap — and delegates all ordering decisions to the scheduling classes through a well-defined callback interface (struct sched_class). This separation is what allows Linux to support five scheduling classes simultaneously without any class needing to know about the others: each class implements .pick_task(), .put_prev_task(), .set_next_task(), and a handful of other hooks, and the core iterates through classes in strict priority order (stop > dl > rt > fair/ext > idle) until one yields a runnable task.

The function __schedule(), defined at line 6748 of core.c in Linux 7.0.12, is the single convergence point. Whether invoked voluntarily (a task calls schedule() after blocking on I/O), involuntarily (the tick handler sets TIF_NEED_RESCHED and preemption fires on return from interrupt), or via the RT-mutex-aware SM_RTLOCK_WAIT path, execution always reaches __schedule(). From there, the flow is: disable interrupts → lock the runqueue → update the clock → handle the outgoing task's state → call pick_next_task() to select the next task → perform context_switch() if the next task differs from the current one → unlock and return in the context of the new task. The context_switch() function itself has two halves: an address-space switch (switch_mm_irqs_off() to install the new page tables, with lazy-TLB optimization for kernel threads) and a register-state switch (switch_to(), which is architecture-specific assembly that swaps stack pointers and saved registers).

The key functions are __schedule(), pick_next_task(), context_switch(), try_to_wake_up(), select_task_rq(), sched_tick(), and sched_fork(). Together they cover the full lifecycle: creating a task, waking it, selecting a CPU, switching to it, charging runtime, and eventually switching away again.

Version history. The shape of this code would still be recognizable from Linux 2.6 — disable interrupts, take the runqueue lock, settle prev, pick next, switch — but almost every step now carries passengers that did not exist then. The outgoing task's fate is decided by try_to_block_task(), split out in 6.13, which also determines whether the task will count toward the load average. The pick is made from rq->donor rather than prev, the distinction that proxy execution introduced in 6.13, and it sits inside a retry loop because resolving a proxy relationship can invalidate the choice just made. An SM_IDLE shortcut added in 6.12 skips class iteration entirely when an idle CPU has nothing to do. The address-space switch has acquired obligations to membarrier, to restartable sequences, and to the concurrency-ID mechanism reworked in 6.19. And preemption itself is no longer a build-time constant: since 6.13's lazy preemption work, the same binary can behave as a voluntary, full, or lazy-preemption kernel depending on a boot parameter, as the discussion of preemption models described.

Entry Points into schedule()

A common misconception is that the scheduler runs "whenever something interesting happens." In reality, the kernel never calls __schedule() directly from arbitrary code paths. Instead, events like wakeups and timer ticks merely set the TIF_NEED_RESCHED flag on the current task's thread_info, which is a lightweight request that says "please reschedule at the next safe opportunity." The actual context switch is then deferred to one of a small number of well-defined entry points — locations in the kernel where the scheduler knows it is safe to switch tasks (no spinlocks held, RCU state consistent, interrupt state well-defined). This two-phase design (flag-then-check) is what allows Linux to support both voluntary and preemptive scheduling models with the same core code: the only difference is where the flag is checked.

Table 7-30. schedule() Entry Points
CallerWhenPreempt Type
schedule()Current task blocks, sleeps, or explicitly yieldsVoluntary
preempt_schedule()preempt_enable() finds TIF_NEED_RESCHED setKernel preemption
preempt_schedule_irq()Return from interrupt notices pending rescheduleIRQ preemption
Return-to-user pathSyscall, exception, or interrupt exit sees TIF_NEED_RESCHEDUser preemption

Those entry points differ in context, but they all converge on the same invariant: by the time __schedule() runs, the CPU has reached a point where switching tasks is safe. That is why rescheduling is deferred to well-defined boundaries instead of happening instantly at every wakeup or timer event.

A Detailed Walk Through __schedule()

Understanding __schedule() is essential because every scheduling decision in Linux — no matter which class makes it — passes through this single function. It is the narrowest point in the entire scheduler: everything above it (entry points, TIF_NEED_RESCHED flag management, preemption models) is about when to reschedule, and everything below it (class-specific pick_task(), context_switch(), architecture-specific register swap) is about how. The function itself is the glue that connects "when" to "how" while maintaining a surprisingly strict set of invariants: IRQs are disabled throughout, the per-CPU runqueue lock is held, the RCU context is correctly transitioned, and the outgoing task's accounting (voluntary vs. involuntary switch, PSI pressure, delay accounting) is updated before the incoming task ever runs. Getting any of these wrong would cause subtle corruption — tasks charged to the wrong CPU, RCU grace periods that never complete, or pressure-stall information that loses events.

__schedule() is the orchestrator. It is one of the largest functions in kernel/sched/core.c — far longer than the familiar textbook sketches — because modern Linux layers in pressure accounting, RCU context tracking, worker-state hooks, and class-specific callbacks on top of the basic pick-and-switch logic. The high-level sequence, however, remains stable:

/* core.c: __schedule() condensed */
static void __sched notrace __schedule(int sched_mode)
{
    struct task_struct *prev = rq->curr;
    struct task_struct *next;
    unsigned long *switch_count;
    unsigned long prev_state;
    bool preempt = sched_mode > SM_NONE;

    local_irq_disable();
    rcu_note_context_switch(preempt);
    rq_lock(rq, &rf);
    smp_mb__after_spinlock();
    update_rq_clock(rq);

    switch_count = &prev->nivcsw;

    preempt = sched_mode == SM_PREEMPT;   /* note: narrower than above */
    prev_state = READ_ONCE(prev->__state);
    if (sched_mode == SM_IDLE) {
        if (!rq->nr_running && !scx_enabled()) {
            next = prev;              /* stay in the idle task */
            rq->next_class = &idle_sched_class;
            goto picked;
        }
    } else if (!preempt && prev_state) {
        try_to_block_task(rq, prev, &prev_state,
                          !task_is_blocked(prev));
        switch_count = &prev->nvcsw;
    }

pick_again:
    next = pick_next_task(rq, rq->donor, &rf);
    rq_set_donor(rq, next);
    rq->next_class = next->sched_class;
    if (unlikely(task_is_blocked(next))) {      /* proxy execution */
        next = find_proxy_task(rq, next, &rf);
        if (!next)
            goto pick_again;
    }
picked:
    clear_tsk_need_resched(prev);
    clear_preempt_need_resched();

    if (likely(prev != next)) {
        rq->nr_switches++;
        RCU_INIT_POINTER(rq->curr, next);
        ++*switch_count;
        psi_sched_switch(prev, next, !task_on_rq_queued(prev) ||
                                     prev->se.sched_delayed);
        rq = context_switch(rq, prev, next, &rf);
    } else {
        rq_unpin_lock(rq, &rf);
        raw_spin_rq_unlock_irq(rq);
    }
}

Several details in that sketch deserve emphasis:

  1. Interrupts are disabled first so that the CPU cannot reenter the scheduler while manipulating runqueue state.
  2. The runqueue clock is refreshed before any class callbacks run, ensuring that all accounting uses a consistent timestamp.
  3. The current task is examined before picking the next one. If it is blocking, it may be dequeued; if it merely got preempted, it stays runnable on the queue.
  4. Reschedule flags are cleared only after a next task has been chosen. This prevents losing a pending preemption while still inside the scheduling critical section.
  5. The actual low-level register switch happens deep inside context_switch(), after the core has already committed to next.

Four things in that listing are worth drawing out, because each of them is a place where the 7.0.12 code has moved away from the textbook sketch.

The first is the sched_mode argument itself, and the fact that preempt is computed from it twice, with two different meanings. The initial sched_mode > SM_NONE is the broad question — did anything other than a plain voluntary schedule() get us here? — and that is what RCU wants to know, because an involuntary switch can land in contexts a voluntary one cannot. The later sched_mode == SM_PREEMPT is the narrow question, and it is the one that decides whether prev is allowed to be taken off the runqueue. The gap between the two is SM_RTLOCK_WAIT, the mode used when a task blocks on a sleeping lock under PREEMPT_RT. Such a task really is going to sleep, so it must be dequeued, which is why the narrower test is used for the blocking decision and the broader one is not.

The second is the SM_IDLE shortcut. When the idle task calls schedule() and the runqueue is still empty, there is provably nothing to pick, so the function jumps straight to picked with next = prev and never enters the class hierarchy at all. This matters more than it looks: on an idle-heavy machine this path runs on every wakeup-then-sleep cycle, and skipping pick_next_task() avoids walking a list of classes that are all guaranteed to return nothing. The !scx_enabled() guard is there because a BPF scheduler may want to be consulted even when the local runqueue is empty — it might have work queued in a shared DSQ that only a full pick would find.

The third is that the pick is made from rq->donor, not prev. On a kernel built without CONFIG_SCHED_PROXY_EXEC — which is nearly all of them, as the discussion of the donor concept explained — the two are the same pointer and the distinction costs nothing. When proxy execution is compiled in, the difference is the whole point: the scheduling context being put back is the one whose priority and runtime were being used, which is not necessarily the task that was physically on the CPU.

The fourth is the pick_again loop. If the task the class hierarchy selected turns out to be blocked on a mutex, find_proxy_task() attempts to locate the lock owner so that the blocked task's scheduling context can be lent to it. That search can fail — the owner may be running elsewhere, or may itself be blocked — in which case it returns NULL and the whole selection restarts. A retry loop around task selection is unusual enough to be worth noticing; it exists because the proxy relationship can only be resolved after a candidate has been chosen, and resolving it may invalidate that choice.

One small detail is easy to read past: ++*switch_count increments through the pointer that was set earlier, which is why switch_count is assigned twice above. It points at prev->nivcsw by default and is redirected to prev->nvcsw only if try_to_block_task() was reached. The counter is therefore chosen before the outcome is known and incremented after, so a task that was about to block but got a signal instead is correctly counted as an involuntary switch.

What Happens to prev?

Before the scheduler can pick a new task, it must first settle the fate of the one currently running. This ordering is not incidental — it is a correctness requirement. If the outgoing task is sleeping, it must be removed from the runqueue before pick_next_task() runs, otherwise the class picker might select the very task that is trying to sleep, creating an infinite reschedule loop. Conversely, if the outgoing task was merely preempted, it must remain on the runqueue so it can compete again in the next scheduling cycle. The prev-handling block in __schedule() resolves this by examining the task's saved state (prev_state) and the sched_mode flag to determine which path to take — all while holding the runqueue lock so no concurrent wakeup can race against the deactivation.

The most subtle part of __schedule() is not selecting next; it is deciding what to do with prev. If the task called into the scheduler voluntarily because it is sleeping, waiting on I/O, or blocking on a lock, the kernel must make sure it disappears from the runnable set before another task is picked.

The logic roughly reads as follows:

  • If the switch is a true preemption, prev remains runnable and will compete again later.
  • If prev->__state indicates sleep, the kernel checks whether a signal should wake it immediately. If so, the state is repaired back to TASK_RUNNING.
  • Otherwise deactivate_task() removes it from its class runqueue, updates load averages, and marks it non-runnable.
  • If the task blocks in I/O wait, additional accounting updates I/O-wait statistics and pressure signals.

Since 6.13 that logic lives in its own function, try_to_block_task(), and it is worth reading in full because three of its four decisions are invisible from the call site.

/* core.c: try_to_block_task() */
static bool try_to_block_task(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *p,
                              unsigned long *task_state_p, bool should_block)
{
    unsigned long task_state = *task_state_p;
    int flags = DEQUEUE_NOCLOCK;

    if (signal_pending_state(task_state, p)) {
        WRITE_ONCE(p->__state, TASK_RUNNING);
        *task_state_p = TASK_RUNNING;
        return false;
    }

    if (!should_block)
        return false;

    p->sched_contributes_to_load =
        (task_state & TASK_UNINTERRUPTIBLE) &&
        !(task_state & TASK_NOLOAD) &&
        !(task_state & TASK_FROZEN);

    if (unlikely(is_special_task_state(task_state)))
        flags |= DEQUEUE_SPECIAL;

    block_task(rq, p, flags);
    return true;
}

The first decision is the signal check, and its position is the interesting part. It runs before the should_block test, and the source comment explains why: a task carrying a pending signal must be woken even if the caller had other plans for it. Making the task TASK_RUNNING here is what turns a signal delivered to a sleeping task into a prompt wakeup rather than something noticed at the next unrelated scheduling event. Note that the corrected state is written back through task_state_p, so that __schedule()'s local prev_state — used much later for the trace_sched_switch() record — does not report a sleep that never happened.

The second is should_block, which __schedule() passes as !task_is_blocked(prev). Under proxy execution a task blocked on a mutex deliberately stays on the runqueue, because it must remain selectable: picking it is precisely how the kernel discovers that its scheduling context should be lent to whoever holds the lock. This is the one case where a task that is not going to run is nonetheless left runnable, and it inverts the rule the rest of this subsection describes.

The third decision is the one with the widest user-visible consequence, and the chapter has not mentioned it until now: sched_contributes_to_load is where Linux decides whether a sleeping task counts toward the load average. The rule is that uninterruptible sleep counts and interruptible sleep does not — which is why a machine stuck on slow storage shows a load average far above its CPU count while thousands of idle network servers show almost nothing. The two exclusions are what keep that number meaningful. TASK_NOLOAD exists so that kernel threads which park themselves indefinitely do not inflate it; since TASK_IDLE is defined as TASK_UNINTERRUPTIBLE | TASK_NOLOAD, any kthread sleeping in TASK_IDLE is uninterruptible for signal purposes but invisible to the load average. TASK_FROZEN does the same for tasks stopped by the cgroup freezer or by suspend, so that resuming a system does not appear to have been under load while it was asleep. The flag is recorded on the task at block time rather than computed later, because by the time the load-average sampler runs the state that justified it may be gone.

Finally, is_special_task_state() covers __TASK_STOPPED, __TASK_TRACED, TASK_PARKED, TASK_DEAD and TASK_FROZEN — states set through set_special_state(), which takes ->pi_lock around the write instead of the plain WRITE_ONCE() that set_current_state() uses. Tagging the dequeue with DEQUEUE_SPECIAL tells the scheduling class that this is not a routine sleep, and the fair class acts on it directly: it disables delayed dequeue for the entity. The reason is given in a one-line comment at the test, and it is a good example of a feature interaction that is obvious only once stated — delayed dequeue works by leaving a task queued and relying on a later spurious wakeup to finish the job, and a task in a special state must never experience a spurious wakeup. A stopped or traced task that briefly appeared runnable again would be a visible bug to ptrace and to job control, so these states leave the runqueue immediately rather than lingering as delayed entities.

This is where voluntary versus involuntary context switches diverge in accounting. A blocking task increments nvcsw (voluntary context switches). A preempted task increments nivcsw (involuntary context switches). Tools such as ps, pidstat, and /proc/<pid>/status ultimately reflect this split.

Table 7-31. Voluntary and Involuntary Switch Accounting
CaseWhy the switch happenedCounter updated
VoluntaryTask slept, blocked, yielded, or otherwise gave up the CPUnvcsw
InvoluntaryTask was preempted by a higher-priority or more eligible runnable tasknivcsw
I/O wait subsetTask blocked specifically waiting for I/O completionnvcsw plus I/O-wait accounting and PSI updates

Fast Path and Class Iteration in pick_next_task()

After the outgoing task's state is settled, the scheduler must answer the single most important question: which task should run next on this CPU? The answer is always "the highest-priority runnable task according to the class hierarchy" — but computing that answer naively (walking every class, asking each one) would add overhead to the most performance-critical path in the kernel. Since the vast majority of scheduling decisions on a typical system involve only CFS tasks (no RT, no DL, no sched_ext loaded), Linux shortcuts this common case with a fast path that checks whether all runnable tasks belong to the fair class and, if so, jumps directly to the CFS picker without touching any other class. This optimization alone eliminates multiple function calls, branch mispredictions, and cache-line accesses from the hottest code path in the scheduler — a path that may execute millions of times per second on a busy system.

pick_next_task() is where policy classes meet the core scheduler. Linux optimizes the overwhelmingly common case first: all runnable tasks belong to the fair class. In that situation, the kernel can skip a full highest-to-lowest class walk and call directly into the fair picker.

This fast path matters because many systems spend nearly all their time there. Servers and desktops usually run no deadline tasks, only sporadic RT work, and perhaps no sched_ext scheduler at all. Avoiding unnecessary class iteration removes branches from the hottest scheduler path in the entire kernel.

/* core.c: pick_next_task() idea */
if (likely(rq->nr_running == rq->cfs.h_nr_queued)) {
    p = pick_next_task_fair(rq, prev, rf);
    if (p)
        return p;
}

return __pick_next_task(rq, prev, rf);

The slow path, implemented in __pick_next_task(), iterates classes in strict priority order: stop, deadline, RT, fair, sched_ext, and idle. The first class that returns a task wins. That ordering is why class semantics stay composable. Each class only needs to answer “do I have the best runnable task among my own entities?” The core handles inter-class priority.

The iteration uses for_each_active_class(), which walks a linked list of scheduling classes ordered by descending priority. Each class provides one of two interfaces: the legacy .pick_next_task() callback (which handles both prev put-back and next selection in one call), or the newer .pick_task() callback (which only selects; the core calls put_prev_set_next_task() separately). This dual interface exists because the newer split design enables optimizations like core scheduling, where the core needs to query what a class would pick without committing to the switch.

Two important details in the slow path deserve attention:

  • The RETRY_TASK sentinel — a class may return RETRY_TASK instead of a real task pointer, indicating that something changed during selection (such as a task migrating away or a sched_ext DSQ becoming empty after a race). When this happens, the entire iteration restarts from the top, including re-running prev_balance() to give classes a chance to rebalance load.
  • prev_balance() and load pulling — before starting the class walk, the slow path calls prev_balance(rq, prev, rf). This gives each class an opportunity to pull work from other CPUs (via balance() callbacks) before the pick decision is made, ensuring that the "no runnable task" case is only reached after load balancing has been attempted.
  • The BUG() at the end — the idle class is guaranteed to always return a task (the per-CPU idle thread), so reaching the end of the loop is a kernel bug. This makes the idle class the unconditional backstop of the entire scheduling hierarchy.

The function is reproduced here in full because this section is about the picking algorithm itself; it also appears in How pick_next_task() Walks the Classes, where the concern is the class hierarchy rather than the fast path. The two listings are the same code.

/* core.c: __pick_next_task() — slow path class iteration */
static inline struct task_struct *
__pick_next_task(struct rq *rq, struct task_struct *prev, struct rq_flags *rf)
{
    const struct sched_class *class;
    struct task_struct *p;

    rq->dl_server = NULL;

    if (scx_enabled())
        goto restart;       /* sched_ext needs full iteration */

    /* CFS-only fast path (same as pick_next_task outer check) */
    if (likely(!sched_class_above(prev->sched_class, &fair_sched_class) &&
               rq->nr_running == rq->cfs.h_nr_queued)) {
        p = pick_next_task_fair(rq, prev, rf);
        if (unlikely(p == RETRY_TASK))
            goto restart;
        if (!p) {
            p = pick_task_idle(rq, rf);
            put_prev_set_next_task(rq, prev, p);
        }
        return p;
    }

restart:
    prev_balance(rq, prev, rf);   /* pull work before picking */

    for_each_active_class(class) {
        if (class->pick_next_task) {
            p = class->pick_next_task(rq, prev, rf);
            if (unlikely(p == RETRY_TASK))
                goto restart;
            if (p)
                return p;
        } else {
            p = class->pick_task(rq, rf);
            if (unlikely(p == RETRY_TASK))
                goto restart;
            if (p) {
                put_prev_set_next_task(rq, prev, p);
                return p;
            }
        }
    }

    BUG(); /* idle class must always provide a task */
}

Inside context_switch()

The context switch is where scheduling theory becomes physical reality. Everything up to this point — class selection, runqueue manipulation, load balancing — has been about making a decision. context_switch() is where that decision is executed: the CPU stops running one task and starts running another. This is the most architecture-sensitive code in the scheduler because it must manipulate hardware state that differs fundamentally between x86_64 and ARM64: page table base registers (CR3 vs. TTBR0/TTBR1), stack pointers (RSP vs. SP_EL0), saved register sets (callee-saved GPRs plus FPU/SIMD state), and TLB invalidation semantics (PCID-tagged vs. ASID-tagged). Despite these differences, the function maintains a clean three-phase structure that is common across all architectures: prepare → switch address space → switch registers.

Once the scheduler has committed to next, context_switch() performs the handoff. The function is half scheduler and half architecture boundary. Chapter 3 (“Performing the Process Switch”) follows the architecture half instruction by instruction—switch_to(), __switch_to_asm on x86_64 and cpu_switch_to on ARM64—so this section concentrates on the scheduler’s side of the handoff.

  1. Prepare the switch — invoke scheduler hooks such as prepare_task_switch(), tracepoints, and lock handoff bookkeeping.
  2. Switch memory context — switch_mm_irqs_off() installs the next task's page tables if it has a different mm_struct; kernel threads use lazy TLB borrowing instead of their own user address space.
  3. Switch register state — the architecture-specific switch_to() macro saves the old CPU registers and stack pointer and restores the new task's context.
  4. Finish on the other side — when the old task eventually runs again, finish_task_switch() completes deferred cleanup such as dropping the last reference to a dead task.
/* context_switch() split */
prepare_task_switch(rq, prev, next);
arch_start_context_switch(prev);

if (next->mm) {
    membarrier_switch_mm(rq, prev->active_mm, next->mm);
    switch_mm_irqs_off(prev->active_mm, next->mm, next);
    lru_gen_use_mm(next->mm);
} else {
    enter_lazy_tlb(prev->active_mm, next);
}

mm_cid_switch_to(prev, next);
rseq_sched_switch_event(next);

prepare_lock_switch(rq, next, rf);
switch_to(prev, next, prev);
barrier();

return finish_task_switch(prev);

The important mental model is that context_switch() returns twice. It returns immediately in the newly selected task, and much later — possibly on another CPU if migration happened — in the old task when that task is scheduled again. That strange control flow is why the scheduler core is so careful about what state it stores before invoking switch_to().

The memory context switch has four cases, driven by whether prev and next are user processes or kernel threads:

Table 7-32. Memory Context Switch Cases
TransitionActionRationale
User → Userswitch_mm_irqs_off() installs next->mmDifferent address spaces require new page tables
User → Kernelenter_lazy_tlb() + mmgrab_lazy_tlb()Kernel thread borrows prev's mm; no TLB flush needed since kernel mappings are shared
Kernel → Userswitch_mm_irqs_off() + deferred mmdrop_lazy_tlb()Must install real user mm; drop the borrowed mm reference later in finish_task_switch()
Kernel → Kernelenter_lazy_tlb() + transfer active_mmPass the borrowed mm along; still no TLB flush needed

The lazy-TLB optimization is significant on workloads with many kernel threads (kworkers, RCU threads, per-CPU daemons). Without it, every switch to a kernel thread would flush the TLB unnecessarily — the kernel thread will never access user addresses, so the existing TLB entries for the previous user process can remain valid. On x86_64 with PCID (Process Context IDentifiers) or ARM64 with ASIDs (Address Space Identifiers), the hardware further reduces flush cost by tagging TLB entries per address space, but lazy-TLB remains valuable because it avoids even the software overhead of reprogramming the page table base register.

Three Passengers on the Address-Space Switch

The address-space switch is a natural synchronization point: it is the exact moment the kernel knows which user address space a CPU is about to run in. Several subsystems that have nothing to do with scheduling have therefore attached themselves to it, and their calls sit in the listing above between switch_mm_irqs_off() and switch_to(). They are easy to skim past, but each one exists to make a user-visible guarantee that would otherwise be unaffordable.

membarrier_switch_mm() maintains a per-runqueue cache of the incoming process's membarrier registration. The sys_membarrier() system call gives userspace a way to force a memory barrier on every CPU running threads of the calling process — the foundation of asymmetric fast paths in RCU libraries and garbage collectors, where the common side does nothing at all and the rare side pays for the synchronization. To deliver that, the kernel must work out which CPUs are currently running the caller's address space. Chasing rq->curr->mm on every CPU would mean dereferencing another CPU's current task while it may be changing underneath. Instead context_switch() copies next->mm->membarrier_state into rq->membarrier_state as it goes, so the system call can scan a single word per runqueue and skip CPUs that cannot be affected. The function returns early when prev_mm == next_mm and again when the value is unchanged, so the common case costs one comparison. This is a recurring shape in the scheduler: push a small amount of work into the switch, which already knows the answer, to keep an unrelated fast path cheap.

mm_cid_switch_to() maintains concurrency IDs. An rseq-using program often wants a small dense integer identifying “which of my threads is running right now,” so it can index per-CPU-ish data structures without the array having to be sized to nr_cpu_ids. A concurrency ID is that integer: unique among the threads of one mm that are concurrently on-CPU, and drawn from a range no larger than the number of such threads. A four-thread process on a 256-CPU machine gets IDs 0–3, not arbitrary CPU numbers, so its per-slot allocations stay tiny. Only the context switch can maintain this, because the identifier is defined by what is running simultaneously. The mechanism was substantially reworked in 6.19, which replaced the older switch_mm_cid() scheme.

rseq_sched_switch_event() flags that the task was scheduled in, so that the return-to-userspace path knows to fix up the restartable-sequence area — abort a critical section that was interrupted mid-flight, and publish the current CPU and concurrency ID. Its position matters: the comment in the source notes it must come after the concurrency-ID update, because it is the step that publishes the value the previous call computed.

The ordering constraint running through all three is the same one that makes context_switch() delicate in general. Everything here must happen after the CPU has committed to next but before switch_to() makes the stack switch that ends this function's ability to observe anything, because after that point the code is running as a different task entirely.

The Full Wakeup Protocol in try_to_wake_up()

If __schedule() is the "put to sleep" side of the scheduler, try_to_wake_up() is the "bring back to life" side — and it is arguably the harder of the two. The difficulty lies in concurrency: wakeups frequently happen on a different CPU from the one where the task is sleeping (or even still in the process of going to sleep). Consider a common scenario: a task on CPU 1 calls schedule() to block on a mutex, but before __schedule() finishes switching it out, CPU 3 releases that mutex and calls wake_up(). At this moment, the task is in a twilight state — it has set its p->__state to TASK_UNINTERRUPTIBLE but may still be the "current" task on CPU 1, with p->on_cpu == 1. If the wakeup side does not handle this race correctly, it could either lose the wakeup entirely (the task sleeps forever) or corrupt the runqueue by enqueueing a task that is still mid-switch on another CPU.

try_to_wake_up() is the other half of scheduling. Instead of removing a task from runnable state, it transitions a blocked task back into competition. This path is deceptively hard because wakeups are often remote: CPU 3 may complete the I/O that wakes a task last seen sleeping on CPU 1.

The protocol therefore revolves around three facts that must never be misread concurrently:

  • p->__state — is the task actually in a wakeable sleep state?
  • p->on_rq — is it already queued somewhere?
