  • p->on_cpu — is another CPU still in the middle of switching it out?

Memory barriers are what make those flags meaningful. The wakeup side must not observe old state before the sleeping side has fully committed the block, and the switch-out side must not lose a wakeup that arrives while the task is between “current” and “queued”. The exact barrier choreography in try_to_wake_up(), ttwu_queue(), and the schedule path exists to close precisely those races.

/* try_to_wake_up() protocol, simplified */
if (!(READ_ONCE(p->__state) & state))
    return 0;

smp_mb__after_spinlock();

cpu = select_task_rq(p, task_cpu(p), wake_flags);

if (task_cpu(p) != cpu)
    set_task_cpu(p, cpu);

ttwu_queue(p, cpu, wake_flags);

/* remote wakeups may queue into a wakelist instead of taking rq lock inline */
wakeup_preempt(rq, p, wake_flags);

ttwu_queue() is itself a performance optimization boundary. If waking the task on the target CPU can be deferred safely, the kernel may append it to a per-CPU wakelist and let the destination CPU take its own runqueue lock later. This reduces cross-CPU lock bouncing on busy machines while preserving correctness.

The barrier choreography inside try_to_wake_up() deserves particular attention because it is one of the most carefully commented pieces of code in the entire kernel. The function must read three fields in a specific order — p->__state, then p->on_rq, then p->on_cpu — with memory barriers between each to prevent the CPU or compiler from reordering them. The reason is a three-way race between the waker, the sleeper's __schedule(), and the sched_ttwu_pending() path that processes deferred wakeups:

  • First barrier (smp_mb__after_spinlock() after pi_lock) — ensures p->__state is read after the condition that triggered the wakeup (e.g., a mutex release) is globally visible. This pairs with set_current_state() on the sleeping side.
  • Second barrier (smp_rmb() between state and on_rq) — prevents falsely observing p->on_rq == 0 when the task was actually re-queued by a concurrent sched_ttwu_pending(). If the task is already on a runqueue, ttwu_runnable() handles it without a full enqueue.
  • Third barrier (smp_acquire__after_ctrl_dep() between on_rq and on_cpu) — if on_rq == 0, the task is genuinely off the runqueue. But the remote CPU might still be mid-switch with this task as prev. The waker must either wait (smp_cond_load_acquire(&p->on_cpu, !VAL)) or defer via ttwu_queue_wakelist() to avoid corrupting the remote CPU's switch-out sequence.

The TASK_WAKING intermediate state is set under pi_lock after all checks pass, signaling that this task is in the process of being woken but not yet enqueued. This prevents a second concurrent try_to_wake_up() from attempting a duplicate wakeup. Once the target CPU is selected and the task is enqueued, the state transitions to TASK_RUNNING.

CPU Selection with select_task_rq()

Choosing which CPU a waking task should run on is one of the most consequential decisions in the scheduler, because it directly affects cache hit rates, memory access latency (NUMA locality), power consumption (packing vs. spreading), and whether the task can start immediately or must wait behind other runnable work. The core function select_task_rq() acts as a dispatcher: if the task's cpus_allowed mask permits more than one CPU and migration is not disabled, it calls the scheduling class's .select_task_rq() callback to make the placement decision. If the task is pinned to a single CPU, the answer is trivial and no class callback is needed. After the class returns its preference, the core applies a safety check: if the chosen CPU is no longer allowed (e.g., a cpuset change raced with the wakeup), select_fallback_rq() picks a legal alternative — a critical backstop that prevents tasks from being enqueued on offline or forbidden CPUs.

The wakeup path delegates CPU choice to the task's scheduling class:

  • Fair — select_task_rq_fair() considers wake-affine placement, energy-aware placement, idle siblings, and cache locality.
  • RT — the RT class favors CPUs where the task can preempt effectively and may later push lower-priority work away.
  • Deadline — the DL class must honor affinity and reservation feasibility, then relies on push/pull migration to restore a good global EDF state.
  • sched_ext — BPF select_cpu() can override the default choice, but the kernel still validates the result.

This split is one of Linux's cleaner abstractions. The core does not need to know why a fair wakeup prefers an LLC-local idle CPU or why a BPF scheduler wants to funnel a task into a custom per-tenant queue. It only needs a legal target CPU before it can queue the task.

What sched_tick() Does Every Tick

In older kernels, the timer tick was the scheduler — the primary mechanism for detecting that a time slice had expired and forcing a context switch. Modern Linux has moved far beyond that model. Wakeups, preemption points, and hrtimer-driven events handle the majority of scheduling decisions, and the tick is now just one of several maintenance signals. On NO_HZ_IDLE systems, idle CPUs receive no ticks at all; on NO_HZ_FULL systems, even busy CPUs can suppress ticks if only one task is runnable. Despite this reduced role, the tick remains essential for running CPUs because certain maintenance tasks — load average decay, hardware pressure sampling, NUMA scanning triggers, misfit detection, and RT/DL bandwidth enforcement — require periodic execution that cannot be efficiently triggered by per-event callbacks alone.

sched_tick() — called scheduler_tick() until it was renamed in 6.10 — is called from the timer interrupt or its NOHZ equivalents. Even on systems that avoid periodic ticks for idle CPUs, running CPUs still need periodic scheduler maintenance. The function's structure is deliberately generic:

  1. update the runqueue clock,
  2. charge execution time to the current task,
  3. call the current class's task_tick(),
  4. refresh load averages and capacity pressure, and
  5. trigger balancing or misfit detection when thresholds say so.

The class callback is where policy-specific consequences happen. task_tick_fair() updates EEVDF state, enforces latency accounting, and checks NUMA and misfit conditions. task_tick_rt() rotates round-robin peers and charges RT runtime limits. task_tick_dl() charges CBS runtime. sched_ext may use its tick hook to age virtual time or kick dispatch decisions.

Thus the timer tick is not “the scheduler” in the 2.6 sense. It is periodic maintenance shared by all classes, while actual context switches remain event driven and may happen far more or far less often than the tick interval.

Initializing New Tasks in sched_fork()

Every task in Linux begins life inside copy_process(), and the scheduler's involvement starts early: sched_fork() runs before the new task has a PID, before it is visible to any other subsystem, and critically before it can possibly be woken up or scheduled. This is the scheduler's one chance to establish a clean initial state without any concurrency concerns — no locks on the child are needed because no other CPU can see it yet. The function must solve several subtle problems simultaneously: prevent the child from inheriting temporary priority boosts (PI boosting from the parent's mutex chain), decide whether the child should inherit the parent's scheduling policy or be reset to SCHED_NORMAL (the sched_reset_on_fork flag), assign the correct scheduling class based on the resulting priority, and initialize all per-entity tracking structures (load averages, virtual runtime, slice budgets) to values that will not cause starvation or unfairness when the child first competes.

Before a newly forked task can run, sched_fork() initializes its scheduler-visible state. This includes clearing stale accounting, setting a starting policy/class, resetting load averages, and ensuring the child cannot run until the rest of the fork path finishes.

/* core.c: sched_fork() condensed */
int sched_fork(u64 clone_flags, struct task_struct *p)
{
    __sched_fork(clone_flags, p);   /* zero se/dl/scx state, load avg, on_rq */

    p->__state = TASK_NEW;

    /* Do not leak PI boosting priority to the child. */
    p->prio = current->normal_prio;

    uclamp_fork(p);

    if (unlikely(p->sched_reset_on_fork)) {
        if (task_has_dl_policy(p) || task_has_rt_policy(p)) {
            p->policy = SCHED_NORMAL;
            p->static_prio = NICE_TO_PRIO(0);
            p->rt_priority = 0;
        } else if (PRIO_TO_NICE(p->static_prio) < 0)
            p->static_prio = NICE_TO_PRIO(0);

        p->prio = p->normal_prio = p->static_prio;
        set_load_weight(p, false);
        p->se.custom_slice = 0;
        p->se.slice = sysctl_sched_base_slice;

        p->sched_reset_on_fork = 0;
    }

    if (dl_prio(p->prio))
        return -EAGAIN;
    ...
}

Three lines in that function carry more weight than their length suggests.

p->prio = current->normal_prio is the anti-inheritance rule for priority inheritance. Because copy_process() produces the child by copying the parent wholesale, a parent that is currently boosted — sitting at an RT priority it borrowed from a waiter on a mutex it holds — would otherwise hand that boost to its child permanently. The boost belongs to the lock, not to the task, and certainly not to its descendants, so the child is given the parent's normal priority: what the parent would have without any boost. Note the asymmetry with p->policy, which is inherited. Priority inheritance changes prio without changing policy, so resetting only prio undoes exactly the boost and nothing else.

The sched_reset_on_fork block is where the SCHED_RESET_ON_FORK flag — set through sched_setscheduler() by OR-ing it into the policy argument — finally does something. Its purpose is to stop privilege escalation by descent: a process granted real-time priority for one job should not be able to spawn an unbounded tree of children that all inherit it. When the flag is set, an RT or deadline child is demoted to SCHED_NORMAL at nice 0, and a child that merely had negative nice is pulled back to 0. Notice what is not reset — positive nice values survive, because lowering your own priority is never a privilege problem. Notice also the last line of the block: the flag is cleared after being acted on, so the reset applies to the immediate child only and does not propagate to grandchildren. A task that wants the behaviour to persist must re-request it.

The -EAGAIN return for a deadline parent is the one case where fork() can fail for a purely scheduling reason. A SCHED_DEADLINE task may not fork into another deadline task, because doing so would silently double the admitted bandwidth without going through the admission test described earlier in this chapter. The kernel refuses rather than over-committing, and the parent sees fork() fail with EAGAIN.

Class-specific fork hooks then tailor the new task. The fair class initializes its sched_entity so that the child does not receive an unfair burst of service. Deadline and RT tasks inherit policy under controlled rules. Only after wake_up_new_task() runs does the child become runnable and enter the ordinary wakeup and placement pipeline.

The scheduler core is therefore not just the place where tasks are switched. It is also the place where tasks are born, made runnable, preempted, put to sleep, and accounted for. All class-specific policy ultimately plugs into this one state machine.

Load Balancing in Multiprocessor Systems

On a single-CPU system, the scheduler's job ends once it picks the best task for that CPU. On a multiprocessor system, an equally important question arises: is this task on the right CPU in the first place? A system with 128 CPUs where all runnable work piles onto 8 of them — while the other 120 sit idle — is catastrophically underutilized regardless of how perfectly each CPU's local scheduling policy runs. Load balancing is the subsystem that prevents this pathology by periodically detecting imbalances and migrating tasks from overloaded CPUs to underloaded ones.

The challenge is that migration is never free. Moving a task from one CPU to another invalidates warm cache lines (L1 and L2 are per-core on most architectures), may cross NUMA boundaries (turning local memory accesses into expensive remote ones), disrupts hardware prefetchers that have learned the task's access pattern, and on heterogeneous systems like ARM64 big.LITTLE, may move a task to a core with different performance characteristics. The balancer must therefore make informed decisions: migrate only when the benefit (reduced wait time, better utilization) exceeds the cost (cache rebuild, NUMA penalty). Linux encodes this cost/benefit reasoning into a hierarchical structure of scheduling domains and scheduling groups, where cheaper migrations at lower topology levels are attempted frequently, and expensive cross-node migrations happen only when severe imbalances justify the cost.

In Linux 7.0.12, the balancing logic is no longer a single monolithic load_balance() routine. The fair scheduler spreads the work across helpers such as sched_balance_rq(), sched_balance_newidle(), active-balance machinery, wake-placement logic, and NUMA-specific code. The conceptual pipeline, however, is still the familiar one: find imbalance, choose a source group, pick a source runqueue, detach movable tasks, and attach them somewhere better.

Version history. The Linux 2.6.11 balancer was a single load_balance() function walking a domain hierarchy that a large machine might build two levels deep. What replaced it is not a better version of the same idea so much as a response to hardware that no longer looks like a flat set of interchangeable CPUs. Load itself is now a decaying per-entity signal rather than a queue length (PELT, 3.8); NUMA balancing moves memory as well as tasks (3.8); tickless idle meant the balancer had to be inverted so that busy CPUs balance on behalf of idle ones; asymmetric capacity, misfit tasks, and energy-aware placement arrived with ARM64 big.LITTLE (4.6 onward); and the search itself is now budgeted against measured cost. One change falls inside the 6.12–7.0 window and is visible in every listing below: a 6.10 cleanup renamed the entire balancing API to a sched_balance_* prefix, so load_balance(), find_busiest_group(), and newidle_balance() — the names used by essentially all existing writing on the subject — no longer appear in the source at all.

Scheduling Domains and Topology Construction

A flat "balance across all CPUs" approach would be ruinous on modern hardware. Consider a 128-CPU, 4-socket NUMA server: migrating a task between hyperthreads on the same core costs essentially nothing (shared L1/L2), moving it to another core in the same LLC package costs a few microseconds of cache warm-up, but pushing it across a NUMA boundary may add tens of microseconds of memory latency on every subsequent access for the task's entire working set. The scheduler needs a data structure that captures these cost tiers so it can attempt cheap migrations frequently and expensive ones only when imbalance is severe. That structure is the scheduling domain.

A sched_domain represents one balancing level in the machine's topology. build_sched_domains() in kernel/sched/topology.c walks the architecture's topology description and constructs a hierarchy: SMT siblings inside a core, cores inside a cluster or LLC, clusters inside a NUMA node, and nodes inside the whole machine. The kernel discovers topology at boot from firmware tables (ACPI SRAT/SLIT on x86_64, device-tree cpu-map on ARM64) and encodes it as a stack of sched_domain_topology_level entries. The default topology array in Linux 7.0.12 defines four base levels — SMT, CLS (cluster), MC (multi-core / LLC), and PKG (package) — with NUMA levels appended dynamically when the platform reports non-uniform memory. Each domain carries flags like SD_SHARE_LLC, SD_NUMA, and SD_ASYM_CPUCAPACITY that tell the balancer which cost model and migration strategy to use at that level.

Table 7-33. Typical Scheduling Domain Hierarchy (x86_64 Server)
LevelScopeBalance IntervalMigration Cost
SMTSibling hyperthreadsVery frequentMinimal — threads share core resources
MC / LLCCores sharing last-level cacheFrequentLow — cache locality mostly preserved
NUMA localCPUs in one memory nodeModerateMedium — memory stays local
NUMA globalAll CPUs across nodesInfrequentHigh — migration may imply remote memory access

An ARM64 big.LITTLE system produces a different hierarchy. The lowest domains may group sibling threads or cores in a LITTLE cluster, the next domain groups that entire cluster, and a higher domain spans the big cluster as well. At the top, balancing sees all CPUs; at the bottom, it sees only the cheapest migration opportunities.

What a sched_group Represents

Domains describe levels of topology — but the balancer also needs to compare one side of a level against another. That is what scheduling groups provide: inside each domain, CPUs are partitioned into a circular linked list of sched_group structures, and each group represents one "opponent" in the busiest-group comparison. The split follows a simple rule: the groups at level N correspond to the child domains at level N−1. Thus at the LLC domain, each group covers one MC child (a single core or cluster); at the NUMA domain, each group covers one LLC child (an entire socket). This recursive nesting means the balancer always compares units of equal "depth" — it never accidentally pits a single CPU against an entire node.

Each sched_group carries a sched_group_capacity companion that aggregates the group's total compute capacity (capacity), per-CPU minimum and maximum capacity (min_capacity, max_capacity), and a group_weight count of CPUs in the group. On heterogeneous ARM64 systems with SD_ASYM_CPUCAPACITY, the big cluster's group has a higher capacity sum than the LITTLE cluster's group, which drives the "misfit" migration logic: when a CPU-bound task is stuck on a LITTLE core that cannot supply its demand, the balancer detects the capacity shortfall at the group level and triggers an active migration to the big cluster.

Inside a domain, CPUs are partitioned into sched_groups. A group is the unit compared against peer groups when the balancer asks "which side of this domain is busiest?" On an SMT domain, a group might be a single core's sibling threads. On an LLC domain, a group might be one core. On a NUMA domain, a group might be an entire node.

This extra layer matters because the balancer does not reason only about individual CPUs. It wants to preserve structure. When balancing across an LLC domain, for example, it is often better to move a task from a busy core to an idle core in the same cache-sharing group before pulling work across sockets. sched_group is the object that encodes that preference.

The Balancing Algorithm Step by Step

The balancing algorithm is a pull model: each CPU periodically asks "is there a better distribution of work that includes me having more tasks?" Rather than a central coordinator pushing tasks to idle CPUs, every CPU independently walks its domain hierarchy bottom-up and attempts to pull work from whichever peer group looks busiest. This distributed, lock-local design avoids a global bottleneck — on a 256-CPU server, 256 independent balancers can run concurrently, each only taking the source runqueue's lock when it actually has tasks to migrate.

The entry point for periodic balancing in Linux 7.0.12 is sched_balance_rq(), which constructs an lb_env environment struct describing the balancing context (destination CPU, domain, idle type, migration budget) and then proceeds through the five-stage pipeline below. A critical detail is the redo loop: if the balancer's chosen source runqueue turns out to have no movable tasks (all pinned, all cache-hot, or migrated by a concurrent balancer), it retries with updated masks rather than give up immediately. The function also respects per-domain serialization (SD_SERIALIZE flag) to avoid thundering-herd problems on NUMA domains where multiple idle CPUs might simultaneously attempt to pull from the same busy node.

The names of the 2.6-era balancing steps still describe the new implementation well enough to be useful:

  1. Find the busiest group — compare group load, capacity, and runnable counts to determine whether this domain is imbalanced.
  2. Find the busiest queue — inside that group, choose the source runqueue whose tasks are most movable and whose pressure is highest.
  3. Compute the imbalance — estimate how much load or how many tasks should move to improve balance without overshooting.
  4. Detach tasks — walk the source queue and remove tasks that are allowed to migrate, avoiding pinned, hot, or otherwise unsuitable tasks where possible.
  5. Attach tasks — enqueue the chosen tasks on the destination queue and update PELT/load statistics.
/* conceptual fair-class balancing loop, with the 7.0.12 function names */
for_each_domain(cpu, sd) {
    if (!(sd->flags & SD_LOAD_BALANCE) || time_before(jiffies, sd->last_balance + interval))
        continue;

    /* sched_balance_rq() body, condensed: */
    if (!should_we_balance(&env))     /* am I the right CPU to do this? */
        break;
    group = sched_balance_find_src_group(&env);   /* also sets env.imbalance */
    busiest = sched_balance_find_src_rq(&env, group);
    detach_tasks(&env);               /* fills env.tasks, honours can_migrate_task() */
    attach_tasks(&env);
}

Linux 7.0.12 scatters those steps across helpers because different triggers want different aggressiveness. A periodic tick balance is conservative. An idle CPU running sched_balance_newidle() is aggressive because the alternative is wasted hardware. Active balance is more forceful still: if ordinary pull balancing cannot fix a severe asymmetry, the kernel may kick migration from the busy side.

Table 7-34. Common Balancing Triggers
TriggerWhen it runsMain goal
Periodic balanceTick-driven at domain-specific intervalsGradually remove drift without excessive migration
Idle balanceA CPU is about to go idle or is newly idlePull any useful work before wasting capacity
Wake balanceTask wakeup pathPlace work well enough to avoid later migration
Fork / exec balanceNew task creation or address-space resetSpread fresh work early, before locality hardens
Active balancePassive pull could not resolve a strong imbalanceForce migration from the overloaded side

Of these triggers, idle stealing (also called idle balance or work stealing) deserves a brief definition because it is referenced repeatedly in later sections. When a CPU's runqueue drains to zero, the scheduler calls sched_balance_newidle() before entering the idle loop. This function walks the domain hierarchy looking for any peer CPU that has more than one runnable task — if it finds one, it "steals" a task by pulling it to the now-idle CPU. The term "stealing" reflects the pull-based nature: the idle CPU takes work without the busy CPU's explicit cooperation. We examine the mechanism in detail in "Why Idle Stealing Still Matters" below.

Who Balances for a Tickless CPU?

Every trigger in Table 7-34 assumes some CPU is running code that can notice an imbalance. On a mostly-idle machine that assumption fails. Periodic balancing is driven from sched_tick(), and an idle CPU under CONFIG_NO_HZ_IDLE — which is to say almost every distribution kernel — stops its tick entirely. A CPU that is not ticking cannot notice that it is idle while its neighbour has a backlog, and waking it up periodically just to check would defeat the purpose of stopping the tick in the first place. This is the problem the NOHZ idle load balancer solves, and it is a mechanism Linux 2.6.11 did not need because tickless idle did not yet exist.

The inversion is simple once stated: a busy CPU does the balancing on behalf of the idle ones. Idle CPUs register themselves in a global nohz.idle_cpus_mask as they stop their ticks. On each tick, a busy CPU runs nohz_balancer_kick() and asks whether the idle CPUs are owed a balancing pass. If they are, it nominates one of them — the idle load balancer, or ILB — and sends it an IPI. That CPU wakes just long enough to run nohz_idle_balance(), which iterates the idle mask and performs a balance pass for each tickless CPU in turn, pulling work to them as if each had run its own periodic balance.

Three details make the mechanism worth reading rather than merely summarizing.

The first is the choice of victim. find_new_ilb() does not pick any idle CPU; it walks nohz.idle_cpus_mask intersected with housekeeping_cpumask(HK_TYPE_KERNEL_NOISE). On a machine configured for latency isolation — CPUs handed to a real-time or DPDK-style workload via nohz_full= and isolcpus= — those CPUs are excluded from the housekeeping set, so they are never chosen to do balancing work for anybody else. This is the practical reason the housekeeping infrastructure exists: without it, an isolated CPU could be woken by an IPI to run load balancing for unrelated tasks, which is exactly the interference the isolation was meant to eliminate.

The second is that the kick carries flags, not a single command. The three that make up NOHZ_KICK_MASK are NOHZ_BALANCE_KICK (actually rebalance), NOHZ_STATS_KICK (only decay stale blocked load), and NOHZ_NEXT_KICK (recompute when the next balance is due). They are combined with atomic_fetch_or() onto the target, and the CPU that sets the first flag owns the rq->nohz_csd call. This separation matters because the commonest reason to disturb an idle CPU is not an imbalance at all — it is that the CPU carries blocked load, the decaying PELT contribution of tasks that have since gone to sleep, and that figure will read as stale and too high until somebody decays it. Waking a CPU to fix an accounting artifact is worth much less than waking it to give it work, so the kernel distinguishes the two rather than treating every kick as a balance.

The third is the escalation ladder in nohz_balancer_kick(), which reads as a list of reasons a busy CPU concludes the idle ones should be disturbed: it has two or more runnable tasks and could clearly share; it has one runnable CFS task but check_cpu_capacity() reports its own capacity is reduced by RT or IRQ pressure, so someone else could run the task faster; an SD_ASYM_PACKING domain wants work moved to a preferred core; or a domain contains a misfit task that needs a bigger CPU. Notice that most of these are heterogeneity arguments rather than queue-length arguments. On a uniform server the first test does most of the work; on an ARM64 big.LITTLE phone or an x86 hybrid laptop the later ones matter far more, which is a good illustration of how much of the modern balancer exists to serve asymmetric machines.

PELT: The Math Behind Load Signals

Before PELT, the scheduler tracked load at the runqueue level: a single, system-wide running average that could not distinguish a CPU running one heavy task from one running twenty light tasks. That coarse signal made load balancing fundamentally imprecise — it could detect "this CPU is busy" but not "this specific task would benefit from moving." PELT replaced that model with per-entity accounting: every schedulable entity (a task or a cgroup) independently tracks its own exponentially decaying load, runnable time, and utilization. The runqueue's aggregate signal is simply the sum of its entities, making it trivially decomposable — the balancer can ask "how much load would move if I migrated this task?" rather than reasoning only about bulk runqueue statistics.

The decay mechanism uses a geometric series with a half-life of 32 ms (LOAD_AVG_PERIOD = 32 in kernel/sched/sched-pelt.h). Every 1 ms (one PELT "period"), the accumulated signal is multiplied by a decay factor y ≈ 0.978, so a signal from 32 ms ago contributes half as much as a signal from right now, and a signal from 320 ms ago is effectively invisible. The maximum possible accumulated value (LOAD_AVG_MAX = 47742) represents a hypothetical entity that has been 100% runnable since the beginning of time — it is the denominator that converts raw sums into the 0–1024 scale that the rest of the scheduler uses. This fixed-point arithmetic (no floating point, no divisions at runtime) makes PELT cheap enough to update on every dequeue and enqueue without measurable overhead.

Per-Entity Load Tracking (PELT) is the numerical language load balancing speaks. Every task and runqueue maintains exponentially decaying sums so that recent activity matters more than old activity, but old activity never disappears abruptly.

Table 7-35. PELT Metrics
MetricWhat it measuresMain users
load_avgWeighted runnable load, incorporating shares/weightGroup comparisons and imbalance estimation
runnable_avgTime spent runnable, whether running or waitingOverload detection and queue pressure
util_avgActual CPU execution demandCapacity fitting, EAS, and schedutil frequency selection

The conceptual recurrence is:

/* exponential decay with ~32 ms half-life */
y(t + Δ) = y(t) * 2^(-Δ / 32ms) + x * (1 - 2^(-Δ / 32ms))

Here x is the instantaneous signal: runnable load, actual execution, or a weighted variant of the two. If a task suddenly becomes CPU-bound, its util_avg rises quickly but not instantly. If it goes idle, the signal decays smoothly rather than dropping to zero in one tick. That smoothness is exactly what prevents load balancing and frequency selection from oscillating wildly.

Because PELT is hierarchical, runqueue and cgroup signals are sums of entity signals. The same mathematics therefore drives both per-task placement and higher-level decisions such as whether a whole domain is overloaded.

NUMA Balancing in Detail

On a multi-socket NUMA machine, task placement and memory placement are two sides of the same coin. A task running on Node 0 whose working set lives entirely on Node 1 pays a latency penalty on every cache miss — often 2–3× compared to local memory access. The load balancer's domain hierarchy can pull tasks toward idle CPUs, but it has no visibility into where the task's pages actually reside. Automatic NUMA balancing fills this gap by adding a feedback loop that observes memory access patterns at runtime, without requiring application modification or manual numactl pinning. The design philosophy is "measure first, migrate second": rather than guessing at placement based on static heuristics, the kernel gathers empirical evidence of each task's memory footprint and acts only when the data clearly indicates a better node exists.

The measurement technique is elegantly simple: task_numa_work() periodically walks a portion of the task's address space and downgrades page table entries by clearing access/young bits (on ARM64) or marking PTEs as pte_protnone (on x86_64). When the task next touches those pages, the resulting "NUMA hint fault" is trapped by the kernel, revealing which node the page resides on and which CPU the access came from. By scanning different portions of the address space on successive intervals (controlled by numa_scan_period, which adapts between 100 ms and 25.6 s based on convergence), the kernel builds a complete picture of the task's memory access topology without ever instrumenting the application.

Automatic NUMA balancing extends the same "measure first, migrate second" philosophy to memory locality. The scheduler periodically samples a task's address space by temporarily clearing PTE access state so that future accesses trigger NUMA hint faults. Those faults reveal which node's memory the task is really touching.

From that data Linux maintains per-task and per-group fault statistics:

  • scan period controls how often a task's address space is probed, increasing for stable workloads and shrinking when placement appears poor,
  • fault buckets track local versus remote memory access patterns,
  • numa_group aggregates tasks that repeatedly touch the same memory so they can move as a unit, and
  • preferred node records the node whose combination of CPU capacity and memory locality appears best for the task or group.

The important consequence is that NUMA balancing is not just “move the task to its hottest node.” Sometimes Linux instead migrates pages to the task, and sometimes it keeps communicating tasks together even if that means not every single thread gets its individually hottest node. The scheduler treats locality as a system-level optimization problem, not a per-thread greedy choice.

Overloaded and Overutilized States

Load balancing has two modes: an energy-efficient mode (Energy Aware Scheduling, or EAS) that tries to pack work onto the fewest CPUs at the lowest power state, and a performance mode that spreads work to maximize throughput. The kernel cannot run both strategies simultaneously — packing for energy conflicts with spreading for performance. Two root-domain-level boolean flags act as the mode switch, telling the scheduler when conditions have deteriorated enough that energy optimization must yield to capacity-finding.

These two domain-level states strongly influence modern balancing decisions:

  • Overloaded — at least one runqueue has more runnable tasks than it can immediately serve while another runqueue sits idle. This is the classic imbalance scenario that drives pull balancing and idle stealing.
  • Overutilized — the measured utilization on at least one CPU approaches or exceeds the capacity margin where energy-aware placement stops making sense. When this flag is set, Linux shifts priority from "save energy" to "find capacity."

The overloaded flag lives on the root_domain and is also used by the RT class to drive push/pull balancing. For CFS, once set it enables the idle-balance fast path and increases the urgency of periodic balancing. The overutilized flag is more subtle: it fires when any single CPU's aggregate utilization (including uclamp min/max adjustments) exceeds what util_fits_cpu() considers safe capacity. Once rd->overutilized is set, the EAS placement logic in find_energy_efficient_cpu() is bypassed entirely — the system has moved past the point where energy-optimal placement produces acceptable latencies.

The distinction between these two flags matters most on heterogeneous machines. A domain may appear balanced in simple runnable-task counts yet still be overutilized because heavy tasks sit on LITTLE cores whose capacity cannot satisfy demand. Conversely, a domain may have many runnable tasks but still not be overutilized if those tasks are mostly light sleepers whose utilization fits comfortably within CPU capacity.

Table 7-36. Selected /proc/sys/kernel/sched_* Tunables Relevant to Balancing
KnobWhat it controlsWhy it matters
sched_migration_cost_nsTime window during which a task is treated as cache-hotDiscourages migrations that would throw away fresh cache locality
sched_nr_migrateMaximum tasks detached in one balancing passBounds balancing latency on very busy queues
sched_tunable_scalingHow scheduler tunables scale with CPU countChanges balance aggressiveness as machines grow larger
sched_util_clamp_minSystem-wide default floor for utilization clampingInfluences placement and frequency because tasks may request minimum effective capacity
sched_util_clamp_maxSystem-wide default ceiling for utilization clampingCan limit how “big” a task looks to balancing and freq selection
sched_schedstatsEnable detailed scheduler statisticsEssential when diagnosing why balancing or migration is behaving unexpectedly

Wake Placement Before Periodic Balancing

Periodic load balancing is inherently reactive — it detects imbalances that already exist and corrects them after the fact. But the cheapest migration is the one that never happens. If the scheduler places a waking task on the right CPU from the start, no subsequent balancing pass needs to move it. This insight makes the wakeup path the single most important load-distribution decision point: on interactive workloads, tasks wake and sleep hundreds of times per second, and each wakeup is an opportunity to redistribute work with zero migration cost (the task hasn't touched any cache lines on its old CPU since it went to sleep).

In Linux 7.0.12, select_task_rq_fair() implements a priority cascade that reflects this philosophy. First, if the root domain is not overutilized, it attempts energy-aware placement via find_energy_efficient_cpu() — trying to find the CPU that minimizes system-wide energy while still meeting the task's capacity requirement. If EAS is disabled or the system is overutilized, it falls through to wake-affine logic: wake_affine() tests whether the waker's CPU (or the task's previous CPU) is lightly loaded enough to keep the task close to its communication partner. Finally, select_idle_sibling() searches for an idle CPU in the same LLC to avoid queuing delays. The wake_wide() heuristic short-circuits affine placement for tasks with many wakees — a producer fanning out to many consumers should spread, not cluster.

Not every imbalance is fixed by a later balancing pass. The fair wakeup path tries to avoid creating the imbalance in the first place. select_task_rq_fair() first considers wake-affine placement — "is the waker's CPU or cache neighborhood still the best place for this task?" — then energy-aware placement, then idle-sibling search. Only if those fast-path heuristics still leave the system skewed does the heavier balancing machinery need to intervene.

This ordering is deliberate. Moving a waking task before it runs even once is cheaper than migrating it after it has built cache state, touched NUMA-local memory, and perhaps raised the destination CPU's frequency. In other words, good wake placement is load balancing with a time machine.

Migration Cost, Hot Tasks, and Detach Filters

Once the balancer identifies a source runqueue, it cannot simply grab the first available task. Each candidate must pass through a gauntlet of filters encoded in can_migrate_task(), and the rationale is straightforward: moving the wrong task can make things worse — destroying cache locality for a task that was about to finish its time slice, violating an affinity constraint that exists for correctness reasons, or pulling a task whose memory is pinned to a remote NUMA node. The filter chain in Linux 7.0.12 rejects tasks that are: (1) delayed-dequeued unless the migration type is migrate_load, (2) in a throttled cgroup hierarchy on the destination, (3) not allowed on the destination CPU by cpus_ptr, (4) per-CPU kthreads, (5) blocked on mutexes (proxy-exec aware), or (6) too cache-hot as measured by task_hot().

The cache-hot test deserves special attention. task_hot() compares the task's last execution time against sysctl_sched_migration_cost (default 500 µs). If the task ran within that window, the kernel assumes its L1/L2 working set is still warm and the cost of rebuilding it on a new core outweighs the benefit of better load distribution. This single tunable is one of the most impactful knobs for balancing behavior: setting it to zero makes the balancer maximally aggressive (never consider anything hot), while setting it to −1 makes everything hot (effectively disable migration). The balancer also tracks sched_task_hot per-task so that a task rejected as hot on one pass can be reconsidered if balance remains unachieved after multiple failed attempts (nr_balance_failed increments).

Detaching a task is never just "pick the heaviest one." The balancer filters out tasks that are pinned by affinity, tasks that are currently running, and tasks judged too cache-hot under sched_migration_cost_ns. It also prefers migrations that repair the domain's measured imbalance rather than simply moving the first movable entity it sees.

That is why debugging balancing bugs often feels counterintuitive. A visibly busy CPU may refuse to shed the task you expected, not because the scheduler failed to notice the imbalance, but because every promising candidate was filtered out by affinity, hotness, utilization clamps, or NUMA locality concerns.

The Cost of Balancing Is Itself Budgeted

The filters above decide which task is cheap enough to move. A separate and easily overlooked question is whether the search is worth running at all. This matters most in sched_balance_newidle(), which runs on the way into the idle loop with the runqueue lock held: every microsecond spent scanning domains is a microsecond the CPU is idle but unavailable, and on a short idle period the scan can plausibly cost more than the work it finds.

Linux resolves this by measuring itself. Each sched_domain carries max_newidle_lb_cost, the observed worst-case time of a newidle balance attempt at that level, maintained by update_newidle_cost(). Against it the scheduler weighs rq->avg_idle, a running estimate of how long this CPU tends to stay idle. The test appears twice, and the pairing is the interesting part: once up front, so a CPU whose idle periods are typically shorter than a level-0 scan skips balancing entirely, and once inside the domain walk, where the accumulated cost so far plus the next level's cost is compared against the same budget. The effect is that the balancer climbs the domain hierarchy only as far as the expected idle duration can pay for — an SMT sibling scan is affordable on almost any idle period, while a NUMA-wide scan is attempted only by a CPU that has been going properly idle.

Because a worst case that is never revisited would ratchet upward forever, the maximum decays by roughly 1% per second ((cost * 253) / 256, once per HZ) whenever a cheaper attempt is observed. A single unlucky, cache-cold scan therefore does not suppress newidle balancing on that domain indefinitely.

A related piece of self-restraint applies at the top of the hierarchy. For domains carrying SD_SERIALIZE — in practice the NUMA levels — only one CPU in the entire system may run a balance pass at a time, enforced by an atomic_try_cmpxchg_acquire() on the global sched_balance_running. Cross-node balancing reads a great deal of remote state, and letting every CPU do it simultaneously would generate more coherence traffic than the resulting migrations could ever repay.

Why Domain Flags Matter

A scheduling domain is more than a set of CPUs at a topology level — it is a policy container that declares which balancing behaviors are permitted within that level. Linux 7.0.12 defines 14 domain flags in include/linux/sched/sd_flags.h, and each flag enables or disables a specific scheduling action. Without the right flags, a balancing algorithm simply will not run — regardless of how severe the imbalance. The key flags are:

  • SD_BALANCE_NEWIDLE — allows idle-balance to search this domain when a CPU goes idle
  • SD_BALANCE_FORK — permits spreading newly forked tasks across this domain
  • SD_BALANCE_EXEC — permits spreading tasks across this domain after exec
  • SD_BALANCE_WAKE — enables wake-time balancing decisions at this level
  • SD_WAKE_AFFINE — permits wake-affine placement across the domain's span
  • SD_SHARE_CPUCAPACITY — CPUs in this domain share physical execution resources (SMT)
  • SD_SHARE_LLC — CPUs share a last-level cache; migration within is cheap
  • SD_SERIALIZE — prevents concurrent balancers from colliding on this domain
  • SD_PREFER_SIBLING — encourages spreading across sibling groups rather than packing
  • SD_ASYM_PACKING — drives priority-based packing for SMT cores with asymmetric throughput
  • SD_ASYM_CPUCAPACITY — marks heterogeneous capacity (e.g., big.LITTLE); enables misfit migration
  • SD_NUMA — identifies NUMA boundaries; triggers longer balance intervals and heavier migration cost
  • SD_CLUSTER — marks cluster-level sharing (between SMT and LLC)

Each scheduling domain also carries flags that decide which balancing behaviors are even legal at that level: wake-affine placement, idle search, active balance, NUMA balancing, SMT-aware sharing, and so on. Two machines with the same CPU count may therefore balance very differently if their topology flags differ.

This is the hidden reason topology setup is so important. build_sched_domains() is not just drawing a tree. It is choosing the rules of motion that later balancing passes are allowed to apply inside each branch of that tree.

The fastest successful balance is the one Linux never has to perform. Good wake placement, sensible affinity masks, and stable NUMA locality often matter more to observed performance than the raw cleverness of the periodic balancer.

Why Idle Stealing Still Matters

Periodic balancing runs on a timer — at best every few milliseconds at the MC level, and much less frequently at NUMA levels. But when a CPU finishes its last runnable task and is about to enter the idle loop, waiting for the next periodic tick is unacceptable: that could be 4–16 ms of wasted compute capacity while other CPUs have queued work. sched_balance_newidle() is called directly from the scheduling path (inside pick_next_task() for CFS when no local task is found) and attempts to pull work immediately. Because the idle CPU has no local cache state to protect, the cost/benefit calculation is heavily biased toward migration: any runnable task is better than doing nothing.

The implementation walks the domain hierarchy just like periodic balancing, but with key differences: it ignores balance intervals (every domain is eligible), it uses CPU_NEWLY_IDLE as the idle type (enabling more aggressive stealing), and it has a time budget (this_rq->max_idle_balance_cost) that limits how long it spends searching before giving up and entering true idle. On large machines with hundreds of CPUs, this budget prevents a thundering herd of newly-idle CPUs from all simultaneously scanning the same busy runqueues. The budget grows when idle-balance succeeds (finding work is worthwhile) and shrinks when it fails (searching was wasteful).

Imagine a four-core cluster where three cores are lightly busy and one core just went idle. Periodic balance may not fire soon enough to matter for latency, but sched_balance_newidle() can pull a ready task immediately and hide the imbalance before user space notices. This is why idle balance remains one of the most visible scheduler behaviors on interactive systems.

The cost model is different from periodic balancing: the destination CPU has nothing useful to lose, so the kernel accepts a wider search and a more aggressive steal attempt than it would during routine housekeeping.

One practical consequence is that scheduler traces often need to be read in domain order. A migration that looks odd at the socket level may be perfectly rational once the lower LLC-level domain state is examined first.

On large systems, even the question “which CPUs are comparable?” is topology dependent. That is precisely what sched domains and groups answer for the rest of the balancer.

Load balancing, then, is not one algorithm but a stack of interacting approximations: topology domains define where migration is cheap, PELT estimates how much demand exists, wake-placement tries to avoid future mistakes, idle balance repairs underuse quickly, and NUMA logic overlays memory locality on top of everything else.

Energy-Aware Scheduling

On battery-powered devices — smartphones, laptops, embedded controllers — raw throughput is not the only metric that matters. A scheduler that always spreads work to maximize parallelism will keep more cores awake at higher frequencies than necessary, draining the battery even when the workload could comfortably run on fewer, slower cores. Energy-Aware Scheduling (EAS) addresses this by adding an energy dimension to CPU placement decisions: instead of asking only "which CPU can run this task?", EAS asks "which CPU can run this task at the lowest total system energy cost while still meeting its performance requirements?"

The key insight behind EAS is that on heterogeneous processors (ARM64 big.LITTLE, DynamIQ, and increasingly x86 hybrid architectures like Intel Alder Lake), different CPUs have dramatically different performance-per-watt curves. A LITTLE core running at maximum frequency may deliver 40% of the throughput of a big core at its maximum, but consume only 15% of the power. For tasks whose utilization fits within LITTLE capacity, placing them on big cores wastes energy without providing benefit. EAS exploits this by modeling the energy cost of each candidate placement and choosing the one that minimizes total system energy — subject to the constraint that the task must actually fit on the chosen CPU.

The implementation spans three layers: an Energy Model (EM) framework that describes power-capacity curves per performance domain, a topology check in kernel/sched/topology.c that validates whether the system provides enough information for trustworthy energy estimates, and the placement algorithm in find_energy_efficient_cpu() that evaluates candidate CPUs by computing predicted energy deltas. The algorithm iterates over all performance domains, computes the base energy with the task's current placement, then compares it against the energy with the task hypothetically placed on the best-fitting CPU in each domain. The CPU whose placement yields the largest energy savings wins.

Version history. Nothing in this section existed in Linux 2.6.11, where every CPU in a machine was assumed interchangeable, frequency was the exclusive business of a cpufreq governor that the scheduler neither consulted nor informed, and the notion that task placement might be chosen to minimize joules rather than latency did not exist. Each piece arrived separately: schedutil in 4.7 gave the governor the scheduler's own utilization signal instead of a sampled load estimate; capacity awareness and misfit handling followed for ARM64 big.LITTLE; the Energy Model framework and find_energy_efficient_cpu() landed together in 5.0; utilization clamping in 5.3 let userspace bias the result per task or per cgroup. The area is still moving. The 6% migration margin described below was removed in 6.0, runtime-modifiable energy tables arrived in 6.9, and roughly twenty commits touched the Energy Model between 6.12 and 7.0.12 alone. Anything written about EAS more than a few releases ago should be checked against the source before it is trusted.

Descriptions of EAS written before Linux 6.0 — including most of what is currently findable on the subject — add a qualification here: that the saving had to exceed a 6% margin before the task would be moved, the idea being to damp migration churn. That margin no longer exists. It was removed by commit b812fc9768e0 in 6.0, and the reasoning is worth repeating because it is a good illustration of how the energy numbers should and should not be read. The margin was computed as a percentage of the total estimated system energy, but compute_energy() is only meaningful as a difference between two hypothetical placements; as an absolute figure it is inflated, because it includes idle CPUs and mixes util_avg with util_est. A system that had been busy recently therefore carried a large absolute energy, hence a large margin, which in turn prevented migrations to more efficient CPUs — a negative feedback loop that kept tasks on the cores the mechanism was supposed to move them off. The effect fell hardest on exactly the workloads EAS exists for: on a Pixel 4 at 5% utilization, the margin demanded a saving of roughly 8 joules, which no task with utilization below about 40 could ever produce, pinning small tasks to the biggest CPU. Since 6.0 any predicted saving, however small, is enough.

The implementation sits partly in topology setup and partly in the fair wakeup path. topology.c decides whether EAS can even be enabled for a root domain; select_task_rq_fair() then calls find_energy_efficient_cpu() when a wakeup is a good candidate for energy-based placement. If the prerequisites fail or the system is already overutilized, the scheduler falls back to simpler capacity-aware rules.

When EAS Is Active

EAS is not a mode the administrator toggles on or off — it activates automatically when the kernel determines that all the pieces needed for reliable energy estimation are in place. The validation happens at domain-build time in sched_is_eas_possible() and build_perf_domains(), both in kernel/sched/topology.c. If any check fails, the kernel logs a diagnostic message (visible with sched_debug enabled) and falls back to capacity-aware placement without energy optimization. This fail-safe design means EAS never produces silently wrong estimates — it either has enough information to be trustworthy, or it does not run at all.

The checks exist because energy estimation makes strong assumptions about the system. If frequency scaling is handled by a governor that ignores utilization signals (e.g., performance or powersave), the scheduler's prediction of "what frequency will this CPU run at?" becomes unreliable. If utilization is not frequency-invariant, comparing a task's util_avg across CPUs running at different clock rates is comparing apples to oranges. If SMT is active, two sibling threads share execution resources in complex ways that the current EM cannot model (one thread's utilization changes the effective capacity available to its sibling). Each prerequisite eliminates a class of estimation error:

Linux enables EAS only when the topology and frequency stack provide enough information to make a trustworthy estimate. The checks in kernel/sched/topology.c boil down to these requirements:

  • the architecture registered an Energy Model (EM),
  • CPU capacities are asymmetric, so placement actually changes energy/performance behavior,
  • SMT is not part of the equation for that domain, because sibling sharing complicates energy estimation,
  • the schedutil governor is active, so frequency decisions use the same utilization signal as the scheduler, and
  • utilization is frequency invariant, meaning util_avg can be compared across CPUs running at different clocks.

If any of those assumptions is false, Linux prefers ordinary capacity-aware placement. Bad energy estimates are worse than no energy estimates because they can systematically place work on the wrong cores.

Diagnosing Why EAS Did Not Activate

Because the decision is made by the kernel rather than by configuration, the common practical problem on a new board is not tuning EAS but discovering why it never turned on. Each rejection in sched_is_eas_possible() is paired with a pr_info() naming the specific condition that failed — missing asymmetric capacities, active SMT, load tracking that is not frequency-invariant, or a cpufreq stack that is not ready. Those messages are gated behind sched_debug(), so they appear only when the kernel is booted with sched_verbose or after writing to /sys/kernel/debug/sched/verbose. Without that flag the failure is completely silent, which is why an otherwise correct big.LITTLE bring-up can run for a long time before anyone notices that energy-aware placement was never in effect.

The result is published in two forms, and the distinction matters when reading the code. The static key sched_energy_present, tested through sched_energy_enabled(), is global: it is enabled if at least one root domain qualifies, and it exists so that kernels on symmetric machines pay nothing for EAS beyond a patched-out branch. The per-domain answer lives in rd->pd, the root domain's list of performance domains. find_energy_efficient_cpu() therefore begins by dereferencing rd->pd and giving up immediately if it is NULL.

That split has a consequence worth stating plainly: EAS is a property of a root domain, not of the system. Partitioning CPUs with cpuset.sched_load_balance creates separate root domains, and each is evaluated on its own. A partition containing only big CPUs has symmetric capacities by construction, so it fails the asymmetry test and runs without EAS, while a partition spanning both clusters on the same machine keeps it. Isolating CPUs for a latency-sensitive workload can therefore switch off energy-aware placement for that partition as a side effect — usually the intended outcome, but rarely an anticipated one.

CPU Capacity and Task Fitness

Before energy can be optimized, the scheduler must answer a more fundamental question: can this CPU actually serve this task's demand? On a heterogeneous system, a LITTLE core's peak capacity might be only 40% of SCHED_CAPACITY_SCALE (1024), meaning a task whose PELT utilization exceeds ~400 cannot be served without permanent saturation — the CPU would run at 100% with the task still waiting for cycles. Placing such a task on a LITTLE core to save energy is counterproductive: the task gets poor latency, the core burns maximum power anyway, and the system appears unresponsive. Capacity fitness is therefore the hard constraint that energy optimization must respect.

The fitness test in Linux 7.0.12 is util_fits_cpu(), which compares a task's effective utilization against the CPU's available capacity. "Effective utilization" incorporates utilization clamping (uclamp_min and uclamp_max): a task boosted to uclamp_min = 800 is treated as needing at least 800 units of capacity even if its measured utilization is lower, while a task capped at uclamp_max = 300 is treated as fitting any CPU with capacity ≥ 300 regardless of its actual utilization. The function returns a three-valued result: +1 (fits), 0 (fits but only due to uclamp cap), or −1 (does not fit). This nuance lets EAS distinguish between CPUs where a task genuinely fits and CPUs where it would only fit if externally throttled.

Table 7-37. CPU Capacity Concepts
ConceptMeaning
cpu_capacityNormalized peak compute capacity of a CPU or performance domain
task_utilPELT utilization of a task, often with utilization clamping applied
Task fitnessA task fits when its effective utilization can be served without saturating the CPU beyond the acceptable margin
Misfit taskA task whose demand is persistently too large for its current CPU, even if the CPU runs near max frequency

Capacity awareness comes first because energy decisions are meaningless if the chosen CPU cannot actually serve the task well. A task that does not fit on a LITTLE core should move toward a bigger core even if the larger core burns more power. EAS therefore starts from feasibility and only then optimizes energy among feasible choices.

The Energy Model Framework

The scheduler does not measure power directly — it has no access to current sensors or voltage regulators at the granularity needed for per-wakeup decisions. Instead, it relies on the Energy Model (EM), a data structure registered by the platform at boot time that describes the power-capacity curve for each group of CPUs that share a frequency/voltage domain. The EM is the bridge between the hardware's electrical reality and the scheduler's software abstraction: platform engineers (or firmware like ACPI/PPTT and device-tree) supply the raw data, and the kernel normalizes it into a form that em_cpu_energy() can evaluate in constant time.

The central abstraction is the performance domain (em_perf_domain), which maps to a set of CPUs whose frequency is scaled together — typically matching a cpufreq policy. Each performance domain contains an ordered table of performance states (em_perf_state), one per operating point (OPP). Each state records four key values: the frequency at that OPP, the power consumed by one CPU at that frequency, the normalized performance (capacity) available, and a pre-computed cost coefficient (cost = 10 × power × max_freq / freq) that allows energy estimation without runtime division. The table is sorted by ascending performance, so the scheduler can binary-search for the lowest state that satisfies the predicted utilization.

A critical design choice is that the EM supports runtime updates via RCU-protected table swaps. On platforms where thermal throttling or dynamic voltage changes alter the available operating points, the kernel can replace the performance state table without stopping the scheduler. The EM also marks inefficient states (EM_PERF_STATE_INEFFICIENT) — states where a higher frequency is available at equal or lower power — and skips them during energy estimation, ensuring the scheduler never recommends a provably suboptimal operating point.

The EM describes each performance domain, typically a set of CPUs that share a frequency and voltage plane. For each operating point, the model records an abstract cost or power value and the capacity available at that point. The scheduler does not need electrical exactness; it needs a monotonic, architecture-provided estimate that lets it compare candidate placements.

/* conceptual EM entry */
struct em_perf_state {
    unsigned long frequency;
    unsigned long power;
    unsigned long cost;
    unsigned long capacity;
};

When evaluating a wakeup, Linux asks: if this task ran on CPU A instead of CPU B, how would each affected performance domain's predicted utilization change, and what power state would that imply? The winning CPU is the one whose total estimated energy is lowest among the CPUs that fit and satisfy affinity.

Not Every Operating Point Is Worth Using

A tacit assumption in the description so far is that running slower saves energy. Real silicon does not always cooperate. When em_dev_compute_costs() builds the table it derives a cost for each state, essentially power / performance, and walks the states in ascending order comparing each against the best cost seen below it. Any state whose cost fails to improve on its predecessor is tagged EM_PERF_STATE_INEFFICIENT. Such states are not a modelling error: voltage must often be raised before frequency can be, so a low OPP can sit at the same voltage as a higher one and burn comparable power while doing less work. Running at the higher frequency finishes sooner and costs less overall.

em_pd_get_efficient_state(), which is what ultimately turns a utilization figure into a power number for find_energy_efficient_cpu(), skips those states when searching for the lowest state that satisfies the requested performance. Without this the scheduler would repeatedly credit placements with savings the hardware cannot deliver.

The gating deserves attention, because the skip is not unconditional. The flag that enables it, EM_PERF_DOMAIN_SKIP_INEFFICIENCIES, is set only after the EM has successfully marked the same frequencies inefficient in the cpufreq policy through cpufreq_table_set_inefficient(). If cpufreq will not accept them, the EM keeps using them. The reasoning is that the scheduler's model must agree with what the governor will actually do: a scheduler that excludes an OPP the governor still selects would be optimizing against a machine that does not exist. The same instinct appears in the min_perf_state and max_perf_state bounds, which keep the model inside the frequency limits currently imposed by the cpufreq policy rather than the full range the hardware advertises.

How find_energy_efficient_cpu() Works

The core EAS algorithm runs entirely within the wakeup path — there is no background thread or periodic scan. Every time a CFS task wakes up and the root domain is not overutilized, select_task_rq_fair() calls find_energy_efficient_cpu() to evaluate whether a different CPU would consume less total energy. This places a hard constraint on algorithmic complexity: the function must complete fast enough that wake-to-run latency remains acceptable even on workloads with thousands of wakeups per second. The design therefore avoids global optimization and instead uses a bounded heuristic that evaluates at most two candidates per performance domain.

The algorithm works by computing energy deltas — the difference in predicted domain energy with and without the task. For each performance domain, it first computes the base energy (energy the PD would consume if the task were removed from its current CPU), then computes the energy with the task hypothetically placed on two candidates: prev_cpu (the task's previous CPU, always evaluated for locality bias) and the max_spare_cap_cpu (the CPU in this PD with the most remaining capacity, since it can absorb the task with the least frequency increase). The candidate whose delta is smallest wins for that PD. Across all PDs, the algorithm tracks the global best. A final three-way comparison decides the winner: a CPU with better fitness always beats one with worse fitness; among equally fitting CPUs, lower energy wins; among equally non-fitting CPUs, higher actual capacity wins (to minimize the misfit penalty).

The energy search is not a brute-force global optimizer. It is a bounded wakeup-time heuristic designed to be cheap enough for the hot path:

  1. enumerate candidate performance domains related to the task's allowed CPUs,
  2. pick representative candidate CPUs within those domains, often favoring idle or previously used CPUs,
  3. estimate post-placement utilization for each affected domain,
  4. map that utilization through the EM to an energy cost, and
  5. choose the CPU with the lowest estimated total cost, subject to fitness and spare-capacity constraints.
/* kernel/sched/fair.c — find_energy_efficient_cpu(), condensed */
target = prev_cpu;

/* A task with no utilization cannot change any domain’s energy. */
sync_entity_load_avg(&p->se);
if (!task_util_est(p) && p_util_min == 0)
    goto unlock;

eenv_task_busy_time(&eenv, p, prev_cpu);   /* the task’s own busy time, once */

for (; pd; pd = pd->next) {                /* once per performance domain */
    cpumask_and(cpus, perf_domain_span(pd), cpu_online_mask);
    eenv.cpu_cap = get_actual_cpu_capacity(cpumask_first(cpus));

    /* Inner loop only *nominates* candidates; it computes no energy. */
    for_each_cpu(cpu, cpus) {
        if (!cpumask_test_cpu(cpu, p->cpus_ptr))
            continue;
        util = cpu_util(cpu, p, cpu, 0);
        fits = util_fits_cpu(util, util_min, util_max, cpu);  /* 1, 0 or -1 */
        if (!fits)
            continue;
        lsub_positive(&cpu_cap, util);     /* spare capacity */
        if (cpu == prev_cpu)
            prev_spare_cap = cpu_cap, prev_fits = fits;
        else if (fits > max_fits || (fits == max_fits && cpu_cap > max_spare_cap))
            max_spare_cap = cpu_cap, max_spare_cap_cpu = cpu, max_fits = fits;
    }

    eenv_pd_busy_time(&eenv, cpus, p);
    base_energy = compute_energy(&eenv, pd, cpus, p, -1);   /* PD without p */

    if (prev_spare_cap > -1) {
        prev_delta = compute_energy(&eenv, pd, cpus, p, prev_cpu) - base_energy;
        best_delta = min(best_delta, prev_delta);
    }
    if (max_spare_cap_cpu >= 0 && max_spare_cap > prev_spare_cap) {
        cur_delta = compute_energy(&eenv, pd, cpus, p, max_spare_cap_cpu)
                    - base_energy;
        if (cur_delta < best_delta)
            best_delta = cur_delta, best_energy_cpu = max_spare_cap_cpu,
            best_fits = max_fits, best_actual_cap = cpu_actual_cap;
    }
}

/* Fitness outranks energy; capacity breaks ties when nothing fits. */
if ((best_fits > prev_fits) ||
    ((best_fits > 0) && (best_delta < prev_delta)) ||
    ((best_fits < 0) && (best_actual_cap > prev_actual_cap)))
    target = best_energy_cpu;

return target;

The algorithm is intentionally conservative. It does not search every CPU equally, and it abandons the energy comparison entirely when a domain is marked overutilized. Once the machine is busy enough, the best energy policy is usually “stop being busy on the wrong CPUs” rather than “micro-optimize cost among already overloaded ones.”

Capacity-Aware Wakeup versus Energy-Aware Wakeup

It is easy to conflate "capacity-aware" with "energy-aware" because both consider CPU heterogeneity. But they answer fundamentally different questions and can produce opposite results. Capacity-aware placement asks "which CPU has enough headroom to run this task without becoming saturated?" — it optimizes for performance by avoiding overloaded CPUs. Energy-aware placement asks "among the CPUs that can serve this task adequately, which one does so at the lowest power cost?" — it optimizes for efficiency by preferring slower cores when they suffice. On a lightly loaded big.LITTLE system, capacity-aware logic might spread a new task to an idle big core (maximum spare capacity), while EAS would place it on a LITTLE core (lower energy, sufficient capacity). The two strategies converge only under heavy load, where every placement decision is dominated by finding any CPU with remaining headroom.

Linux runs both strategies but not simultaneously. When EAS prerequisites are met and the root domain is not overutilized, the energy-aware path runs first and its result is final — the capacity-aware fallback is never consulted. When EAS is disabled or the system enters the overutilized state, the code falls through to the capacity-aware path (wake-affine + idle-sibling search). This clean separation means there is no ambiguous blending: at any given wakeup, exactly one strategy makes the placement decision.

Table 7-38. Capacity-Aware versus Energy-Aware Wakeup
ModeMain questionTypical outcome
Capacity-aware wakeupWhich CPU can run this task with enough spare capacity and good locality?Often prefers an idle or cache-local CPU, especially under pressure or when EAS prerequisites are absent
Energy-aware wakeupAmong CPUs that can run the task well, which placement minimizes predicted energy?Often keeps light work on efficient cores and reserves big cores for heavier or latency-critical tasks

The distinction is practical. Capacity-aware wakeup is always available and forms the baseline. EAS is an extra optimization layer only when the machine is cool enough, informed enough, and asymmetric enough for the estimate to be worth its cost.

Misfit Task Detection and Migration

EAS places tasks proactively at wakeup time — but what happens when a task's demand grows after placement? A thread might start with low utilization (PELT tracks history, and a newly-woken task has decayed signals), get placed on a LITTLE core by EAS, then enter a CPU-intensive phase where its utilization ramps up beyond what the LITTLE core can deliver. Without a reactive mechanism, this task would be permanently trapped on an insufficient CPU, because periodic load balancing compares load across CPUs — it does not inherently know that a CPU is too small for its current task. Misfit detection fills this gap by explicitly flagging the condition "this task needs more capacity than this CPU can ever provide."

The detection logic lives in update_misfit_status(), called from the scheduler tick path. It checks three escape conditions: if the task's affinity pins it to this CPU alone (nr_cpus_allowed == 1), if the current CPU already has the highest capacity the task is allowed to reach (arch_scale_cpu_capacity(cpu) == p->max_allowed_capacity), or if the task actually fits (task_fits_cpu() returns true). If none apply, the runqueue's misfit_task_load field is set to the task's hierarchical load. The load balancer then sees this as a group_misfit_task imbalance type and selects migrate_misfit as the migration strategy — which specifically targets moving this task to a CPU with higher capacity rather than merely equalizing load counts.

A misfit task is a task whose effective utilization persistently exceeds the capacity of its current CPU. The classic case is a CPU-bound thread stuck on a LITTLE core. Even at maximum frequency, that core cannot provide the requested throughput, so latency rises and total energy can actually worsen because the task runs longer.

task_tick_fair() participates in detecting this state. When the current task looks too large for the CPU, the kernel marks the runqueue so that balancing will try to migrate the task toward a bigger core. This is one of the places where EAS and generic balancing intersect: misfit handling is not merely an energy optimization; it is a correctness and performance repair mechanism.

Interaction with the schedutil Governor

Traditional cpufreq governors (ondemand, conservative) sample CPU idle time from a timer callback and react after the fact — often too late for bursty workloads and with no coordination with the scheduler's placement decisions. schedutil fundamentally changed this relationship: it is not a periodic sampler but a callback-driven governor that receives utilization updates directly from the scheduler via cpufreq_update_util(). Every time the scheduler updates PELT signals (task enqueue, dequeue, tick), it fires this hook, giving schedutil a real-time view of demand. The governor then maps the CPU's aggregate utilization to a target frequency using the same signal the scheduler used for placement — creating a closed-loop system where placement and frequency co-evolve.

This tight coupling is what makes EAS predictions reliable. When find_energy_efficient_cpu() estimates that placing a task on a CPU will produce a certain utilization level, it implicitly assumes that schedutil will respond by selecting the corresponding frequency from the Energy Model. If a different governor were active — one that ignores utilization and pins frequency to maximum — the energy prediction would be meaningless (the CPU would always run at max power regardless of placement). This is why schedutil is a hard prerequisite for EAS: the two subsystems form a single feedback loop where the scheduler controls where work runs and schedutil controls how fast it runs, both driven by the same PELT signal.

EAS depends on schedutil because both subsystems consume the same PELT util_avg signal. The scheduler picks a CPU partly by predicting future utilization, and schedutil raises or lowers that CPU's frequency based on the same estimate. Without that shared signal, the scheduler might place a task assuming one frequency response while the governor chooses another.

This is also why frequency invariance matters. A utilization of 400 must mean roughly the same amount of demanded work whether it was observed on a LITTLE core at one frequency or a big core at another. Only then can EAS compare candidate CPUs meaningfully.

Asymmetric Packing and Hybrid Systems

Symmetric multiprocessing assumes that any CPU is interchangeable with any other, so the scheduler's only concern is to spread work evenly. Heterogeneous hardware breaks that assumption: some CPUs are designed for efficiency and others for performance. Merely balancing load across them wastes energy when demand is light — big cores sit at idle voltages but still draw leakage power — and wastes performance when demand spikes — small cores saturate while big cores remain underused. Asymmetric packing is the scheduling policy that resolves this tension: during light workloads, cluster tasks onto the most efficient cores and let performance cores power-gate; when a task's demand exceeds what a small core can deliver, migrate it to a big core immediately. The goal is not fairness across CPUs but optimal use of each CPU's design point.

The kernel implements asymmetric packing through two complementary mechanisms. The first is SD_ASYM_CPUCAPACITY, a domain flag set when CPUs within a scheduling domain have different compute capacities. When this flag is present, the load balancer adds capacity-aware logic: it detects misfit tasks on small cores, considers capacity headroom when selecting the busiest group, and prefers to pull work toward higher-capacity CPUs when they are idle. The second mechanism is SD_ASYM_PACKING, which assigns explicit priority ordering to CPUs via arch_asym_cpu_priority(). The balancer treats a higher-priority idle CPU as a valid migration target even when load is balanced, pulling work from lower-priority CPUs. On x86 hybrid processors, SD_ASYM_PACKING drives the preference for P-cores over E-cores: the architecture assigns P-cores higher priority, so the balancer naturally packs work onto them first and only spills onto E-cores when P-cores are busy.

Heterogeneous systems do not always want to spread work evenly. Sometimes they want asymmetric packing: keep light work clustered on efficient CPUs and leave high-performance cores idle until a task truly needs them. At other times the opposite is true: latency-sensitive work should prefer performance cores immediately. Linux supports both behaviors through the combination of capacity fitting, utilization clamping, wakeup heuristics, and energy estimation.

On x86 hybrid processors the story is similar in spirit but different in detail. SMT, firmware-driven performance hints, and platform-specific priority schemes often reduce how often full EAS applies. Capacity-aware placement, hybrid CPU priorities, and schedutil-like frequency response may dominate even when a complete ARM-style EM-based EAS path is not available for every domain.

Table 7-39. EAS-Oriented Comparison: ARM64 big.LITTLE versus x86 Hybrid
PropertyARM64 big.LITTLEx86 hybrid (P-core / E-core)
Capacity asymmetryUsually explicit and large; central to mobile SoC designPresent, but often combined with SMT and firmware-guided priority hints
Energy Model availabilityCommon on platforms designed around EASMore platform dependent; capacity-aware heuristics may be used even without full EM-based placement everywhere
Typical scheduler goalKeep light tasks on LITTLE cores, migrate heavy or urgent work to big coresPrefer P-cores for throughput/latency-critical tasks while using E-cores for background parallelism and efficiency
Common blocker for full EASMissing EM or freq invarianceSMT interactions, firmware policy, or incomplete energy-model coverage

What Happens When the System Becomes Overutilized?

The overutilized transition is the kernel's safety valve for EAS. Under light load, EAS optimizes for energy — it tries every performance domain, computes energy deltas, and picks the globally cheapest placement. That search is only valid when spare capacity exists everywhere and the scheduler can freely move tasks between efficiency and performance cores. Once any CPU in the root domain reports that its utilization no longer fits its capacity (checked via cpu_overutilized() using util_fits_cpu() with uclamp bounds), the root domain's overutilized flag is set. From that point, select_task_rq_fair() skips the call to find_energy_efficient_cpu() entirely and falls through to the traditional wake-affine and load-balancing path.

The transition is deliberately sticky in one direction: any single overutilized CPU sets the flag immediately, but clearing it requires the periodic load balancer to scan the entire domain and confirm that every CPU has sufficient headroom. This asymmetry prevents rapid toggling under bursty workloads — the system remains in performance-first mode until it genuinely settles back to light load. While the flag is set, the load balancer also ignores the energy model and focuses purely on distributing load for throughput, pulling work away from saturated CPUs and toward any CPU with spare cycles.

EAS is intentionally a light-load optimization. Once the root domain is marked overutilized, the scheduler stops assuming that minimizing energy on the next wakeup is the dominant goal. At that point spare capacity is scarce, misfit migration becomes urgent, and wake placement shifts toward "find a CPU that can run this now" rather than "find the globally cheapest CPU."

This fallback behavior is easy to miss when reading only the high-level algorithm, but it is critical in practice. Without it, an energy-aware policy could keep trying to pack tasks onto efficient cores long after that packing stopped being efficient in any real sense.

Worked Placement Example
