To see how these mechanisms interact in practice, consider the complete decision path for a single wakeup on a phone SoC with four Cortex-A55 LITTLE cores (capacity 446) and four Cortex-A78 big cores (capacity 1024). The energy model reports that a LITTLE core at 1.8 GHz consumes roughly 100 mW while a big core at the same utilization level consumes 280 mW — but the big core delivers 2.3× more throughput per watt at high utilization where the LITTLE core would saturate. When a task wakes, find_energy_efficient_cpu() iterates over both performance domains: for each, it computes base_energy (current domain energy without the task), then adds the task's utilization to the candidate CPU and recomputes energy via em_cpu_energy(). The energy delta (new − base) for each domain tells the scheduler which placement costs less in milliwatts.

Consider a phone SoC with four LITTLE cores and two big cores. A background sync thread with util_avg = 80 wakes while a LITTLE core is idle; EAS usually keeps it there because the EM predicts low energy and the task clearly fits. A UI render thread with util_avg = 500 waking under latency pressure is different: capacity filtering may eliminate LITTLE cores first, after which the energy comparison is really between the available big cores and their recent locality.

That example shows the proper role of EAS: it is neither "always choose the LITTLE core" nor "always choose the CPU with lowest instantaneous power." It is "choose the lowest-energy placement among placements that still satisfy the task's performance needs."

Utilization Clamping and EAS

Without external hints, the scheduler bases all placement and frequency decisions on historical utilization — the PELT-computed util_avg. But history alone cannot express intent: a task that has been sleeping for seconds has near-zero utilization, yet user-space knows it will need to render the next frame within 16 ms. Conversely, a background compression thread may consume all available cycles but does not need high performance. Utilization clamping (uclamp) bridges this gap by allowing user-space or system services to attach minimum and maximum utilization bounds to individual tasks or task groups via sched_setattr() or the cgroup cpu.uclamp.min/cpu.uclamp.max knobs. The scheduler treats the clamped utilization as the effective demand for both CPU selection and frequency selection.

Inside find_energy_efficient_cpu(), uclamp participates directly: the per-CPU aggregated uclamp_min and uclamp_max are passed to util_fits_cpu() to determine whether a candidate CPU can satisfy the task's performance floor without exceeding its capacity ceiling. A task with uclamp_min = 600 will never be placed on a LITTLE core whose capacity is 446 — even if its historical util_avg is only 200. Similarly, uclamp_max = 300 on a background task tells the scheduler that the task needs no more than 300 units of capacity, allowing EAS to keep it on an efficient core and instructing schedutil to cap the frequency accordingly. The combination of EAS energy prediction plus uclamp policy hints gives the scheduler a vocabulary for expressing both "this task must be fast" and "this task must be cheap."

Utilization clamping further refines this picture. A latency-sensitive task may request a higher effective minimum utilization, making the scheduler treat it as if it needs a bigger CPU or faster frequency even before raw history alone would justify that choice. Conversely, a capped background task may look small enough to remain on efficient cores longer.

That interaction is a reminder that EAS is not only about hardware asymmetry. It is also about policy hints layered onto demand estimation: affinity masks, uclamp, thermal pressure, and recent utilization all shape what "energy efficient" actually means for a given task at a given instant.

Thermal pressure feeds into the same placement story. A CPU that is architecturally “big” may temporarily behave like a smaller CPU under thermal throttling, so EAS and capacity fitting must react to delivered capacity, not just nominal hardware labels.

Asymmetric Packing in Practice

The packing behavior emerges from the interplay of several mechanisms, not from a single "pack here" instruction. At wakeup time, find_energy_efficient_cpu() naturally favors efficient cores because the energy model predicts lower milliwatts for them when utilization is low — no explicit packing rule is needed. During periodic load balancing, the group_asym_packing classification triggers when an idle high-priority CPU detects that a lower-priority CPU still has runnable tasks. The balancer then sets migration_type = migrate_task and imbalance = sum_h_nr_running, meaning it will try to pull all runnable tasks from the lower-priority group — an aggressive migration that quickly consolidates work onto the preferred cores. On ARM64 big.LITTLE, this means tasks migrate toward big cores when they are idle; on x86 hybrid, it means P-cores pull work from E-cores whenever they have spare capacity.

Observing this behavior on a real system is straightforward. On an idle phone, /proc/stat shows LITTLE cores accumulating almost all user-space ticks while big cores show near-zero activity. Launch a demanding application and within a few hundred milliseconds the pattern inverts: big cores absorb the render and compute threads while LITTLE cores handle only light background services. The transition back to packing is slower — the overutilized flag must clear first, requiring the load balancer to confirm that every CPU in the root domain has headroom — so the system errs on the side of performance for a few extra balance intervals before re-entering energy-saving mode.

Asymmetric packing is the visible user-facing effect of EAS. During light background activity, Linux tries to keep work clustered on efficient CPUs so that larger cores can stay idle or at low frequency. During bursts, the scheduler relaxes that packing quickly and spreads urgent work toward higher-capacity CPUs.

The result is a machine that feels contradictory in a good way: frugal when demand is low, but willing to spend energy immediately when latency or throughput demands it. That “save when you can, spend when you must” posture is exactly what heterogeneous hardware was built to exploit.

For readers tracing the code, this also explains why many EAS decisions appear inside the fair wakeup path rather than in a separate "energy scheduler." Energy is a placement criterion layered onto ordinary wakeup and balancing machinery, not a parallel subsystem.

That is why EAS reads less like a new class and more like a new cost function for existing placement decisions.

It succeeds when that extra cost model disappears into ordinary wakeup behavior and users only notice better battery life or lower thermals.

Energy-aware scheduling is therefore best understood as a policy overlay on top of the ordinary scheduler, not as a replacement for it. PELT supplies demand, capacity fitting filters impossible placements, the Energy Model ranks the remaining choices, and classic balancing still takes over when the machine becomes overloaded.

Special Features

The previous sections described the scheduling classes (STOP, Deadline, RT, CFS, idle) and the infrastructure that supports them (PELT, load balancing, EAS). Together, these components decide which task runs next and where it runs. But modern Linux kernels face requirements that no single scheduling class can address alone: hardware-level security threats from SMT side channels, desktop interactivity that suffers when all terminal windows share one cgroup, system-wide pressure signals that user-space daemons need for memory-reclaim decisions, and power management that must adjust CPU frequency thousands of times per second. These concerns cut across all scheduling classes and cannot be solved by adding yet another sched_class.

Linux 7.0.12 addresses them with a set of cross-cutting features — modular subsystems that hook into the scheduler's existing decision points rather than replacing them. Each feature is implemented in its own source file under kernel/sched/ (core_sched.c, autogroup.c, psi.c, cpufreq_schedutil.c, and the uclamp/cgroup machinery in core.c and fair.c) and interacts with the core scheduler through well-defined hooks: task enqueue/dequeue callbacks, context-switch notifications, periodic tick handlers, and runqueue-state queries. This design means that disabling any single feature — via Kconfig, boot parameter, or sysctl — removes its overhead without affecting the rest of the scheduler.

The main scheduling classes explain who runs next, but Linux 7.0.12 also layers several cross-cutting features on top of that core machinery. Some of them exist for security, some for desktop responsiveness, some for observability, and some for power management; all of them modify how the scheduler interprets runnable work without inventing an entirely new policy class.

Version history. Every feature in this section postdates Linux 2.6.11, so there is no 2.6.11 counterpart to compare against. Autogroup is the oldest, added in 2.6.38, and is the only one that fits the 2.6 model — it changes fair grouping and nothing else. The rest answer questions the 2.6 kernel was not asked. schedutil (4.7) exists because frequency selection turned out to belong to the scheduler rather than to a sampling governor beside it; PSI (4.20) because load average cannot distinguish work being done from work waiting; utilization clamping (5.3) because heterogeneous CPUs made "how fast does this task need to run" a question userspace could usefully answer; and core scheduling (5.14) because speculative-execution attacks made SMT co-residency a security boundary, which is not a scheduling concern any earlier kernel would have recognised. Read together they show the scheduler acquiring responsibilities — power, observability, isolation — that in Linux 2.6.11 belonged to other subsystems or to nobody.

These features are easiest to understand if you read them as overlays. Core scheduling constrains which SMT siblings may run together. Autogroup changes the fair scheduler's grouping granularity for interactive sessions. PSI exports contention as a time-based signal that user space can act on. schedutil turns PELT utilization into frequency requests. Utilization clamping and cgroup v2 CPU controls then let administrators bias those decisions without rewriting the scheduler itself.

Core Scheduling (SMT Security)

Before core scheduling existed, administrators who wanted to eliminate SMT side channels had only one option: disable hyperthreading entirely via the nosmt boot parameter, sacrificing up to 20–30 % throughput on server workloads. Core scheduling offers a middle ground. It keeps SMT enabled for mutually trusted tasks — threads of the same application, pods in the same security domain, or processes that share a cookie — while preventing untrusted pairs from ever executing simultaneously on sibling hardware threads. The feature is especially important in multi-tenant environments (cloud VMs, container orchestrators) where disabling SMT is too expensive but cross-tenant side-channel leakage is unacceptable.

The scheduling decision itself is more expensive than ordinary CFS picking because it must coordinate across all SMT siblings atomically. When one sibling calls into __schedule(), the core scheduling path in core.c takes the runqueue lock for every sibling, iterates each class's pick_task() method, and searches for a compatible set: all siblings must either run tasks with the same cookie or be forced idle. The kernel tracks this with three sequence numbers — core_task_seq (incremented on enqueue/dequeue), core_pick_seq (the task_seq at which a selection was made), and core_sched_seq (the pick_seq that was actually scheduled) — to avoid redundant cross-sibling coordination when the runqueue state hasn't changed. If no compatible task exists for a sibling, it runs the idle thread, accepting a throughput loss to preserve isolation.

Simultaneous multithreading improves throughput by letting two hardware threads share one physical core, but that same sharing exposes side channels. Attacks such as L1TF, MDS, TAA, and several Spectre-family variants exploit the fact that sibling threads share predictor state, private caches, fill buffers, and execution resources. Traditional scheduling only reasons about logical CPUs; it does not ask whether the task on CPU 4 and the task on CPU 5 are mutually trusted when those logical CPUs are really two threads of the same core.

Core scheduling adds exactly that missing question. Instead of treating each SMT sibling independently, the scheduler tries to co-schedule only tasks that carry the same core_cookie. If CPU 4 is about to run a task with cookie A, the sibling thread may run another task with cookie A, or it may be forced idle. It may not run a task with cookie B, because that would recreate the cross-thread information leak the feature was designed to block.

The key implementation detail in kernel/sched/core_sched.c is that a cookie is not just an integer tag. It is a refcounted heap object allocated on demand, and each task's core_cookie stores the pointer value of that shared object. That design makes inheritance and sharing cheap: two tasks are compatible if their pointers match. It also lets the kernel manage lifetimes correctly when tasks exit, cookies are shared across thread groups, or a caller explicitly drops access to a cookie.

Changing a task's cookie is more intrusive than flipping a flag. The scheduler must dequeue the task from any runqueue where it is currently visible, update the cookie pointer, enqueue it again under the new compatibility regime, and potentially trigger rescheduling so sibling CPUs reevaluate whether the current pair is still legal. That dequeue/re-enqueue plus resched pattern is the central cost of dynamic cookie management.

Core scheduling is a mitigation, not a magic eraser. It narrows one class of SMT leakage by controlling co-tenancy on sibling threads, but it does not replace microcode fixes, speculation barriers, or application-level isolation for highly sensitive workloads.

Table 7-40. PR_SCHED_CORE Operations and Their Meaning
OperationUser-space intentKernel actionTypical use
PR_SCHED_CORE_GETRead a task's current cookieLookup target task, copy the cookie identity to user space, and take the needed references while readingIntrospection or verifying that a share operation succeeded
PR_SCHED_CORE_CREATECreate a fresh cookie for the caller or target scopeAllocate a new refcounted cookie object, attach it to the selected tasks, and reschedule affected siblingsIsolate a service, container, or login session from unrelated SMT peers
PR_SCHED_CORE_SHARE_TOPush the caller's cookie into another task or scopeWalk target tasks through sched_core_share_pid(), get the caller's cookie, then update each targetMake worker processes mutually trusted so they may still co-run on one core
PR_SCHED_CORE_SHARE_FROMPull a cookie from another task into the caller or scopeResolve the source task's cookie, bump the refcount, then apply it to the destination tasksJoin an existing trust domain after exec or supervisor handoff
PR_SCHED_CORE_SCOPE_THREAD, _THREAD_GROUP, _PROCESS_GROUPSelect how far the operation propagatesTranslate the PID/scope pair into one task, one thread group, or one process groupChoose whether to isolate one thread, one process, or a cooperating batch

The user-space API is deliberately exposed through prctl() rather than a new scheduler-specific syscall. That matches the feature's role: it is not a new policy class with priorities and timeslices, but a protection attribute that constrains otherwise normal scheduling. The important operations are get, create, share-to, and share-from; scopes decide whether the action applies to one thread, an entire thread group, or a process group.

#define _GNU_SOURCE
#include <errno.h>
#include <linux/prctl.h>
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <sys/prctl.h>
#include <unistd.h>

static int create_cookie_for_process(void)
{
    return prctl(PR_SCHED_CORE,
                 PR_SCHED_CORE_CREATE,
                 0,
                 PR_SCHED_CORE_SCOPE_THREAD_GROUP,
                 0);
}

static int get_cookie(pid_t pid, unsigned long long *cookie)
{
    return prctl(PR_SCHED_CORE,
                 PR_SCHED_CORE_GET,
                 pid,
                 PR_SCHED_CORE_SCOPE_THREAD,
                 cookie);
}

int main(void)
{
    unsigned long long cookie = 0;

    if (create_cookie_for_process() < 0) {
        fprintf(stderr, "PR_SCHED_CORE_CREATE failed: %s\n", strerror(errno));
        return 1;
    }

    if (get_cookie(0, &cookie) < 0) {
        fprintf(stderr, "PR_SCHED_CORE_GET failed: %s\n", strerror(errno));
        return 1;
    }

    printf("current thread cookie: 0x%llx\n", cookie);
    return 0;
}

The share operations are where the kernel's internal refcount discipline matters. sched_core_share_pid() resolves the source and destination task sets, gets a stable reference to the source cookie, and then updates each destination task one by one. Because cookies are objects rather than raw integers, the kernel can drop the old cookie only after the new one is installed and all participating tasks are safely requeued.

Forced idle is the feature's visible trade-off. If one sibling has security-sensitive work and no compatible peer exists for the other sibling, the core cannot reach full SMT throughput. On workloads that naturally mix many unrelated tenants on the same package, that lost sibling capacity often costs roughly 10-30% throughput, though the exact penalty depends on how often compatible peers exist and how much the workload actually benefits from SMT in the first place.

That trade-off explains when core scheduling is preferable to disabling SMT entirely. Disabling SMT removes the side channel by never using the sibling thread, which is simpler but throws away capacity all the time. Core scheduling keeps SMT available for trusted pairings such as threads in one JVM, workers in one web service, or tasks inside one VM. Systems that run a small number of large trusted applications often recover much of SMT's throughput that way. Systems hosting many short-lived untrusted tenants may still choose to disable SMT because forced-idle frequency approaches the fully-disabled case anyway.

Administrators should also remember that the feature is topology-sensitive. It only matters on CPUs where multiple logical CPUs share one core. On non-SMT systems there is nothing to co-schedule, so cookies become irrelevant. On hybrid systems, the scheduler must also combine cookie legality with ordinary affinity, capacity, and energy constraints; a legal cookie match is necessary, but it does not override cpuset restrictions or make an overloaded CPU suddenly attractive.

How the Siblings Are Made to Agree

Co-scheduling only compatible tasks sounds like a filter applied during picking, but it cannot be implemented that way. Two SMT siblings run __schedule() independently, on different CPUs, at unrelated moments; a decision made by one is worthless if the other may simultaneously choose a task with a different cookie. The siblings must agree, and agreement requires a shared serialization point.

Linux obtains one by changing the locking topology at runtime. Each rq gains a core pointer identifying the leader runqueue of its physical core, and the accessor every scheduler path uses to find a runqueue's lock reads:

/* kernel/sched/sched.h */
static inline raw_spinlock_t *rq_lockp(struct rq *rq)
{
    if (sched_core_enabled(rq))
        return &rq->core->__lock;

    return &rq->__lock;
}

When core scheduling is active, taking "the runqueue lock" of any CPU on a core takes the same lock for every sibling. The siblings are thereby serialized against each other for free, and the picking code can select for the whole core at once and publish the result to each sibling. This is why the section above describes the decision as more expensive than ordinary picking: the cost is not extra comparisons but a coarser lock, since two threads on one core can no longer schedule in parallel.

It also explains why core scheduling is enabled globally rather than per task. The static key that sched_core_enabled() tests is switched on when the first cookie in the system is created and off when the last one is released, and the transition has to stop all the affected runqueues to swap locks safely. A per-task on/off switch would mean changing the meaning of the scheduler's most contended lock while it was being taken.

Measuring the Cost

Because the price of core scheduling is paid in idle cycles rather than in latency or throughput counters, it is easy to deploy the feature and never learn what it cost. The kernel accounts it explicitly: when a sibling is idled for want of a cookie-compatible task, the time is added to core_forceidle_sum in the victim's scheduler statistics, readable per task through /proc/<pid>/sched on kernels built with CONFIG_SCHEDSTATS and with schedstats enabled at runtime. The runqueue side keeps core_forceidle_count and core_forceidle_start so the current occupancy of the core is known without scanning.

There is no forced-idle-specific hook in PSI, but the cost is visible there all the same, because a sibling is forced idle precisely when its runqueue holds runnable tasks that may not be co-scheduled. Those tasks are runnable and not running, which is the definition of CPU some pressure, so a machine paying heavily for core scheduling reports stall time that no utilization figure explains. That combination — CPU pressure with idle CPUs — is usually how the cost is first noticed in production, and core_forceidle_sum is what confirms the diagnosis. The practical guidance follows directly: if forced-idle time approaches the throughput disabling SMT would forfeit, core scheduling has stopped being the cheaper mitigation and nosmt is the simpler answer.

Autogroup (Per-Session Grouping)

Autogroup is a scheduler feature whose goal is almost the opposite of core scheduling. Instead of enforcing isolation for security, it reshapes fair-class competition so that interactive work remains responsive in the presence of process explosions. The classic desktop problem is familiar: a user starts make -j64 in one terminal, and suddenly every window, browser tab, and compositor feels sticky because the scheduler sees dozens of CPU-bound compiler tasks and only a handful of UI threads.

Without grouping, each compiler thread is an independent fair entity competing directly with the desktop's threads. With autogroup enabled, the scheduler automatically creates a task group for each session and attaches fair tasks in that session to the group's task_group. The compile farm still competes fairly inside its own session, but at the top level it competes as one session against the login shell, the graphical session, or other terminals.

In Linux 7.0.12 this logic lives in kernel/sched/autogroup.c. autogroup_create() allocates a new autogroup structure and an associated task group. When a new session appears, typically through setsid(), the scheduler can create or discover the appropriate autogroup and then move tasks into it with autogroup_move_group(). The movement is not purely cosmetic: changing task groups changes which cfs_rq hierarchy a task contributes to and therefore how shares are interpreted.

Table 7-41. Autogroup Rules in Practice
QuestionAnswer in Linux 7.0.12Why it matters
When is a group created?When a new session needs one, typically around the setsid()-driven session boundaryInteractive sessions become first-class competitors rather than giant piles of individual tasks
Which tasks participate?Fair-class tasks in the sessionDesktop, shell, and batch jobs gain separation without affecting every class equally
What about RT or deadline tasks?They are effectively redirected to the root task group for scheduling purposesHard-priority classes must not inherit a desktop-oriented share model that could violate their semantics
How is priority expressed?A group nice value maps to CFS shares for the whole sessionOne knob can raise or lower the aggregate weight of all tasks in the session
Can user-managed cgroups override it?Yes; explicit cgroup placement is a stronger administrative policyAutogroup is a convenience default, not a replacement for resource control

The group nice knob is exposed through /proc/[pid]/autogroup. The file reports the autogroup identity and current nice setting, and writing a new nice value invokes proc_sched_autogroup_set_nice(). Internally the kernel converts that nice value into the same share weights used elsewhere in CFS hierarchies. As a result, changing the autogroup nice affects the aggregate share of the whole session rather than the static priority of one process.

That distinction is subtle but important. A task nice value answers “how much of the CPU should this task receive relative to its siblings?” An autogroup nice value answers “how much of the CPU should this session receive relative to other sessions?” Inside the session, per-task nice still matters. The desktop therefore gains a two-level hierarchy: sessions compete as groups, then tasks inside each session compete according to ordinary fair-class rules.

Autogroup improves the common single-user desktop case, but it is intentionally lightweight. If an administrator already uses cgroup v2 to partition workloads by service, container, or tenant, those explicit cgroup boundaries are usually the more important control surface.

One practical way to think about autogroup is that it fixes an old mismatch between Unix process trees and human expectations. Users think in terms of “my terminal”, “my browser”, and “my music player”, not in terms of the exact number of worker threads each application forks. Autogroup moves the fair scheduler closer to that mental model without forcing administrators to design cgroup layouts by hand.

There are also clear limits. Autogroup does not create hard quotas. A CPU-hungry session can still consume most of the machine if no competing session needs the CPU. Conversely, if a service manager already placed a process tree into a cgroup with cpu.max or a custom cpu.weight, the cgroup hierarchy dominates. The session group then becomes either redundant or nested inside a broader policy boundary.

Pressure Stall Information (PSI)

CPU load averages tell you that work exists; PSI tells you that work is waiting. That difference is why PSI has become one of the most useful scheduler-adjacent interfaces added in the last several kernel generations. A load average of 8 on an eight-core machine might mean perfect saturation with no user-visible pain, or it might mean sustained backlog with tasks regularly stalled. PSI turns that ambiguity into time-based measurements.

The model has two central terms: SOME and FULL. For CPU pressure, some means that at least one runnable task is waiting because no CPU is currently available. Full is stricter: it means all non-idle productive work is stalled, so the system has reached a point where there is effectively no forward progress to schedule without more CPU time becoming available. Memory and I/O pressure make this distinction particularly intuitive, but it is still meaningful for CPU saturation on overloaded systems.

Consider three examples. If one background compile thread is queued behind seven running threads on an eight-core system, CPU SOME time increases because at least one runnable task is waiting. If all cores are busy and every task that wants to run is queued behind somebody else, CPU FULL also increases because the entire productive set is stalled. If half the machine is idle, neither SOME nor FULL should rise because runnable tasks are not being denied CPU service.

The kernel computes PSI from local observations and then aggregates them. Each CPU tracks scheduler-visible stall states in its runqueue context; periodic aggregation rolls those per-CPU deltas into global pressure statistics exposed through /proc/pressure/* and cgroup-local pressure files such as cpu.pressure. This design keeps the hot path lightweight while still producing system-wide time series.

Table 7-42. /proc/pressure/cpu Fields
FieldExample tokenMeaningOperational use
somesome avg10=2.31 avg60=1.84 avg300=0.77 total=41819234At least one runnable task could not obtain CPU timeDetect backlog before the machine becomes completely throughput-bound
fullfull avg10=0.21 avg60=0.08 avg300=0.01 total=287004All productive work was stalled waiting for CPUDetect severe saturation and trigger shedding or throttling
avg10avg10=2.31Exponential moving average over 10 seconds, expressed as percentage of wall timeFast feedback loops
avg60avg60=1.84Same idea over 60 secondsShort-term trend smoothing
avg300avg300=0.77Same idea over 300 secondsLonger trend detection
totaltotal=41819234Cumulative stall time in microseconds since bootAccurate accounting for custom monitoring and trigger baselines

Those averages are not sample counts; they are fractions of time. If avg10=12.50 on the some line, then during the last ten seconds the system spent about 12.5% of wall-clock time with at least one runnable task waiting for CPU. That interpretation is precisely why higher-level tools like systemd-oomd and Android's LMKD can use PSI as an action signal. They do not need to guess whether “load 30” is bad on this particular machine; they can act when stall time crosses a threshold.

#include <errno.h>
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <string.h>

int main(void)
{
    FILE *fp = fopen("/proc/pressure/cpu", "r");
    char line[256];

    if (!fp) {
        perror("fopen");
        return 1;
    }

    while (fgets(line, sizeof(line), fp))
        fputs(line, stdout);

    if (ferror(fp)) {
        fprintf(stderr, "read error: %s\n", strerror(errno));
        fclose(fp);
        return 1;
    }

    fclose(fp);
    return 0;
}

PSI also supports event triggers. A process opens the pressure file, writes a trigger specification such as “wake me when CPU SOME exceeds X microseconds within Y microseconds”, and then waits with poll() or epoll(). That mechanism is intentionally efficient: rather than scraping text every second, user-space daemons can sleep until the kernel detects sustained contention. Android LMKD uses this style of signal to react before the device feels irrecoverably janky, and systemd-oomd uses the analogous memory pressure interfaces to decide when reclaim is failing.

Because PSI is exported both globally and per-cgroup, it also closes the observability gap introduced by hierarchical scheduling. A whole machine may look healthy while one service's cpu.pressure file shows constant SOME time because its cgroup weight is too low. Conversely, rising system-wide CPU pressure with low per-service pressure often indicates a placement or topology issue rather than a single abusive tenant.

CPU Frequency Integration (schedutil)

Old cpufreq governors such as ondemand periodically sampled CPU usage and then decided whether to raise or lower frequency. That worked reasonably well on homogeneous servers, but it created lag: the scheduler already knew a CPU had become busy, yet the frequency governor only noticed after the next sample window. The schedutil governor removes most of that lag by wiring frequency selection directly into scheduler utilization updates. This section covers the scheduler's side of that contract; the cpufreq core, its policies, and the hardware drivers beneath the governor are described in Chapter 21, §7.

The fundamental input is PELT utilization, especially util_avg. Every time the scheduler updates runnable or running time for a CPU, it may invoke cpufreq_update_util(). If the active governor is schedutil, that hook eventually lands in either sugov_update_single() or sugov_update_shared(). The single-CPU path handles policies where each CPU has its own frequency domain; the shared path handles clusters that must change frequency together because they share a PLL or hardware power domain.

The governor's target is conceptually simple: choose a frequency proportional to recent utilization, scaled by the CPU's maximum effective capacity, with a bit of headroom so the CPU can absorb short bursts without immediately missing latency goals. The exact arithmetic varies by architecture and driver, but the mental model is:

  • measure demand with PELT util_avg,
  • clamp that demand with uclamp and policy limits,
  • scale it by the CPU or policy maximum capacity, and
  • translate the result into the nearest legal frequency.

Rate limiting is important because hardware frequency changes are not free. A governor that reacted to every tiny utilization fluctuation would spend too much time bouncing PLLs, burning energy, and fighting firmware. schedutil therefore maintains per-policy timing data so that frequency updates happen quickly enough for responsiveness but not so often that transitions dominate the workload. This is one reason the hot path records intent first and lets the cpufreq layer enforce hardware-safe cadence.

Thermal throttling complicates the story further. When thermal pressure reduces the CPU's available capacity, the scheduler sees a smaller effective maximum. A task whose util_avg once represented 50% of a cool CPU may now represent 70% of a throttled CPU. At the same time, the cpufreq driver or thermal framework may cap the maximum frequency outright. The result is that placement, capacity fitting, and frequency control all respond to the same thermal event from different angles.

Table 7-43. cpufreq Governor Comparison from a Scheduler Perspective
GovernorMain signalStrengthWeakness
schedutilScheduler PELT utilizationLow latency, topology-aware, naturally aligned with EAS and uclampDepends on accurate scheduler accounting and policy tuning
ondemandPeriodic load samplingSimple and historically widespreadSampling lag and weaker integration with wakeup bursts
performanceAlways request maximum frequencyPredictable peak performanceHigh energy cost and unnecessary thermals for bursty work
powersaveBias toward minimum frequencyLow energy use on sustained light workloadsPoor latency under sudden demand

Compared with ondemand, schedutil is much better suited to heterogeneous systems. EAS already reasons about the energy cost of placing a task on one CPU versus another. schedutil then uses the same scheduler-side demand signal to choose a frequency inside the selected domain. That shared accounting base avoids the old “placement logic thinks one thing, frequency logic thinks another” split.

What the Governor Actually Measures

Describing the input as "PELT utilization" is a useful simplification, but it is a simplification, and on a system with real-time or interrupt load it is the wrong one. Frequency selection does not read cfs_rq utilization; it calls effective_cpu_util(), which assembles a picture of everything competing for the CPU:

/* kernel/sched/fair.c — effective_cpu_util(), condensed */
scale = arch_scale_cpu_capacity(cpu);
irq   = cpu_util_irq(rq);

if (unlikely(irq >= scale))
    return scale;                    /* interrupts alone saturate the CPU */

/* A floor: reserved DL bandwidth plus IRQ pressure, or uclamp_min. */
*min = max(irq + cpu_bw_dl(rq), uclamp_rq_get(rq, UCLAMP_MIN));
if (!uclamp_is_used() && rt_rq_is_runnable(&rq->rt))
    *min = max(*min, scale);         /* runnable RT => full speed */

/* A ceiling, which uclamp_max may push below actual utilization. */
*max = min(scale, uclamp_rq_get(rq, UCLAMP_MAX));

/* The utilization itself: measured PELT for all three classes. */
util  = util_cfs + cpu_util_rt(rq);
util += cpu_util_dl(rq);

util  = scale_irq_capacity(util, irq, scale);
util += irq;

return min(scale, util);

PELT tracks utilization separately for the fair, real-time and deadline classes, and for interrupt time; the function sums the three class signals and then handles interrupts specially. Interrupt time is not simply added on: the class utilizations are first scaled into the capacity that remains once interrupts have taken their share, and only then is the interrupt share added back, so a CPU spending a third of its time in interrupt context does not have its task utilization silently deflated by that third. The early return above it is the limiting case — when interrupts alone saturate the CPU the governor stops reasoning and asks for maximum frequency, since nothing it can compute will help.

The two output parameters are as important as the return value, and they are where several threads of this chapter meet. *min is a floor on the requested performance rather than a measurement, and the deadline class contributes to it twice over: its measured utilization joins the sum, while its reserved bandwidth, cpu_bw_dl(), is combined with IRQ pressure to form a performance level the CPU must be able to reach. A reservation is a promise about runtime, and runtime is only meaningful at a frequency that can deliver it, so the guarantee has to survive even when measured usage is low. *max is the corresponding ceiling. Both are also the point at which utilization clamping enters frequency selection: uclamp_rq_get(rq, UCLAMP_MIN) and UCLAMP_MAX appear directly in the floor and the ceiling, which is the mechanism behind the claim in the next subsection that uclamp influences clock speed and not only placement.

One rule in that fragment is easy to miss and surprising in production: if uclamp is not in use and any RT task is runnable, the floor is raised to full capacity. On such a kernel a single runnable SCHED_FIFO task pins the CPU to its maximum frequency — a conservative choice for latency, and a reason RT workloads on uclamp-less kernels show much higher idle power than their utilization suggests.

Utilization Clamping and cgroup v2 CPU Control

Utilization clamping, usually shortened to uclamp, lets user space bound the scheduler's view of a task's utilization. The interface is exposed through struct sched_attr using SCHED_FLAG_UTIL_CLAMP_MIN and SCHED_FLAG_UTIL_CLAMP_MAX together with the sched_util_min and sched_util_max fields. Values are expressed on the scheduler's capacity scale, where 1024 approximately means “full capacity of a CPU at maximum performance”.

A minimum clamp says “treat this task as if it needs at least this much capacity”, which helps latency-sensitive work avoid being packed onto tiny or low-frequency CPUs. A maximum clamp says “do not let this task look more expensive than this”, which is useful for background work whose energy footprint matters more than raw speed. Because clamps feed into both placement and schedutil, they are a much more direct performance hint than traditional nice values.

cgroup v2 extends the same idea from one task to whole workload groups. The CPU controller's most visible files are cpu.weight for proportional fair sharing, cpu.max for quota/period style bandwidth control, and cpu.pressure for PSI visibility inside the group. Modern systems often pair those with cpu.uclamp.min and cpu.uclamp.max, letting a service manager say not just “how much CPU time may this group consume?” but also “what performance floor or ceiling should the scheduler assume while serving it?”

Table 7-44. cgroup v2 CPU Control Files Most Relevant to Scheduling
FileMeaningScheduler effect
cpu.weightRelative fair-share weight for the groupChanges how much CFS bandwidth the group receives when there is contention
cpu.maxQuota and period pairApplies hard bandwidth throttling once the group's quota is consumed
cpu.pressurePSI statistics scoped to the cgroupLets managers detect backlog or starvation inside one workload boundary
cpu.uclamp.minMinimum utilization clamp for the groupBiases placement and frequency upward for latency-sensitive services
cpu.uclamp.maxMaximum utilization clamp for the groupCaps perceived demand to contain energy use or protect foreground work

Clamps Are Stored in Buckets, Not Exactly

The interface accepts any value in 0..1024, which makes it natural to assume the kernel stores what was asked for. Per task it does. What the runqueue aggregates, however, is quantized. Each clamp value is mapped to one of UCLAMP_BUCKETS buckets — CONFIG_UCLAMP_BUCKETS_COUNT, permitted range 5 to 20 and defaulting to 5, so by default each bucket spans about 20% of the capacity scale — and the runqueue refcounts tasks per bucket rather than tracking every distinct value.

The reason is cost. The effective clamp of a runqueue is the maximum over its runnable tasks, and that maximum has to be recomputed whenever a task is enqueued or dequeued, on paths that already dominate scheduler profiles. Refcounting a handful of buckets makes enqueue and dequeue O(1) with a very small constant; tracking exact values would require a priority structure on the hottest path in the kernel.

The consequence is that clamps behave as a coarse grouping, and tasks sharing a bucket influence one another. A bucket holds the highest clamp value among the tasks refcounted in it, so a task asking for util_min of 25 and one asking for 30 share the 20–39 bucket, whose effective value becomes 30 while both are runnable; when the more demanding task leaves, the bucket falls back. Two clamp requests a few percent apart are therefore not reliably distinguishable, and a workload tuned by nudging util_min in small steps may see no effect at all until a step crosses a bucket boundary. Increasing CONFIG_UCLAMP_BUCKETS_COUNT buys finer granularity at the cost of a larger per-runqueue structure and more work in the aggregation.

The most useful synthesis is this: autogroup is a convenience feature for fair interactive sessions, uclamp is a performance hinting mechanism for tasks or cgroups, and cgroup v2 CPU controls are administrative policy boundaries. All three influence fair scheduling, but they operate at different layers and with different strengths.

System Calls Related to Scheduling

Everything discussed so far — scheduling classes, runqueue management, PELT, load balancing, EAS, and the special features — operates entirely inside the kernel. User space sees none of those internal structures directly. Instead, it communicates with the scheduler through a small, stable set of system calls that have evolved from the original POSIX real-time extensions of the 1990s into a modern extensible interface. These calls can be grouped into four functional categories:

  • Policy and priority — nice(), setpriority(), sched_setscheduler(), sched_setattr()
  • CPU affinity — sched_setaffinity() / sched_getaffinity()
  • Voluntary yielding and introspection — sched_yield(), sched_rr_get_interval(), sched_get_priority_min() / sched_get_priority_max()
  • Security attributes — prctl(PR_SCHED_CORE, ...)

In Linux 7.0.12, most of them live in kernel/sched/syscalls.c, a file split out of core.c in 6.11 precisely so that the validation and permission-checking layer could be audited independently of the scheduling algorithms. The consolidation is not quite total, and the exceptions are worth knowing when you go looking for the code: setpriority() and getpriority() are still in kernel/sys.c because they are generic process-attribute calls that happen to act on nice values, and prctl(PR_SCHED_CORE, ...) dispatches into kernel/sched/core_sched.c. Every call that modifies scheduler state follows the same pattern: copy user-space arguments into a kernel structure, validate ranges and permissions (checking CAP_SYS_NICE, RT bandwidth limits, deadline admission control, and cgroup constraints), find the target task by PID, then call into the internal __sched_setscheduler() path which handles the actual policy transition — dequeuing the task from its current class, updating its parameters, and re-enqueuing it under the new class. The modern sched_setattr() interface uses an extensible struct sched_attr with a leading size field, so new attributes (utilization clamping, sched_ext parameters) can be added without breaking older binaries — the kernel zero-fills any fields beyond what the caller supplied.

User space does not manipulate runqueues directly. It speaks to the scheduler through a small set of system calls that change policy, priority, affinity, or advisory behavior. In Linux 7.0.12 the interfaces are still compact, but the validation behind them is much richer than in the Linux 2.6 era because the kernel now must account for deadline reservations, RT bandwidth limits, utilization clamping, cgroup constraints, and SMT security features.

Version history. The system-call surface is the most conservative part of this chapter, and that is the point: a program written against nice(), sched_setscheduler() and sched_setaffinity() in 2005 still compiles and still works, even though every algorithm behind those calls has been replaced at least once. The additions since 2.6.11 are almost all extensions rather than replacements. sched_setattr() and sched_getattr() arrived with the deadline class in 3.14 and became the interface through which everything subsequently new is expressed — utilization clamps in 5.3, custom EEVDF slices in 6.12 — without a single new system-call number. prctl(PR_SCHED_CORE) in 5.14 is the one genuinely new entry point, and it was added under prctl() rather than as a sched_* call precisely because it controls a security property rather than a scheduling policy. What has changed most is not the calls but what happens behind them: the validation chain a request now passes through — deadline admission control, RT bandwidth limits, cpuset intersection, uclamp bucketing, sched_ext veto — did not exist in any form in 2.6.11, which is why the error codes in Table 7-50 are far more interesting than the arguments in Table 7-45.

Table 7-45. Scheduling System Calls
System callMain parametersWhat it controlsCore implementation path
nice()incAdjusts the caller's nice value relative to its current valuesys_nice() → nice validation → fair-priority update
setpriority() / getpriority()which, who, prioSet or query nice values for a process, process group, or userpriority helpers → set_user_nice() / query helpers
sched_setscheduler()pid, policy, paramSet policy and RT prioritysched_setscheduler() → __sched_setscheduler()
sched_setparam()pid, paramChange RT priority without changing policysched_setparam() → __sched_setscheduler()
sched_setattr()pid, attr, flagsUniversal interface for nice, RT, deadline, util clamp, and reset-on-fork attributessched_setattr() → copy/size checks → __sched_setscheduler()
sched_getscheduler()pidReturn policy for a tasksched_getscheduler() → task lookup → policy extraction
sched_getparam()pid, paramReturn RT priority fieldsched_getparam() → task lookup → copy to user
sched_getattr()pid, attr, size, flagsReturn extensible scheduling attributessched_getattr() → fill struct → copy to user
sched_setaffinity() / sched_getaffinity()pid, mask size, CPU maskRestrict or query the CPUs on which a task may runaffinity syscall → cpumask validation → migration if needed
sched_yield()noneVoluntarily give up the CPU within the current policy classsched_yield() → class-specific yield_task()
sched_get_priority_min/max()policyReport priority range for a policypolicy validation → constant return
sched_rr_get_interval()pid, timespecReturn the round-robin quantum for a tasksched_rr_get_interval() → class callback / default logic
prctl(PR_SCHED_CORE, ...)operation, pid, scope, cookie pointerManage core scheduling cookiesprctl() → core-sched helpers in core_sched.c

nice(), setpriority(), and getpriority()

nice(int inc) is the oldest scheduling API, and it remains deliberately narrow: it changes the calling task's nice value by a relative increment. Negative increments ask for more CPU share, positive increments ask for less. The kernel entry point validates the requested delta, checks permission rules, computes the final nice value inside the legal range [-20, 19], and then applies it through the fair scheduler's weight machinery.

The permission rule is simple in principle but easy to misuse: lowering the nice value (for example from 0 to -5) increases priority and therefore requires privilege. In practice that means CAP_SYS_NICE or an equivalent rlimit-mediated privilege path. Raising the nice value toward 19 is always permitted because the task is making itself less competitive.

setpriority() generalizes the same concept. Its which parameter chooses whether who names one process, one process group, or one real user ID. The kernel then iterates the relevant tasks and applies the requested nice level to each legal target. getpriority() selects the same way but returns the best priority found among the targets, and here the raw kernel interface and the C library part company. Internally the kernel converts each candidate with nice_to_rlimit(), which computes 20 - nice and therefore maps the nice range [-20, 19] onto the strictly positive range [40, 1]; the syscall returns the numerically largest such value, meaning the lowest nice value. The offset is not decoration. A syscall that returned the nice value directly could not distinguish a legitimate result of -1 from the error indication -1, so glibc subtracts the offset again on the way out and asks the caller to detect errors by clearing errno before the call and inspecting it afterwards. Programs that invoke getpriority() through syscall() rather than the library wrapper see the unconverted [1, 40] value and often misread it as a nice value.

Errors are correspondingly modest. ESRCH means the target process or group no longer exists; EACCES or EPERM means the caller tried to raise priority without sufficient privilege; and EINVAL covers malformed selectors. On success, nice() returns the new nice value while the set/query interfaces return 0 or the queried priority as defined by POSIX and libc wrappers.

sched_setscheduler(), sched_setparam(), and sched_setattr()

These are the most important scheduler-control calls because they can move a task across policy classes. sched_setscheduler(pid, policy, param) changes the task's policy and, for RT classes, its sched_priority. sched_setparam() leaves the policy unchanged but updates the priority field. sched_setattr() is the modern superset API: it can express fair-class nice, RT priority, deadline parameters, reset-on-fork, sched_ext attributes, and utilization clamps in one extensible structure.

The structure begins with a size field so the kernel can remain backward compatible as new attributes are appended. A caller that uses an older, shorter structure can still run on a newer kernel because the kernel knows exactly how many bytes were supplied, and zero-fills the rest.

Table 7-46. Key struct sched_attr Fields
FieldUsed byMeaning
sizeAll callsStructure length for extensibility and compatibility checks
sched_policyAll class-changing callsTarget policy such as SCHED_NORMAL, SCHED_FIFO, SCHED_RR, or SCHED_DEADLINE
sched_flagsExtended APIBehaviour modifiers: SCHED_FLAG_RESET_ON_FORK, the two util-clamp flags, the two deadline flags (SCHED_FLAG_RECLAIM, SCHED_FLAG_DL_OVERRUN), and the two read-modify-write flags (SCHED_FLAG_KEEP_POLICY, SCHED_FLAG_KEEP_PARAMS)
sched_niceFair and batch policiesNice value used by fair-class entities
sched_priorityRT policiesPriority in the 1-99 RT range
sched_runtime, sched_deadline, sched_periodSCHED_DEADLINEReservation parameters interpreted by CBS/EDF logic
sched_runtime (reused)Fair policiesSince 6.12, requests a custom EEVDF slice length in nanoseconds rather than a reservation; see The Four Core Quantities
sched_util_min, sched_util_maxuclamp-aware kernelsMinimum and maximum utilization clamps on the 0-1024 capacity scale

How the Size Field Negotiates

The size field is easy to describe loosely and easy to get wrong, so it is worth following sched_copy_attr() exactly once. The kernel first zeroes its own struct sched_attr, then reads only the leading size word from user space. A size of zero is accepted and silently promoted to SCHED_ATTR_SIZE_VER0, the length of the original 3.14 structure, as an explicit ABI quirk for callers that never learned to set the field. Anything below that, or above PAGE_SIZE, is rejected outright.

The interesting case is a caller whose structure is longer than the kernel’s. It is tempting to assume this must fail, since the kernel cannot know what the extra bytes mean. It does not. copy_struct_from_user() copies the prefix it understands and then verifies that every remaining byte the caller supplied is zero; only if some unknown field is non-zero does it return -E2BIG. In other words, a program built against a newer header runs unchanged on an older kernel for as long as it does not actually use the newer attributes. That is the property that makes the extensible-struct pattern worth its complexity, and it is why the same idiom appears in perf_event_open() and clone3().

When the call does fail this way, the kernel does something helpful that is easy to miss: before returning -E2BIG it writes its own sizeof(struct sched_attr) back into the caller’s uattr->size. A caller that is prepared for this can discover the running kernel’s native structure size from the failed call, trim its request, and retry — feature detection without a version number anywhere. Note also that -E2BIG, not -EINVAL, is the signal for every size-related problem, which is a useful discriminator when debugging: EINVAL means the kernel understood the request and disliked its contents, while E2BIG means it could not agree with the caller on the shape of the request in the first place.

One field pair breaks the general rule and is worth remembering. Setting either utilization-clamp flag while declaring a structure shorter than SCHED_ATTR_SIZE_VER1 returns -EINVAL rather than being ignored, because the clamp values live beyond the version-0 boundary; asking for clamping while providing no room to express it is a contradiction the kernel refuses rather than silently drops.

Setting a Fair Slice Through sched_setattr()

Table 7-46 lists sched_runtime under SCHED_DEADLINE, which was the whole story until 6.12. Since then the same field carries a second, unrelated meaning for fair tasks: it requests a specific EEVDF slice length. This is the user-space half of the custom_slice flag introduced with the sched_entity fields earlier in this chapter, and it is the only way to set that flag.

The handling is in __setparam_fair(), and it is short enough to state completely. A non-zero sched_runtime sets se->custom_slice and clamps the requested value into the range 100 µs to 100 ms; a zero value clears the flag and returns the entity to the system-wide sysctl_sched_base_slice. The clamp bounds are not arbitrary — they are one tenth of a tick at HZ=1000 and ten ticks at HZ=100 respectively, chosen so that the request stays meaningful at either extreme of the tick-rate range the kernel supports.

The consequence for latency tuning is direct, and it is the reason this interface exists. A short slice makes a task cede the CPU sooner and be re-picked sooner, trading throughput for responsiveness; a long slice does the reverse. Because the value is per task rather than per system, an audio thread and a batch compile can now express opposite preferences on the same machine without either of them touching sysctl_sched_base_slice and affecting everything else. Note that the request is a suggestion: EEVDF still enforces eligibility, so a task that asks for a long slice does not thereby acquire a larger share of the CPU — it acquires the same share delivered in fewer, longer visits.

Two flags exist to make this and every other attribute change composable. SCHED_FLAG_KEEP_POLICY tells the kernel to leave the policy alone, and SCHED_FLAG_KEEP_PARAMS tells it to leave the class parameters alone; the pair is available together as SCHED_FLAG_KEEP_ALL. They exist because sched_setattr() otherwise writes every field it is given, so adjusting one attribute would mean first reading the others back with sched_getattr() and hoping nothing changed in between. With SCHED_FLAG_KEEP_PARAMS the kernel calls get_params() to refill the parameter fields from the task itself under the same lock that will apply the update, turning a read-modify-write into a single atomic operation. This is how one adjusts a task’s utilization clamps without needing to know, or accidentally rewriting, its nice value or slice.

The kernel path is shared on purpose. Whether the request comes from sched_setscheduler(), sched_setparam(), or sched_setattr(), Linux normalizes the user input into one internal attribute bundle and then enters __sched_setscheduler(). That helper performs the real work: policy validation, privilege checks, deadline and RT bandwidth admission, priority normalization, class transition handling, dequeue/re-enqueue, and possible rescheduling.

The permission model is stricter as requests move away from ordinary fair scheduling. Moving a task into SCHED_FIFO or SCHED_RR, or raising its RT priority, generally requires CAP_SYS_NICE. Non-root callers may sometimes raise RT priority within their RLIMIT_RTPRIO allowance, but only up to that rlimit and only if the rest of the validation chain accepts the request. SCHED_DEADLINE is not merely stricter, it is currently absolute: user_check_sched_setscheduler() branches to the privileged path for any deadline request, with or without a matching rlimit, so CAP_SYS_NICE is always required. There is no RLIMIT_DEADLINE to relax it. The comment beside that check records the reasoning and the intended future: the safest behaviour for now is to refuse everything, though the maintainers would eventually like to let an unprivileged deadline task lengthen its own relative deadline or shorten its own runtime, since both directions reduce the utilization it has already been granted and therefore cannot overcommit the machine.

Bandwidth gating is the next major checkpoint. Before the kernel accepts a new RT or deadline task, it verifies that the requested reservation fits the machine's configured bandwidth model. RT policies are limited by the global RT period/runtime knobs, and deadline policies are rejected if their runtime/deadline/period tuple fails admission control. This is why an otherwise privileged call can still fail with EBUSY: the task is allowed in principle, but the CPU bandwidth accounting says the reservation would overcommit the system.

Table 7-47. Validation Steps Inside __sched_setscheduler()
StepWhat is checkedTypical failure
Structure and flag validationKnown policy, supported flags, legal attribute sizeEINVAL
Per-policy field sanityNice only for fair classes, RT priority only for RT classes, valid deadline tuple for DLEINVAL
Permission checksCAP_SYS_NICE, RLIMIT_RTPRIO, ownership rulesEPERM or EACCES
Bandwidth admissionRT runtime/period budget and deadline reservation feasibilityEBUSY
Class transition bookkeepingDequeue, update priority, attach class-specific state, re-enqueueUsually not user-visible; if migration constraints fail, the syscall returns an error

SCHED_FLAG_RESET_ON_FORK is worth separate emphasis. If this flag is set, children created by fork() do not blindly inherit privileged scheduler state. RT or deadline policy is reset to ordinary fair scheduling in the child, and negative nice values are likewise reset toward the non-privileged default. This protects systems from a common foot-gun: a supervisor briefly grants a task aggressive scheduling, the task forks a worker farm, and suddenly an entire subtree inherits policies that were intended for only one control thread.

#define _GNU_SOURCE
#include <errno.h>
#include <linux/sched.h>
#include <linux/types.h>
#include <sched.h>
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <sys/syscall.h>
#include <unistd.h>

struct sched_attr_local {
    __u32 size;
    __u32 sched_policy;
    __u64 sched_flags;
    __s32 sched_nice;
    __u32 sched_priority;
    __u64 sched_runtime;
    __u64 sched_deadline;
    __u64 sched_period;
    __u32 sched_util_min;
    __u32 sched_util_max;
};

static int sched_setattr_local(pid_t pid, struct sched_attr_local *attr)
{
    return syscall(SYS_sched_setattr, pid, attr, 0);
}

int main(void)
{
    struct sched_attr_local attr = {
        .size = sizeof(attr),
        .sched_policy = SCHED_DEADLINE,
        .sched_flags = SCHED_FLAG_RESET_ON_FORK,
        .sched_runtime = 10 * 1000 * 1000,
        .sched_deadline = 30 * 1000 * 1000,
        .sched_period = 30 * 1000 * 1000,
    };

    if (sched_setattr_local(0, &attr) < 0) {
        fprintf(stderr, "sched_setattr(SCHED_DEADLINE) failed: %s\n",
                strerror(errno));
        return 1;
    }

    puts("deadline policy installed");
    return 0;
}

The deadline example shows why sched_setattr() is the preferred modern interface: no legacy scheduler call can express runtime, relative deadline, period, and reset-on-fork in one atomic transaction. The return value is 0 on success and -1 with errno set on failure. The most common errno values here are EINVAL for malformed tuples, EPERM for insufficient privilege, and EBUSY when admission control rejects the reservation.

#include <errno.h>
#include <sched.h>
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>

int main(void)
{
    struct sched_param sp = { .sched_priority = 20 };

    if (sched_setscheduler(0, SCHED_RR, &sp) < 0) {
        fprintf(stderr, "sched_setscheduler(SCHED_RR) failed: %s\n",
                strerror(errno));
        return 1;
    }

    puts("now running as SCHED_RR/20");
    return 0;
}

For RT policies, the kernel ignores sched_nice and instead interprets sched_priority on the fixed RT range. sched_get_priority_min() and sched_get_priority_max() exist precisely so portable user space can discover that range rather than hard-coding 1 and 99, though Linux in practice uses those values for FIFO and RR.

sched_getscheduler(), sched_getparam(), and sched_getattr()

The getter calls are straightforward but still useful to map onto implementation. sched_getscheduler(pid) validates that the caller may inspect the target task, looks up the task under RCU or tasklist protection as appropriate, and returns its current policy constant. sched_getparam(pid, param) then exposes the class-specific priority field, which is primarily meaningful for RT tasks. For fair tasks the priority field is normally zero because nice is carried elsewhere.

sched_getattr() is the richer counterpart, and its size parameter behaves symmetrically with the setter rather than simply truncating. The kernel builds a full struct sched_attr, sets kattr.size to min(usize, sizeof(kattr)) so the caller can see how much it actually received, and then hands the copy to copy_struct_to_user(). If the caller’s buffer is shorter than the kernel’s structure, that helper does not blindly truncate: it first checks whether the fields being dropped are all zero, and returns -E2BIG if any of them is not. The practical consequence is worth stating plainly, because it is the opposite of what “copies out what fits” would suggest. An old binary querying a task that uses only attributes it understands succeeds. The same binary querying a task that has, say, utilization clamps set on a kernel whose structure is longer than its own fails with -E2BIG rather than silently reporting an incomplete picture. The interface would rather refuse than lie.

Both directions therefore share one design rule: unknown-but-zero is compatibility, unknown-and-non-zero is an error. The remaining failures are EINVAL for a non-zero flags argument, a size below SCHED_ATTR_SIZE_VER0 or above PAGE_SIZE, or a negative pid; ESRCH for a vanished task; and EFAULT if the user buffer is invalid.

CPU Affinity: sched_setaffinity() and sched_getaffinity()

Affinity calls are deceptively powerful because they constrain every future scheduling decision. The user-space type is typically cpu_set_t, but the kernel ultimately converts the supplied bitmask into a cpumask and intersects it with all other legality constraints: CPUs that exist, CPUs that are online, CPUs allowed by cpusets/cgroup placement, and CPUs permitted by architecture-specific constraints such as housekeeping isolation.

sched_setaffinity(pid, cpusetsize, mask) therefore answers more than “did the caller set bit 7?” It must validate that the buffer is large enough to describe the machine, reject masks that contain no legal CPU after intersection, and then, if the task is currently running on a disallowed CPU, trigger migration. If the task is sleeping, the new mask constrains only its future wakeups; if it is runnable, the kernel may dequeue and requeue it on a legal runqueue. If it is running on the wrong CPU right now, the scheduler sets up a stop or reschedule path so that it moves at the next safe point.

Table 7-48. Affinity Outcomes When the Requested Mask Changes
SituationKernel responseUser-visible result
Mask includes current CPU and other legal CPUsUpdate allowed mask; task may keep running where it isCall succeeds with no immediate migration
Mask excludes current CPU but still includes at least one legal CPUMark task for migration and move it to a legal runqueueCall succeeds; execution may continue elsewhere almost immediately
Mask contains offline or non-existent CPUs onlyIntersection becomes emptyEINVAL
Mask partially overlaps the effective cpusetIntersect with the cpuset mask and apply the result; remember the full requested mask in user_cpus_ptrCall succeeds, but the mask read back is narrower than the one written
Mask is disjoint from the effective cpusetIntersection is empty; nothing is appliedEINVAL even though the raw user mask was non-empty
Target is a per-CPU kernel thread (PF_NO_SETAFFINITY)Rejected before any mask processingEINVAL
Target is a deadline task and the mask is narrower than the root domaindl_task_check_affinity() refuses, to keep admission control validEBUSY
 
#define _GNU_SOURCE
#include <errno.h>
#include <sched.h>
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <unistd.h>

static void print_mask(const cpu_set_t *set)
{
    for (int cpu = 0; cpu < CPU_SETSIZE; cpu++)
        if (CPU_ISSET(cpu, set))
            printf("%d ", cpu);
    putchar('\n');
}

int main(void)
{
    cpu_set_t mask;

    CPU_ZERO(&mask);
    if (sched_getaffinity(0, sizeof(mask), &mask) < 0) {
        perror("sched_getaffinity");
        return 1;
    }

    printf("current affinity: ");
    print_mask(&mask);

    CPU_ZERO(&mask);
    CPU_SET(0, &mask);
    CPU_SET(1, &mask);

    if (sched_setaffinity(0, sizeof(mask), &mask) < 0) {
        fprintf(stderr, "sched_setaffinity failed: %s\n", strerror(errno));
        return 1;
    }

    puts("affinity updated to CPUs 0 and 1");
    return 0;
}

sched_getaffinity() returns the task's current allowed mask after all effective restrictions have been applied. That makes it the right way to discover what is actually legal, especially inside containers or managed environments where cpuset controllers may have narrowed the visible CPU set. Typical errors are ESRCH for a vanished task, EFAULT for bad user memory, and EINVAL for an invalid mask size or an empty effective result.

The Mask You Asked For Is Remembered Separately

A description of affinity as “the kernel intersects your mask with what is legal and stores the result” is accurate but loses something important, because it implies the original request is discarded. It is not. Since 5.15 every task carries a second cpumask, user_cpus_ptr, holding the mask user space last asked for, unintersected. sched_setaffinity() allocates and fills it on every call and passes it down inside a struct affinity_context tagged SCA_USER; __sched_setaffinity() then intersects the request with the effective cpuset to produce what is actually installed.

The reason for keeping both is that the effective mask can legitimately widen again later, and when it does the kernel needs to know what the task wanted rather than merely what it was last granted. Without the saved copy, a task placed briefly into a narrow cpuset would emerge from it permanently pinned to whatever subset it happened to be given, because no record of the original intent would survive. With it, relax_compatible_cpus_allowed_ptr() can re-run the intersection against the widened constraint and restore the task to the largest legal subset of its own request.

The mechanism exists for a specific and instructive ARM64 problem, and it is the clearest example of the scheduler ABI bending around a hardware asymmetry. On a big.LITTLE system where only some cores implement AArch32, a 32-bit process simply cannot run everywhere. When such a binary is executed on a kernel that has detected mismatched 32-bit support, arch/arm64/kernel/process.c calls force_compatible_cpus_allowed_ptr(), overriding the task’s affinity down to the cores that can actually execute its instruction set — a restriction the task never asked for and cannot refuse. If that same task later executes a 64-bit binary, the restriction becomes unnecessary, and the matching relax_compatible_cpus_allowed_ptr() call restores the affinity the user originally requested. Neither transition is visible as a system call; both are consequences of execve().

Two practical points follow. First, sched_getaffinity() reports the effective mask, never the remembered request, so a program cannot read back what it asked for — only what it currently has. Second, the saved mask is inherited across fork() by dup_user_cpus_ptr(), so a child of a temporarily constrained task inherits the intent as well as the constraint. On x86_64, where no comparable instruction-set asymmetry exists, the field is allocated and maintained but the forcible narrowing path is never taken; the cpuset interaction described above is the only way it comes into play.

When Affinity Is Refused, and What the Call Returns

Affinity failures are more varied than the single EINVAL most documentation mentions, and the checks happen in a fixed order that determines which error a caller actually sees.

The first gate is the target itself: a task carrying PF_NO_SETAFFINITY is rejected with EINVAL before its mask is even examined. This flag marks per-CPU kernel threads, whose correctness depends on staying where they are, so the refusal is absolute rather than privilege-dependent — not even root can move them. The second gate is permission, and it is subtler than the usual root check: check_same_owner() compares the caller’s effective UID against the target’s effective and real UIDs, and only if that fails does the kernel look for CAP_SYS_NICE in the target task’s user namespace. A container manager holding the capability in its own namespace but not the target’s therefore gets EPERM, which is the intended containment property rather than a bug.

The third gate applies only to deadline tasks and is the one most likely to surprise. dl_task_check_affinity() requires the new mask to be a superset of the task’s root domain, not merely a non-empty subset of the legal CPUs, and returns EBUSY otherwise. The reason is that deadline admission control is computed per root domain, as described under Admission Control and Root-Domain Bandwidth: a reservation accepted against the bandwidth of an eight-CPU domain is not necessarily feasible if the task is subsequently confined to two of them. Rather than re-run admission on every affinity change, the kernel forbids the narrowing. The practical effect is that taskset fails on a deadline task unless the requested mask covers the whole domain; partitioning must be done by moving the task into a cpuset whose CPUs form their own root domain, which is what SMP Push/Pull Migration and Affinity Constraints describes.

The return conventions of the two calls are also asymmetric in a way that trips up anyone using them through syscall() rather than glibc. sched_setaffinity() accepts any buffer length: a mask shorter than the kernel’s is zero-extended, a longer one is truncated, and neither is an error. sched_getaffinity() is strict in both directions — it returns EINVAL if the buffer cannot represent nr_cpu_ids bits, and again if the length is not a multiple of sizeof(unsigned long). More importantly, on success it does not return zero: it returns the number of bytes written. The glibc wrapper converts that to zero, so code written against the library and later ported to a raw syscall interface will see an apparently successful call returning 8 or 16 and may treat it as an error. Finally, the mask it reports is intersected with cpu_active_mask, so CPUs that are offline at the moment of the call are absent from the answer even though they remain in the task’s stored mask and will become usable again if they come back online.

sched_yield() and why it is usually a bad fair-class hint

sched_yield() looks attractive because it sounds polite: “I am willing to let somebody else run.” But politeness is not a scheduling policy, and the effect varies sharply by class. The syscall simply enters the scheduler and invokes class-specific yield logic. What that means for progress depends entirely on the class semantics.

Table 7-49. Class-Specific Meaning of sched_yield()
ClassKernel effectPractical consequence
FairSince 6.19, forfeit the rest of the current request: set vruntime to deadline and take a fresh deadline one slice further out (only if the task is currently eligible)Often hurts throughput or latency because the task gives up cached progress without any guarantee that the “right” peer runs
SCHED_FIFOMove the task to the tail of its current priority queueLets equal-priority peers run, while higher priorities would have preempted anyway
SCHED_RRSimilar tail movement among equal-priority RR peersActs like an early end to the current quantum
SCHED_DEADLINEGive up remaining current runtime and wait for future replenishment rulesCan be meaningful for reservation-based work, but it is not a generic “sleep until useful” primitive

For fair tasks, busy-wait loops built around sched_yield() are almost always a design mistake. If the task is waiting for I/O, a futex, or an eventfd, it should block and let the scheduler remove it from the runnable set entirely. Yielding keeps the task runnable, distorts fairness, and can actually increase CPU pressure by waking the task back into competition immediately.

sched_get_priority_min/max() and sched_rr_get_interval()

The priority-range calls are simple query helpers. They take one policy constant and return the numeric range supported for that policy. The kernel path is little more than a switch over the policy constant returning a compile-time value, so the only error is EINVAL for a policy the kernel does not recognise.

The values repay a moment’s attention because only two policies get a non-trivial answer. SCHED_FIFO and SCHED_RR report 1 and MAX_RT_PRIO-1, that is 99. Every other policy — SCHED_NORMAL, SCHED_BATCH, SCHED_IDLE, SCHED_EXT, and, perhaps surprisingly, SCHED_DEADLINE — reports 0 for both ends. Deadline tasks outrank every real-time task on the machine, so a reader could reasonably expect them to sit above 99; they report 0 instead because sched_priority is simply not the field that orders them. A deadline task’s urgency lives in its absolute deadline, which no fixed numeric range could express. The same reasoning covers fair tasks, whose competition is decided by weight and virtual deadline, and sched_ext tasks, whose ordering is whatever the loaded BPF scheduler decides. Writing a portable priority-setting helper on top of these calls therefore requires treating a returned range of 0..0 as “this policy does not use priorities” rather than as “this policy has exactly one priority.”

sched_rr_get_interval(pid, timespec) is more interesting because the answer depends on the task’s class, and because a zero answer is meaningful rather than a failure. The path in syscalls.c performs the task lookup and the security_task_getscheduler() check, takes the runqueue lock, and then asks the task’s class for an interval through the optional get_rr_interval hook. The local variable is initialised to zero, so a class that does not implement the hook at all answers zero by omission — and the syscall’s own documentation defines a returned value of zero as infinity, meaning “this task is not preempted by quantum expiry.”

Only two classes implement the hook. get_rr_interval_rt() returns sched_rr_timeslice for a SCHED_RR task and, explicitly, zero for a SCHED_FIFO one, which is the correct answer: a FIFO task really does run until it blocks or is preempted, so its quantum is unbounded. Deadline, idle and sched_ext tasks fall through to the same zero for want of a hook, though for different reasons in each case.

The fair class is the one whose answer has changed. get_rr_interval_fair() returns NS_TO_JIFFIES(se->slice) — the entity’s EEVDF request length, converted to jiffies — but only when rq->cfs.load.weight is non-zero; on an otherwise idle runqueue it too returns zero, on the reasoning that a task with no competition is not going to be rotated away from the CPU. Because se->slice is now settable per task, this call reports whatever slice user space last requested rather than a system-wide constant, which makes it a convenient way to confirm that a custom slice was accepted and clamped as expected.

#define _GNU_SOURCE
#include <errno.h>
#include <linux/sched.h>
#include <linux/types.h>
#include <sched.h>
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <sys/syscall.h>
#include <unistd.h>

struct sched_attr_local {
    __u32 size;
    __u32 sched_policy;
    __u64 sched_flags;
    __s32 sched_nice;
    __u32 sched_priority;
    __u64 sched_runtime;
    __u64 sched_deadline;
    __u64 sched_period;
    __u32 sched_util_min;
    __u32 sched_util_max;
};

static int sched_setattr_local(pid_t pid, struct sched_attr_local *attr)
{
    return syscall(SYS_sched_setattr, pid, attr, 0);
}

int main(void)
{
    struct sched_attr_local attr = {
        .size = sizeof(attr),
        .sched_policy = SCHED_OTHER,
        .sched_flags = SCHED_FLAG_UTIL_CLAMP_MIN |
                       SCHED_FLAG_UTIL_CLAMP_MAX,
        .sched_nice = 0,
        .sched_util_min = 256,
        .sched_util_max = 768,
    };

    if (sched_setattr_local(0, &attr) < 0) {
        fprintf(stderr, "sched_setattr(uclamp) failed: %s\n", strerror(errno));
        return 1;
    }

    puts("utilization clamps installed");
    return 0;
}
Table 7-50. Common Scheduler Syscall Error Codes
ErrorTypical causeCommon calls
EINVALMalformed policy, bad attribute size, empty effective affinity mask, or illegal per-policy fieldssched_setattr(), sched_setscheduler(), sched_setaffinity()
EPERM / EACCESInsufficient privilege to raise priority or install RT/DL policynice(), sched_setscheduler(), sched_setattr()
EBUSYAdmission control rejects RT or deadline bandwidth requestsched_setattr(), sched_setscheduler()
ESRCHTarget task disappearedMost pid-based scheduler calls
EFAULTUser pointer was invalidAny call that copies structs or masks to/from user space

Core-scheduling control via prctl()

Unlike the other scheduling system calls, core-scheduling control is exposed through prctl() rather than a dedicated sched_* syscall, reflecting its nature as a security constraint rather than a policy parameter.
