The call signature is prctl(PR_SCHED_CORE, cmd, pid, type, uaddr), where cmd selects the operation (GET, CREATE, SHARE_TO, SHARE_FROM), pid identifies the target (0 for self), type controls the scope (THREAD, THREAD_GROUP, or PROCESS_GROUP, mapped directly to kernel pid_type values), and uaddr is an optional user pointer for reading the cookie identity. The entry point sched_core_share_pid() in core_sched.c first checks that SMT is actually present — on non-SMT machines the call returns -ENODEV because there are no sibling threads to isolate. Permission is enforced via ptrace_may_access(PTRACE_MODE_READ_REALCREDS), the same check used for debugger attachment, which ensures that one process cannot unilaterally join another's trust domain without appropriate privilege.

The four operations cover the complete lifecycle of a cookie. CREATE allocates a fresh refcounted cookie and applies it to the target scope — every thread in the scope is dequeued, re-tagged, and re-enqueued so that sibling CPUs immediately see the new compatibility constraint. SHARE_TO clones the caller's cookie onto a target task or group, creating a shared trust domain. SHARE_FROM does the reverse: the caller adopts the target's cookie. GET returns a hashed identity of the target's cookie (not the raw pointer, for security) to user space for introspection. When the scope is a thread group or process group, the kernel holds tasklist_lock and iterates all matching tasks via do_each_pid_thread(), applying the cookie atomically to the entire set.

prctl(PR_SCHED_CORE, ...) sits slightly outside the classic scheduling syscalls, but it belongs in the same mental map because it changes legal co-scheduling relationships. The parameters are operation, target pid, scope, and optionally a user pointer for cookie transfer. Privilege rules are intentionally conservative because one process should not be able to join itself to another task's trust domain arbitrarily. On success the call returns 0; failures typically report EINVAL for bad operations or scopes, ESRCH for missing targets, and EPERM when the caller lacks authority over the target task set.

Summary

This chapter has covered the Linux 7.0.12 process scheduler in depth — from the high-level design principles and scheduling classes through the per-entity load tracking (PELT) infrastructure, the multi-level load balancing framework, energy-aware scheduling on heterogeneous hardware, the cross-cutting special features, and finally the system-call interface that exposes it all to user space. Each subsystem was presented in isolation for clarity, but they do not operate independently. A single task wakeup may traverse wake-affine heuristics, EAS energy prediction, schedutil frequency adjustment, uclamp policy enforcement, NUMA locality checks, and core-scheduling cookie validation — all within a few microseconds. The scheduler's power lies not in any one algorithm but in how these components compose into a coherent decision pipeline.

The source code mirrors this composition. kernel/sched/ holds a little over 63,000 lines across 33 source files and 12 headers, each answering one question — who runs next (fair.c, rt.c, deadline.c, ext.c), where should it run (topology.c, cpudeadline.c, cpupri.c), how fast should the CPU be (cpufreq_schedutil.c, pelt.c), who may share an SMT core (core_sched.c), and how does user space ask for any of it (syscalls.c) — while communicating through the internal API in sched.h. __schedule() in core.c is the serialization point where all the answers converge into a context switch.

That file layout is organizational rather than physical, and the distinction is worth knowing before you go looking for a symbol. The Makefile builds exactly four objects: core.o, fair.o, build_policy.o, and build_utility.o. Almost every other file is #included as source into one of the last two — rt.c, deadline.c, syscalls.c, pelt.c and idle.c are pulled into build_policy.c, while the utility half absorbs the rest. Two consequences follow. The compiler sees a handful of very large translation units, so it can inline freely across what look like file boundaries without link-time optimization, which matters for a fast path measured in hundreds of nanoseconds; and a static function in one of those files is visible to every other file in the same aggregate, so the usual inference that static means “private to this file” does not hold here. The Makefile comment records the more prosaic motivation as well: the four units are of comparable size, so a parallel build finishes them at roughly the same moment.

By this point the Linux 7.0.12 scheduler should no longer look like one algorithm. It is a stack: system calls and wakeup paths feed the scheduler core, the core arbitrates among classes, each class maintains its own runqueue state, topology code decides where runnable work should live, and frequency plus energy logic shape how that work is executed on real hardware. The scheduler is therefore best understood as a coordination framework whose fast path happens to include several specialized algorithms.

Lifecycle of a Scheduling Decision

Readers who have followed the chapter section by section now know each piece of the scheduler, but may still wonder how those pieces interact in real time. The lifecycle walkthrough below ties them together by tracing a single end-to-end path: from the moment a task voluntarily blocks through its eventual return to execution on a (potentially different) CPU. This is the common case that dominates scheduler execution — far more frequent than periodic tick preemptions or load-balance migrations — and it exercises nearly every subsystem covered in this chapter: sleep accounting, wakeup barriers, class-specific CPU selection (including EAS and NUMA heuristics), PELT signal updates on both enqueue and dequeue, schedutil frequency reactions, preemption logic, and the final context switch.

Understanding this lifecycle as a chain of discrete phases also explains why scheduler bugs are notoriously hard to reproduce: a subtle ordering change in any one phase — a stale PELT value, a missed resched IPI, a load-balance race with a concurrent wakeup — can produce symptoms that manifest many phases later. Thinking in phases rather than in "the scheduler function" is the key mental model for both reading the source and diagnosing scheduling anomalies.

A single scheduling decision is best read as a chain of state transitions rather than a single function call. The following walkthrough tracks the common case of a task that blocked on I/O and is later awakened on an SMP system:

  1. The task blocks. A system call or kernel path sets the task state to a sleep value and calls into schedule(). Inside __schedule() the task is dequeued from its class runqueue, I/O-wait and PSI accounting are updated if appropriate, and another runnable task is chosen.
  2. The external event completes. An interrupt handler, softirq, or kernel worker notices that the awaited I/O has finished and calls a wakeup helper that eventually reaches try_to_wake_up().
  3. Wakeup legality is checked. The kernel verifies that the task is still sleeping in a wakeable state and serializes against any concurrent switch-out using the wakeup memory-barrier protocol.
  4. A target CPU is chosen. select_task_rq() asks the task's class which CPU should host the wakeup. Fair tasks may consider wake affinity, idle CPUs, EAS, and capacity. RT and deadline tasks use their own placement logic.
  5. The task is enqueued. ttwu_queue() places the task directly on the target runqueue or on a remote wakelist, then the class-specific enqueue_task() callback makes it runnable again.
  6. Preemption is considered. wakeup_preempt() compares the awakened task against the run queue's highest class and its current scheduling context. If the wakee should run sooner, the kernel sets TIF_NEED_RESCHED or an equivalent reschedule condition.
  7. A preemption point is reached. The current task may switch immediately if the wakeup happened in a fully preemptible context, or it may continue until return to user mode, IRQ exit, a voluntary block, or another explicit scheduling checkpoint.
  8. __schedule() runs again. The core scheduler locks the runqueue, updates clocks and accounting, and invokes pick_next_task().
  9. The next task is selected. The fast path may pick fair directly if only fair tasks are runnable; otherwise the class hierarchy is walked in strict order. Core scheduling, affinity, bandwidth, and class semantics all remain in force.
  10. The context switch happens. context_switch() performs the MM/TLB handoff, switch_to() swaps register context, and the newly chosen task resumes execution as if it had simply continued from its last preemption or wake point.

The Short Circuit: When Steps 4 and 5 Never Happen

The ten steps describe the full path, but in 7.0.12 a large fraction of wakeups skip most of it. Two independent mechanisms cut the chain short, and both are recent enough that older descriptions of the wakeup path do not cover them.

The first is delayed dequeue. Step 1 claims that a blocking task is dequeued from its class runqueue, and since 6.12 that is often false. When a fair task blocks while it still has positive lag — it has received less service than its weight entitled it to — dequeue_task_fair() declines to remove it and instead marks p->se.sched_delayed. The task remains on_rq but is not runnable; as the comment above task_is_runnable() puts it, such tasks “are on the runqueue but will be dequeued as soon as they get picked again”. Leaving the entity in the tree lets it keep competing for the lag it is owed, which is what stops a task from erasing its own debt by sleeping.

The second follows directly from the first. try_to_wake_up() begins by calling ttwu_runnable(), which takes the runqueue lock and asks whether the task is still queued. If it is — because it was delayed, or because it was merely preempted rather than blocked — the function re-enqueues it with ENQUEUE_DELAYED to clear the delayed state, calls wakeup_preempt() against the CPU it is already on, marks it running, and returns success. Steps 4 and 5 of the walkthrough never execute: there is no select_task_rq() call, no placement decision, no remote wakelist, and no possibility of migration.

This is worth internalizing because it inverts a common assumption. A task that sleeps and wakes quickly — the classic ping-pong between a client and a server thread, or a worker that blocks on a lock held for a few microseconds — will frequently be woken on the CPU it left, not because the wakeup path chose that CPU for affinity reasons, but because the wakeup path was never asked. Traces that show suspiciously good CPU locality for short-sleeping tasks usually have this explanation rather than a successful wake_affine() heuristic. It also means that tuning wake-affinity or EAS parameters will have no effect at all on that class of wakeup.

One further piece of vocabulary is worth naming, because it appears throughout the 7.0.12 sources and looks alarming until explained. Steps 8 and 9 speak of “the current task”, but __schedule(), pick_next_task(), and do_sched_yield() all operate on rq->donor rather than rq->curr. The two names separate the scheduling context — whose policy and priority decide what runs — from the execution context, the task whose registers are actually loaded. Proxy execution makes them diverge, letting a task blocked on a mutex lend its scheduling context to the lock holder so the holder runs with the waiter's urgency and releases the lock sooner.

In practice the divergence is rare, and the code makes that explicit. CONFIG_SCHED_PROXY_EXEC depends on EXPERT, excludes PREEMPT_RT, and excludes SCHED_CLASS_EXT, so it is off in essentially every shipping configuration — a kernel with sched_ext compiled in cannot have it at all. When it is disabled, struct rq declares donor and curr as a union of the same pointer and rq_set_donor() compiles to nothing, so the two spellings are the same variable and cost nothing. Reading rq->donor as “rq->curr, unless proxy execution is enabled” is therefore correct on any kernel a reader is likely to be running.

This lifecycle also explains why the scheduler feels distributed in the source tree. Wakeup code, tick code, load balancing, CPU frequency hooks, cgroup accounting, and class pickers all participate in one state machine. __schedule() is the serialization point, but not the whole story.

Revisiting the 2.6 → 7.0.12 Transition

The original comparison table is useful, but the scale of the architectural shift is easier to appreciate when more subsystems are lined up side by side. The modern scheduler is not merely a faster task picker; it is a more observable, more hierarchical, more energy-aware, and more security-conscious control plane.

Table 7-51. What Changed: Linux 2.6 vs 7.0.12 Scheduler
AspectLinux 2.6Linux 7.0.12
Fair scheduling algorithmO(1) priority bitmap + active/expired arraysEEVDF with virtual deadlines on RB-tree ordered entities
Interactive tuningHeuristic sleep bonuses and interactivity detectionLatency and fairness emerge from EEVDF accounting rather than ad hoc bonuses
Runnable accountingMainly queue lengths and timeslicesPELT util_avg, load_avg, runnable_avg, misfit and thermal signals
Deadline schedulingUnavailableSCHED_DEADLINE with EDF + CBS + admission control
Real-time containmentMore limited global RT handlingExplicit RT bandwidth period/runtime limits and stronger admission checks
Bandwidth control for fair workloadsMinimal group controlsCFS bandwidth control, hierarchical cgroup CPU quota, weight, and pressure visibility
Security against SMT side channelsNo core schedulingCookie-based core scheduling with forced idle fallback
Session fairnessNo autogroupAutomatic per-session fair grouping for desktop responsiveness
Observability of contentionLoad average and ad hoc countersPSI, /proc/[pid]/sched, /proc/schedstat, rich tracepoints
CPU placementTopology-aware domains, but simpler heuristicsWake affinity, capacity awareness, EAS, thermal pressure, NUMA balancing, sched_ext hooks
Energy model integrationSeparate from main scheduler thinkingEAS plus schedutil frequency selection from scheduler utilization
CPU frequency policyMostly external governors like ondemandIn-scheduler hooks through schedutil and utilization clamps
Task groupingBasic cgroup-era beginningsMature hierarchical task groups, autogroup, cpuset, cgroup v2 CPU controller
Starvation protection for fair tasksGlobal RT throttling was the only backstopPer-CPU deadline servers (fair_server, ext_server) reserve bandwidth for fair and sched_ext tasks under sustained RT load
ProgrammabilityKernel patches requiredsched_ext permits BPF-defined scheduling policy experiments
Preemption modelsPREEMPT_NONE or early low-latency preemption optionsPREEMPT_RT merged into mainline; PREEMPT_LAZY is the default and the only alternative to full preemption on x86_64 and ARM64, with the model selectable at boot via preempt=
Affinity semanticsImportant, but less intertwined with cgroups and energyAffinity intersects with cpusets, isolation, core scheduling, thermal capacity, and migration policy
User-space control surfaceMostly legacy policy/priority syscallssched_setattr(), uclamp, core-sched prctl(), pressure interfaces, cgroup v2 knobs
Debugging workflowProcfs and printk-heavyTracepoints, perf sched, BPF observability, PSI triggers, schedstats

Key Tunables Summary

The scheduler exposes many knobs, but a surprisingly small subset accounts for most real-world tuning. The following table focuses on the /proc/sys/kernel/sched_* settings that materially affect latency, bandwidth, or observability in Linux 7.0.12.

Table 7-52. Important /proc/sys/kernel/sched_* Tunables
KnobMeaningWhen to care
sched_autogroup_enabledEnable or disable autogroup session schedulingDesktop responsiveness experiments or troubleshooting surprising session-level fairness
sched_rr_timeslice_msRound-robin quantum for SCHED_RRRT systems that need a different equal-priority rotation cadence
sched_rt_period_usPeriod used by RT bandwidth accountingControlling how much CPU time RT workloads may monopolize globally
sched_rt_runtime_usRuntime budget for RT tasks inside each RT periodProtecting fair tasks from starvation while still allowing RT service
sched_cfs_bandwidth_slice_usTransfer slice for CFS bandwidth quota replenishmentQuota-heavy cgroup deployments where throttling granularity matters
sched_deadline_period_min_usMinimum accepted deadline periodValidating extremely small reservation requests
sched_deadline_period_max_usMaximum accepted deadline periodBounding impractically large deadline reservations
sched_util_clamp_minSystem-wide floor for utilization clamp minimaPlatform-level performance floor policies
sched_util_clamp_maxSystem-wide ceiling for utilization clamp maximaEnergy containment or thermal policy enforcement
sched_util_clamp_min_rt_defaultDefault uclamp minimum for RT tasksEnsuring RT tasks request an appropriate baseline capacity
sched_schedstatsEnable extra scheduler statistics collection; present only with CONFIG_SCHEDSTATS=y and gated by a static key that is off at boot unless schedstats=enable is passedDeep debugging and benchmarking; leave off in production so the accounting sites stay patched out
sched_energy_awareEnable or disable EAS where supportedPower/performance experiments on heterogeneous platforms

That is the complete sched_* set under /proc/sys/kernel/ in 7.0.12 — twelve entries, no more. Two further scheduler sysctls sit alongside them without the prefix, numa_balancing and numa_balancing_promote_rate_limit_MBps, which enable automatic NUMA page migration and cap how fast pages may be promoted toward a faster memory tier. They belong to the same tuning surface even though a sched_* glob will not find them.

Readers arriving from older documentation should note one absence. sched_child_runs_first, which asked the scheduler to place a freshly forked child ahead of its parent, no longer exists. It had already stopped doing anything in 6.6, when commit 5e963f2bd465 (“sched/fair: Commit to EEVDF”) removed the placement heuristic it fed, and the now-inert file was deleted outright in 6.7 by commit 17e7170645e3. The underlying reason is structural rather than incidental: EEVDF decides ordering from eligibility and virtual deadline, quantities the fork path can influence only by choosing the child's initial lag. There is no longer a place in the algorithm for a boolean that says “this task first”, so the knob could not have been preserved even in principle. Writing to the path today returns ENOENT.

Where the EEVDF Knobs Actually Live

A reader who searches /proc/sys/kernel/ for the fair scheduler's central tunable will not find it, and may reasonably conclude that EEVDF is not tunable at all. The conclusion is wrong; the location is. Since the CFS-era latency knobs were retired, the fair scheduler's parameters have lived under /sys/kernel/debug/sched/, registered by sched_init_debug() in kernel/sched/debug.c rather than by a sysctl table.

The move was deliberate. A file under /proc/sys/ is part of the kernel's stable interface to user space; a file under debugfs explicitly is not, and requires debugfs to be mounted and the reader to be privileged. Placing base_slice_ns there is the maintainers' way of saying that the value is an implementation detail of the current algorithm, not a contract — the same reasoning that let sched_latency_ns and sched_min_granularity_ns disappear when EEVDF replaced CFS. Tuning here is supported for experimentation and debugging, but nothing promises the file will survive the next algorithm change.

The entries most likely to matter are:

  • base_slice_ns — the default request size handed to every fair task that has not set a custom slice through sched_setattr(). This is the single knob that most directly trades throughput against latency under EEVDF, and it is the successor to the CFS latency target.
  • features — the read-write list of sched_feat() toggles. Writing NO_ in front of a name disables it, which makes this the fastest way to test whether a specific heuristic is responsible for an observed behavior.
  • preempt — selects the preemption model at runtime on kernels built with dynamic preemption, so none, voluntary, full, and lazy can be compared without rebooting.
  • migration_cost_ns and nr_migrate — the load balancer's estimate of what a migration costs and the cap on how many tasks one balancing pass may move.
  • numa_balancing/ — scan delay and period bounds, scan size, and the hot-page threshold used by automatic NUMA balancing.
  • fair_server/cpuN/ and ext_server/cpuN/ — per-CPU runtime and period for the deadline servers that guarantee bandwidth to fair and sched_ext tasks under RT pressure, described in Deadline Servers.
  • debug, verbose, and domains/ — read-only views of runqueue state, scheduler-domain construction, and per-domain flags.

The practical rule is a division by lifetime. Policy that user space is entitled to depend on — RT bandwidth limits, deadline admission bounds, uclamp ceilings — is a sysctl. Parameters of whichever algorithm currently implements fair scheduling are in debugfs. When a tuning guide names a file that does not exist, checking the other location is usually faster than searching the commit history.

Most scheduler tuning mistakes come from changing a low-level knob to mask an application-level problem. If latency is poor because threads spin with sched_yield(), or because a service's cgroup quota is too low, retuning global scheduler sysctls rarely fixes the real issue.

Debugging Tools and Further Reading Paths

The modern scheduler is observable enough that most performance investigations should begin with measurement rather than guesswork. Several interfaces are especially valuable:

Table 7-53. Useful Scheduler Debugging Interfaces
InterfaceWhat it showsTypical question answered
/proc/[pid]/schedPer-task scheduler statistics, vruntime-like state, switch counts, migration counts, wait metricsIs this task waiting too long, migrating too often, or being throttled?
/proc/[pid]/statusCpus_allowed, Cpus_allowed_list, and voluntary/involuntary context-switch counts. Policy and priority are not here; they are fields 18, 19, 40 and 41 of /proc/[pid]/statDid the affinity change take effect, and is the task being preempted or blocking?
/proc/schedstatPer-CPU and per-domain scheduler statistics; the leading version line reads 17 in 7.0.12 and is bumped whenever the format or a field’s meaning changesWhere is balancing time or idle-pull activity occurring?
sched:sched_switch, sched:sched_waking, sched:sched_wakeup, sched:sched_migrate_taskTrace events for switches, both halves of a wakeup, and migrationWhich event caused the latency spike or unexpected preemption?
perf sched timehistTimeline of runnable, running, and sleeping intervalsWhich tasks are blocking progress, and where are wakeup delays accumulating?
BPF / bpftraceProgrammable aggregation of scheduler eventsCan I count wakeups, off-CPU time, or switch reasons live with low friction?

Two proc files are especially good first stops. /proc/[pid]/sched gives a task-centric view that often reveals whether a workload is CPU-bound, I/O-bound, starved behind cgroup quota, or bouncing across CPUs. /proc/[pid]/status is simpler, and confirms the affinity mask and the split between voluntary and involuntary context switches — a ratio that by itself distinguishes a task that keeps blocking on I/O from one that keeps being preempted. If a tuning change “did nothing”, these files usually explain why.

It is worth being precise about which file holds what, because the natural guess is wrong. /proc/[pid]/status does not report scheduling policy or priority; task_state() in fs/proc/array.c emits Cpus_allowed, Cpus_allowed_list, voluntary_ctxt_switches, and nonvoluntary_ctxt_switches, and nothing about class membership. Policy and priority are in the machine-readable /proc/[pid]/stat, written by do_task_stat() as the numeric priority and nice fields and, near the end of the line, rt_priority and policy. In practice nobody should count fields by hand: chrt -p pid prints the policy and priority in words, and taskset -p pid prints the affinity mask. Confirming that a sched_setscheduler() call took effect is a job for chrt, not for grep on status.

/proc/schedstat and trace events become useful once the question broadens from one task to the whole machine. Tracepoints such as sched:sched_switch, sched:sched_wakeup, and sched:sched_migrate_task let tools reconstruct latency chains with far more precision than aggregate CPU usage can offer. perf sched record and perf sched timehist build directly on those tracepoints and are often the quickest route to a human-readable timeline.

Two Tracepoints for One Wakeup

Wakeup latency is the measurement people most often get subtly wrong, because a wakeup is not one event but two, and the interval between them is itself meaningful. trace_sched_waking() fires inside try_to_wake_up() on the waker’s CPU, under p->pi_lock, before select_task_rq() has chosen where the task will run. trace_sched_wakeup() fires later, from ttwu_do_wakeup(), at the moment the task is marked TASK_RUNNING on its target runqueue — which for a remote wakeup is on the target CPU, after the reschedule IPI has been delivered and sched_ttwu_pending() has drained the queue.

That gives three intervals rather than one, and each names a different problem:

  • sched_waking → sched_wakeup is delivery cost: lock contention on pi_lock or rq->lock, the cost of select_task_rq(), and IPI latency to the target CPU. A large value here points at the wakeup path itself, often on a machine where the target was in a deep idle state.
  • sched_wakeup → sched_switch (with the woken task as next) is runqueue delay: the task was runnable and eligible, but something else held the CPU. This is the number that scheduler tuning actually influences.
  • sched_waking → sched_switch is the end-to-end latency the application perceives, and is the sum of the other two.

A tool that correlates only sched_wakeup with sched_switch therefore reports runqueue delay while appearing to report wakeup latency, and will show near-zero latency on a system whose real problem is IPI delivery into idle CPUs. Conversely, attributing the whole sched_waking-to-sched_switch interval to the scheduler blames it for idle-exit costs it does not control. perf sched latency and the standard BPF wakeup tools use sched_waking as the start event for exactly this reason; when writing a custom bpftrace script, prefer it and record both boundaries so the two components can be separated afterwards.

One further subtlety: a newly forked task emits sched_wakeup_new, not sched_wakeup. Scripts that key only on sched_wakeup silently miss every process start, which matters when the workload under study is fork-heavy.

BPF tools are increasingly the lightweight middle ground between procfs inspection and full trace capture. Useful one-liners include counting switches by process name, measuring off-CPU time, or printing wakeup-to-run latency histograms. A typical bpftrace command might aggregate tracepoint:sched:sched_switch by comm, while another correlates sched_wakeup and sched_switch to show runqueue delay for one PID.

  • perf sched timehist — quick runnable/running/sleeping timeline for the whole machine.
  • perf sched record followed by perf sched latency — capture and then summarize wakeup-to-run delays.
  • bpftrace -e 'tracepoint:sched:sched_switch { @[comm] = count(); }' — count context switches by task name.
  • bpftrace -e 'tracepoint:sched:sched_wakeup { @[pid] = count(); }' — see which PIDs are being awakened most often.

Those tools complement rather than replace one another. Procfs is ideal for a static snapshot of one task, perf sched is ideal for timeline reconstruction, and BPF is ideal for low-friction custom aggregation when you already know which scheduler event you care about.

Put differently, the scheduler is no longer a black box that one debugs by intuition alone. Between per-task procfs state, schedstats, tracepoints, perf, PSI, and BPF, Linux 7.0.12 gives both kernel engineers and application developers a rich path from “the system feels slow” to a concrete explanation.

The kernel source files this chapter works from are collected by topic in Source Trails.
